\tilde{x}\in\widetilde{\mathcal{P}}
,
\gamma:[0,1]\rightarrow\mathcal{P}
an arc inside the equivalence class defined by
\tilde{x}
,
V
an open disk in
\mathcal{P}-\Gamma
containing
x
and
s\in(0,1)
such that
\gamma([s,1])\subset V
. We define the set
\widetilde{V}:=\{\widetilde{\delta}|\delta\in\text{Curv}_{1},\forall t\in[0,s]\quad\delta(t)=\gamma(t),\delta([s,1])\subset V\}
to be an open neighborhood of
\tilde{x}
in
\widetilde{\mathcal{P}}
. It is easy to see that
\widetilde{V}
does not depend on our choice of
\gamma
or
s
. We endow
\widetilde{\mathcal{P}}
with the topology generated by the previous open sets. It is known that for our previous choice of topology the map
\tilde{\pi}
is continuous and that for every
\widetilde{x}\in\widetilde{\mathcal{P}}
the set

\{\widetilde{W}|W\text{ open disk in $\mathcal{P}-\Gamma$ containing $\tilde{\pi}(x)$}\}

forms a neighborhood basis of
\tilde{x}
in
\widetilde{\mathcal{P}}
.

Now let us define the space
\overline{\mathcal{P}}
. Take
\text{Curv}_{2}=\{\gamma:[0,1]\overset{C^{0}}{\rightarrow}\mathcal{P}|\penalty\ \gamma(0)=x_{0},\gamma[0,1)\cap\Gamma=\emptyset\}
. For any
\gamma_{1},\gamma_{2}\in\text{Curv}_{2}
, we will say that
\gamma_{1}
and
\gamma_{2}
are
homotopic for
\sim_{2}
or
\gamma_{1}\sim_{2}\gamma_{2}
if there exists
H:[0,1]^{2}\overset{C^{0}}{\rightarrow}\mathcal{P}
such that
H(0,\cdot)=\gamma_{1}
,
H(1,\cdot)=\gamma_{2}
,
H(\cdot,1)
is constant and for every
t\in[0,1]
we have
H(t,\cdot)\in\text{Curv}_{2}
. We define

\overline{\mathcal{P}}:=\text{Curv}_{2}\big/\sim_{2}

We are going to call
\overline{\mathcal{P}}
the
bifoliated plane of
\rho
up to surgeries on
\Gamma
. Any point
\overline{x}\in\overline{\mathcal{P}}
corresponds to an equivalence class of arcs of
\text{Curv}_{2}
starting from
x_{0}
and ending at the same point of
\mathcal{P}
, say
x
. We define the projection
\overline{\pi}
from
\overline{\mathcal{P}}
on
\mathcal{P}
as the function associating to
\overline{x}
the point
x
. The function
\overline{\pi}
is clearly a surjection.

Let us remark at this moment that by definition,
\widetilde{\mathcal{P}}
is naturally included in
\overline{\mathcal{P}}
. Indeed, following our previous notations, notice that
\text{Curv}_{1}\subset\text{Curv}_{2}
. More precisely,
\text{Curv}_{2}-\text{Curv}_{1}
consists exactly of the curves in
\text{Curv}_{2}
ending at a point of
\Gamma
. Moreover, if
\gamma_{1},\gamma_{2}\in\text{Curv}_{1}\subset\text{Curv}_{2}
, then
\gamma_{1}\sim_{1}\gamma_{2}
if and only if
\gamma_{1}\sim_{2}\gamma_{2}
. This implies the existence of an inclusion map
\phi:\widetilde{\mathcal{P}}\rightarrow\overline{\mathcal{P}}
such that
\widetilde{\pi}=\overline{\pi}\circ\phi
and
\overline{\mathcal{P}}-\phi(\widetilde{\mathcal{P}})=\overline{\Gamma}
, where
\overline{\Gamma}:=\overline{\pi}^{-1}(\Gamma)
. One can thus think of the points in
\overline{\Gamma}
as points in the boundary at infinity of
\widetilde{\mathcal{P}}
.

Let us now define a topology on
\overline{\mathcal{P}}
. In order to do so, we will provide an alternative definition of
\overline{\mathcal{P}}
using a smooth structure on
\mathcal{P}
.

6.1.1.
A geometric construction of
\overline{\mathcal{P}}
and its topology

Endow
\mathcal{P}
with a smooth manifold structure and blow-up every point in
\Gamma
to a circle. By doing so, since
\Gamma
is discrete (see Proposition
5.2
), we obtain a surface
\mathcal{P}_{exp}
3
3

3

exp stands for explosion
homeomorphic to a plane minus a discrete infinite set of open disks. Notice that there exists a smooth projection map
p_{exp}:\mathcal{P}_{exp}\rightarrow\mathcal{P}
, whose restriction in the interior of
\mathcal{P}_{exp}
is a diffeomorphism.

Denote by
X_{0}\in\mathcal{P}_{exp}
the unique preimage by
p_{exp}
of
x_{0}
(the base point of
\mathcal{P}
used in the definition of
\text{Curv}_{1}
,
\text{Curv}_{2}
) and by
\text{Curv}_{3}
the set of continuous arcs in
\mathcal{P}_{exp}
starting from
X_{0}
. By classical covering space theory, the space
\widetilde{\mathcal{P}_{exp}}
obtained when we identify any two arcs in
\text{Curv}_{3}
up to homotopy relatively to their boundaries is the universal cover of
\mathcal{P}_{exp}
. Notice that
\widetilde{\mathcal{P}_{exp}}
is a surface with infinite many line boundaries and that every point in
\widetilde{\mathcal{P}_{exp}}
, or otherwise said every class of arcs in
\text{Curv}_{3}
up to homotopy relatively to their boundaries, is projected by
p_{exp}
to unique class of homotopic arcs for
\sim_{2}
in
\text{Curv}_{2}
or otherwise said a unique element in
\overline{\mathcal{P}}
.

The previous map from
\widetilde{\mathcal{P}_{exp}}
to
\overline{\mathcal{P}}
is surjective, but not injective. Indeed, two different curves in
\text{Curv}_{3}
that are very close and that end at different points of the same boundary circle in
\mathcal{P}_{exp}
always fail to be homotopic relatively to their boundaries in
\mathcal{P}_{exp}
; however their projections in
\mathcal{P}
by
p_{exp}
are close and end at the same point in
\Gamma
, thus they are homotopic for
\sim_{2}
.

Let us now define a homotopy
\sim_{3}
between arcs in
\text{Curv}_{3}
in order to repair this injectivity default. Consider
\gamma_{1},\gamma_{2}\in\text{Curv}_{3}
. We will say that
\gamma_{1}
and
\gamma_{2}
are
homotopic for
\sim_{3}
or
\gamma_{1}\sim_{3}\gamma_{2}
if one of the two following properties is satisfied:

•

\gamma_{1}(1)
belongs in the interior of
\mathcal{P}_{exp}
and
there exists
H:[0,1]^{2}\overset{C^{0}}{\rightarrow}\mathcal{P}_{exp}
such that

H(0,\cdot)=\gamma_{1},H(1,\cdot)=\gamma_{2},H(\cdot,0)=X_{0},\text{ and }H(\cdot,1)=\gamma_{1}(1)

•

\gamma_{1}(1)
belongs in a circle boundary component
C
of
\mathcal{P}_{exp}
and there exists
H:[0,1]^{2}\overset{C^{0}}{\rightarrow}\mathcal{P}
such that

H(0,\cdot)=\gamma_{1},H(1,\cdot)=\gamma_{2},H(\cdot,0)=X_{0},\text{ and }H(\cdot,1)\in C

One can easily check that :

Proposition 6.1
.

Let
\gamma_{1},\gamma_{2}\in\text{Curv}_{3}
. We have that
\gamma_{1}\sim_{3}\gamma_{2}
if and only if
p_{exp}(\gamma_{1})\sim_{2}p_{exp}(\gamma_{2})

Thanks to the above proposition we get that the map
p_{exp}
is well defined on the quotient by
\sim_{3}
; hence there exists a bijection

(6.1)

\phi_{exp}:\text{Curv}_{3}\big/\sim_{3}\longrightarrow\overline{\mathcal{P}}=\text{Curv}_{2}\big/\sim_{2}

Moreover, it is easy to see that the space
\text{Curv}_{3}
quotiented by
\sim_{3}
is the surface obtained by collapsing all boundary lines of
\widetilde{\mathcal{P}_{exp}}
to points. Denote by
proj
the projection map from
\widetilde{\mathcal{P}_{exp}}
to
\text{Curv}_{3}\big/\sim_{3}
. We endow
\text{Curv}_{3}\big/\sim_{3}
with the quotient topology for
proj
and
\overline{\mathcal{P}}
with the topology rendering
\phi_{exp}
a homeomorphism.

The previous construction of
\overline{\mathcal{P}}
and its topology relies on the usage of a smooth manifold structure on
\mathcal{P}
. Keeping in mind that Markovian actions are only continuous, the previous definition of the topology on
\overline{\mathcal{P}}
is not always easy to utilize in the
C^{0}
setting. In the following lines, we would like to provide an alternative and also useful construction of the previous topology on
\overline{\mathcal{P}}
, which resembles closely the construction of the topology of
\widetilde{\mathcal{P}}
that we described in Section
6.1
.

6.1.2.
A second construction of the topology of
\overline{\mathcal{P}}

Let
\overline{x}\in\overline{\mathcal{P}}-\overline{\Gamma}
,
x=\overline{\pi}(\overline{x})
and
U
an open disk in
\mathcal{P}-\Gamma
containing
x
. As in the case of
\widetilde{\mathcal{P}}
, for any
\delta\in\text{Curv}_{2}
, we will denote by
\overline{\delta}
the equivalence class for
\sim_{2}
containing the curve
\delta
. Consider
\gamma\in\text{Curv}_{2}
a curve contained in the equivalence class defined by
\overline{x}
and
s\in[0,1]
such that
\gamma([s,1])\subset U
(see Figure
6.1
). We define the set

\overline{V}(U)=\{\overline{\delta}|\delta\in\text{Curv}_{2},\forall t\in[0,s]\quad\delta(t)=\gamma(t),\delta([s,1])\subset U\}

to be a neighbourhood of
\overline{x}
in
\overline{\mathcal{P}}
. It is easy to see that
\overline{V}(U)
depends only on our choice of
U
and not on our choice of
\gamma
or
s
.

Figure 6.1
.
A neighborhood of a point in
\overline{\mathcal{P}}-\overline{\Gamma}

Special care is needed when defining the neighborhoods of the points in
\overline{\Gamma}
. Fix
\overline{y}\in\overline{\Gamma}
,
y=\overline{\pi}(\overline{y})
,
S
any standard polygon containing
y
in its interior,
(U_{n})_{n\in\mathbb{Z}}
a sequence of (embedded) closed disks in
S
containing
y
such that

(1)

for every
n\in\mathbb{Z}
there exists
Q_{n}
a quadrant of
y
in
S
such that
U_{n}\subseteq Q_{n}
and
U_{n}
is a neighborhood of
y
in
Q_{n}

(2)

for every
n\in\mathbb{Z}
we have that
Q_{n+1}
is the quadrant right after
Q_{n}
in the counter-clockwise order given by our orientation of
\mathcal{P}

Any sequence of disks
(U_{n})_{n\in\mathbb{Z}}
in
\mathcal{P}
satisfying the previous conditions will be called
a sequence of quadrant neighborhoods of
y\in\Gamma
.

Fix
(U_{n})_{n\in\mathbb{Z}}
of
\mathcal{P}
a sequence of quadrant neighborhoods of
y
. Consider
\gamma_{0}
a curve in
\text{Curv}_{2}
going from
x_{0}
(the base point in
\text{Curv}_{2}
) to a point in the interior of
U_{0}
such that for any embedded arc
e
in
U_{0}
going from the endpoint of
\gamma_{0}
to
y
, the juxtaposition of
\gamma_{0}
followed by
e
gives a curve in
\text{Curv}_{2}
inside the equivalence class defined by
\overline{y}
. Any curve
\gamma_{0}
in
\text{Curv}_{2}
satisfying the previous conditions will be called
a reference curve
for the sequence of quadrant neighborhoods
(U_{n})_{n\in\mathbb{Z}}
and the point
\overline{y}
.

Figure 6.2
.
A neighborhood of a point in
\overline{\Gamma}

We define by induction the curves
\gamma_{n}
for any
n\in\mathbb{Z}-\{0\}
as follows (see Figure
6.2
):

•

if
n>0
then
\gamma_{n}:[0,1]\rightarrow\mathcal{P}
is a curve obtained by the juxtaposition of
\gamma_{n-1}
followed by an arc contained in the interior of
U_{n-1}\cup U_{n}
and going from the endpoint of
\gamma_{n-1}
to any point in the interior of
U_{n}

•

if
n<0
then
\gamma_{n}:[0,1]\rightarrow\mathcal{P}
is a curve obtained by the juxtaposition of
\gamma_{n+1}
followed by an arc contained in the interior of
U_{n+1}\cup U_{n}
and going from the endpoint of
\gamma_{n+1}
to a point in the interior of
U_{n}

Let
s_{n}\in(0,1)
be such that
\gamma_{n}([s_{n},1])\subset U_{n}
. We define the following set to be a neighborhood of
\overline{y}
:

\overline{V}_{\overline{y}}(\gamma_{0},(U_{n})_{n\in\mathbb{Z}})=\underset{n\in\mathbb{Z}}{\bigcup}\{\overline{\delta}|\delta\in\text{Curv}_{2},\forall t\in[0,s_{n}]\quad\delta(t)=\gamma_{n}(t),\delta([s_{n},1])\subset U_{n}\}

A representation of
\overline{V}_{\overline{y}}(\gamma_{0},(U_{n})_{n\in\mathbb{Z}})
using our geometric construction of
\overline{\mathcal{P}}
can be found in Figure
6.3
. One can check that
\overline{V}_{\overline{y}}(\gamma_{0},(U_{n})_{n\in\mathbb{Z}})
only depends on our choice of
S
,
(U_{n})_{n\in\mathbb{Z}}
and of
\gamma_{0}
and does not depend on our choice of
s_{n}
(for
n\in\mathbb{Z}
) or
\gamma_{n}
(for
n\neq 0
).

We endow
\overline{\mathcal{P}}
with the topology generated by all the previous neighborhoods.

Figure 6.3
.
In the figure in the left, we see a representation of a sequence of quadrant neighborhoods of
y
in
\mathcal{P}_{exp}
and
\widetilde{\mathcal{P}_{exp}}
. In the figure in the right, we collapse the line boundary of the left picture to a point in order to obtain a representation of
\overline{V}_{\overline{y}}(\gamma_{0},(U_{n})_{n\in\mathbb{Z}})
as a neighborhood of
\overline{y}
.

The reason why we defined the neighborhoods of the points in
\overline{\Gamma}
so much differently than the neighborhoods of the points in
\overline{\mathcal{P}}-\overline{\Gamma}
can be explained by using our geometric construction of
\overline{\mathcal{P}}
: a point
\overline{y}\in\overline{\Gamma}
corresponds to a boundary line
\widetilde{C}
in
\widetilde{\mathcal{P}_{exp}}
that was collapsed to a point. If
\widetilde{C}
projects to the boundary circle
C
in
\mathcal{P}_{exp}
, then the lifts on
\widetilde{\mathcal{P}_{exp}}
of the neighborhoods of
C
inside
\mathcal{P}_{exp}
do not form a basis of neighborhoods of
\widetilde{C}
inside
\widetilde{\mathcal{P}_{exp}}
. In other words, if we would like that the topologies defined in Sections
6.1.1
and
6.1.2
coincide, then contrary to the case of the points inside
\overline{\mathcal{P}}-\overline{\Gamma}
, one can not construct a basis of neighborhoods of
\overline{y}\in\overline{\Gamma}
by simply lifting on
\overline{\mathcal{P}}
different neighborhoods of its projection
\overline{\pi}(\overline{y})\in\mathcal{P}
.

6.2.
\overline{\mathcal{P}}
as a topological space

Denote by
\mathcal{T}
the topology on
\overline{\mathcal{P}}
generated by the neighborhoods defined in Section
6.1.2
and by
\mathcal{T}_{geom}
the topology on
\overline{\mathcal{P}}
defined in Section
6.1.1
thanks to our geometric construction. Our main goals in this section consist in constructing a base for
\mathcal{T}
(see Proposition
6.4
) and in proving that the topologies
\mathcal{T}
and
\mathcal{T}_{geom}
are equivalent (see Theorem
6.5
). In order to do so, we will first need to prove two useful propositions.

Proposition 6.2
.

Consider
\overline{y}\in\overline{\Gamma},\overline{\pi}{(\overline{y})}=y
,
S
a standard polygon containing
y
in its interior,
x\in S-y
,
(U_{n})_{n\in\mathbb{Z}}
a sequence of quadrant neighborhoods of
y
in
S
and
\gamma_{0}
a reference curve for
(U_{n})_{n\in\mathbb{Z}}
and
\overline{y}
. Denote by
\gamma,\gamma^{\prime}
any two curves in
\text{Curv}_{2}
going from
x_{0}
(the base point in
\text{Curv}_{2}
) to
x
and such that for any embedded arc
e\subset S
going from
x
to
y
, the juxtaposition of
\gamma
followed by
e
and the juxtaposition of
\gamma^{\prime}
followed by
e
define two curves in
\text{Curv}_{2}
that belong in the equivalence class defined by
\overline{y}
.

Denote by
\delta
a curve in
S-y
starting and ending at
x
and going once around
y
in the counter-clockwise direction. We have that there exists
k\in\mathbb{N}
such that one of the two following situations arises:

•

the juxtaposition of the curve
\gamma
followed
k
times by the curve
\delta
defines a curve homotopic to
\gamma^{\prime}
for
\sim_{2}

•

the juxtaposition of the curve
\gamma^{\prime}
followed
k
times by the curve
\delta
defines a curve homotopic to
\gamma
for
\sim_{2}

.

Proposition 6.3
.

Consider
\overline{y}\in\overline{\Gamma},\overline{\pi}{(\overline{y})}=y
,
S
a standard polygon containing
y
in its interior,
2p
be the total number of quadrants of
y
in
S
,
(U_{n})_{n\in\mathbb{Z}}
a sequence of quadrant neighborhoods of
y
in
S
and
\gamma_{0}
a reference curve for
(U_{n})_{n\in\mathbb{Z}}
and
\overline{y}
. Fix
k\in\mathbb{N}
(resp.
k\in-\mathbb{N}
) and
l\in\llbracket 0,2p-1\rrbracket
(resp.
l\in-\llbracket 0,2p-1\rrbracket
). Denote by
\gamma_{0}^{\prime}
a curve in
\mathcal{P}
obtained by the juxtaposition of
\gamma_{0}
followed by a curve in
S-y
turning counter-clockwise (resp. clockwise) around
y
and stopping at a point in the interior of
U_{2pk+l}
after making
k
(resp.
-k
) full turns around
y
. We have that

\overline{V}_{\overline{y}}(\gamma_{0}^{\prime},(U_{n+2pk+l})_{n\in\mathbb{Z}})=\overline{V}_{\overline{y}}(\gamma_{0},(U_{n})_{n\in\mathbb{Z}})

Proposition
6.3
is a relatively easy consequence of the construction of
\overline{V}_{\overline{y}}(\gamma_{0},(U_{n})_{n\in\mathbb{Z}})
; we thus leave its proof to the reader. Let us now prove Proposition
6.2
.

Proof of Proposition
6.2
.

In order to prove the desired result, we will make use of our geometric construction of
\overline{\mathcal{P}}
(see Section
6.1.1
). Endow
\mathcal{P}
with a smooth structure and blow-up every point in
\Gamma
in order to obtain
\mathcal{P}_{exp}
, a surface homeomorphic to a plane minus a countable discrete set of open disks. Denote by
p_{exp}
the projection map from
\mathcal{P}_{exp}
to
\mathcal{P}
. Recall that the restriction of
p_{exp}
in the interior of
\mathcal{P}_{exp}
is a diffeomorphism.

Take
e:[0,1]\rightarrow S
an arc with no self-intersections going from
x
to
y
and denote by
\gamma_{exp},\gamma_{exp}^{\prime}:[0,1]\rightarrow\mathcal{P}_{exp}
the curves
p_{exp}^{-1}\circ\gamma
and
p_{exp}^{-1}\circ\gamma^{\prime}
. By eventually performing a homotopy in
\sim_{2}
, we can assume that
p_{exp}^{-1}\circ e_{|[0,1)}
extends continuously to a curve
e_{exp}:[0,1]\rightarrow\mathcal{P}_{exp}
ending at some point in the boundary component
C
of
\mathcal{P}_{exp}
. Let
(\gamma e)_{exp}
(resp.
\gamma e
,
(\gamma^{\prime}e)_{exp}
,
\gamma^{\prime}e
) be the curve obtained by the juxtaposition of
\gamma_{exp}
(resp.
\gamma
,
\gamma^{\prime}_{exp}
,
\gamma^{\prime}
) followed by
e_{exp}
(resp.
e
,
e_{exp}
,
e
). The curves
(\gamma e)_{exp}
and
(\gamma^{\prime}e)_{exp}
belong in
\text{Curv}_{3}
, begin at
X_{0}=p_{exp}^{-1}(x_{0})
and end at the same point in
C
. By Proposition
6.1
, since
\gamma e\sim_{2}\gamma^{\prime}e
, the curves
(\gamma e)_{exp}
and
(\gamma^{\prime}e)_{exp}
are homotopic for
\sim_{3}
. Therefore,
(\gamma e)_{exp}
can be obtained from
(\gamma^{\prime}e)_{exp}
by performing a homotopy forcing
(\gamma^{\prime}e)_{exp}
to go around
C
a certain number of times. In other words, if
x
is the common endpoint of
\gamma
and
\gamma^{\prime}
,
X=p_{exp}^{-1}(x)
and
\delta_{exp}=p_{exp}^{-1}\circ\delta
, then up to exchanging the roles of
\gamma_{exp}
and
\gamma^{\prime}_{exp}
, there exists
k\in\mathbb{N}
such that the juxtaposition of
\gamma_{exp}
followed
k
times by
\delta_{exp}
and finally by
e_{exp}
is homotopic relatively to its boundary to
(\gamma^{\prime}e)_{exp}
. This gives us the desired result.
∎

We are now ready to begin proving the two main results of this section.

Proposition 6.4
.

(1)

Fix
\overline{x}\in\overline{\mathcal{P}}-\overline{\Gamma}
and
x=\overline{\pi}(x)
. For any open disk
W
inside
\mathcal{P}-\Gamma
containing
x
, denote by
\overline{V}_{\overline{x}}(W)
the neighborhood of
\overline{x}
associated to
W
that was constructed in Section
6.1.2
. The following set forms a neighborhood basis of
\overline{x}

\{\overline{V}_{\overline{x}}(W)|W\text{ open disk in $\mathcal{P}-\Gamma$ containing $x$}\}

(2)

Fix
\overline{y}\in\overline{\Gamma}
,
y=\overline{\pi}(y)
and
S
a standard polygon containing
y
. For any sequence of quadrant neighborhoods
(U_{n})_{n\in\mathbb{Z}}
of
y
in
S
and any reference curve
\gamma_{0}
denote by
\overline{V}_{\overline{y}}(\gamma_{0},(U_{n})_{n\in\mathbb{Z}})
the neighborhood of
\overline{y}
associated to
\gamma_{0}
and
(U_{n})_{n\in\mathbb{Z}}
that was constructed in Section
6.1.2
. The following set forms a neighborhood basis of
\overline{y}

\displaystyle\Bigl\{\overline{V}_{\overline{y}}(\gamma_{0},(U_{n})_{n\in\mathbb{Z}})|

\displaystyle(U_{n})_{n\in\mathbb{Z}}\text{ is a sequence of quadrant neighborhoods of $y$ in $S$ }

\displaystyle\gamma_{0}\text{ is a reference curve for $(U_{n})_{n\in\mathbb{Z}}$ and $\overline{y}$}\Bigl\}

Proof.

Take
\overline{x}\in\overline{\mathcal{P}}-\overline{\Gamma}
and
\mathcal{V}_{\overline{x}}
the set of subsets
\mathcal{N}
of
\overline{\mathcal{P}}
for each one of which there exists
W
an open disk in
\mathcal{P}-\Gamma
containing
x
such that
\overline{V}_{\overline{x}}(W)\subset\mathcal{N}
. The set
\mathcal{V}_{\overline{x}}
satisfies the following properties:

(1)

\overline{\mathcal{P}}\in\mathcal{V}_{\overline{x}}

(2)

every element of
\mathcal{V}_{\overline{x}}
contains
\overline{x}

(3)

if
\mathcal{N}\in\mathcal{V}_{\overline{x}}
and
\mathcal{N}\subset\mathcal{N}^{\prime}
then
\mathcal{N}^{\prime}\in\mathcal{V}_{\overline{x}}

(4)

for every element
\mathcal{N}
of
\mathcal{V}_{\overline{x}}
there exists
\mathcal{N}^{\prime}\in\mathcal{V}_{\overline{x}}
a subset of
\mathcal{N}
such that for all
\overline{u}\in\mathcal{N}^{\prime}
we have that
\mathcal{N}\in\mathcal{V}_{\overline{u}}

(5)

\mathcal{V}_{\overline{x}}
is stable by finite intersections

Indeed, the proofs of (1)-(3) are direct applications of the definition of
\mathcal{V}_{\overline{x}}
. Let us now prove (4). Take
\mathcal{N}\in\mathcal{V}_{\overline{x}}
containing
\overline{V}_{\overline{x}}(W)
, where
W
is an open disk in
\mathcal{P}-\Gamma
containing
x
. It is easy to see that
\overline{V}_{\overline{x}}(W)\in\mathcal{V}_{\overline{u}}
for every
\overline{u}\in\overline{V}_{\overline{x}}(W)
. Hence, thanks to (3),
\mathcal{N}\in\mathcal{V}_{\overline{u}}
for every
\overline{u}\in\overline{V}_{\overline{x}}(W)
. Finally, the proof of (5) follows from the fact that for any
W,W^{\prime}
open disks inside
\mathcal{P}-\Gamma
containing
x
, we have
\overline{V}_{\overline{x}}(D)\subset\overline{V}_{\overline{x}}(W)\cap\overline{V}_{\overline{x}}(W^{\prime})
, where
D\subset W\cap W^{\prime}
is an open disk containing
x
.

Thanks to the above facts and by definition of the topology generated by a family of neighborhoods, the set
\mathcal{V}_{\overline{x}}
is exactly the set of all neighborhoods of
\overline{x}
in
\overline{\mathcal{P}}
with respect to the topology
\mathcal{T}
, which finishes the proof of the first part of the proposition.

Take now
\overline{y}\in\overline{\Gamma}
,
S
a standard polygon of
y
and
\mathcal{V}_{\overline{y}}
the set of subsets
\mathcal{N}
of
\overline{\mathcal{P}}
for each one of which there exist
(U_{n})_{n\in\mathbb{Z}}
a sequence of quadrant neighborhoods of
y
in
S
and
\gamma_{0}
a reference curve for
(U_{n})_{n\in\mathbb{Z}}
and
\overline{y}
such that
\overline{V}_{\overline{y}}(\gamma_{0},(U_{n})_{n\in\mathbb{Z}})\subset\mathcal{N}
.

Similarly to before, let us prove that the set
\mathcal{V}_{\overline{y}}
satisfies the following properties:

(1)

\overline{\mathcal{P}}\in\mathcal{V}_{\overline{y}}

(2)

every element of
\mathcal{V}_{\overline{y}}
contains
\overline{y}

(3)

if
\mathcal{N}\in\mathcal{V}_{\overline{y}}
and
\mathcal{N}\subset\mathcal{N}^{\prime}
, then
\mathcal{N}^{\prime}\in\mathcal{V}_{\overline{y}}

(4)

for every element
\mathcal{N}
of
\mathcal{V}_{\overline{y}}
there exists
\mathcal{N}^{\prime}\in\mathcal{V}_{\overline{y}}
a subset of
\mathcal{N}
such that for all
\overline{u}\in\mathcal{N}^{\prime}
we have that
\mathcal{N}\in\mathcal{V}_{\overline{u}}

(5)

\mathcal{V}_{\overline{y}}
is stable by finite intersections

(6)

\mathcal{V}_{\overline{y}}
does not depend on our choice of standard polygon
S

As before, the proofs of (1)-(3) follow directly from the definition of
\mathcal{V}_{\overline{y}}
. Let us now explain why (4) is true. Consider
\mathcal{N}
an element of
\mathcal{V}_{\overline{y}}
containing
\overline{V}_{\overline{y}}(\gamma_{0},(U_{n})_{n\in\mathbb{Z}})
, where
(U_{n})_{n\in\mathbb{Z}}
is a sequence of quadrant neighborhoods of
y
in
S
and
\gamma_{0}
is a reference curve for
(U_{n})_{n\in\mathbb{Z}}
and
\overline{y}
. Denote by
Q_{n}
the quadrant of
y
in
S
containing
U_{n}
. For every
n\in\mathbb{Z}
consider
U^{\prime}_{n}\subset U_{n}
a very small closed disk forming a neighborhood of
y
in
Q_{n}
. The sequence
(U^{\prime}_{n})_{n\in\mathbb{Z}}
defines a sequence of quadrant neighborhoods of
y
such that
\overline{V}_{\overline{y}}(\gamma_{0},(U^{\prime}_{n})_{n\in\mathbb{Z}})\subset\overline{V}_{\overline{y}}(\gamma_{0},(U_{n})_{n\in\mathbb{Z}})
. Moreover, by eventually taking the disks in
(U^{\prime}_{n})_{n\in\mathbb{Z}}
smaller, it is easy to see that
\overline{V}_{\overline{y}}(\gamma_{0},(U_{n})_{n\in\mathbb{Z}})\in\mathcal{V}_{\overline{u}}
for every
\overline{u}\in\overline{V}_{\overline{y}}(\gamma_{0},(U^{\prime}_{n})_{n\in\mathbb{Z}})
, which implies thanks to (3) that
\mathcal{N}\in\mathcal{V}_{\overline{u}}
for every
\overline{u}\in\overline{V}_{\overline{y}}(\gamma_{0},(U^{\prime}_{n})_{n\in\mathbb{Z}})
. This finishes the proof of (4).

Next, let us prove (5). Consider
\mathcal{N},\mathcal{N}^{\prime}
two sets in
\mathcal{V}_{\overline{y}}
containing respectively
\overline{V}_{\overline{y}}(\gamma_{0},(U_{n})_{n\in\mathbb{Z}})
and
\overline{V}_{\overline{y}}(\gamma^{\prime}_{0},(U^{\prime}_{n})_{n\in\mathbb{Z}})
, where
(U_{n})_{n\in\mathbb{Z}},(U^{\prime}_{n})_{n\in\mathbb{Z}}
are two sequences of quadrant neighborhoods of
y
in
S
,
\gamma_{0}
is a reference curve for
(U_{n})_{n\in\mathbb{Z}}
and
\overline{y}
, and
\gamma^{\prime}_{0}
is a reference curve for
(U^{\prime}_{n})_{n\in\mathbb{Z}}
and
\overline{y}
. Thanks to Proposition
6.3
, by an eventual reindexation of
(U^{\prime}_{n})_{n\in\mathbb{Z}}
and a modification of our choice of reference curve
\gamma_{0}^{\prime}
, we can assume that
\overset{\circ}{U_{0}}\cap\overset{\circ}{U^{\prime}_{0}}\neq\emptyset
. Furthermore, thanks to the same proposition (applied for
k=l=0
) by eventually changing once again our choices of
\gamma_{0}
and
\gamma_{0}^{\prime}
, we can assume without any loss of generality that
\gamma_{0}
and
\gamma_{0}^{\prime}
end at the same point. Finally,
by Proposition
6.3
and Proposition
6.2
, by eventually reindexing
(U^{\prime}_{n})_{n\in\mathbb{Z}}
we can also assume that
\gamma_{0}=\gamma^{\prime}_{0}
. It follows that there exist
(U_{n})_{n\in\mathbb{Z}},(U^{\prime}_{n})_{n\in\mathbb{Z}}
two sequences of quadrant neighborhoods of
y
and
\gamma_{0}
a reference curve for both
(U_{n})_{n\in\mathbb{Z}}
and
(U^{\prime}_{n})_{n\in\mathbb{Z}}
such that

\overline{V}_{\overline{y}}(\gamma_{0},(U_{n})_{n\in\mathbb{Z}})\subset\mathcal{N}\text{ and }\overline{V}_{\overline{y}}(\gamma_{0},(U^{\prime}_{n})_{n\in\mathbb{Z}})\subset\mathcal{N}^{\prime}

Denote by
Q_{n}
the quadrant of
y
in
S
containing
U_{n}
and
U^{\prime}_{n}
. For every
n\in\mathbb{Z}
consider
U^{\prime\prime}_{n}\subset U_{n}\cap U^{\prime}_{n}
a closed disk forming a neighborhood of
y
inside
Q_{n}
. It is easy to see that

\overline{V}_{\overline{y}}(\gamma_{0},(U^{\prime\prime}_{n})_{n\in\mathbb{Z}})\subset\overline{V}_{\overline{y}}(\gamma_{0},(U_{n})_{n\in\mathbb{Z}})\cap\overline{V}_{\overline{y}}(\gamma_{0},(U^{\prime}_{n})_{n\in\mathbb{Z}})

which implies that
\mathcal{N}\cap\mathcal{N}^{\prime}\in\mathcal{V}_{\overline{y}}
. This finishes the proof of (5).

Finally, consider
\mathcal{V}_{\overline{y},S}:=\mathcal{V}_{\overline{y}}
and
\mathcal{V}_{\overline{y},S^{\prime}}
the set defined in the same way as
\mathcal{V}_{\overline{y},S}
after replacing
S
by another standard polygon
S^{\prime}
containing
y
in its interior. Similarly to our proof of (5), for any two elements
\mathcal{N}\in\mathcal{V}_{\overline{y},S}
and
\mathcal{N}^{\prime}\in\mathcal{V}_{\overline{y},S^{\prime}}
, we can construct a small neighborhood of
\overline{y}
of the form
\overline{V}_{\overline{y}}(\gamma_{0},(U^{\prime\prime}_{n})_{n\in\mathbb{Z}})
, such that
\overline{V}_{\overline{y}}(\gamma_{0},(U^{\prime\prime}_{n})_{n\in\mathbb{Z}})\in\mathcal{V}_{\overline{y},S}\cap\mathcal{V}_{\overline{y},S^{\prime}}
and
\overline{V}_{\overline{y}}(\gamma_{0},(U^{\prime\prime}_{n})_{n\in\mathbb{Z}})\subset\mathcal{N}\cap\mathcal{N}^{\prime}
. Thanks to (3), this implies that
\mathcal{V}_{\overline{y},S}=\mathcal{V}_{\overline{y},S^{\prime}}
.

From all the above facts and by definition of the topology generated by a family of neighborhoods, the set
\mathcal{V}_{\overline{y}}
is exactly the set of all neighborhoods of
\overline{y}
in
\overline{\mathcal{P}}
with respect to the topology
\mathcal{T}
, which proves the second part of the proposition.

∎

Theorem 6.5
.

Denote by
\widetilde{\mathcal{P}}
the universal cover of
\mathcal{P}-\Gamma
,
\overline{\mathcal{P}}
the bifoliated plane of
\rho
up to surgeries on
\Gamma
endowed with the topology
\mathcal{T}
,
\phi:\widetilde{\mathcal{P}}\rightarrow\overline{\mathcal{P}}
and
\overline{\pi}:\overline{\mathcal{P}}\rightarrow\mathcal{P}
the inclusion map and the projection map defined in Section
6.1
.

(1)

The map
\phi
is a continuous embedding

(2)

The set
\overline{\Gamma}
is discrete

(3)

The map
\overline{\pi}
is continuous on
\overline{\mathcal{P}}
and locally injective on
\overline{\mathcal{P}}-\overline{\Gamma}

(4)

The topologies
\mathcal{T}
and
\mathcal{T}_{geom}
are equivalent

Proof of (1).

Recall that by our discussion in Section
6.1
, we have that
\text{Curv}_{1}\subset\text{Curv}_{2}
, that if
\gamma_{1},\gamma_{2}\in\text{Curv}_{1}
then
\gamma_{1}\sim_{1}\gamma_{2}
if and only if
\gamma_{1}\sim_{2}\gamma_{2}
(thus
\phi
is injective) and finally that
\phi(\widetilde{\mathcal{P}})=\overline{\mathcal{P}}-\overline{\Gamma}
. Next, recall that we defined open sets for
\overline{\mathcal{P}}-\overline{\Gamma}
and for
\widetilde{\mathcal{P}}
in the exact same way. Using this fact together with Proposition
6.4
, we get that
\phi
is a homeomorphism onto its image, which proves the desired result.
∎

Proof of (2).

Consider for every point
y\in\Gamma
a standard polygon
S_{y}
containing
y
in its interior and such that no two distinct standard polygons
S_{y},S_{y^{\prime}}
intersect (the existence of such a collection of polygons follows from the discreteness of
\Gamma
in
\mathcal{P}
, see Proposition
5.2
). Take for every
y\in\Gamma
a sequence of quadrant neighborhoods of
y
in
S
, say
(U^{y}_{n})_{n\in\mathbb{Z}}
, and for every
\overline{y}\in\overline{\Gamma}
denote by
\gamma_{\overline{y}}
a reference curve of
\overline{y}
for the sequence of quadrant neighborhoods
(U^{\overline{\pi}(\overline{y})}_{n})_{n\in\mathbb{Z}}
. It suffices to prove that the neighborhoods
(\overline{V}_{\overline{y}}(\gamma_{\overline{y}},(U^{\overline{\pi}(\overline{y})}_{n})_{n\in\mathbb{Z}}))_{\overline{y}\in\overline{\Gamma}}
are two by two disjoint.

Assume that there exist
\overline{y},\overline{y^{\prime}}\in\overline{\Gamma}
such that
\overline{V}_{\overline{y}}(\gamma_{\overline{y}},(U^{\overline{\pi}(\overline{y})}_{n})_{n\in\mathbb{Z}})\cap\overline{V}_{\overline{y^{\prime}}}(\gamma_{\overline{y^{\prime}}},(U^{\overline{\pi}(\overline{y^{\prime}})}_{n})_{n\in\mathbb{Z}})\neq\emptyset
. By our construction, since the
(S_{y})_{y\in\Gamma}
are two by two disjoint, we have that
\overline{\pi}(\overline{y})=\overline{\pi}(\overline{y^{\prime}})
. This implies that there exists a curve
\delta
whose equivalence class for
\sim_{2}
belongs in
\overline{V}_{\overline{y}}(\gamma_{\overline{y}},(U^{\overline{\pi}(\overline{y})}_{n})_{n\in\mathbb{Z}})
and two embedded arcs
e,e^{\prime}
in
S_{y}
going from the endpoint of
\delta
to
y
, such that the equivalence classes for
\sim_{2}
of the juxtaposition of
\delta
followed by
e
and the juxtaposition of
\delta
followed by
e^{\prime}
are not the same. This is impossible.
∎

Proof of (3).

Indeed, if
\widetilde{\pi}
is the projection map from
\widetilde{\mathcal{P}}
to
\mathcal{P}
, by classical covering space theory
\widetilde{\pi}
is continuous and locally injective on
\widetilde{\mathcal{P}}
. Thanks to Item (1) and the fact that
\widetilde{\pi}=\overline{\pi}\circ\phi
(see Section
6.1
), we also get that
\overline{\pi}
is continuous and locally injective on
\overline{\mathcal{P}}-\overline{\Gamma}
. Take now
y\in\Gamma
and
S_{y}
a small standard polygon containing
y
in its interior. The quadrants of
y
in
S_{y}
define a periodic sequence of quadrant neighborhoods of
y
, say
(U_{n})_{n\in\mathbb{Z}}
. For every
\overline{y}\in\overline{\pi}^{-1}(y)
denote by
\gamma_{\overline{y}}
a reference curve of
\overline{y}
for
(U_{n})_{n\in\mathbb{Z}}
. It is easy to see that

\overline{\pi}^{-1}(S_{y})=\underset{\overline{y}\in\overline{\pi}^{-1}(y)}{\cup}\overline{V}_{\overline{y}}(\gamma_{\overline{y}},(U_{n})_{n\in\mathbb{Z}})

which gives the desired result.
∎

Sketch of proof of (4).

We remind some of our notations used in Section
6.1.1
(see also Figure
6.4
). Let
\mathcal{P}_{exp}
be the manifold obtained from
\mathcal{P}
by blowing-up every point of
\Gamma
to a circle,
\widetilde{\mathcal{P}_{exp}}
its universal cover,
p_{exp}
the projection from
\mathcal{P}_{exp}
to
\mathcal{P}
,
X_{0}\in\mathcal{P}_{exp}
the unique preimage by
p_{exp}
of the point
x_{0}
(the base point of
\text{Curv}_{2}
) and
\text{Curv}_{3}
the set of continuous arcs in
\mathcal{P}_{exp}
starting from
X_{0}
.

Figure 6.4
.
The above diagram is commutative

Thanks to Proposition
6.1
and the definition of
\sim_{3}
, we defined in Section
6.1.1
a bijection
\phi_{exp}
from
\text{Curv}_{3}\big/\sim_{3}
to
\overline{\mathcal{P}}
. Recall that
\text{Curv}_{3}\big/\sim_{3}
is homeomorphic to the space obtained by collapsing all boundary lines of
\widetilde{\mathcal{P}_{exp}}
to points. Also, by definition of
\mathcal{T}_{geom}
, if we endow
\overline{\mathcal{P}}
with the topology
\mathcal{T}_{geom}
, then
\phi_{exp}
becomes a homeomorphism. It therefore suffices to prove that
\phi_{exp}
is also a homeomorphism when we endow
\overline{\mathcal{P}}
with
\mathcal{T}
.

From now on we endow
\overline{\mathcal{P}}
with the topology
\mathcal{T}
. Denote by
\Gamma_{geom}
the set of points of
\text{Curv}_{3}\big/\sim_{3}
whose lifts on
\widetilde{\mathcal{P}_{exp}}
are boundary lines and by
\widetilde{p_{exp}}
the continuous projection from
\widetilde{\mathcal{P}_{exp}}
to
\mathcal{P}
. Every boundary line of
\widetilde{\mathcal{P}_{exp}}
projects by
\widetilde{p_{exp}}
to a unique point in
\Gamma
. It follows that
\widetilde{p_{exp}}
defines a projection map
\overline{\pi}_{geom}
from
\text{Curv}_{3}\big/\sim_{3}
to
\mathcal{P}
. One can easily check that
\overline{\pi}_{geom}
is continuous on
\text{Curv}_{3}\big/\sim_{3}
, locally injective on
\text{Curv}_{3}\big/\sim_{3}-\penalty\ \Gamma_{geom}
and finally that
\overline{\pi}_{geom}=\overline{\pi}\circ\phi_{exp}
. Thanks to Item (3) and the previous facts, we get that the restriction of
\phi_{exp}
on
\text{Curv}_{3}\big/\sim_{3}-\penalty\ \Gamma_{geom}
defines a homeomorphism onto
\overline{\mathcal{P}}-\overline{\Gamma}
.

It remains to prove that
\phi_{exp}
is continuous on
\Gamma_{geom}
and that its inverse is continuous on
\overline{\Gamma}
. Consider
y_{geom}\in\Gamma_{geom}
,
\overline{y}=\phi_{exp}(y_{geom})\in\overline{\Gamma}
,
y=\overline{\pi}_{geom}(y_{geom})=\overline{\pi}(\overline{y})\in\Gamma
,
S
a standard polygon in
\mathcal{P}
containing
y
in its interior and
U_{geom}
a neighborhood of
y_{geom}
such that
\overline{\pi}_{geom}(U_{geom})\subset\overset{\circ}{S}
. By eventually taking
U_{geom}
smaller, we can assume that, if
C
is the line boundary of
\widetilde{\mathcal{P}_{exp}}
that was collapsed to
y_{geom}
, then the lift of
U_{geom}
on
\widetilde{\mathcal{P}_{exp}}
is homeomorphic to a band
[0,1]\times\mathbb{R}
, one boundary component of which is
C
. The map
\widetilde{p_{exp}}
sends
C
to
y
and the other boundary component of the previous band to a curve
\gamma
in
S-y
going infinitely many times around
y
. Endow
\gamma
with some parametrization. By eventually taking
U_{geom}
even smaller, we can assume that if
(t_{n})_{n\in\mathbb{Z}}
are the successive times in which
\gamma
intersects the stable or unstable leaves of
y
in
S
, then
\gamma_{|[t_{n},t_{n+1}]}
bounds a neighborhood of
y
in a quadrant of
S
that is homeomorphic to a closed disk. Using those neighborhoods one can construct a sequence of quadrant neighborhoods of
y
in
S
and prove that
\phi_{exp}(U_{geom})
is a neighborhood of
\overline{y}
for the topology
\mathcal{T}
. This proves the continuity of
\phi_{exp}^{-1}
on
\overline{\Gamma}
. The continuity of
\phi_{exp}
on
\Gamma_{geom}
follows from a similar argument.
∎

6.3.
\overline{\mathcal{P}}
as a bifoliated plane
