Remark 6.6
.

Every leaf
\overline{L}
of
\overline{\mathcal{F}^{s}}
(resp.
\overline{\mathcal{F}^{u}}
) satisfies exactly one of the following :

•

\overline{L}
is homeomorphic to
\mathbb{R}
,
\overline{\pi}
is injective on
\overline{L}
and
\overline{\pi}(\overline{L})
is a leaf of
\mathcal{F}^{s}
(resp.
\mathcal{F}^{u}
) that does not intersect
\Gamma

•

\overline{L}
is homeomorphic to
\mathbb{R}_{\geq 0}
,
\overline{\pi}
is injective on
\overline{L}
and
\overline{\pi}(\overline{L})
is a stable (resp. unstable) separatrix of a point in
\Gamma

We will call the foliations
\overline{\mathcal{F}^{s}}
and
\overline{\mathcal{F}^{u}}
the
stable
and
unstable foliations
of
\overline{\mathcal{P}}
. By construction,
\overline{\mathcal{F}^{s}}
and
\overline{\mathcal{F}^{u}}
define a pair of transverse foliations inside
\overline{\mathcal{P}}-\overline{\Gamma}
and also every point in
\overline{\Gamma}
is singular, as it admits infinitely many prongs. Despite the fact that
\overline{\mathcal{F}^{s}}
and
\overline{\mathcal{F}^{u}}
do not satisfy the criteria of Definitions
2.1
and
2.4
, by an abuse of language, we will say that
\overline{\mathcal{F}^{s}}
and
\overline{\mathcal{F}^{u}}
form a pair of transverse singular foliations.

Moreover, even though
\overline{\mathcal{F}^{s}}
and
\overline{\mathcal{F}^{u}}
may be much more complex than the foliations
\mathcal{F}^{s}
or
\mathcal{F}^{u}
, we have that:

Remark 6.7
.

(1)

Every singular point of
\overline{\mathcal{F}^{s}}
and
\overline{\mathcal{F}^{u}}
belongs in
\overline{\Gamma}
and conversely every point in
\overline{\Gamma}
is singular and admits infinitely many prongs

(2)

The restrictions on
\overline{\mathcal{P}}-\overline{\Gamma}
of the foliations
\overline{\mathcal{F}^{s}}
and
\overline{\mathcal{F}^{u}}
are orientable and transversely orientable.

(3)

The leaves of the restrictions on
\overline{\mathcal{P}}-\overline{\Gamma}
of the foliations
\overline{\mathcal{F}^{s}}
and
\overline{\mathcal{F}^{u}}
are closed, non-compact and properly embedded into
\overline{\mathcal{P}}-\overline{\Gamma}
.

(4)

Any stable leaf in
\overline{\mathcal{F}^{s}}
intersects at most once any unstable leaf in
\overline{\mathcal{F}^{u}}
.

(5)

The complement of any stable or unstable leaf in
\overline{\mathcal{P}}
consists of exactly two simply connected components.

Indeed, Item (1) is a direct consequence of our construction of
\overline{\mathcal{F}^{s}}
and
\overline{\mathcal{F}^{u}}
. Item (2) results from the fact that
\overline{\mathcal{P}}-\overline{\Gamma}
is homeomorphic to a plane and that the restrictions on
\overline{\mathcal{P}}-\overline{\Gamma}
of the foliations
\overline{\mathcal{F}^{s}}
and
\overline{\mathcal{F}^{u}}
define two line foliations. Item (3) is a consequence of Proposition
2.62
and Item (4) is a consequence of the same proposition and Remark
6.6
. Finally, one can prove Item (5) by using the fact that
\overline{\mathcal{P}}
is a topological plane with some points at infinity and the fact that
\overline{\mathcal{F}^{s}}
and
\overline{\mathcal{F}^{u}}
were obtained by a continuous extension of two foliations on the plane
\overline{\mathcal{P}}-\overline{\Gamma}
consisting of properly embedded leaves.

Finally, before finishing this section let us describe the form of the foliations
\overline{\mathcal{F}^{s}}
and
\overline{\mathcal{F}^{u}}
near a singular point in
\overline{\Gamma}
:

Proposition 6.8
.

For every
\overline{\gamma}\in\overline{\Gamma}
, the stable leaves in
\overline{\mathcal{F}^{s}}
that intersect
\overline{\gamma}
form a countable set of leaves that can be indexed by
\mathbb{Z}
–denote by
...,s_{-2},s_{-1},s_{0},s_{1},s_{2}...
the previous indexation– so that:

•

if
\gamma:=\overline{\pi}(\overline{\gamma})
and
\gamma
is a
p
-prong singularity in
\mathcal{F}^{s}
(with
p\geq 2
), then for all
k,l\in\mathbb{Z}
such that
(k-l)
is a multiple of
p
we have that
\overline{\pi}(s_{k})=\overline{\pi}(s_{l})

•

for all
k\in\mathbb{Z}

s_{k}\cap\overline{\Gamma}=\{\overline{\gamma}\}

•

for all
k,m\in\mathbb{Z}

s_{k}
is not separated from
s_{m}
in
\overline{\mathcal{P}}
(i.e.
s_{k}-\{\overline{\gamma}\}
and
s_{m}-\{\overline{\gamma}\}
are non-separated stable leaves in
\overline{\mathcal{P}}-\overline{\Gamma}
) if and only if
|k-m|=1

Proof.

Indeed, take
\overline{\gamma}\in\overline{\Gamma}
and
\gamma=\overline{\pi}(\overline{\gamma})
. Recall that when we collapsed every boundary line of
\widetilde{\mathcal{P}_{exp}}
to a point, we obtained a space homeomorphic to
\overline{\mathcal{P}}
. Using this fact, denote by
\widetilde{C}
the boundary line in
\widetilde{\mathcal{P}_{exp}}
that was collapsed to
\overline{\gamma}
and by
C
the projection of
\widetilde{C}
in
\mathcal{P}_{exp}
(see Figure
6.4
). Assume that
\gamma
is a
p
-prong singularity in
\mathcal{F}^{s}
. By our construction of
\mathcal{F}^{s}_{exp}
and thanks to the fact that the leaves of
\mathcal{F}^{s}
are closed, we have that only leaves in
\mathcal{F}^{s}_{exp}
that get arbitrarily close to the boundary circle
C
are the
p
leaves in
\mathcal{F}^{s}_{exp}
that project via
p_{exp}
to the stable separatrices of
\gamma
(see Figure
6.5
). Extend continuously the previous leaves by a point so that they intersect
C
and denote by
L_{1},...,L_{p}
the previous collection of extended leaves. Assume without any loss of generality that
L_{1},...,L_{p}
are cyclically ordered. Lift everything on
\widetilde{\mathcal{P}_{exp}}
, the universal cover of
\mathcal{P}_{exp}
, and consider the subset of all the lifts of
L_{1},...,L_{p}
on
\widetilde{\mathcal{P}_{exp}}
that intersect
\widetilde{C}
(see Figure
6.6
). We can index the previous set of lifts by
\mathbb{Z}
–denote by
(\widetilde{L_{n}})_{n\in\mathbb{Z}}
the previous indexation– so that :

•

for any two distinct
i,j\in\mathbb{Z}
we have that
\widetilde{L_{i}}\cap\widetilde{L_{j}}=\emptyset

•

if
n\equiv i\text{ mod }p
, where
i\in\llbracket 1,p\rrbracket
, then
\widetilde{L_{n}}
is a lift of
L_{i}
intersecting
\widetilde{C}

•

for any
n\in\mathbb{Z}
, if
\widetilde{x_{i}}
the unique point in
\widetilde{L_{i}}\cap\widetilde{C}
, then there does not exist
m\in\mathbb{Z}
such that
\widetilde{x_{m}}
lies in the interior of the interval in
\widetilde{C}
defined by
\widetilde{x_{n}}
and
\widetilde{x_{n+1}}
. In other words, the points in
(\widetilde{x_{n}})_{n\in\mathbb{Z}}
form the successive intersections of the line
\widetilde{C}
with the lifts of
L_{1},...,L_{p}
.

The first point of the above proposition is obtained by simply collapsing
\widetilde{C}
into a point and by using the fact that the diagram in Figure
6.4
is commutative. Next, if
s_{k}
intersects two points in
\overline{\Gamma}
, then so does its projection by
\overline{\pi}
. This is impossible, because by our definition of a Markovian action, a stable leaf in
\mathcal{F}^{s}
contains at most one periodic point. This proves the second point of the proposition. Finally, the fact that
s_{k}
is not separated from
s_{k+1}
can be seen in Figure
6.6
. Conversely, if
|l-k|\neq 1
, then
s_{k}
and
s_{l}
are separated because for any
n\in\mathbb{Z}
the line
s_{n}-\{\overline{\gamma}\}
is properly embedded in
\overline{\mathcal{P}}-\overline{\Gamma}
and thus separates
\overline{\mathcal{P}}-\overline{\Gamma}
into two connected components.
∎

6.4.
A group action on
\overline{\mathcal{P}}

Fix
\mathcal{P}
a plane endowed with an orientation,
\rho:G\rightarrow\text{Homeo}(\mathcal{P})
an orientation preserving strong Markovian action, preserving the pair of singular foliations
\mathcal{F}^{s}
and
\mathcal{F}^{u}
and leaving invariant a strong Markovian family
\mathcal{R}
. Denote by
\Gamma
the boundary periodic points of
\mathcal{R}
and by
\overline{\mathcal{P}}
the bifoliated plane of
\rho
up to surgeries on
\Gamma
.

Our goal in this section consists in showing that, in addition to a pair of transverse singular foliations
\overline{\mathcal{F}^{s}},\overline{\mathcal{F}^{u}}
, the bifoliated plane of
\rho
up to surgeries on
\Gamma
admits a natural group action by homeomorphisms. Intuitively, recall that our construction of
\overline{\mathcal{P}}
(see Section
6.1
) resembled very closely the classical construction of
\widetilde{\mathcal{P}-\Gamma}
, the universal cover of
\mathcal{P}-\Gamma
. By lifting on
\widetilde{\mathcal{P}-\Gamma}
all the homeomorphisms in
\rho(G)
, we obtain a group action (this group is generally not isomorphic to
G
) on
\widetilde{\mathcal{P}-\Gamma}
by homeomorphisms. By treating
\overline{\mathcal{P}}
as the universal cover of
\mathcal{P}
with respect to the homotopy
\sim_{2}
, we will similarly define a group action on
\overline{\mathcal{P}}
by a group that is larger than
G
.

More precisely, recall that by our construction in Section
6.1
,
\overline{\mathcal{P}}=\text{Curv}_{2}\big/\sim_{2}
, where

\text{Curv}_{2}=\{\gamma:[0,1]\overset{C^{0}}{\rightarrow}\mathcal{P}|\penalty\ \gamma(0)=x_{0},\gamma[0,1)\cap\Gamma=\emptyset\}

and
x_{0}\in\mathcal{P}-\Gamma
. We define

\displaystyle\text{Curv}_{2}(x_{0})

\displaystyle:=\{\gamma:[0,1]\overset{C^{0}}{\rightarrow}\mathcal{P}|\penalty\ \gamma(0)=x_{0},\gamma(1)\in\rho(G)(x_{0}),\gamma[0,1)\cap\Gamma=\emptyset\}

\displaystyle:=\{\gamma\in\text{Curv}_{2}|\gamma(1)\in\rho(G)(x_{0})\}

Consider
\gamma\in\text{Curv}_{2}
,
\gamma_{0}\in\text{Curv}_{2}(x_{0})

and
g\in G
such that
\rho(g)(\gamma_{0}(0))=\rho(g)(x_{0})=\gamma_{0}(1)
. We define
\overline{\rho}(g,\gamma_{0})(\gamma)
to be the curve in
\text{Curv}_{2}
obtained by the juxtaposition of
\gamma_{0}
followed by
\rho(g)(\gamma)
.

It is easy to see that

Proposition 6.9
.

Consider
\gamma_{0},\gamma_{0}^{\prime}\in\text{Curv}_{2}(x_{0})
,
\gamma,\gamma^{\prime}\in\text{Curv}_{2}
and
g\in G
such that
\rho(g)(\gamma_{0}(0))=\rho(g)(\gamma^{\prime}_{0}(0))=\rho(g)(x_{0})=\gamma_{0}(1)=\gamma^{\prime}_{0}(1)

•

\gamma_{0}\sim_{2}\gamma_{0}^{\prime}
if and only if
\overline{\rho}(g,\gamma_{0})(\gamma)\sim_{2}\overline{\rho}(g,\gamma^{\prime}_{0})(\gamma)

•

\gamma\sim_{2}\gamma^{\prime}
if and only if
\overline{\rho}(g,\gamma_{0})(\gamma)\sim_{2}\overline{\rho}(g,\gamma_{0})(\gamma^{\prime})

For any
\delta\in\text{Curv}_{2}
, denote by
\overline{\delta}
the class in
\text{Curv}_{2}\big/\sim_{2}=\overline{\mathcal{P}}
containing
\delta
. Thanks to the above proposition,
\overline{\rho}(g,\overline{\gamma_{0}})
defines an injective map from
\overline{\mathcal{P}}
to
\overline{\mathcal{P}}
. The previous map is also surjective. Indeed, consider
\delta\in\text{Curv}_{2}
and
\delta^{\prime}
a curve in
\mathcal{P}
starting at the endpoint of
\gamma_{0}
and such that the juxtaposition of
\gamma_{0}
followed by
\delta^{\prime}
defines a curve in
\text{Curv}_{2}
homotopic to
\delta
for
\sim_{2}
. By construction, we have that
\overline{\rho}(g,\overline{\gamma_{0}})(\overline{\rho(g^{-1})(\delta^{\prime})})=\overline{\delta}
.

Furthermore, we have that:

Proposition 6.10
.

Let
\gamma_{0}\in\text{Curv}_{2}(x_{0})
and
g\in G
be such that
\rho(g)(\gamma_{0}(0))=\rho(g)(x_{0})=\gamma_{0}(1)
. We have that

(1)

\overline{\pi}\circ\overline{\rho}(g,\overline{\gamma_{0}})=\rho(g)\circ\overline{\pi}

(2)

for any
x\in\mathcal{P}
,
\overline{x},\overline{x^{\prime}}\in\overline{\pi}^{-1}(x)
there exists
\delta_{0}
a closed curve in
\text{Curv}_{2}(x_{0})
such that
\overline{x^{\prime}}=\overline{\rho}(e,\overline{\delta_{0}})(\overline{x})
, where
e
the trivial element in
G

(3)

if
\delta_{0}
is a closed in
\text{Curv}_{2}(x_{0})
, then
\overline{\rho}(e,\overline{\delta_{0}})=id
if and only if
\delta_{0}
is homotopically trivial for
\sim_{2}

(4)

if
g^{\prime}\in G
and
\gamma_{1}\in\text{Curv}_{2}(x_{0})
are such that
\rho(g^{\prime})(\gamma_{1}(0))=\rho(g^{\prime})(x_{0})=\gamma_{1}(1)
, then

\overline{\rho}(g,\overline{\gamma_{0}})\circ\overline{\rho}(g^{\prime},\overline{\gamma_{1}})=\overline{\rho}(gg^{\prime},\overline{\rho(g)(\gamma_{1})\gamma_{0}})

where
\rho(g)(\gamma_{1})\gamma_{0}
denotes the curve in
\text{Curv}_{2}(x_{0})
obtained by the juxtaposition of
\gamma_{0}
followed by
\rho(g)(\gamma_{1})
. In particular,

\overline{\rho}(g,\overline{\gamma_{0}})\circ\overline{\rho}(g^{-1},\overline{\rho(g^{-1})(\gamma_{0}^{-})})=\overline{\rho}(g^{-1},\overline{\rho(g^{-1})(\gamma_{0}^{-})})\circ\overline{\rho}(g,\overline{\gamma_{0}})=id

where
\gamma_{0}^{-}:[0,1]\rightarrow\mathcal{P}
denotes the curve defined by
\gamma_{0}^{-}(t)=\gamma_{0}(1-t)
for every
t\in[0,1]
.

(5)

\overline{\rho}(g,\overline{\gamma_{0}}):\overline{\mathcal{P}}\rightarrow\overline{\mathcal{P}}
is a homeomorphism

Proof of (1).

Consider
\gamma\in\text{Curv}_{2}
. By construction,
\overline{\rho}(g,\overline{\gamma_{0}})(\overline{\gamma})\in\overline{\mathcal{P}}=\text{Curv}_{2}\big/\sim_{2}
is a class of curves homotopic for
\sim_{2}
containing the curve obtained by the juxtaposition of
\gamma_{0}
followed by
\rho(g)(\gamma)
. By definition of
\overline{\pi}
, the point
\overline{\pi}(\overline{\rho}(g,\overline{\gamma_{0}})(\overline{\gamma}))
is equal to the endpoint of the previous curve, which is equal to the image by
\rho(g)
of
\gamma(1)
. This gives us the desired result.
∎

Proof of (2).

Consider
\gamma\in\text{Curv}_{2}
and
\gamma^{\prime}\in\text{Curv}_{2}
belonging in the equivalence classes defined by
\overline{x}
and
\overline{x^{\prime}}
respectively. Notice that
\gamma(0)=\gamma^{\prime}(0)=x_{0}
as
\gamma,\gamma^{\prime}\in\text{Curv}_{2}
and
\gamma(1)=\gamma^{\prime}(1)=x
as
\overline{\pi}(\overline{x})=\overline{\pi}(\overline{x^{\prime}})=x
. It is easy now to see that there exists
\delta_{0}
a closed curve in
\text{Curv}(x_{0})
such that the curve obtained by the juxtaposition of
\delta_{0}
followed by
\gamma
is homotopic to
\gamma^{\prime}
for
\sim_{2}
. This gives us the desired result.
∎

Proof of (3).

Consider
\gamma\in\text{Curv}_{2}
and
\delta_{0}
a closed curve in
\text{Curv}(x_{0})
. By construction,
\overline{\rho}(e,\overline{\delta_{0}})(\overline{\gamma})\in\overline{\mathcal{P}}=\text{Curv}_{2}\big/\sim_{2}
corresponds to a class of curves homotopic for
\sim_{2}
containing the curve obtained by the juxtaposition of
\delta_{0}
followed by
\gamma
. If
\delta_{0}
is homotopically trivial for
\sim_{2}
, the previous curve is homotopic to
\gamma
for
\sim_{2}
. Conversely, if for any curve
\gamma
in
\text{Curv}_{2}
, the curve obtained as the juxtaposition of
\delta_{0}
followed by
\gamma
is homotopic to
\gamma
for
\sim_{2}
, then
\delta_{0}
is homotopically trivial for
\sim_{2}
.
∎

Proof of (4).

Consider
\gamma\in\text{Curv}_{2}
. By construction,
\overline{\rho}(g^{\prime},\overline{\gamma_{1}})(\overline{\gamma})\in\overline{\mathcal{P}}=\text{Curv}_{2}\big/\sim_{2}
is a class of curves homotopic for
\sim_{2}
containing the curve obtained by the juxtaposition of
\gamma_{1}
followed by
\rho(g^{\prime})(\gamma)
. Similarly,
\overline{\rho}(g,\overline{\gamma_{0}})\circ\overline{\rho}(g^{\prime},\overline{\gamma_{1}})(\overline{\gamma})
is a class of curves homotopic for
\sim_{2}
containing the curve obtained by the juxtaposition of
\gamma_{0}
followed by
\rho(g)(\gamma_{1})
followed by
\rho(g)(\rho(g^{\prime})(\gamma))
, which gives us the desired result.
∎

Proof of (5).

We have already established that
\overline{\rho}(g,\overline{\gamma_{0}}):\overline{\mathcal{P}}\rightarrow\overline{\mathcal{P}}
is a bijection. Let us now prove that it is a homeomorphism by using Proposition
6.4
.

Consider
\overline{x}\in\overline{\mathcal{P}}-\overline{\Gamma}
,
\overline{y}\in\overline{\Gamma}
,
x=\overline{\pi}(\overline{x})
,
y=\overline{\pi}(\overline{y})
,
W
a small open disk in
\mathcal{P}-\Gamma
around
x
,
S
a standard polygon containing
y
,
(U_{n})_{n\in\mathbb{Z}}
a sequence of quadrant neighborhoods of
y
in
S
and finally
\delta_{0}
a reference curve of
\overline{y}
for the sequence of neighborhoods
(U_{n})_{n\in\mathbb{Z}}
. Denote respectively by
\overline{V}_{\overline{x}}(W)
and by
\overline{V}_{\overline{y}}(\delta_{0},(U_{n})_{n\in\mathbb{Z}})
the neighborhoods of
\overline{x}
and
\overline{y}
that were constructed in Section
6.1.2
. Recall that if
\gamma_{1}\in\text{Curv}_{2}
belongs in the equivalence class defined by
\overline{x}\in\overline{\mathcal{P}}=\text{Curv}_{2}\big/\sim_{2}
and
s\in(0,1)
is such that
\gamma_{1}([s,1])\subset W
, then

\overline{V}_{\overline{x}}(W)=\{\overline{\gamma}|\gamma\in\text{Curv}_{2},\forall t\in[0,s]\quad\gamma(t)=\gamma_{1}(t),\gamma([s,1])\subset W\}

It follows that if
\rho(g)(\gamma_{1})\gamma_{0}:[0,1]\rightarrow\mathcal{P}
is the curve obtained by the juxtaposition of
\gamma_{0}
followed by
\rho(g)(\gamma_{1})
and
s^{\prime}\in(0,1)
is such that
(\rho(g)(\gamma_{1})\gamma_{0})([s^{\prime},1])\subset\rho(g)(W)
then

\displaystyle\overline{\rho}(g,\overline{\gamma_{0}})(\overline{V}_{\overline{x}}(W))

\displaystyle=\{\overline{\gamma}|\gamma\in\text{Curv}_{2},\forall t\in[0,s^{\prime}]\quad\gamma(t)=(\rho(g)(\gamma_{1})\gamma_{0})(t),\gamma([s^{\prime},1])\subset\rho(g)(W)\}

\displaystyle=\overline{V}_{\overline{\rho}(g,\overline{\gamma_{0}})(\overline{x})}(\rho(g)(W))

Similarly, using the definition of
\overline{\rho}(g,\overline{\gamma_{0}})
and
\overline{V}_{\overline{y}}(\delta_{0},(U_{n})_{n\in\mathbb{Z}})
, one can easily check that

\overline{\rho}(g,\overline{\gamma_{0}})(\overline{V}_{\overline{y}}(\delta_{0},(U_{n})_{n\in\mathbb{Z}}))
is equal to the following neighborhood of
\overline{\rho}(g,\overline{\gamma_{0}})(\overline{y})

\overline{V}_{\overline{\rho}(g,\overline{\gamma_{0}})(\overline{y})}(\rho(g)(\delta_{0})\gamma_{0},(\rho(g)(U_{n}))_{n\in\mathbb{Z}})

where
\rho(g)(\delta_{0})\gamma_{0}
denotes the curve obtained by the juxtaposition of
\gamma_{0}
followed by
\rho(g)(\delta_{0})
. Thanks to Proposition
6.4
, our previous arguments prove the continuity of the inverse of
\overline{\rho}(g,\overline{\gamma_{0}})
. The fact that
\overline{\rho}(g,\overline{\gamma_{0}})
defines a homeomorphism follows from Item (4).
∎

Consider

\overline{G}=\{(g,\overline{\gamma})|g\in G\text{, }\overline{\gamma}\in\text{Curv}_{2}(x_{0})\big/\sim_{2}\text{ and }\rho(g)(x_{0})=\overline{\pi}(\overline{\gamma})\}

We endow
\overline{G}
with the group structure given by

(g,\overline{\gamma_{0}})\cdot(g^{\prime},\overline{\gamma_{1}})=(gg^{\prime},\overline{\rho(g)(\gamma_{1})\gamma_{0}})

where
\overline{\rho(g)(\gamma_{1})\gamma_{0}}
is the equivalence class in
\text{Curv}_{2}(x_{0})\big/\sim_{2}
obtained by the juxtaposition of a representative of
\overline{\gamma_{0}}
followed by the image by
\rho(g)
of a representative of
\overline{\gamma_{1}}
. It is easy to check, thanks to Proposition
6.10
, that the previous operation defines indeed group structure on
\overline{G}
such that:

•

if
e
is the trivial element in
G
and
\gamma_{triv}\in\text{Curv}_{2}(x_{0})
a homotopically trivial loop for
\sim_{2}
then
(e,\overline{\gamma_{triv}})
is the identity element in
\overline{G}

•

(g,\overline{\gamma_{0}})^{-1}=(g^{-1},\overline{\rho(g^{-1})(\gamma_{0}^{-})})
, where
\gamma_{0}^{-}:[0,1]\rightarrow\mathcal{P}
is defined as follows : if
\gamma_{0}:[0,1]\rightarrow\mathcal{P}
is a representative in
\text{Curv}_{2}
if the class
\overline{\gamma_{0}}\in\text{Curv}_{2}(x_{0})\big/\sim_{2}
, then
\gamma_{0}^{-}(t)=\gamma_{0}(1-t)
for every
t\in[0,1]

We will call the previous group, the
extension of
G
associated to
\Gamma
. Notice that since
G
is countable (see Definition
3.29
) and also since the homotopy classes in
\text{Curv}_{2}(x_{0})
for
\sim_{2}
are countable, we get that

Remark 6.11
.

The group
\overline{G}
is a countable group.

Denote by
\mathfrak{p}
the map associating every
(g,\overline{\gamma})\in\overline{G}
to
g\in G
. The map defines a homomorphism from
\overline{G}
to
G
, whose kernel consists of the elements of
\overline{G}
of the form
(e,\overline{\gamma})
, where
\overline{\gamma}
is a class of closed curves in
\text{Curv}_{2}(x_{0})
that are homotopic for
\sim_{2}
. Since
x_{0}\in\mathcal{P}-\Gamma
and two closed curves in
\text{Curv}_{2}(x_{0})
are homotopic in
\mathcal{P}-\Gamma
if and only if they are homotopic for
\sim_{2}
, we have that the kernel of
\mathfrak{p}
is identified with
\pi_{1}(\mathcal{P}-\Gamma,x_{0})
. Recall now that by our geometric construction of
\overline{\mathcal{P}}
, the space
\overline{\mathcal{P}}-\overline{\Gamma}
can be identified with the universal cover of
\mathcal{P}-\Gamma
. Furthermore, notice that when
\gamma
is a closed curve in
\text{Curv}_{2}(x_{0})
, our definition of
\overline{\rho}(e,\overline{\gamma})\in\text{Homeo}(\overline{\mathcal{P}}-\overline{\Gamma})
coincides with the definition of the deck transformation on the universal cover of
\mathcal{P}-\Gamma
associated to the homotopy class of
\gamma
in
\pi_{1}(\mathcal{P}-\Gamma,x_{0})
; hence, the action of
\pi_{1}(\mathcal{P}-\Gamma,x_{0})\leq\overline{G}
on
\overline{\mathcal{P}}-\overline{\Gamma}
can be identified with the action of
\pi_{1}(\mathcal{P}-\Gamma,x_{0})
by deck transformations on
\overline{\mathcal{P}}-\overline{\Gamma}
. We have thus obtained that:

Proposition 6.12
.

Let
\mathfrak{p}
be the projection from
\overline{G}
to
G
. We have that

\text{ker}(\mathfrak{p})=\pi_{1}(\mathcal{P}-\Gamma,x_{0})

In other words,
\overline{G}\big/\pi_{1}(\mathcal{P}-\Gamma,x_{0})\cong G
.

Moreover, the action of
\text{ker}(\mathfrak{p})
on
\overline{\mathcal{P}}-\overline{\Gamma}
can be identified with the action of
\pi_{1}(\mathcal{P}-\Gamma,x_{0})
by deck transformations on the universal cover of
\mathcal{P}-\Gamma
. In particular, for any
\overline{g}\in\text{ker}(\mathfrak{p})
, we have that
\overline{\rho}(\overline{g})=id
or
\overline{\rho}(\overline{g})
does not fix any point in
\overline{\mathcal{P}}-\overline{\Gamma}
.

Finally, thanks to Proposition
6.10
,

Proposition 6.13
.

There exists a faithful action
\overline{\rho}:\overline{G}\rightarrow\text{Homeo}(\overline{\mathcal{P}})
such that

\overline{\pi}\circ\overline{\rho}(\overline{g})=\rho(\mathfrak{p}(\overline{g}))\circ\overline{\pi}

In conclusion, by combining the results of the two previous sections we get that, similarly to
\mathcal{P}
, the bifoliated plane of
\rho
up to surgeries on
\Gamma
can be naturally endowed with a pair of transverse singular foliations and a group action. As we will prove in Section
7
, contrary to
\mathcal{P}
, when
\rho
is associated to a pseudo-Anosov flow, the previous singular foliations and group action do not describe the original flow up to orbital equivalence, but rather up to specific surgeries (see Theorem C). In other words, once we consider the universal cover of
\mathcal{P}-\Gamma
, we lose track of how the original flow behaves around a finite number of periodic orbits, namely the periodic orbits associated to
\Gamma
.

6.5.
On the properties of the action
\overline{\rho}

Following the notations of the previous section, consider
(\overline{\mathcal{P}},\overline{\mathcal{F}^{s}},\overline{\mathcal{F}^{u}},\overline{\rho})
the bifoliated plane of
\rho
up to surgeries on
\Gamma
endowed with its stable and unstable foliations and group action. By construction
\overline{\mathcal{P}}
is homeomorphic to the universal cover of
\mathcal{P}-\Gamma
together with some points at infinity. It is therefore natural for the action
\overline{\rho}
to inherit several properties from
\rho
related to its strong Markovian action nature:

Proposition 6.14
.

(1)

\overline{\rho}
preserves the pair of singular foliations
(\overline{\mathcal{F}^{s}},\overline{\mathcal{F}^{u}})

(2)

Two points
\overline{x},\overline{y}
in
\overline{\mathcal{P}}
are in the same
\overline{G}
-orbit if and only if the points
\overline{\pi}(\overline{x}),\overline{\pi}(\overline{y})
are in the same
G
-orbit.

(3)

Let
\overline{g}
be a non-trivial element in
\overline{G}
. If
\overline{\rho}(\overline{g})
preserves a leaf
\overline{L}\in\overline{\mathcal{F}^{s}}
or
\overline{L}\in\overline{\mathcal{F}^{u}}
, then it has a unique fixed point in
\overline{L}
. Moreover, this fixed point is the same for every non-trivial element in
\overline{G}
that preserves
\overline{L}

(4)

The stabilizer in
\overline{G}
of any point
\overline{x}
in
\overline{\mathcal{P}}-\overline{\Gamma}
is either trivial or isomorphic to
\mathbb{Z}
. Moreover,

\mathfrak{p}(\text{Stab}_{\overline{\rho}}(\overline{x}))=\text{Stab}_{\rho}(\overline{\pi}(\overline{x}))

(5)

The stabilizer in
\overline{G}
of any leaf
\overline{L}
in
\overline{\mathcal{F}^{s}}
or
\overline{\mathcal{F}^{u}}
is either trivial or isomorphic to
\mathbb{Z}
. Moreover,

\mathfrak{p}(\text{Stab}_{\overline{\rho}}(\overline{L}))=\text{Stab}_{\rho}(\overline{\pi}(\overline{L}))

(6)

For any non-trivial
\overline{g}\in\overline{G}
and any
\overline{x}\in\overline{\mathcal{P}}-\overline{\Gamma}
(resp.
\overline{x}\in\overline{\Gamma}
) fixed by
\overline{\rho}(\overline{g})
, up to changing
\overline{g}
to
\overline{g}^{-1}
, we have that
\overline{\rho}(\overline{g})
is
topologically expanding on
\overline{\mathcal{F}^{u}}(\overline{x})
(resp. any of the unstable leaves of
\overline{x}
) and topologically contracting on
\overline{\mathcal{F}^{s}}(\overline{x})
(resp. any of the stable leaves of
\overline{x}
)

(7)

There exists a finite set of stable (resp. unstable) leaves in
\overline{\mathcal{F}^{s}}
(resp.
\overline{\mathcal{F}^{u}}
) with non-trivial stabilizers in
\overline{G}
, whose orbit by
\overline{G}
forms a dense subset of
\overline{\mathcal{P}}

Proof of (1).

Consider
\overline{L}
a leaf of
\overline{\mathcal{F}^{s}}
and
\overline{g}\in\overline{G}
. Assume for the sake of simplicity that
\overline{L}
does not intersect
\overline{\Gamma}
(the general case follows from a similar argument). First of all notice that, since
\overline{\rho}(\overline{g})
is a homeomorphism of
\overline{\mathcal{P}}
,
\overline{\rho}(\overline{g})(\overline{L})
is connected. Next, using Remark
6.6
, Proposition
6.13
and our original hypothesis, we get that both
\overline{\pi}(\overline{L})
and
\overline{\pi}(\overline{\rho}(\overline{g})(\overline{L}))
are stable leaves in
\mathcal{F}^{s}
that do not intersect
\Gamma
. Thanks to our discussion prior to Remark
6.6
, every connected component of the preimage by
\overline{\pi}
of
\overline{\pi}(\overline{\rho}(\overline{g})(\overline{L}))\in\mathcal{F}^{s}
defines a leaf in
\overline{\mathcal{F}^{s}}
, which proves that
\overline{\rho}(\overline{g})(\overline{L})\in\overline{\mathcal{F}^{s}}
.
∎

Proof of (2).

Indeed, if
\overline{x},\overline{y}
are in the same
\overline{G}
-orbit, then their projections are in the same
G
-orbit thanks to Proposition
6.13
. Conversely, recall that by Proposition
6.12
for every
\gamma\in\mathcal{P}-\Gamma
the group
\text{ker}(\mathfrak{p})
acts transitively on
\overline{\pi}^{-1}(\gamma)
. It therefore suffices to show that the same result is true if
\gamma\in\Gamma
. Indeed, take
\gamma\in\Gamma
,
\overline{\gamma},\overline{\gamma^{\prime}}\in\overline{\Gamma}
any two lifts of
\gamma
on
\overline{\mathcal{P}}
,
\overline{L},\overline{L^{\prime}}
two leaves of
\overline{\mathcal{F}^{s}}
containing
\overline{\gamma},\overline{\gamma^{\prime}}
respectively and such that
\overline{\pi}(\overline{L})=\overline{\pi}(\overline{L^{\prime}})=L
, where
L
is a stable separatrix of
\gamma
. Thanks to Proposition
6.12
, there exists
\overline{g}\in\text{ker}(\mathfrak{p})
such that
\overline{\rho}(\overline{g})(\overline{L})=\overline{L^{\prime}}
. By Propositions
6.13
and
6.8
, we deduce that
\overline{\rho}(\overline{g})(\overline{\gamma})=\overline{\gamma^{\prime}}
.
∎

Proof of (3).

Consider
\overline{g}
a non-trivial element in
\overline{G}
such that
\overline{\rho}(\overline{g})
preserves a leaf
\overline{L}\in\overline{\mathcal{F}^{s}}
. Let us first show that
\mathfrak{p}(\overline{g})\neq e\in G
, where
e
is the trivial element in
G
. Indeed, if
\mathfrak{p}(\overline{g})=e
, then thanks to Proposition
6.13
and to the injectivity of
\overline{\pi}
on
\overline{L}
(see Remark
6.6
), we get that
\overline{\rho}(\overline{g})
fixes every point in
\overline{L}
. Using Proposition
6.12
and the faithfulness of the action
\overline{\rho}
, the previous fact implies that
\overline{\rho}(\overline{g})=id
and thus that
\overline{g}
is trivial in
\overline{G}
, which is absurd.

We can therefore suppose from now on that
\mathfrak{p}(\overline{g})\neq e
. In that case, since
\overline{\rho}(\overline{g})
preserves
\overline{L}
, thanks to Proposition
6.13
, we get that
\rho(\mathfrak{p}(\overline{g}))\neq id
preserves
\overline{\pi}(\overline{L})
. Recall now that by Remark
6.6
,
\overline{\pi}(\overline{L})
could either be a stable leaf of
\mathcal{F}^{s}
that does not intersect
\Gamma
or a stable separatrix of a point in
\Gamma
. In both cases, since
\rho(\mathfrak{p}(\overline{g}))
preserves
\overline{\pi}(\overline{L})
and since
\rho
is a Markovian action,
\rho(\mathfrak{p}(\overline{g}))
fixes a point in
\overline{\pi}(\overline{L})
. Using Proposition
6.13
and the injectivity of
\overline{\pi}
on
\overline{L}
(see Remark
6.6
), we get that
\overline{\rho}(\overline{g})
also fixes a point in
\overline{L}
.

By a similar argument, we can also prove that any two elements in
\overline{G}
that preserve
\overline{L}
fix the same point in
\overline{L}
, which finishes the proof of Item (3).

∎

Proof of (4).

Take
\overline{x}\in\overline{\mathcal{P}}-\overline{\Gamma}
. By Proposition
6.12
, for any
\overline{g}\in\text{ker}(\mathfrak{p})
we have that
\overline{\rho}(\overline{g})=id
or
\overline{\rho}(\overline{g})
does not fix any point in
\overline{\mathcal{P}}-\overline{\Gamma}
. The previous fact together with Proposition
6.13
imply that
\mathfrak{p}
defines a monomorphism from
\text{Stab}_{\overline{\rho}}(\overline{x})
into
\text{Stab}_{\rho}(\overline{\pi}(\overline{x}))
. Therefore,
\text{Stab}_{\overline{\rho}}(\overline{x})
can be seen as a subgroup of
\text{Stab}_{\rho}(\overline{\pi}(\overline{x}))
, which is either trivial or isomorphic to
\mathbb{Z}
, by the definition of a Markovian action. This proves that
\text{Stab}_{\overline{\rho}}(\overline{x})
is either trivial or isomorphic to
\mathbb{Z}
.

It remains to prove that
\mathfrak{p}
defines an epimorphism from
\text{Stab}_{\overline{\rho}}(\overline{x})
to
\text{Stab}_{\rho}(\overline{\pi}(\overline{x}))
. Take
g\in\text{Stab}_{\rho}(\overline{\pi}(\overline{x}))
and
\overline{g}
such that
\mathfrak{p}(\overline{g})=g
. Thanks to Proposition
6.13
, we have that
\overline{\rho}(\overline{g})(\overline{x})\in\overline{\pi}^{-1}(\overline{\pi}(\overline{x}))
and since
\text{ker}(\mathfrak{p})
acts transitively on
\overline{\pi}^{-1}(\overline{\pi}(\overline{x}))
(see Proposition
6.12
) we have that there exists
\overline{h}\in\text{ker}(\mathfrak{p})
such that
\overline{\rho}(\overline{g}\overline{h})
fixes
\overline{x}
. Since
\mathfrak{p}(\overline{g}\overline{h})=g
, this proves the desired result.
∎

Proofs of (5),(6).

The proof of (4) follows from the same argument that was used in the proof of (3). The proof of (5) is a direct application of (3) and of Proposition
6.13
.
∎

Proof of (7).

Take
L_{1},...,L_{n}
a set of stable leaves in
\mathcal{F}^{s}
, whose orbits by
G
form altogether a dense subset of
\mathcal{P}
. Assume for the sake of simplicity that
L_{1},...,L_{n}
do not intersect any point in
\Gamma
(the general case follows from a similar argument). In that case, we claim that if
\overline{L_{1}},...,\overline{L_{n}}\in\overline{\mathcal{F}^{s}}
are lifts of
L_{1},...,L_{n}
(see our discussion prior to Remark
6.6
), then their orbits by
\overline{G}
form a dense subset of
\overline{\mathcal{P}}
.

If the orbits by
\overline{G}
of
\overline{L_{1}},...,\overline{L_{n}}
do not form a dense set in
\overline{\mathcal{P}}
, then there would exist
U
an open set in
\overline{\mathcal{P}}-\overline{\Gamma}
that would not intersect the set
\overline{\rho}(\overline{G})(\underset{i\in\llbracket 1,n\rrbracket}{\cup}\overline{L_{i}})
. By Proposition
6.13
and thanks to Item (2), this would imply that the neighborhood
\overline{\pi}(U)\subset\mathcal{P}
(this is indeed a neighborhood thanks for instance to our geometric construction of
\overline{\mathcal{P}}
) does not intersect
\rho(G)(\underset{i\in\llbracket 1,n\rrbracket}{\cup}L_{i})
, which leads to a contradiction.
∎

Thanks to the previous proposition, we can see that
\overline{\rho}
shares several properties with
\rho
, as it satisfies most of the axioms defining a Markovian action. The main property that differentiates
\overline{\rho}
from
\rho
and any other Markovian action consists in the fact that the stabilizers in
\overline{G}
of any point in
\overline{\Gamma}
is isomorphic to a group that is bigger than
\mathbb{Z}
:

Proposition 6.15
.

The stabilizer in
\overline{G}
of any point in
\overline{\Gamma}
is isomorphic to
\mathbb{Z}^{2}
.

Proof.

Take
\overline{\gamma}\in\overline{\Gamma}
. By Proposition
6.8
, the set of stable leaves in
\overline{\mathcal{F}^{s}}
intersecting
\overline{\gamma}
is countable and ordered along
\mathbb{Z}
. We will denote the previous set of leaves by
...s_{-2},s_{-1},s_{0},s_{1},s_{2}...
Since the elements of
\overline{G}
preserve
\overline{\mathcal{F}^{s,u}}
, any element in
\text{Stab}(\overline{\gamma})
permutes the set of leaves
\{s_{k}|k\in\mathbb{Z}\}
and even more associates non-separated leaves to non-separated leaves. Therefore, by Proposition
6.8
any element
\overline{g}\in\text{Stab}(\overline{\gamma})
acts on the set
\{s_{k}|k\in\mathbb{Z}\}\equiv\mathbb{Z}
as the composition of some symmetry on
\mathbb{Z}
and some translation. But since the action
\rho
is orientation preserving, by Proposition
6.13
,
\overline{G}
acts on
\overline{\mathcal{P}}
by orientation preserving homeomorphisms too. Hence,
\overline{\rho}(\overline{g})
can only act as a translation on
\{s_{k}|k\in\mathbb{Z}\}
.

Consider first the set of elements in
\text{Stab}(\overline{\gamma})
acting on
\{s_{k}|k\in\mathbb{Z}\}
as zero translations and thus the set of elements in
\overline{G}
that fix every stable and unstable leaf of
\overline{\gamma}
. Thanks to Proposition
6.14
, the stabilizer in
\overline{G}
of a leaf
s_{i}
is isomorphic to
\mathbb{Z}
. It follows the elements of
\text{Stab}(\overline{\gamma})
stabilizing every
s_{i}
form a subgroup of
\overline{G}
isomorphic to
\mathbb{Z}
. Take
z
the generator of
\text{Stab}(s_{0})
that acts on
s_{0}
as a topological expansion (see Proposition
6.14
).

Next, by Proposition
6.8
, there exist stable leaves of
\overline{\gamma}
that project to the same separatrix in
\mathcal{P}
. It follows from Proposition
6.14
that there exist elements in
\overline{G}
acting as non-zero translations on
\{s_{k}|k\in\mathbb{Z}\}
. Take
t
an element in
\overline{G}
acting with the smallest possible strictly positive translation on
\{s_{k}|k\in\mathbb{Z}\}
. Recall now that since
\overline{\mathcal{P}}-\overline{\Gamma}
is the universal cover of
\mathcal{P}-\Gamma
, any element of
\overline{G}
that fixes one point in
\overline{\mathcal{P}}-\overline{\Gamma}
is the identity (see Proposition
6.12
). Hence, we have that
\text{Stab}(\overline{\gamma})=<z,t>
. It therefore suffices to show that
z
and
t
commute.

Indeed, by the definition of
z
and
t
we have
tzt^{-1}\in\text{Stab}(s_{0})
. Since
tzt^{-1}
is a conjugate of
z
, it also acts as an expansion on
s_{0}
. Furthermore, by Proposition
6.13
,
\mathfrak{p}(t)
and
\mathfrak{p}(z)
fix
\overline{\pi}(\overline{\gamma})\in\mathcal{P}
and therefore
\mathfrak{p}(tzt^{-1})=\mathfrak{p}(t)\mathfrak{p}(z)\mathfrak{p}(t)^{-1}=\mathfrak{p}(z)
, since
\text{Stab}(\overline{\pi}(\overline{\gamma}))=\mathbb{Z}
.

By our previous arguments, both
z
and
tzt^{-1}
preserve
s_{0}
and
\mathfrak{p}(tzt^{-1}z^{-1})=e
, where
e
is the trivial element in
G
. By Proposition
6.14
, it follows that
tzt^{-1}z^{-1}
is equal to the trivial element in
\overline{G}
, which proves that
z
and
t
commute. Finally, it is clear that neither
z
or
t
are of finite order. It follows that
<z,t>=\mathbb{Z}^{2}
.
∎

7.
Proof of Theorem C

Our goal in this section consists in proving Theorem C. Contrary to Theorems A, D and E that admit natural generalizations for strong Markovian actions, Theorems B and C consider pseudo-Anosov flows up to surgery. Although I believe that it is technically possible to define a “surgery” operation for a strong Markovian action and using this to generalize Theorems B and C for Markovian actions, this goes well beyond the scope of this paper. We will therefore restrict ourselves to proving Theorem C for strong Markovian actions arising from pseudo-Anosov flows.

7.1.
\overline{\mathcal{P}}-\overline{\Gamma}
as the bifoliated plane of a flow

In the previous section, we defined, for any general strong Markovian action
\rho:G\rightarrow\text{Homeo}(\mathcal{P})
, the bifoliated plane
(\overline{\mathcal{P}},\overline{\mathcal{F}^{s,u}},\overline{\rho})
of
\rho
up to surgeries on
\Gamma
together with its stable/unstable foliations and natural group action by
\overline{G}
, the extension of
G
associated to
\Gamma
. Our previous definition does not require
\rho
to be associated with some pseudo-Anosov flow. However, when
\rho
arises from some pseudo-Anosov flow
(M,\Phi)
, our definition does not provide a direct link between
(\overline{\mathcal{P}},\overline{\mathcal{F}^{s,u}},\overline{\rho})
and the dynamics of
\Phi
inside
M
. Our goal in this section is to clarify the relation between
(M,\Phi)
and
(\overline{\mathcal{P}},\overline{\mathcal{F}^{s,u}},\overline{\rho})
and to provide an alternative definition for
\overline{G}
using the fundamental group of
M
.

Consider
M
to be a closed, orientable, connected 3-manifold,
\Phi
a pseudo-Anosov flow on
M
,
\mathcal{P}
its bifoliated plane endowed with an orientation together with its pair of singular foliations
\mathcal{F}^{s}
and
\mathcal{F}^{u}
and its associated orientation-preserving action
\rho:\pi_{1}(M)\rightarrow\text{Homeo}(\mathcal{P})
. Consider
\mathcal{R}
a Markovian family preserved by
\rho
(recall that any Markovian family preserved by
\rho
is strong thanks to Lemmas
3.18
,
3.19
,
3.21
and
3.22
). Denote by
\Gamma
the boundary periodic points of
\mathcal{R}
,
\Gamma_{M}
the finite set of periodic orbits of
\Phi
associated to
\Gamma
,
\overline{\mathcal{P}}
the bifoliated plane of
\rho
up to surgeries on
\Gamma
together with its pair of singular foliations
\overline{\mathcal{F}^{s}}
and
\overline{\mathcal{F}^{u}}
and its associated orientation-preserving action
\overline{\rho}:\overline{\pi_{1}(M)}\rightarrow\text{Homeo}(\mathcal{P})
, where
\overline{\pi_{1}(M)}
is the extension of
\pi_{1}(M)
associated to
\Gamma
.

Proposition 7.1
.

Consider
(M-\Gamma_{M},\Phi_{\Gamma_{M}})
the restriction of
\Phi
on
M-\Gamma_{M}
. We have that the universal cover of
M-\Gamma_{M}
is homeomorphic to
\mathbb{R}^{3}
and that the orbit space of the lift on
\mathbb{R}^{3}
of the flow
(M-\Gamma_{M},\Phi_{\Gamma_{M}})
can be identified with the universal cover of
\mathcal{P}-\Gamma
or equivalently with the space
\overline{\mathcal{P}}-\overline{\Gamma}
. The projections on
\overline{\mathcal{P}}-\overline{\Gamma}
of the lifts on
\mathbb{R}^{3}
of the stable and unstable leaves of
\Phi_{\Gamma_{M}}
define on
\overline{\mathcal{P}}-\overline{\Gamma}
a pair of transverse regular foliations. Finally, the action of
\pi_{1}(M-\Gamma_{M})
on
\mathbb{R}^{3}
by deck transformations projects to a faithful action by homeomorphisms on
\overline{\mathcal{P}}-\overline{\Gamma}
.

Proof.

Denote by
\widetilde{\Phi}
the lift of
\Phi
on
\widetilde{M}=\mathbb{R}^{3}
. Let us first show that the universal cover of
M-\Gamma_{M}
is homeomorphic to
\mathbb{R}^{3}
. Let
\widetilde{\Gamma_{M}}
be the lift of
\Gamma_{M}
on
\widetilde{M}
. Since
\widetilde{M}-\widetilde{\Gamma_{M}}
is a covering space of
M-\Gamma_{M}
, the universal cover of
M-\Gamma_{M}
, denoted by
\widetilde{M-\Gamma_{M}}
, coincides with the universal cover of
\widetilde{M}-\widetilde{\Gamma_{M}}
. The fact that
\widetilde{M-\Gamma_{M}}\cong\mathbb{R}^{3}
follows from the fact that the universal cover of
\mathbb{R}^{3}
minus a countable and transversely discrete set of properly embedded lines is homeomorphic to
\mathbb{R}^{3}
.

Similarly, the lift
\widetilde{\Phi_{\Gamma_{M}}}
of
\Phi_{\Gamma_{M}}
on
\widetilde{M-\Gamma_{M}}
can be identified with the lift of
\widetilde{\Phi}
minus the orbits
\widetilde{\Gamma_{M}}
on the universal cover of
\widetilde{M}-\widetilde{\Gamma_{M}}
. This proves that the orbit space of
\widetilde{\Phi_{\Gamma_{M}}}
can be identified with the universal cover of
\mathcal{P}-\Gamma
. Furthermore, since
\Gamma_{M}
contains all the circle prong singularities of
\Phi
(see Remark
5.3
), the restrictions of the stable and unstable foliations of
\Phi
on
M-\Gamma_{M}
define a pair of transverse regular foliations. Hence, their lifts on
\widetilde{M-\Gamma_{M}}
project on the orbit space of
\widetilde{\Phi_{\Gamma_{M}}}
, thus endowing
\overline{\mathcal{P}}-\overline{\Gamma}
with a pair of transverse regular foliations.

Finally, the action of
\pi_{1}(M-\Gamma_{M})
on
\widetilde{M-\Gamma_{M}}
by deck transfomrations preserves the orbits of
\widetilde{\Phi_{\Gamma_{M}}}
and thus defines on its orbit space an action by homeomorphisms. Since
\Phi
admits non-closed orbits in
M-\Gamma_{M}
, any element of
\pi_{1}(M-\Gamma_{M})
preserving every orbit of
\widetilde{\Phi_{\Gamma_{M}}}
is necessarily trivial. This proves that the action of
\pi_{1}(M-\Gamma_{M})
on
\overline{\mathcal{P}}-\overline{\Gamma}
is faithful and finishes the proof of the proposition.

∎

Similarly to our discussion after Theorem-Definition
2.58
, by classical cover space theory,
\pi_{1}(M-\Gamma_{M})
admits infinitely many actions on
\widetilde{M-\Gamma_{M}}
by deck transformations. Furthermore, all the previous actions on
\widetilde{M-\Gamma_{M}}
coincide up to performing an inner automorphism on
\pi_{1}(M-\Gamma_{M})
. It follows that Proposition
7.1
defines infinitely many actions of
\pi_{1}(M-\Gamma_{M})
on
\overline{\mathcal{P}}-\overline{\Gamma}
that are all the same up to performing an inner automorphism on
\pi_{1}(M-\Gamma_{M})
. For the sake of simplicity and by an abuse of language, each of the previous actions of
\pi_{1}(M-\Gamma_{M})
on
\overline{\mathcal{P}}-\overline{\Gamma}
, will be called
the
action by
\pi_{1}(M-\Gamma_{M})
on
\overline{\mathcal{P}}-\overline{\Gamma}
associated to
\Phi
.

In addition to the action by
\pi_{1}(M-\Gamma_{M})
on
\overline{\mathcal{P}}-\overline{\Gamma}
associated to
\Phi
, thanks to Propositions
6.12
and
6.13
, we know that
\overline{\rho}:\overline{\pi_{1}(M)}\rightarrow\text{Homeo}(\overline{\mathcal{P}})
defines another faithful action on
\overline{\mathcal{P}}-\overline{\Gamma}
preserving a pair of transverse foliations. In the next proposition, we will show that the previous actions coincide:

Proposition 7.2
.

The group
\pi_{1}(M-\Gamma_{M})
is isomorphic to
\overline{\pi_{1}(M)}
. Furthermore, the foliations in
\overline{\mathcal{P}}-\overline{\Gamma}
that were defined in Proposition
7.1
coincide with the restrictions of
\overline{\mathcal{F}^{s}}
and
\overline{\mathcal{F}^{u}}
on
\overline{\mathcal{P}}-\overline{\Gamma}
. Finally, if
\overline{\rho}_{\pi_{1}(M-\Gamma_{M})}
denotes the action of
\pi_{1}(M-\Gamma_{M})
on
\overline{\mathcal{P}}-\overline{\Gamma}
associated to
\Phi
, there exists
H
an isomorphism from
\pi_{1}(M-\Gamma_{M})
to
\overline{\pi_{1}(M)}
such that for every
g\in\pi_{1}(M-\Gamma_{M})

\overline{\rho}_{\pi_{1}(M-\Gamma_{M})}(g)=\overline{\rho}(H(g))

Proof.

Let
\widetilde{M}
be the universal cover of
M
and
\widetilde{\Gamma_{M}}
the lift of
\Gamma_{M}
on
\widetilde{M}
. Let us first show that

Claim.

\pi_{1}(\widetilde{M}-\widetilde{\Gamma_{M}})=\text{ker}(\mathfrak{i})
, where
\mathfrak{i}
is the natural morphism from
\pi_{1}(M-\Gamma_{M})
to
\pi_{1}(M)
given by the inclusion
M-\Gamma_{M}\hookrightarrow M
.

Proof of the claim.
Indeed, consider
O
a point in
\widetilde{M}-\widetilde{\Gamma_{M}}
and its projection
O_{M}
in
M-\Gamma_{M}
. Take
\gamma
a non-homotopically trivial loop in
\widetilde{M}-\widetilde{\Gamma_{M}}
starting and ending at
O
. Its projection
\gamma_{M}
in
M-\Gamma_{M}
is also non-homotopically trivial. However, by adding back the lines
\widetilde{\Gamma_{M}}
in
\widetilde{M}-\widetilde{\Gamma_{M}}
the loop
\gamma
becomes homotopically trivial (as
\widetilde{M}
is simply connected) and therefore by adding back the circles
\Gamma_{M}
in
M-\Gamma_{M}
, the loop
\gamma_{M}
becomes homotopically trivial. Hence, the map
\gamma\rightarrow\gamma_{M}
defines a morphism
i:\pi_{1}(\widetilde{M}-\widetilde{\Gamma_{M}},O)\rightarrow\text{ker}\big(\pi_{1}(M-\Gamma_{M},O_{M})\rightarrow\pi_{1}(M,O_{M})\big)=ker(\mathfrak{i})
.

Conversely, consider a loop
\gamma_{M}\in ker(\mathfrak{i})=\text{ker}\big(\pi_{1}(M-\Gamma_{M},O_{M})\rightarrow\pi_{1}(M,O_{M})\big)
. Take the lift
\gamma
of
\gamma_{M}
that starts at
O
. By definition, if we add
\Gamma_{M}
in
M-\Gamma_{M}
, then
\gamma_{M}
becomes homotopically trivial. Therefore, if we add
\widetilde{\Gamma_{M}}
in
\widetilde{M}-\widetilde{\Gamma_{M}}
the curve
\gamma
must also become homotopically trivial. We deduce that
\gamma
is a loop that starts and ends at
O
. The map
\gamma_{M}\rightarrow\gamma
defines a morphism
j:ker(\mathfrak{i})\rightarrow\pi_{1}(\widetilde{M}-\widetilde{\Gamma_{M}},O)
. It is easy to check that
i
and
j
are inverses of each other and therefore
\pi_{1}(\widetilde{M}-\widetilde{\Gamma_{M}})=ker(\mathfrak{i})
. This finishes the proof of the claim.

From the previous claim we get that the cover
\widetilde{M}-\widetilde{\Gamma_{M}}
of
M-\Gamma_{M}
is a normal covering of
M-\Gamma_{M}
and also that

\text{Aut}(\widetilde{M}-\widetilde{\Gamma_{M}}\rightarrow M-\Gamma_{M})={\raisebox{1.99997pt}{$\pi_{1}(M-\Gamma_{M})$}\left/\raisebox{-1.99997pt}{$ker(\mathfrak{i})$}\right.}\cong\pi_{1}(M)

Notice that the restriction on
\widetilde{M}-\widetilde{\Gamma_{M}}
of the action of
\text{Aut}(\widetilde{M}\rightarrow M)\cong\pi_{1}(M)
coincides (up to inner automorphism) with the action of
\text{Aut}(\widetilde{M}-\widetilde{\Gamma_{M}}\rightarrow M-\Gamma_{M})\cong\pi_{1}(M)
on
\widetilde{M}-\widetilde{\Gamma_{M}}
.

Construction of the group isomorphism
H
.

First of all, let us recall the definition of
\overline{\pi_{1}(M)}
, the extension of
\pi_{1}(M)
associated to
\Gamma
(see Section

for more details). Consider
x_{0}\in\mathcal{P}-\Gamma
and

\text{Curv}_{2}:=\{\gamma:[0,1]\overset{C^{0}}{\rightarrow}\mathcal{P}|\penalty\ \gamma(0)=x_{0},\gamma[0,1)\cap\Gamma=\emptyset\}

\text{Curv}_{2}(x_{0}):=\{\gamma\in\text{Curv}_{2}|\gamma(1)\in\rho(\pi_{1}(M))(x_{0})\}

Recall that every element of
\overline{\pi_{1}(M)}
is a couple consisting of:

(1)

an element

(2)

the homotopy class relatively to
\sim_{2}
(or equivalently relatively to its boundary) of a curve in

starting from
x_{0}
and ending at
\rho(h)(x_{0})

Before beginning the construction of the desired isomorphism, let us introduce some notations. Denote by

to
\widetilde{M}-\widetilde{\Gamma_{M}}

By Theorem-Definition

, there exists

\psi_{\widetilde{M}-\widetilde{\Gamma_{M}}}:\pi_{1}(M)\rightarrow\text{Homeo}(\widetilde{M}-\widetilde{\Gamma_{M}})

an action of

(7.1)

\pi_{\mathcal{P}}\circ\psi_{\widetilde{M}-\widetilde{\Gamma_{M}}}(h)=\rho(h)\circ\pi_{\mathcal{P}}

Similarly, by lifting

on
\widetilde{M-\Gamma_{M}}
, we get that there exists

on

verifying for every

to

.

Fix

an element of
\overline{\pi_{1}(M)}
. Take

We define

H(g):=(\mathfrak{i}(g),\overline{\pi_{\mathcal{P}}(\gamma_{g})})\in\overline{\pi_{1}(M)}

. Finally, the proof of the fact that

On the elements in

Consider

to be a closed, orientable, connected 3-manifold,
\Phi
a pseudo-Anosov flow on

,
\mathcal{P}
its bifoliated plane endowed with an orientation together with its pair of singular foliations

and

. Denote by

,

the bifoliated plane of

up to surgeries on

together with its pair of singular foliations

In the second case, we already know that the stabilizer (up to conjugacy) of the point is generated by the homotopy class of its associated periodic orbit in
M
. Our goal in this section, consists in describing the homotopy classes in

forming the

Denote by

. Consider an orientation on

.

Fix for the rest of this section
\overline{\gamma}\in\overline{\Gamma}
. Let
...,s_{-1},s_{0},s_{1},s_{2},...
be the stable leaves in

containing

.

6

Formally speaking, as the

, this sentence makes little sense. However, one can make sense of this by using a foliation chart.

In Proposition

Notice that

. We would now like to define a different basis of

A different basis of
\text{Stab}(\overline{\gamma})
.
Consider
\gamma:=\overline{\pi}(\overline{\gamma})
,

(

Both

Proposition 7.3
.

We have that
<m,p>=<z,t>=\text{Stab}(\overline{\gamma})

Proof.

Indeed, by our definition of
p
and
m
, we have that
<p,m>\leq\text{Stab}(\overline{\gamma})
. Conversely, using Proposition
3.26
in addition to the fact that the action of
\pi_{1}(M)
on
\mathcal{P}
is orientation preserving and that
n
corresponds to the number of prongs of
\gamma
, there exists
r\in\llbracket 0,n-1\rrbracket
such that the action of
g
close to
\gamma
is conjugated to
\phi_{n,r}
, a local model for a pseudo-hyperbolic fixed point with
n
prongs, rotation
r
and positive orientation. Using the previous fact, we get that

\text{Stab}_{\pi_{1}(M)}(\overline{\pi}(s_{0}))=<g^{{\frac{n}{\text{gcd(n,r)}}}}>

where
gcd(n,r)
is the greatest common divisor of
n
and
r
(by convention
\text{gcd}(n,0)=n
). Since both
g^{{\frac{n}{\text{gcd(n,r)}}}}\in\pi_{1}(M)
and
\mathfrak{p}(z)\in\pi_{1}(M)
generate the group
\text{Stab}_{\pi_{1}(M)}(\overline{\pi}(s_{0}))\cong\mathbb{Z}
and act as expansions on
\mathcal{F}^{s}(\gamma)
, we get that

\mathfrak{p}(z)=g^{{\frac{n}{\text{gcd(n,r)}}}}

. It follows that
z\cdot p^{-\frac{n}{\text{gcd(n,r)}}}
is equal to a power of
m
and thus
z\in<m,p>
. Next, since
g
generates
\text{Stab}(\gamma)
, it is not difficult to see that the group
<p,m>
acts transitively on the set of stable leaves
s_{i}
that belong in the
\overline{\rho}
-orbit of
s_{0}
. Using the previous fact, the fact that
t
is only defined up to a power of
z
and that
z\in<m,p>
, we get that
t\in<m,p>
, which gives us the desired result.

Recall that our goal in this section consists in describing the homotopy classes in

On the description of the homotopy classes

and

.

Definition of

.
Using our choice of orientation on

, endow

with its inward orientation. Next, denote by

Consider now any periodic orbit

Definition of
p_{\gamma}
.

Recall that
\gamma_{m}
and
\gamma_{P}
are simple closed curves, that intersect transversely and that verify

i_{a}(\gamma_{P},\gamma_{m})=i_{g}(\gamma_{P},\gamma_{m})>0

In other words,
\gamma_{m}
and
\gamma_{P}
intersect at least once and performing a homotopy on either

or

can only increase the number of intersections between the two curves.
More precisely, if

is associated by Proposition

to a local model for a pseudo-hyperbolic orbit with

, then thanks to Remark

, we get that the total number of intersections between

and

.

Consider now

with all the periodic orbits of
\Phi^{*}
on

. By eventually reindexing the

, we can assume that

and that when following positively

, then

,…, and so on till we return to

. Following positively

starting from

, let

be the first point of intersection between
\gamma_{P}
and
\gamma_{m}
after

, where

. Let us remark here that

•

•

following positively
\gamma_{P}
starting from

, the point

Consider

,

. The juxtaposition of

.

We are now ready to state the main result of this section. Recall that in the beginning of this section, we defined
\overline{\gamma}\in\overline{\Gamma}
,
(s_{i})_{i\in\mathbb{Z}}
the set of ordered stable manifolds of

in

,
\gamma=\overline{\pi}(\overline{\gamma})
,

and

.

Identify from now on the interior of

. Push by a homotopy

in

(3)

\gamma^{\prime}_{P}

By a small abuse of language, denote respectively by

,

the homotopy classes of

,

and

.

Figure 7.2
.

Proposition 7.4
.

We have that there exists
h\in\pi_{1}(M-\Gamma_{M},x_{0}^{\prime})
such that
m_{\gamma}=h\cdot m\cdot h^{-1}
,
p_{\gamma}=h\cdot p\cdot h^{-1}
and
P_{\gamma}=h\cdot z\cdot h^{-1}
. In particular, for every
i\in\mathbb{Z}

•

\overline{\rho}(m_{\gamma})(s_{i})=s_{i+n}

•

\overline{\rho}(p_{\gamma})(s_{i})=s_{i+n-k}

•

\overline{\rho}(P_{\gamma})(s_{i})=s_{i}

Proof.

Thanks to the fact that
p,m,z\in\pi_{1}(M-\Gamma_{M})
are uniquely defined by their action on
\overline{\mathcal{P}}
, in order to prove the above proposition, it suffices to understand how the elements
p_{\gamma},m_{\gamma},P_{\gamma}
act on
\overline{\mathcal{P}}
via
\overline{\rho}
. First of all, notice that the three curves
\gamma^{\prime}_{P}
,
\gamma^{\prime}_{m}
and
\gamma^{\prime}_{p}
can be moved inside
\gamma_{M}
by a homotopy in
M
. It follows, thanks to Proposition
7.2
, that
\overline{\rho}(m_{\gamma}),\overline{\rho}(p_{\gamma})
and
\overline{\rho}(P_{\gamma})
fix a point in
\overline{\Gamma}
that is in the same
\overline{\rho}
-orbit as
\overline{\gamma}
. By eventually conjugating
P_{\gamma}
,
m_{\gamma}
and
p_{\gamma}
by an element in
\pi_{1}(M-\Gamma_{M},x_{0}^{\prime})
assume without any loss of generality that
\overline{\rho}(m_{\gamma}),\overline{\rho}(p_{\gamma})
and
\overline{\rho}(P_{\gamma})
fix
\overline{\gamma}
. Using our previous hypothesis, we will now show that
m_{\gamma}=m
,
p_{\gamma}=p
and
P_{\gamma}=z
.

First, notice that if we add
\Gamma_{M}
back to
M-\Gamma_{M}
the curve
\gamma^{\prime}_{m}
becomes homotopically trivial. Hence,
m_{\gamma}\in\text{ker}(\mathfrak{p})
, where
\mathfrak{p}
is the natural morphism from
\pi_{1}(M-\Gamma_{M},x^{\prime}_{0})
to
\pi_{1}(M,x^{\prime}_{0})
. Also, take
L
to be any stable separatrix of
X_{M}
in
S
and
Y
the unique point of intersection of
\gamma^{\prime}_{m}
and
L
. Thanks to our choice of orientation of
\gamma_{m}
, we have that the direction of the flow
\Phi
at
Y
followed by the direction of
\gamma_{m}^{\prime}
at
Y
, followed finally by the direction in
L
pointing away from
X_{M}
defines an orientation on
M
compatible with our original choice of orientation
7
7

7

Once again, this sentence makes little sence as we are working in a
C^{0}
setting. However, one can make sense of this by using a well chosen chart
. Thanks to the previous fact and the fact that
\gamma^{\prime}_{m}
goes once around
\gamma_{M}
, we get that
\overline{\rho}(m_{\gamma})(s_{i})=s_{n+i}
for every
i\in\mathbb{Z}
; hence,
m_{\gamma}=m
.

Next, if we add
\Gamma_{M}
back to
M-\Gamma_{M}
the curve
\gamma^{\prime}_{p}
becomes homotopic to
\gamma_{M}
, which implies thanks to Proposition
3.26
that
\mathfrak{p}(p_{\gamma})
is a generator of
\text{Stab}_{\pi_{1}(M)}(\gamma)
, where
\gamma=\overline{\pi}(\overline{\gamma})
. More specifically, it is the generator of
\text{Stab}(\gamma)
acting on
\mathcal{F}^{s}(\gamma)
, the stable leaf of
\gamma
in
\mathcal{P}
, as an expansion. Recall that
\gamma^{\prime}_{p}
is homotopic to a curve consisting first of a segment in
\gamma^{\prime}_{m}
going from
x^{\prime}_{0}
to
x^{\prime}_{n-k}
followed by a segment inside a stable separatrix of
\gamma_{M}
. By looking at the action of
p_{\gamma}
as a deck transformation on the universal cover of
M-\Gamma_{M}
, we get that
\overline{\rho}(p_{\gamma})(s_{i})=s_{i+n-k}
for every
i\in\mathbb{Z}
; hence,
p_{\gamma}=p
.

Finally, if we add
\Gamma_{M}
back to
M-\Gamma_{M}
the curve
\gamma_{P}
becomes homotopic to
\gamma_{M}
followed positively
\frac{n}{\text{gcd}(n,k)}
times. Thanks to the previous fact, the fact that
\gamma_{M}
is associated by Proposition
2.23
to a local model for a pseudo-hyperbolic orbit with
n\geq 2
prongs and rotation
r\in\llbracket 0,p-1\rrbracket
and since
k\in\{r,n-r\}
, we get that
\mathfrak{p}(P_{\gamma})=\mathfrak{p}(p_{\gamma})^{\frac{n}{\text{gcd}(n,k)}}
is the generator of
\text{Stab}_{\pi_{1}(M)}(\overline{\pi}(s_{0}))
acting on
\overline{\pi}(s_{0})
as an expansion. Recall that
\gamma^{\prime}_{P}
is homotopic to a loop inside a stable separatrix of
\gamma_{M}
. By looking at the action of
P_{\gamma}
as a deck transformation on the universal cover of
M-\Gamma_{M}
, we get that
\overline{\rho}(P_{\gamma})(s_{i})=s_{i}
for every
i\in\mathbb{Z}
; hence,
P_{\gamma}=z
.

∎

7.3.
Proof of Theorem C

Let
(\Phi_{1},M_{1})
,
(\Phi_{2},M_{2})
be two pseudo-Anosov flows on two closed, orientable 3-manifolds,
(\mathcal{P}_{1},\mathcal{F}_{1}^{s,u},\rho_{1}),(\mathcal{P}_{2},\mathcal{F}_{2}^{s,u},\rho_{2})
their bifoliated planes endowed with their natural foliations and group actions,
\mathcal{R}_{1}
,
\mathcal{R}_{2}
two Markovian families preserved by
\rho_{1}
and
\rho_{2}
and
\Gamma_{1},\Gamma_{2}
their boundary periodic points. Denote by
\Gamma^{M_{1}}_{1}
,
\Gamma^{M_{2}}_{2}
the two finite sets of periodic orbits of
\Phi_{1}
,
\Phi_{2}
corresponding to
\Gamma_{1}
and
\Gamma_{2}
. Denote also by
(\overline{\mathcal{P}_{1}},\overline{\mathcal{F}_{1}^{s,u}},\overline{\rho_{1}})
,
(\overline{\mathcal{P}_{2}},\overline{\mathcal{F}_{2}^{s,u}},\overline{\rho_{2}})
the bifoliated planes of
\Phi_{1}
,
\Phi_{2}
up to surgeries on
\Gamma_{1}
,
\Gamma_{2}
endowed with their natural foliations and group actions by
\pi_{1}(M_{1}-\Gamma^{M_{1}}_{1})
and
\pi_{1}(M_{2}-\Gamma^{M_{2}}_{2})
respectively.

Theorem 7.5

(Theorem C)
.

We can perform Dehn-Goodman-Fried surgeries on the orbits
\Gamma^{M_{1}}_{1}
of
(\Phi_{1},M_{1})
so that the flow obtained after surgery is orbitally equivalent to
(\Phi_{2},M_{2})
and so that
\Gamma^{M_{2}}_{2}
are the orbits of
\Phi_{2}
corresponding to
\Gamma^{M_{1}}_{1}
after surgery (see Definition
3.41
) if and only if there exists a homeomorphism
h:\overline{\mathcal{P}_{1}}\rightarrow\overline{\mathcal{P}_{2}}
such that:

(1)

the image by
h
of any stable (resp. unstable) leaf in
\overline{\mathcal{F}_{1}^{s}}
(resp.
\overline{\mathcal{F}_{1}^{u}}
) is a stable (resp. unstable) leaf in
\overline{\mathcal{F}_{2}^{s}}
(resp.
\overline{\mathcal{F}_{2}^{u}}
)

(2)

there exists an isomorphism
\alpha:\pi_{1}(M_{1}-\Gamma^{M_{1}}_{1})\rightarrow\pi_{1}(M_{2}-\Gamma^{M_{2}}_{2})
such that for every
g\in\pi_{1}(M_{1}-\Gamma^{M_{1}}_{1})
and every
x\in\overline{\mathcal{P}_{1}}
we have

h(\overline{\rho_{1}}(g)(x))=\overline{\rho_{2}}(\alpha(g))(h(x))

Proof.

Suppose that after performing surgeries on
(\Phi_{1},M_{1})
along the orbits
\Gamma_{1}^{M_{1}}
, we obtain a flow that is orbitally equivalent to
(\Phi_{2},M_{2})
and that
\Gamma_{2}^{M_{2}}
is the set of periodic orbits of
\Phi_{2}
corresponding to
\Gamma_{1}^{M_{1}}
after surgery. Denote by
\Phi_{1}-\Gamma^{M_{1}}_{1}
and
\Phi_{2}-\Gamma^{M_{2}}_{2}
the restrictions of the flows
\Phi_{1}
and
\Phi_{2}
on
M_{1}-\Gamma^{M_{1}}_{1}
and
M_{2}-\Gamma^{M_{2}}_{2}
and by
\widetilde{\Phi_{1}-\Gamma^{M_{1}}_{1}}
and
\widetilde{\Phi_{2}-\Gamma^{M_{2}}_{2}}
the lifts of
\Phi_{1}-\Gamma^{M_{1}}_{1}
and
\Phi_{2}-\Gamma^{M_{2}}_{2}
on
\widetilde{M_{1}-\Gamma^{M_{1}}_{1}}
and
\widetilde{M_{2}-\Gamma^{M_{2}}_{2}}
, the universal covers of
M_{1}-\Gamma^{M_{1}}_{1}
and
M_{2}-\Gamma^{M_{2}}_{2}
. Thanks to Theorem
3.40
, the flows
(\Phi_{1}-\Gamma^{M_{1}}_{1},M_{1}-\Gamma^{M_{1}}_{1})
and
(\Phi_{2}-\Gamma^{M_{2}}_{2},M_{2}-\Gamma^{M_{2}}_{2})
are orbitally equivalent. The orbital equivalence, say
H
, between
\Phi_{1}-\Gamma^{M_{1}}_{1}
and
\Phi_{2}-\Gamma^{M_{2}}_{2}
defines an isomorphism
\alpha:\pi_{1}(M_{1}-\Gamma^{M_{1}}_{1})\rightarrow\pi_{1}(M_{2}-\Gamma^{M_{2}}_{2})
and lifts to an orbital equivalence
\widetilde{H}
between
\widetilde{\Phi_{1}-\Gamma^{M_{1}}_{1}}
and
\widetilde{\Phi_{2}-\Gamma^{M_{2}}_{2}}
, such that:

(1)

\widetilde{H}
sends the lift on
\widetilde{M_{1}-\Gamma^{M_{1}}_{1}}
of a stable (resp. unstable) leaf of
\Phi_{1}-\Gamma_{1}
to the lift on
\widetilde{M_{2}-\Gamma^{M_{2}}_{2}}
of a stable (resp. unstable) leaf of
\Phi_{2}-\Gamma_{2}

(2)

if
\psi_{1}
and
\psi_{2}
denote the actions by deck transformations of
\pi_{1}(M_{1}-\Gamma_{1}^{M_{1}})
and
\pi_{1}(M_{2}-\Gamma_{2}^{M_{2}})
on
\widetilde{M_{1}-\Gamma^{M_{1}}_{1}}
and
\widetilde{M_{2}-\Gamma^{M_{2}}_{2}}
, then for every
g\in\pi_{1}(M_{1}-\Gamma_{1}^{M_{1}})
and every
x\in\widetilde{M_{1}-\Gamma^{M_{1}}_{1}}
we have

\widetilde{H}(\psi_{1}(g)(x))=\psi_{2}(\alpha(g))(\widetilde{H}(x))

Since
\widetilde{H}
sends orbits of
\widetilde{\Phi_{1}-\Gamma^{M_{1}}_{1}}
to orbits of
\widetilde{\Phi_{2}-\Gamma^{M_{2}}_{2}}
, it induces a homeomorphism
h:\overline{\mathcal{P}_{1}}-\overline{\Gamma_{1}}\rightarrow\overline{\mathcal{P}_{2}}-\overline{\Gamma_{2}}
between the orbit spaces of the lifted flows (see Proposition
7.1
) such that

(1)

the image by
h
of any stable/unstable leaf in
\overline{\mathcal{F}_{1}^{s,u}}
is a stable/unstable leaf in
\overline{\mathcal{F}_{2}^{s,u}}

(2)

for every
g\in\pi_{1}(M_{1}-\Gamma^{M_{1}}_{1})
and every
x\in\overline{\mathcal{P}_{1}}-\overline{\Gamma_{1}}
we have

h(\overline{\rho_{1}}(g)(x))=\overline{\rho_{2}}(\alpha(g))(h(x))

Since
H
sends a neighborhood of an orbit in
\Gamma^{M_{1}}_{1}
to the neighborhood of a unique orbit in
\Gamma_{2}
, the homeomorphism
h
sends a punctured neighborhood of any point in
\overline{\Gamma_{1}}
to a punctured neighborhood of a unique point in
\overline{\Gamma_{2}}
. Therefore,
h
can be extended to a homeomorphism from
\overline{\mathcal{P}_{1}}
to
\overline{\mathcal{P}_{2}}
, which gives us the desired result.

Let us now show the converse. Assume that there exists a homeomorphism
h:\overline{\mathcal{P}_{1}}\rightarrow\overline{\mathcal{P}_{2}}
with the above properties. Fix an orientation on
\overline{\mathcal{P}_{1}}
and
\overline{\mathcal{P}_{2}}
, so that
h
is orientation preserving. First, notice that

Claim.

h(\overline{\Gamma_{1}})=\overline{\Gamma_{2}}
; hence the number of orbits in
\Gamma^{M_{1}}_{1}
is equal to the number of orbits in
\Gamma^{M_{2}}_{2}

Proof of Claim.
Indeed, using the Proposition
6.15
, the number of orbits in
\Gamma^{M_{1}}_{1}
(resp.
\Gamma^{M_{2}}_{2}
) is equal to the number of
\overline{\rho_{1}}
-orbits (resp.
\overline{\rho_{2}}
-orbits) in
\overline{\Gamma_{1}}
(resp.
\overline{\Gamma_{2}}
) or equivalently to the number of
\overline{\rho_{1}}
-orbits (resp.
\overline{\rho_{2}}
-orbits) of points
\overline{\mathcal{P}_{1}}
(resp.
\overline{\mathcal{P}_{2}}
), whose stabilizers are isomorphic to
\mathbb{Z}^{2}
. By hypothesis,
h
is equivariant with respect to the action of the fundamental groups and sends points with
\mathbb{Z}^{2}
stabilizers to points with
\mathbb{Z}^{2}
stabilizers. We conclude that
\Gamma^{M_{1}}_{1}
and
\Gamma^{M_{2}}_{2}
contain the same number of orbits. ∎

Recall that
\overline{\mathcal{P}_{1}}-\overline{\Gamma_{1}}
is the bifoliated plane of
\Phi_{1}-\Gamma_{1}
. By taking a pair of directions on
\overline{\mathcal{P}_{1}}-\overline{\Gamma_{1}}
that define an orientation on
\overline{\mathcal{P}_{1}}
compatible with our original choice of orientation and by adding at the end the direction of the flow in
\widetilde{M_{1}-\Gamma_{1}^{M_{1}}}
, we define an orientation on
\widetilde{M_{1}-\Gamma^{M_{1}}_{1}}
and therefore an orientation on
M_{1}
. Similarly, we can define an orientation on
M_{2}
.

Denote by
...,s_{-1,\overline{\gamma}},s_{0,\overline{\gamma}},s_{1,\overline{\gamma}},s_{2,\overline{\gamma}},...
the stable leaves in
\overline{\mathcal{F}^{s}}
containing
\overline{\gamma}\in\overline{\Gamma_{1}}
(resp.
\overline{\gamma}\in\overline{\Gamma_{2}}
) ordered clockwisely for our choice of orientation of
\overline{\mathcal{P}_{1}}-\overline{\Gamma_{1}}
(resp.
\overline{\mathcal{P}_{2}}-\overline{\Gamma_{2}}
). In other words, we assume that the
...,s_{-1,\overline{\gamma}},s_{0,\overline{\gamma}},s_{1,\overline{\gamma}},s_{2,\overline{\gamma}},...
are ordered in such a way that for every
i\in\mathbb{Z}
the leaf
s_{i,\overline{\gamma}}
is not separated from
s_{i+1,\overline{\gamma}}
in the stable leaf space of
\overline{\mathcal{F}^{s}_{1}}
(resp.
\overline{\mathcal{F}^{s}_{2}}
) and that for any
x\in s_{i,\overline{\gamma}}
the direction at
x
pointing inside the connected component of
\overline{\mathcal{P}_{1}}-s_{i,\overline{\gamma}}
(resp.
\overline{\mathcal{P}_{2}}-s_{i,\overline{\gamma}}
) containing
s_{i+1,\overline{\gamma}}
followed by the direction at
x
pointing inside
s_{i,\overline{\gamma}}
and away from
\overline{\gamma}
defines an orientation compatible with our original orientation of
\overline{\mathcal{P}_{1}}-\overline{\Gamma}_{1}
(resp.
\overline{\mathcal{P}_{2}}-\overline{\Gamma_{2}}
). Recall that thanks to the previous orientations on
M_{1}
and
M_{2}
, one can construct as in Section
7.2
, for every element
\overline{\gamma}
in
\overline{\Gamma_{1}}
(resp.
\overline{\Gamma_{2}}
) a basis
p^{M_{1}}_{\overline{\gamma}},m^{M_{1}}_{\overline{\gamma}}
(resp.
p^{M_{2}}_{\overline{\gamma}},m^{M_{2}}_{\overline{\gamma}}
) of
\text{Stab}(\overline{\gamma})
satisfying Proposition
7.4
.

Using our previous Claim and the fact that
h
is equivariant with respect to the group actions on
\overline{\mathcal{P}_{1}}
and
\overline{\mathcal{P}_{2}}
, we get that for every
\overline{\gamma}\in\overline{\Gamma_{1}}
, the isomorphism
\alpha
sends a basis of
\text{Stab}(\overline{\gamma})
to a basis of
\text{Stab}(h(\overline{\gamma}))
, where
h(\overline{\gamma})\in\overline{\Gamma_{2}}
. Fix
\overline{\gamma}\in\overline{\Gamma_{1}}
and
\gamma\in\Gamma^{M_{1}}_{1}
the periodic orbit in
M_{1}
that is associated to
\overline{\gamma}
(see Proposition
3.26
). Thanks to the previous fact, there exists a matrix
A_{\gamma}\in\text{GL}_{2}(\mathbb{Z})
such that
A_{\gamma}=\begin{pmatrix}a_{\gamma}&c_{\gamma}\\
b_{\gamma}&d_{\gamma}\end{pmatrix}
and

(7.3)

\alpha(m^{M_{1}}_{\overline{\gamma}})=(m^{M_{2}}_{h(\overline{\gamma})})^{a_{\gamma}}\cdot(p^{M_{2}}_{h(\overline{\gamma})})^{b_{\gamma}}

(7.4)

\alpha(p^{M_{1}}_{\overline{\gamma}})=(m^{M_{2}}_{h(\overline{\gamma})})^{c_{\gamma}}\cdot(p^{M_{2}}_{h(\overline{\gamma})})^{d_{\gamma}}

As indicated by our choice of notation,

Claim.

A_{\gamma}
only depends on
\gamma
and not on the choice of lift of
\gamma
in
\overline{\mathcal{P}_{1}}

Proof of Claim.
Indeed, take
g\in\pi_{1}(M_{1}-\Gamma^{M_{1}}_{1})
. By our definition of
m^{M_{1}}_{\overline{\gamma}},p^{M_{1}}_{\overline{\gamma}}
(see Section
7.2
) and since the actions
\overline{\rho_{1}}
and
\overline{\rho_{2}}
are orientation preserving, we have that

•

m^{M_{1}}_{\overline{\rho_{1}}(g)(\overline{\gamma})}=gm^{M_{1}}_{\overline{\gamma}}g^{-1}
,
p^{M_{1}}_{\overline{\rho_{1}}(g)(\overline{\gamma})}=gp^{M_{1}}_{\overline{\gamma}}g^{-1}

•

m^{M_{2}}_{h(\overline{\rho_{1}}(g)(\overline{\gamma}))}=\alpha(g)m^{M_{2}}_{h(\overline{\gamma})}\alpha(g)^{-1}
,
p^{M_{2}}_{h(\overline{\rho_{1}}(g)(\overline{\gamma}))}=\alpha(g)p^{M_{2}}_{h(\overline{\gamma})}\alpha(g)^{-1}

Therefore,

\alpha(m^{M_{1}}_{\overline{\rho_{1}}(g)(\overline{\gamma})})=(m^{M_{2}}_{h(\overline{\rho_{1}}(g)(\overline{\gamma}))})^{a_{\gamma}}\cdot(p^{M_{2}}_{h(\overline{\rho_{1}}(g)(\overline{\gamma}))})^{b_{\gamma}}

\alpha(p^{M_{1}}_{\overline{\rho_{1}}(g)(\overline{\gamma})})=(m^{M_{2}}_{h(\overline{\rho_{1}}(g)(\overline{\gamma}))})^{c_{\gamma}}\cdot(p^{M_{2}}_{h(\overline{\rho_{1}}(g)(\overline{\gamma}))})^{d_{\gamma}}

∎

By the above, to every
\gamma\in\Gamma^{M_{1}}_{1}
we can associate a matrix

A_{\gamma}=\begin{pmatrix}a_{\gamma}&c_{\gamma}\\
b_{\gamma}&d_{\gamma}\end{pmatrix}\in\text{GL}_{2}(\mathbb{Z})

In addition to belonging to
\text{GL}_{2}(\mathbb{Z})
the matrices
A_{\gamma}
satisfy several additional properties that will crucial for the construction of our proof.

On the properties of the coefficients of
A_{\gamma}
.
Once again fix
\overline{\gamma}\in\overline{\Gamma_{1}}
and
\gamma\in\Gamma^{M_{1}}_{1}
the periodic orbit in
M_{1}
that is associated to
\overline{\gamma}
. First let us show that,

(7.5)

\alpha(P_{\overline{\gamma}}^{M_{1}})=P_{h(\overline{\gamma})}^{M_{2}}

Proof of Claim.
Since the action
\overline{\rho_{1}}
preserves the orientation of
\overline{\mathcal{P}_{1}}
, we have that
\text{Stab}(s_{i,\overline{\gamma}})=\text{Stab}(s_{j,\overline{\gamma}})
for every
i,j\in\mathbb{Z}
. The same result of course applies for the action of
\overline{\rho_{2}}
on
\overline{\mathcal{P}_{2}}
. As
h
is equivariant with respect to
\overline{\rho_{1}},\overline{\rho_{2}}
, we have that
\alpha(\text{Stab}(s_{0,\overline{\gamma}}))=\text{Stab}(h(s_{0,\overline{\gamma}}))
. By Proposition
6.14
, the previous stabilizers are isomorphic to
\mathbb{Z}
and thanks to Proposition
7.4
, they are respectively generated by
P_{\overline{\gamma}}^{M_{1}}
and
P_{h(\overline{\gamma})}^{M_{2}}
. Finally, using the equivariance of
h
and the fact that by definition
P_{\overline{\gamma}}^{M_{1}}
and
P_{h(\overline{\gamma})}^{M_{2}}
are the unique generators
\text{Stab}(s_{0,\overline{\gamma}})
and
\text{Stab}(h(s_{0,\overline{\gamma}}))
acting on
s_{0,\overline{\gamma}}
and
h(s_{0,\overline{\gamma}})
as topological expansions, we get the desired result. ∎

Equation
7.5
translates to a condition on the coefficients of
A_{\gamma}
(see Equation
7.9
). Indeed, recall that thanks to Proposition
7.4
and Lemma
3.42
, there exist
n_{1},n_{2}\in\mathbb{N}^{*}
,
k_{1}\in\llbracket 1,n_{1}\rrbracket
and
k_{2}\in\llbracket 1,n_{2}\rrbracket
such that

(7.6)

P_{\overline{\gamma}}^{M_{1}}=(m_{\overline{\gamma}}^{M_{1}})^{-\frac{(n_{1}-k_{1})}{gcd(n_{1},k_{1})}}\cdot(p_{\overline{\gamma}}^{M_{1}})^{\frac{n_{1}}{gcd(n_{1},k_{1})}}

(7.7)

P_{h(\overline{\gamma})}^{M_{2}}=(m_{h(\overline{\gamma})}^{M_{2}})^{-\frac{(n_{2}-k_{2})}{gcd(n_{2},k_{2})}}\cdot(p_{h(\overline{\gamma})}^{M_{2}})^{\frac{n_{2}}{gcd(n_{2},k_{2})}}

where
gcd(n_{1},k_{1})
and
gcd(n_{2},k_{2})
denote respectively the greatest common divisors of
n_{1},k_{1}
and
n_{2},k_{2}
. We remind the reader that prior to Lemma
3.42
, we provided an explicit definition of
n_{1},k_{1}
thanks to which we have that if
\gamma
is associated by Proposition
2.23
to a local model for a pseudo-hyperbolic orbit with
n(\gamma)\geq 2
prongs and rotation
r(\gamma)\in\llbracket 0,p-1\rrbracket
, then
n_{1}=n(\gamma)
and
r(\gamma)\in\{k_{1},n_{1}-k_{1}\}
. Furthermore, thanks to Proposition
7.4
, we have that
\overline{\rho_{1}}(m_{\overline{\gamma}}^{M_{1}})
and
\overline{\rho_{1}}(p_{\overline{\gamma}}^{M_{1}})
act as translations by
n_{1}
and
n_{1}-k_{1}
on the set of
\{s_{i,\overline{\gamma}}|i\in\mathbb{Z}\}
. Similar results hold for
n_{2},k_{2}
. It follows that
\{s_{i,\overline{\gamma}}|i\in\mathbb{Z}\}
(resp.
\{s_{i,h(\overline{\gamma})}|i\in\mathbb{Z}\}
) consists of
gcd(n_{1},k_{1})
(resp.
gcd(n_{2},k_{2})
) stable leaves up to the action of
\text{Stab}(\overline{\gamma})
(resp.
\text{Stab}(h(\overline{\gamma}))
).

By our previous arguments, we can conclude, thanks to the equivariance of
h
, that

(7.8)

gcd(n_{1},k_{1})=gcd(n_{2},k_{2})

Moreover, thanks to Equations
7.3
,
7.4
and
7.6
we have that

\displaystyle\alpha(P^{M_{1}}_{\gamma})

\displaystyle=\alpha((m_{\overline{\gamma}}^{M_{1}})^{-\frac{(n_{1}-k_{1})}{gcd(n_{1},k_{1})}}\cdot(p_{\overline{\gamma}}^{M_{1}})^{\frac{n_{1}}{gcd(n_{1},k_{1})}})

\displaystyle=(m_{h(\overline{\gamma})}^{M_{2}})^{-\frac{a_{\gamma}\cdot(n_{1}-k_{1})}{gcd(n_{1},k_{1})}}\cdot(p_{h(\overline{\gamma})}^{M_{2}})^{-\frac{b_{\gamma}\cdot(n_{1}-k_{1})}{gcd(n_{1},k_{1})}}(m_{h(\overline{\gamma})}^{M_{2}})^{\frac{c_{\gamma}\cdot n_{1}}{gcd(n_{1},k_{1})}}\cdot(p_{h(\overline{\gamma})}^{M_{2}})^{\frac{d_{\gamma}\cdot n_{1}}{gcd(n_{1},k_{1})}}

By combining the above equation with Equations
7.5
,
7.7
, we get that

\left\{\begin{aligned} &{-\frac{(n_{2}-k_{2})}{gcd(n_{2},k_{2})}}=-\frac{a_{\gamma}\cdot(n_{1}-k_{1})}{gcd(n_{1},k_{1})}+\frac{c_{\gamma}\cdot n_{1}}{gcd(n_{1},k_{1})}\\
&\frac{n_{2}}{gcd(n_{2},k_{2})}=-\frac{b_{\gamma}\cdot(n_{1}-k_{1})}{gcd(n_{1},k_{1})}+\frac{d_{\gamma}\cdot n_{1}}{gcd(n_{1},k_{1})}\end{aligned}\right.

Finally, by using Equation
7.8
and by multiplying everywhere by
gcd(n_{1},k_{1})
, we get that

(7.9)

\left\{\begin{aligned} &-(n_{2}-k_{2})=-a_{\gamma}\cdot(n_{1}-k_{1})+c_{\gamma}\cdot n_{1}\\
&n_{2}=-b_{\gamma}\cdot(n_{1}-k_{1})+d_{\gamma}\cdot n_{1}\end{aligned}\right.

The previous system of equations forms an important relation between the coefficients of the matrix
A_{\gamma}
and
n_{1},k_{1},n_{2},k_{2}
. In particular, the following inequality (which is an immediate consequence of the fact that
\Phi_{2}
is pseudo-Anosov and therefore does not admit circle
1
-prong singularities) will be used multiple times in this proof.

(7.10)

-b_{\gamma}\cdot(n_{1}-k_{1})+d_{\gamma}\cdot n_{1}\geq 2

Finally, before moving on with the proof of Theorem C, let us show that, by using the previous conditions on the coefficients of
A_{\gamma}
and also our choices of orientations of
\overline{\mathcal{P}_{1}}-\overline{\Gamma_{1}}
and
\overline{\mathcal{P}_{2}}-\overline{\Gamma_{2}}
, we get that

(7.11)

\text{det}(A_{\gamma})=1

Proof of Claim.
Indeed, recall that we oriented
\overline{\mathcal{P}_{1}}-\overline{\Gamma_{1}}
and
\overline{\mathcal{P}_{2}}-\overline{\Gamma_{2}}
so that
h
be orientation preserving. This guarantees that if we order clockwisely the stable leaves of
\overline{\gamma}
for our choice of orientation of
\overline{\mathcal{P}_{1}}-\overline{\Gamma_{1}}
, then their images by
h
are also ordered clockwisely for our choice of orientation of
\overline{\mathcal{P}_{2}}-\overline{\Gamma_{2}}
. By Proposition
7.4
,
\overline{\rho_{2}}(m_{h(\overline{\gamma})}^{M_{2}})
acts as a positive translation on the set
\{s_{i,h(\overline{\gamma})}|i\in\mathbb{Z}\}
. By the equivariance of
h
, this implies that
\overline{\rho_{1}}(\alpha^{-1}(m_{h(\overline{\gamma})}^{M_{2}}))
also acts as a positive translation on the set of
\{s_{i,\overline{\gamma}}|i\in\mathbb{Z}\}
. It is easy to check that

\alpha^{-1}(m^{M_{2}}_{h(\overline{\gamma})})=(m^{M_{1}}_{\overline{\gamma}})^{\frac{d_{\gamma}}{\text{det}(A_{\gamma})}}\cdot(p^{M_{1}}_{\overline{\gamma}})^{-\frac{b_{\gamma}}{\text{det}(A_{\gamma})}}

Using Proposition
7.4
, we deduce that

\frac{d_{\gamma}\cdot n_{1}}{\text{det}(A_{\gamma})}-\frac{b_{\gamma}\cdot(n_{1}-k_{1})}{\text{det}(A_{\gamma})}>0

Thanks to the second equation of
7.9
, we get the desired result. ∎

Let us now move on with the proof of Theorem C. Consider
(M_{3},\Phi_{3})
the flow obtained from
(M_{1},\Phi_{1})
by performing a
(d_{\gamma},-b_{\gamma})
-surgery on
\gamma
for every
\gamma\in\Gamma^{M_{1}}_{1}
. In order to finish the proof of the theorem, it suffices to show that
(M_{3},\Phi_{3})
is a well defined pseudo-Anosov flow that is orbitally equivalent to
(M_{2},\Phi_{2})
.

Let us begin by showing that

Claim.

(M_{3},\Phi_{3})
is a pseudo-Anosov flow

Proof of Claim.
Consider
(M^{*},\Phi^{*})
a flow obtained after blowing-up the orbits of
\Gamma_{1}^{M_{1}}
(see our construction in Section
3.4.2
). The points in
\overline{\Gamma_{1}}
correspond to periodic orbits in
\Gamma_{1}^{M_{1}}
that were blown-up to tori in
M^{*}
. Let
\Pi^{*}_{M}:M^{*}\rightarrow M
be the blow-down map associated to the previous blow-up. Fix
\overline{\gamma}\in\overline{\Gamma_{1}}
,
\gamma
its associated periodic orbit in
\Gamma_{1}^{M_{1}}
and
T_{\gamma}
its associated torus in
M^{*}
. Let
\gamma_{P},\gamma_{p},\gamma_{m}
be the curves in
T_{\gamma}
constructed in Section
7.2
, whose homotopy classes in
\pi_{1}(M^{*})\cong\pi_{1}(M_{1}-\Gamma_{1})
are respectively
P^{M_{1}}_{\overline{\gamma}},p^{M_{1}}_{\overline{\gamma}}
and
m^{M_{1}}_{\overline{\gamma}}
. Performing a
(d_{\gamma},-b_{\gamma})
-surgery on
\gamma
consists in crushing
T_{\gamma}
into a circle by collapsing to points the leaves of a collapsible foliation
\mathcal{F}_{\sigma_{\overline{\gamma}}}
(see our discussion prior to Lemma
3.36
for a definition of a collapsible foliation), whose every leaf is freely homotopic to
\sigma_{\overline{\gamma}}:=(m_{\overline{\gamma}}^{M_{1}})^{d_{\gamma}}\cdot(p_{\overline{\gamma}}^{M_{1}})^{-b_{\gamma}}
.

Thanks to Equations
7.6
and
7.10
,
\sigma_{\overline{\gamma}}
is not freely homotopic to
P_{\overline{\gamma}}
and every leaf of
\mathcal{F}_{\sigma_{\overline{\gamma}}}
will intersect
\gamma_{P}
at least twice. It follows from Theorem
3.40
that
(M_{3},\Phi_{3})
is indeed a pseudo-Anosov flow. ∎

It suffices now to show that
(M_{3},\Phi_{3})
is orbitally equivalent to
(M_{2},\Phi_{2})
. Denote by
\Gamma^{M_{3}}_{3}\subset M_{3}
the periodic orbits corresponding to
\Gamma^{M_{1}}_{1}
after surgery, by
\overline{\mathcal{P}_{3}}
the bifoliated plane up of
\Phi_{3}
up to surgeries on
\Gamma^{M_{3}}_{3}
,
\overline{\mathcal{F}_{3}^{s,u}}
the stable and unstable foliations of
\overline{\mathcal{P}_{3}}
,
\overline{\Gamma_{3}}
the lifts on
\overline{\mathcal{P}_{3}}
of the orbits in
\Gamma^{M_{3}}_{3}
and by
\overline{\rho_{3}}
the action of
\pi_{1}(M_{3}-\Gamma_{3}^{M_{3}})
on
\overline{\mathcal{P}_{3}}
. Since
M_{1}-\Gamma_{1}^{M_{1}}
and
M_{3}-\Gamma_{3}^{M_{3}}
are homeomorphic,
M_{3}
is naturally endowed with an orientation. Using this orientation, define as in Section
7.2
for every
\overline{\gamma}\in\overline{\Gamma_{3}}
the elements
m^{M_{3}}_{\overline{\gamma}},p^{M_{3}}_{\overline{\gamma}},P^{M_{3}}_{\overline{\gamma}}\in\text{Stab}(\overline{\gamma})
.

By the first part of this proof, there exists a homeomorphism
H:\overline{\mathcal{P}_{1}}\rightarrow\overline{\mathcal{P}_{3}}
satisfying the following:

(1)

the image by
H
of any stable (resp. unstable) leaf in
\overline{\mathcal{F}_{1}^{s}}
(resp.
\overline{\mathcal{F}_{1}^{u}}
) is a stable (resp. unstable) leaf in
\overline{\mathcal{F}_{3}^{s}}
(resp.
\overline{\mathcal{F}_{3}^{u}}
)

(2)

there exists
\delta:\pi_{1}(M_{1}-\Gamma^{M_{1}}_{1})\rightarrow\pi_{1}(M_{3}-\Gamma^{M_{3}}_{3})
a group isomorphism such that for every
g\in\pi_{1}(M_{1}-\Gamma_{1})
and every
x\in\overline{\mathcal{P}_{1}}
, we have

H(\overline{\rho_{1}}(g)(x))=\overline{\rho_{3}}(\delta(g))(H(x))

Furthermore, let us show that for any
\overline{\gamma}\in\overline{\Gamma_{1}}
we have that

(7.12)

\delta(P^{M_{1}}_{\overline{\gamma}})=P^{M_{3}}_{H(\overline{\gamma})}

(7.13)

\delta^{-1}(m^{M_{3}}_{H(\overline{\gamma})})=(m_{\overline{\gamma}}^{M_{1}})^{d_{\gamma}}\cdot(p_{\overline{\gamma}}^{M_{1}})^{-b_{\gamma}}

(7.14)

\delta^{-1}(p^{M_{3}}_{H(\overline{\gamma})})=(m_{\overline{\gamma}}^{M_{1}})^{-c_{\gamma}}\cdot(p_{\overline{\gamma}}^{M_{1}})^{a_{\gamma}}

Indeed, take
\overline{\gamma}\in\overline{\Gamma_{1}}
,
\gamma
its associated orbit in
\Gamma_{1}^{M_{1}}
and
\gamma^{\prime}\in\Gamma_{3}^{M_{3}}
the periodic orbit of
\Phi_{3}
corresponding to
\gamma
after surgery. One can prove Equation
7.12
either by repeating the argument that lead us to the proof of Equation
7.5
or by noticing that
\Phi^{*}
is a flow obtained from
\Phi_{1}
(resp.
\Phi_{3}
) after blowing-up every orbit in
\Gamma_{1}^{M_{1}}
(resp.
\Gamma_{3}^{M_{3}}
) and that
P^{M_{1}}_{\overline{\gamma}}
(resp.
P^{M_{3}}_{H(\overline{\gamma})}
) corresponds to the homotopy class of a periodic orbit of
\Phi^{*}
on
T_{\gamma}
(resp.
T_{\gamma^{\prime}}=T_{\gamma}
).

Proof of
7.13
.
Notice that since after the surgery every leaf of the collapsible foliation
\mathcal{F}_{\sigma_{\overline{\gamma}}}
became homotopically trivial, the natural meridian on
T_{\gamma^{\prime}}=T_{\gamma}
when blowing up
\gamma^{\prime}
is homotopic to a leaf of
\mathcal{F}_{\sigma_{\overline{\gamma}}}
, whereas the natural meridian on
T_{\gamma}
when blowing up
\gamma
is freely homotopic to
\gamma_{m}
. This implies that one of the following is true :

•

\delta^{-1}(m^{M_{3}}_{H(\overline{\gamma})})=\sigma_{\overline{\gamma}}=(m_{\overline{\gamma}}^{M_{1}})^{d_{\gamma}}\cdot(p_{\overline{\gamma}}^{M_{1}})^{-b_{\gamma}}
or

•
