\delta^{-1}(m^{M_{3}}_{H(\overline{\gamma})})=\sigma_{\overline{\gamma}}^{-1}=(m_{\overline{\gamma}}^{M_{1}})^{-d_{\gamma}}\cdot(p_{\overline{\gamma}}^{M_{1}})^{b_{\gamma}}

Fix
\overline{\gamma}\in\overline{\Gamma_{1}}
. Recall that, thanks to our choice of orientations on
M_{1}
and
M_{3}
, the orbital equivalence between
\Phi_{1}
minus
\Gamma_{1}^{M_{1}}
and
\Phi_{3}
minus
\Gamma_{3}^{M_{3}}
is orientation preserving. By lifting the previous orientations, one can canonically orient the universal covers of
M_{1}-\Gamma_{1}^{M_{1}}
and
M_{3}-\Gamma_{3}^{M_{3}}
. Moreover, using the direction of the lifted flows the previous orientations define respectively an orientation on
\overline{\mathcal{P}_{1}}-\overline{\Gamma_{1}}
and on
\overline{\mathcal{P}_{3}}-\overline{\Gamma_{3}}
. It is easy to see that
H
is orientation preserving for our previous choice of orientations; hence
H
takes the set of stable leaves of
\overline{\gamma}
ordered clockwisely to the set of stable leaves of
H(\overline{\gamma})
ordered clockwisely. Since by Proposition
7.4
,
\overline{\rho_{3}}(m^{M_{3}}_{H(\overline{\gamma})})
acts as a positive translation on the clockwise ordered set of stable leaves of
H(\overline{\gamma})
, the equivariance of
H
forces
\overline{\rho_{1}}(\delta^{-1}(m^{M_{3}}_{H(\overline{\gamma})}))
to also acts as a positive translation on the clockwise ordered set of stable leaves of
\overline{\gamma}
. Thanks to Proposition
7.4
,
\overline{\rho_{1}}(\sigma_{\overline{\gamma}})=\overline{\rho_{1}}((m_{\overline{\gamma}}^{M_{1}})^{d_{\gamma}}\cdot(p_{\overline{\gamma}}^{M_{1}})^{-b_{\gamma}})
acts as a translation by

d_{\gamma}\cdot n_{1}-b_{\gamma}\cdot(n_{1}-k_{1})

on the clockwise ordered set of stable leaves of
\overline{\gamma}
. This number is positive thanks to Equation
7.9
. It follows that
\delta^{-1}(m^{M_{3}}_{H(\overline{\gamma})})=(m_{\overline{\gamma}}^{M_{1}})^{d_{\gamma}}\cdot(p_{\overline{\gamma}}^{M_{1}})^{-b_{\gamma}}
. ∎

Proof of
7.14
.
First, notice that since
H
is equivariant with respect to the group actions, we have that
\delta^{-1}(\text{Stab}(H(\overline{\gamma})))=\text{Stab}(\overline{\gamma})
and thus the isomorphism
\delta^{-1}
takes a base of
\text{Stab}(H(\overline{\gamma}))
to a base of
\text{Stab}(\overline{\gamma})
. It follows from Equation
7.13
that there exist
x,y\in\mathbb{Z}
such that

(7.15)

\delta^{-1}(p^{M_{3}}_{H(\overline{\gamma})})=(m_{\overline{\gamma}}^{M_{1}})^{x}\cdot(p_{\overline{\gamma}}^{M_{1}})^{y}

(7.16)

\Delta_{\gamma}:=\begin{pmatrix}d_{\gamma}&x\\
-b_{\gamma}&y\end{pmatrix}\in\text{GL}_{2}(\mathbb{Z})

The fact that
\Delta_{\gamma}\in\text{GL}_{2}(\mathbb{Z})
, implies that
d_{\gamma}\cdot y+b_{\gamma}\cdot x=\text{det}(\Delta_{\gamma})=\pm 1
. Furthermore, using the fact that

\text{det}\begin{pmatrix}d_{\gamma}&-c_{\gamma}\\
-b_{\gamma}&a_{\gamma}\end{pmatrix}=1

we get that there exists
l\in\mathbb{Z}
such that

(7.17)

\left\{\begin{aligned} &x=-\frac{c_{\gamma}}{\text{det}(\Delta_{\gamma})}+l\cdot d_{\gamma}\\
&y=\frac{a_{\gamma}}{\text{det}(\Delta_{\gamma})}-l\cdot b_{\gamma}\end{aligned}\right.

It therefore suffices to prove that
\text{det}(\Delta_{\gamma})=1
and that
l=0
. As before, thanks to Proposition
7.4
and Lemma
3.42
, we have that there exist
n_{3}\in\mathbb{N}^{*}
and
k_{3}\in\llbracket 1,n_{1}\rrbracket
such that

(7.18)

P_{H(\overline{\gamma})}^{M_{3}}=(m_{H(\overline{\gamma})}^{M_{3}})^{-\frac{(n_{3}-k_{3})}{gcd(n_{3},k_{3})}}\cdot(p_{H(\overline{\gamma})}^{M_{3}})^{\frac{n_{3}}{gcd(n_{3},k_{3})}}

where
gcd(n_{3},k_{3})
denotes the greatest common divisors of
n_{3},k_{3}
. Once again, if
\gamma^{\prime}\in\Gamma_{3}^{M_{3}}
is the periodic orbit of
\Phi_{3}
associated to
H(\overline{\gamma})
, then if
\gamma^{\prime}
is associated by Proposition
2.23
to a local model for a pseudo-hyperbolic orbit with
n(\gamma^{\prime})
prongs and rotation
r(\gamma^{\prime})
, then
n_{3}=n(\gamma^{\prime})
and
r(\gamma^{\prime})\in\{k_{3},n_{3}-k_{3}\}
. Also, as before, by Proposition
7.4
,
\overline{\rho_{3}}(m_{H(\overline{\gamma})}^{M_{3}})
and
\overline{\rho_{3}}(p_{H(\overline{\gamma})}^{M_{3}})
act as translations by
n_{3}
and
n_{3}-k_{3}
on the set of clockwise ordered stable leaves of
H(\overline{\gamma})
and
gcd(n_{3},k_{3})
corresponds to the total number of stable leaves of
H(\overline{\gamma})
up to the action of
\text{Stab}(H(\overline{\gamma}))
.

The equivariance of
H
implies that

(7.19)

gcd(n_{3},k_{3})=gcd(n_{1},k_{1})

Moreover, thanks to Equations
7.12
and
7.15
, we have that

\displaystyle\delta^{-1}(P^{M_{3}}_{H(\overline{\gamma})})

\displaystyle=\delta^{-1}((m_{\overline{H(\gamma)}}^{M_{3}})^{-\frac{(n_{3}-k_{3})}{gcd(n_{3},k_{3})}}\cdot(p_{\overline{H(\gamma)}}^{M_{3}})^{\frac{n_{3}}{gcd(n_{3},k_{3})}})

\displaystyle=(m_{\overline{\gamma}}^{M_{1}})^{-\frac{d_{\gamma}\cdot(n_{3}-k_{3})}{gcd(n_{3},k_{3})}}\cdot(p_{\overline{\gamma}}^{M_{1}})^{\frac{b_{\gamma}\cdot(n_{3}-k_{3})}{gcd(n_{3},k_{3})}}(m_{\overline{\gamma}}^{M_{1}})^{\frac{x\cdot n_{3}}{gcd(n_{3},k_{3})}}\cdot(p_{\overline{\gamma}}^{M_{1}})^{\frac{y\cdot n_{3}}{gcd(n_{3},k_{3})}}

By combining the above equation with Equations
7.6
,
7.12
,
7.19
, we get that

\left\{\begin{aligned} &{-\frac{(n_{1}-k_{1})}{gcd(n_{1},k_{1})}}=-\frac{d_{\gamma}\cdot(n_{3}-k_{3})}{gcd(n_{1},k_{1})}+\frac{x\cdot n_{3}}{gcd(n_{1},k_{1})}\\
&\frac{n_{1}}{gcd(n_{1},k_{1})}=\frac{b_{\gamma}\cdot(n_{3}-k_{3})}{gcd(n_{1},k_{1})}+\frac{y\cdot n_{3}}{gcd(n_{1},k_{1})}\end{aligned}\right.

Multiplying everywhere by
gcd(n_{1},k_{1})
, we get that

(7.20)

\left\{\begin{aligned} &-(n_{1}-k_{1})=-d_{\gamma}\cdot(n_{3}-k_{3})+x\cdot n_{3}\\
&n_{1}=b_{\gamma}\cdot(n_{3}-k_{3})+y\cdot n_{3}\end{aligned}\right.

Thanks to the above, we can show that
\text{det}(\Delta_{\gamma})=1
. Indeed, by multiplying the first of the above equations by
b_{\gamma}
, the second by
d_{\gamma}
and by adding the resulting equations we get that:

(x\cdot b_{\gamma}+y\cdot d_{\gamma})n_{3}=d_{\gamma}\cdot n_{1}-b_{\gamma}\cdot(n_{1}-k_{1})

The previous equation, together with Equation
7.9
and the definition of
\Delta_{\gamma}
imply that

(7.21)

\text{det}(\Delta_{\gamma})\cdot n_{3}=n_{2}

Since both
n_{2}
and
n_{3}
are positive, we get that
\text{det}(\Delta_{\gamma})=1
and that
n_{3}=n_{2}
. Let us now show that
l=0
.

Notice now that since
\Delta_{\gamma}
is invertible
b_{\gamma}
and
d_{\gamma}
cannot both be equal to zero. Assume without any loss of generality that
b_{\gamma}\neq 0
. By solving the second equation of
7.20
with respect to
k_{3}
and then by using Equation
7.17
together with the fact that
\text{det}(\Delta_{\gamma})=1
, we get that:

\displaystyle k_{3}

\displaystyle=n_{3}-\frac{n_{1}}{b_{\gamma}}+\frac{y\cdot n_{3}}{b_{\gamma}}

\displaystyle=n_{3}-\frac{n_{1}}{b_{\gamma}}+\frac{a_{\gamma}\cdot n_{3}}{b_{\gamma}}-l\cdot n_{3}

\displaystyle=(1+\frac{a_{\gamma}}{b_{\gamma}}-l)\cdot n_{3}-\frac{n_{1}}{b_{\gamma}}

Using Equation
7.9
it is easy to check that

n_{1}=b_{\gamma}\cdot(n_{2}-k_{2})+a_{\gamma}\cdot n_{2}

By solving the above equation with respect to
k_{2}
we get that

k_{2}=(1+\frac{a_{\gamma}}{b_{\gamma}})\cdot n_{2}-\frac{n_{1}}{b_{\gamma}}

By definition,
k_{2}\in\llbracket 1,n_{2}\rrbracket
. Therefore, since
n_{2}=n_{3}
(see Equation
7.21
),
k_{3}\in\llbracket 1,n_{3}\rrbracket
if and only if
k_{3}=k_{2}
or equivalently
l=0
. We thus get the desired result. ∎

We are now ready to prove that
(M_{3},\Phi_{3})
is orbitally equivalent to
(M_{2},\Phi_{2})
. Consider the homeomorphism
K=h\circ H^{-1}:\overline{\mathcal{P}_{3}}\rightarrow\overline{\mathcal{P}_{2}}
. Using our definition of
H
and
h
together with Equations
7.3
,
7.4
,
7.5
,
7.13
and
7.14
we have that
K
satisfies the following:

(1)

the image by
K
of any stable (resp. unstable) leaf in
\overline{\mathcal{F}_{3}^{s}}
(resp.
\overline{\mathcal{F}_{3}^{u}}
) is a stable (resp. unstable) leaf in
\overline{\mathcal{F}_{2}^{s}}
(resp.
\overline{\mathcal{F}_{2}^{u}}
)

(2)

there exists an isomorphism
\beta:=\alpha\circ\delta^{-1}:\pi_{1}(M_{3}-\Gamma_{3})\rightarrow\pi_{1}(M_{2}-\Gamma_{2})
such that for every
g\in\pi_{1}(M_{3}-\Gamma_{3})
,
\overline{\gamma}\in\overline{\Gamma_{3}}
and
x\in\overline{\mathcal{P}_{3}}
we have

K(\overline{\rho_{3}}(g)(x))=\overline{\rho_{2}}(\beta(g))(K(x))

\displaystyle\beta(m^{M_{3}}_{\overline{\gamma}})

\displaystyle=\alpha((m_{H^{-1}(\overline{\gamma})}^{M_{1}})^{d_{\gamma}}\cdot(p_{H^{-1}(\overline{\gamma})}^{M_{1}})^{-b_{\gamma}})

(7.22)

\displaystyle=(m_{K(\overline{\gamma})}^{M_{2}})^{a_{\gamma}\cdot d_{\gamma}}\cdot(p_{K(\overline{\gamma})}^{M_{2}})^{d\gamma\cdot b_{\gamma}}\cdot(m_{K(\overline{\gamma})}^{M_{2}})^{-b_{\gamma}\cdot c_{\gamma}}\cdot(p_{K(\overline{\gamma})}^{M_{2}})^{-d\gamma\cdot b_{\gamma}}

\displaystyle=(m_{K(\overline{\gamma})}^{M_{2}})^{\text{det}(A_{\gamma})}=m_{K(\overline{\gamma})}^{M_{2}}

\displaystyle\beta(p^{M_{3}}_{\overline{\gamma}})

\displaystyle=\alpha((m_{H^{-1}(\overline{\gamma})}^{M_{1}})^{-c_{\gamma}}\cdot(p_{H^{-1}(\overline{\gamma})}^{M_{1}})^{a_{\gamma}})

(7.23)

\displaystyle=(m_{K(\overline{\gamma})}^{M_{2}})^{-a_{\gamma}\cdot c_{\gamma}}\cdot(p_{K(\overline{\gamma})}^{M_{2}})^{-c_{\gamma}\cdot b_{\gamma}}\cdot(m_{K(\overline{\gamma})}^{M_{2}})^{a_{\gamma}\cdot c_{\gamma}}\cdot(p_{K(\overline{\gamma})}^{M_{2}})^{d\gamma\cdot a_{\gamma}}

\displaystyle=(p_{K(\overline{\gamma})}^{M_{2}})^{\text{det}(A_{\gamma})}=p_{K(\overline{\gamma})}^{M_{2}}

Consider now
\overline{\gamma_{1}},...,\overline{\gamma_{n}}
a representative of each
\overline{\rho_{3}}
-orbit in
\overline{\Gamma_{3}}
. By the equivariance of
K
and the fact that
K(\overline{\Gamma_{3}})=\overline{\Gamma_{2}}
,
K(\overline{\gamma_{1}}),...,K(\overline{\gamma_{n}})
are representatives of each
\overline{\rho_{2}}
-orbit in
\overline{\Gamma_{2}}
. Let
\gamma_{i}
be the periodic orbit in
\Gamma_{3}^{M_{3}}
associated to
\overline{\gamma_{i}}
. Recall that
m_{K(\overline{\gamma_{i}})}^{M_{3}}
corresponds to the meridian of the torus obtained after blowing up
\gamma_{i}
. Therefore, by the Seifert-Van Kampen theorem,

\text{ker}(\pi_{1}(M_{3}-\Gamma_{3})\rightarrow\pi_{1}(M_{3}))=<m^{M_{3}}_{\overline{\gamma_{1}}},...,m^{M_{3}}_{\overline{\gamma_{n}}}>^{\pi_{1}(M_{3}-\Gamma_{3})}

where
<A>^{\pi_{1}(M_{3}-\Gamma_{3})}
stands for the normal subgroup of
\pi_{1}(M_{3}-\Gamma_{3})
generated by
A
.

Similarly,

\text{ker}(\pi_{1}(M_{2}-\Gamma_{2})\rightarrow\pi_{1}(M_{2}))=<m^{M_{2}}_{K(\overline{\gamma_{1}})},...,m^{M_{2}}_{K(\overline{\gamma_{n}})}>^{\pi_{1}(M_{2}-\Gamma_{2})}

Using the two previous equalities together with (
2
), we get that

(7.24)

\beta(\text{ker}(\pi_{1}(M_{3}-\Gamma_{3})\rightarrow\pi_{1}(M_{3})))=\text{ker}(\pi_{1}(M_{2}-\Gamma_{2})\rightarrow\pi_{1}(M_{2}))

Also, by Propositions
6.12
and
6.13
, if
(\mathcal{P}_{2},\mathcal{F}_{2}^{s,u},\rho_{2})
and
(\mathcal{P}_{3},\mathcal{F}_{3}^{s,u},\rho_{3})
are the bifoliated planes of
\Phi_{2}
and
\Phi_{3}
together with their stable/unstable foliations and group actions

(7.25)

{\raisebox{1.99997pt}{$\overline{\mathcal{P}_{2}}$}\left/\raisebox{-1.99997pt}{$\text{ker}(\pi_{1}(M_{2}-\Gamma_{2})\rightarrow\pi_{1}(M_{2}))$}\right.}=\mathcal{P}_{2}

(7.26)

{\raisebox{1.99997pt}{$\overline{\mathcal{P}_{3}}$}\left/\raisebox{-1.99997pt}{$\text{ker}(\pi_{1}(M_{3}-\Gamma_{3})\rightarrow\pi_{1}(M_{3}))$}\right.}=\mathcal{P}_{3}

Finally, thanks to Proposition
6.13
, to Equations (
7.24
), (
7.25
), (
7.26
) and to the equivariance of
K
with respect to
\overline{\rho_{2}}
and
\overline{\rho_{3}}
, we get that
K
projects to a homeomorphism
k:\mathcal{P}_{3}\rightarrow\mathcal{P}_{2}
that satisfies the following:

(1)

the image by
k
of any stable (resp. unstable) leaf in
\mathcal{F}_{3}^{s}
(resp.
\mathcal{F}_{3}^{u}
) is a stable (resp. unstable) leaf in
\mathcal{F}_{2}^{s}
(resp.
\mathcal{F}_{2}^{u}
)

(2)

there exists an isomorphism
\mu:\pi_{1}(M_{3})\rightarrow\pi_{1}(M_{2})
such that for every
g\in\pi_{1}(M_{3})
and
x\in\mathcal{P}_{3}
we have

k(\rho_{3}(g)(x))=\rho_{2}(\mu(g))(k(x))

We conclude that
\Phi_{2}
is orbitally equivalent to
\Phi_{3}
thanks to Theorem
2.59
.
∎

8.
Markovian families as coordinate systems in
\overline{\mathcal{P}}

Having proven Theorem C, from now on we will turn our attention to the proof of Theorem B. Thanks to Theorem C, in order to prove Theorem B, it suffices to show that the geometric type of a strong Markovian family
\mathcal{R}
contains enough information to completely describe not only the bifoliated plane up to surgeries along the boundary periodic points of
\mathcal{R}
, but also its associated foliations and its associated group action.

The proof of the above result is rather long and technical and thus will be split into three sections. In this section we will define several tools, which are going to be important for us throughout the proof of Theorem B. More specifically, in Section
8.1
, we will define the notion of strong Markovian family on the bifoliated plane up to surgeries and its set of associated geometric types. We will also show that the previous set coincides with the set of geometric types associated to the original Markovian family on the bifoliated plane (see Proposition
8.6
). Next, in Section
8.2
, we will define the notion of rectangle path, which can be thought as a discretized curve in the bifoliated plane up to surgeries that can be described via a geometric type. In Section
8.3
, we will explain how these rectangle paths can be used as “discrete coordinates” in the bifoliated plane up to surgeries and how they can be used in order to compare two strong Markovian actions preserving two strong Markovian families that are associated to the same equivalence class of geometric types.

8.1.
Strong Markovian families on the bifoliated plane up to surgeries

Once again, in this section we will work in most general setting in which our results hold, namely strong Markovian actions. Fix
\mathcal{P}
a plane endowed with an orientation,
\rho:G\rightarrow\text{Homeo}(\mathcal{P})
an orientation preserving strong Markovian action, preserving the pair of singular foliations
\mathcal{F}^{s}
and
\mathcal{F}^{u}
(see Definition
2.13
) and leaving invariant a strong Markovian family
\mathcal{R}
. Denote by
\Gamma
the boundary periodic points of
\mathcal{R}
, by
\overline{\mathcal{P}}
the bifoliated plane of
\rho
up to surgeries on
\Gamma
, by
\overline{\mathcal{F}^{s}}
and
\overline{\mathcal{F}^{u}}
the stable and unstable foliations in
\overline{\mathcal{P}}
, by
\overline{\rho}:\overline{G}\rightarrow\overline{\mathcal{P}}
the lift of
\rho
on
\overline{\mathcal{P}}
, by
\overline{\Gamma}
the lift of
\Gamma
on
\overline{\mathcal{P}}
and by
\overline{\pi}
the projection from
\overline{\mathcal{P}}
to
\mathcal{P}
. Using as a starting point our definition of strong Markovian family in
\mathcal{P}
, we will now extend the notion of strong Markovian family in
\overline{\mathcal{P}}
.

We define rectangles in
\overline{\mathcal{P}}
in the exact same way as for
\mathcal{P}
(see Definition
2.6
). Notice that any rectangle in
\mathcal{P}
whose interior does not contain any point of
\Gamma
lifts to a rectangle in
\overline{\mathcal{P}}
. This is why, we will define a
strong Markovian family
in
\overline{\mathcal{P}}
as the lift on
\overline{\mathcal{P}}
of a strong Markovian family of
\mathcal{P}
, whose boundary periodic points are exactly
\Gamma
. Fix for the rest of this section
\overline{\mathcal{R}}
to be the strong Markovian family in
\overline{\mathcal{P}}
obtained by lifting
\mathcal{R}
.

Definition 8.1
.

For any two rectangles
R_{1},R_{2}\in\overline{\mathcal{R}}
, we will say that
R_{1}
is a
predecessor
(resp.
successor
) of
R_{2}
if
\overset{\circ}{R_{1}}\cap\overset{\circ}{R_{2}}\neq\emptyset
and
\overline{\pi}(R_{1})\subset\mathcal{P}
is a predecessor (resp. successor) of
\overline{\pi}(R_{2})
.

We similarly define
predecessors/successors of generation
k\geq 2
and
s
-crossing predecessors/successors
on
\overline{\mathcal{P}}
.
∎

It is not hard to show that

Remark 8.2
.

•

Defining a strong Markovian family (resp. a predecessor/successor or a predecessor/succesor of generation
k
or a crossing predecessor/successor) as in Definition
3.14
(resp.
4.3
or
5.7
) would lead to a definition equivalent to the one given here above.

•

Predecessor/successors, predecessor/successors of generation
k
, crossing predecessor/successors in
\overline{\mathcal{R}}
still satisfy the results of Lemmas
3.21
,
3.22
,
4.2
,
4.5
,
4.6
,
5.5
and
5.6
.

Definition 8.3
.

A point in
\overline{\mathcal{P}}
will be called
periodic
(resp.
boundary arc
,
boundary periodic
) if its projection on
\mathcal{P}
is a periodic (resp. boundary arc, boundary periodic) point. A stable/unstable leaf
f
of
\overline{\mathcal{P}}
will be called
periodic
if there exists a non-trivial
g\in\overline{G}
such that
\overline{\rho}(g)(f)=f

∎

Once again, it is not hard to show that

Remark 8.4
.

•

Defining a periodic point (resp. boundary periodic point or a boundary arc point) as in Definition
5.1
would lead to a definition equivalent to Definition
8.1
.

•

Periodic stable/unstable leaves, boundary arc and boundary periodic points still satisfy the results of Lemma
4.1
, Propositions
5.2
,
5.4
, Remark
5.9
.

•

The boundary periodic points of
\overline{\mathcal{R}}
coincide with
\overline{\Gamma}
and every boundary arc point of
\overline{\mathcal{R}}
is contained in the stable or unstable leaf of some point in
\overline{\Gamma}
.

Next, by repeating the same construction as in Definition
4.7
, we can associate to the strong Markovian family
\overline{\mathcal{R}}
a finite number of geometric types, that we will call once again
the geometric types of
\overline{\mathcal{R}}
or
the geometric types associated to
\overline{\mathcal{R}}
. Furthermore, by the same arguments as in the proof of Theorem
4.10
, we can show that the geometric types of
\overline{\mathcal{R}}
are pairwise equivalent and that Remark
4.9
remains true for Markovian families in
\overline{\mathcal{P}}
. In other words,

Remark 8.5
.

Given
\overline{\mathcal{R}}
a strong Markovian family preserved by
\overline{\rho}
, the following triplet of choices of representatives and orientations defines a unique geometric type of
\overline{\mathcal{R}}
:

(1)

a choice of representatives
r_{1},...,r_{n}
of every rectangle orbit in
\overline{\mathcal{R}}

(2)

a choice of orientation of
\overline{\mathcal{P}}-\overline{\Gamma}

(3)

a choice of orientation of the foliations inside every
r_{i}
such that the positive stable direction followed by the positive unstable direction inside every
r_{i}
define an orientation compatible with our choice of orientation of
\overline{\mathcal{P}}-\overline{\Gamma}

Recall that thanks to Remark
6.7
, the foliations
\overline{\mathcal{F}^{s}}
and
\overline{\mathcal{F}^{u}}
are always orientable and transversely orientable (even if
\mathcal{F}^{s}
and
\mathcal{F}^{u}
are not). Thanks to Remark
8.5
, the following couple of choices of representatives and orientations defines a unique geometric type of
\overline{\mathcal{R}}
:

•

a choice of representatives of every rectangle orbit in
\overline{\mathcal{R}}

•

a choice of orientations of
\overline{\mathcal{F}^{s}}
and
\overline{\mathcal{F}^{u}}

The set of geometric types of
\overline{\mathcal{R}}
obtained by all the above pairs of choices will be called the set of
special geometric types of
\overline{\mathcal{R}}
. Before finishing this section, let us prove two results providing respectively a better description of the set of geometric types and of the set of special geometric types of
\overline{\mathcal{R}}
.

Proposition 8.6
.

Let
\overline{\mathcal{R}}
be a strong Markovian family in
\overline{\mathcal{P}}
preserved by
\overline{\rho}
and
\mathcal{R}
its projection on
\mathcal{P}
. The set of geometric types associated to the Markovian families
\overline{\mathcal{R}}
and
\mathcal{R}
coincide.

Lemma 8.7

(Realisability lemma)
.

Consider the unique equivalence class of geometric types containing all the geometric types of
\overline{\mathcal{R}}
and

\mathcal{G}=(n,(h_{i})_{i\in\llbracket 1,n\rrbracket},(v_{i})_{i\in\llbracket 1,n\rrbracket},\mathcal{H},\mathcal{V},\phi,u)

a geometric type in this class. If
\overline{\rho}
does not preserve the orientations of the foliations
\overline{\mathcal{F}^{s}}
and
\overline{\mathcal{F}^{u}}
, then
\mathcal{G}
is a special geometric type associated to
\overline{\mathcal{R}}
. If we also assume that
u=+1
, the same result is true when
\overline{\rho}
preserves the orientations of the foliations
\overline{\mathcal{F}^{s}}
and
\overline{\mathcal{F}^{u}}
.

Proof of the proposition.

Consider a choice of representatives
r_{1},...,r_{n}
of every rectangle orbit in
\mathcal{R}
and a lift
\overline{r_{i}}\in\overline{\mathcal{R}}
of every
r_{i}
. Thanks to Propositions
6.13
and
6.14
, the rectangles
\overline{r_{i}}
are representatives of every rectangle orbit in
\overline{\mathcal{R}}
. Consider next a choice of orientation of
\mathcal{P}
and of the stable and unstable foliations of every
r_{i}
so that the positive stable direction followed by the positive unstable direction inside every
r_{i}
define an orientation compatible with our choice of orientation of
\mathcal{P}
. By pulling back the previous choices of orientations by
\overline{\pi}
, we can define an orientation on
\overline{\mathcal{P}}
and an orientation of the stable and unstable foliations inside every
\overline{r_{i}}
. It is easy to see that the positive stable direction followed by the positive unstable direction inside every
\overline{r_{i}}
define an orientation compatible with our choice of orientation of
\overline{\mathcal{P}}
.

According to Remarks
4.9
and
8.5
, together with these choices of representatives and orientations, the Markovian family
\overline{\mathcal{R}}
(resp.
\mathcal{R}
) defines a unique geometric type
\overline{\mathcal{G}}=(\overline{n},(\overline{h_{i}})_{i\in\llbracket 1,n\rrbracket},(\overline{v_{i}})_{i\in\llbracket 1,n\rrbracket},\overline{\mathcal{H}},\overline{\mathcal{V}},\overline{\phi},\overline{u})
(resp.
\mathcal{G}=(n,(h_{i})_{i\in\llbracket 1,n\rrbracket},(v_{i})_{i\in\llbracket 1,n\rrbracket},\mathcal{H},\mathcal{V},\phi,u)
).

It suffices to show that
\mathcal{G}
is equal to
\overline{\mathcal{G}}
. In order to do so, let us go back to the construction of
\mathcal{G}
and
\overline{\mathcal{G}}
(see Definition
4.7
). The number
n
(resp.
\overline{n}
) in
\mathcal{G}
(resp.
\overline{\mathcal{G}}
) is equal to the number of distinct orbits of rectangles in
\mathcal{R}
(resp.
\overline{\mathcal{R}}
) by the action of
\rho
(resp.
\overline{\rho}
). Once again, thanks to Propositions
6.13
and
6.14
,
n=\overline{n}
.

Next, the number
h_{i}
(resp.
v_{i}
) corresponds to the number of successors (resp. predecessors) of
r_{i}
. Similarly, the number
\overline{h_{i}}
(resp.
\overline{v_{i}}
) corresponds to the number of successors (resp. predecessors) of
\overline{r_{i}}
. By Definition
8.1
,

\overline{r_{i}}
and
r_{i}
have the same number of predecessors and successors. In other words,
h_{i}=\overline{h}_{i}
and
v_{i}=\overline{v}_{i}
.

Now, let us order the successors (resp. predecessors) of every
r_{i}
from bottom to top (resp. from left to right) using our choice of orientation of the unstable (resp. stable) foliation inside
r_{i}
. We will denote by
H_{i}^{k}
(resp.
V_{i}^{k}
) the
k
-th successor (resp. predecessor) of
r_{i}
for our previous choice of order. Recall that
\mathcal{H}=\{H^{k}_{i},i\in\llbracket 1,n\rrbracket,k\in\llbracket 1,h_{i}\rrbracket\}
and
\mathcal{V}=\{V^{k}_{i},i\in\llbracket 1,n\rrbracket,k\in\llbracket 1,v_{i}\rrbracket\}
. We similarly define the families
(\overline{V_{i}^{k}})_{k\in\llbracket 1,v_{i}\rrbracket}
,
(\overline{H^{k}_{i}})_{k\in\llbracket 1,h_{i}\rrbracket}
for every rectangle
\overline{r_{i}}
and also the sets
\overline{\mathcal{H}},\overline{\mathcal{V}}
. By our choice of orientations of the stable and unstable foliations inside every
\overline{r_{i}}
, we have that
\overline{\pi}(\overline{H^{k}_{i}})=H_{i}^{k}
and
\overline{\pi}(\overline{V_{i}^{k}})=V_{i}^{k}
. Therefore, we can canonically identify
\mathcal{H}
with
\overline{\mathcal{H}}
and
\mathcal{V}
with
\overline{\mathcal{V}}
.

It remains to prove that
\phi
is equal to
\overline{\phi}
and that
u
is equal to
\overline{u}
(modulo the identification of
\mathcal{H}
with
\overline{\mathcal{H}}
and of
\mathcal{V}
with
\overline{\mathcal{V}}
). First, recall

(1)

that
\phi(H^{k}_{i})=V^{l}_{j}
if and only if there exists
g\in G
such that
\rho(g)(H^{k}_{i})=r_{j}
and
\rho(g)(r_{i})
is the
l
-th predecessor (from left to right) of
r_{j}

(2)

that the previous
g
is uniquely defined by the property
\rho(g)(H^{k}_{i})=r_{j}
(see Proposition
4.8
)

(3)

that as
H^{k}_{i}
intersects
r_{i}
, it inherits a natural orientation for both its stable and unstable foliations and that
u(H^{k}_{i})=+1
if and only if
\rho(g)
sends the previous oriented stable and unstable foliations in
H^{k}_{i}
to the oriented stable and unstable foliations in
r_{j}
, according to our initial choices of orientations

The functions
\overline{\phi}
and
\overline{u}
are similarly defined. Thanks to Propositions
6.13
and
6.14
, we deduce that
\phi(H^{k}_{i})=V^{l}_{j}
if and only if
\overline{\phi}(\overline{H^{k}_{i}})=\overline{V^{l}_{j}}
. Similarly, it is not difficult to check using Proposition
6.13
that
u(H^{k}_{i})=1
if and only if
\overline{u}(\overline{H^{k}_{i}})=1
. Finally, using the identifications between
\mathcal{H}
and
\overline{\mathcal{H}}
,
\mathcal{V}
and
\overline{\mathcal{V}}
, we have that
\phi
and
\overline{\phi}
(resp.
u
and
\overline{u}
) define the same functions from
\mathcal{H}
to
\mathcal{V}
(resp. from
\mathcal{H}
to
\{\pm 1\}
), which proves that
\mathcal{G}
is equal to
\overline{\mathcal{G}}
and thus finishes the proof of the proposition.
∎

Proof of the realisability lemma.

Throughout this proof, we will assume that
\overline{\rho}
does not preserve the orientations of

\overline{\mathcal{F}^{s,u}}
. The case where
\overline{\rho}
preserves the orientations of the foliations follows from similar arguments.

Take
\mathcal{G}=(n,(h_{i})_{i\in\llbracket 1,n\rrbracket},(v_{i})_{i\in\llbracket 1,n\rrbracket},\mathcal{H},\mathcal{V},\phi,u)
a geometric type associated to
\overline{\mathcal{R}}
. We will show that
\mathcal{G}
is a special geometric type of
\overline{\mathcal{R}}
. In order to do so, it suffices to show that there exists a set of representatives of every
\overline{\rho}
-orbit of rectangles in
\overline{\mathcal{R}}
and a choice of orientations of
\overline{\mathcal{F}^{s,u}}
such that
\mathcal{G}
is the special geometric type associated to
\overline{\mathcal{R}}
for this choice of representatives and orientations.

Choose arbitrarily a set of representatives
r_{1},...,r_{n}
of every rectangle orbit in
\overline{\mathcal{R}}
and choose also arbitrary orientations for
\overline{\mathcal{F}^{s}}
and
\overline{\mathcal{F}^{u}}
. By Theorem
4.10
applied for
\overline{\mathcal{R}}
, the family
\overline{\mathcal{R}}
together with our previous choices of representatives and orientations defines a unique special geometric type

\mathcal{G}^{\prime}=(n,(h^{\prime}_{i})_{i\in\llbracket 1,n^{\prime}\rrbracket},(v^{\prime}_{i})_{i\in\llbracket 1,n^{\prime}\rrbracket},\mathcal{H^{\prime}},\mathcal{V^{\prime}},\phi^{\prime},u^{\prime})

that is equivalent to
\mathcal{G}
. Our goal from now on consists in adapting our choices of representatives and orientations in order to produce, thanks to Remark
8.5
, a special geometric type equal to
\mathcal{G}
.

First, notice that, thanks to Proposition
4.8
, up to reindexing the
(h^{\prime}_{i},v^{\prime}_{i})
, we have that
h^{\prime}_{i}=h_{i}
and
v^{\prime}_{i}=v_{i}
. Hence,
\mathcal{G}^{\prime}
is of the form
(n,(h_{i})_{i\in\llbracket 1,n\rrbracket},(v_{i})_{i\in\llbracket 1,n\rrbracket},\mathcal{H^{\prime}},\mathcal{V^{\prime}},\phi^{\prime},u^{\prime})
.

Using the geometric interpretation of a geometric type (see Section
3.1
), from now on we will think of a geometric type as a set of rectangles and subrectangles. More specifically, given any geometric type
\mathcal{K}=(n,(h_{i})_{i\in\llbracket 1,n\rrbracket},(v_{i})_{i\in\llbracket 1,n\rrbracket},\mathcal{H}_{\mathcal{K}},\mathcal{V}_{\mathcal{K}},\phi_{\mathcal{K}},u_{\mathcal{K}})
, we will denote by
R_{1}(\mathcal{K}),
…,
R_{n}(\mathcal{K})
the rectangles associated to
\mathcal{K}
, by
\{H_{i}^{j}(\mathcal{K})|j\in\llbracket 1,h_{i}\rrbracket\}\subset\mathcal{H}_{\mathcal{K}}
the horizontal subrectangles of
R_{i}(\mathcal{K})
ordered from bottom to top and by
\{V_{i}^{j}(\mathcal{K})|j\in\llbracket 1,v_{i}\rrbracket\}\subset\mathcal{V}_{\mathcal{K}}
the vertical subrectangles of
R_{i}(\mathcal{K})
ordered from left to right. Recall that thanks to the choice of orientations of
\overline{\mathcal{F}^{s,u}}
that gave rise to
\mathcal{G}^{\prime}
, each of the elements of
\{H_{i}^{j}(\mathcal{K})|j\in\llbracket 1,h_{i}\llbracket\}
(resp.
\{V_{i}^{j}(\mathcal{K})|j\in\llbracket 1,v_{i}\llbracket\}\subset\mathcal{V}_{\mathcal{K}}
) corresponds to a unique successor (resp. predecessors) of
r_{i}
.

Denote by
H
the equivalence between
\mathcal{G}
and
\mathcal{G}^{\prime}
. Following the notations of Definition
3.10
, consider for every
i\in\llbracket 1,n\rrbracket
the integers
\epsilon_{i}(\mathcal{G}^{\prime}),\epsilon_{i}^{\prime}(\mathcal{G}^{\prime})\in\{-1,+1\}
. Recall that

(1)

for every
i\in\llbracket 1,n\rrbracket
, the equivalence
H
defines a bijection between the sets of horizontal/vertical subrectangles of
R_{i}(\mathcal{G})
and
R_{i}(\mathcal{G^{\prime}})
. More specifically,
H
defines a monotonous bijection between the sets
\{V_{i}^{j}(\mathcal{G})|j\in\llbracket 1,v_{i}\rrbracket\}
(resp.
\{H_{i}^{j}(\mathcal{G})|j\in\llbracket 1,h_{i}\rrbracket\}
) and
\{V_{i}^{j}(\mathcal{G^{\prime}})|j\in\llbracket 1,v_{i}\rrbracket\}
(resp.
\{H_{i}^{j}(\mathcal{G^{\prime}})|j\in\llbracket 1,h_{i}\rrbracket\}
)

(2)

if the previous bijection respects the order of the vertical (resp. horizontal) subrectangles, then
\epsilon_{i}(\mathcal{G}^{\prime})=+1
(resp.
\epsilon_{i}^{\prime}(\mathcal{G}^{\prime})=+1
) and if it reverses it, then
\epsilon_{i}(\mathcal{G}^{\prime})=-1
(resp.
\epsilon_{i}^{\prime}(\mathcal{G}^{\prime})=-1
)

(3)

thanks to Definition
3.10
we have either that
\epsilon_{i}(\mathcal{G}^{\prime})\cdot\epsilon^{\prime}_{i}(\mathcal{G}^{\prime})=-1
for all
i\in\llbracket 1,n\rrbracket
or that
\epsilon_{i}(\mathcal{G}^{\prime})\cdot\epsilon_{i}^{\prime}(\mathcal{G}^{\prime})=+1
for all
i\in\llbracket 1,n\rrbracket

We will first show that by eventually changing one of our initial choices of orientations, we can assume that
\epsilon_{i}(\mathcal{G}^{\prime})\cdot\epsilon_{i}^{\prime}(\mathcal{G}^{\prime})=+1
for all
i\in\llbracket 1,n\rrbracket
. Indeed, changing the orientation of
\overline{\mathcal{F}^{s}}
without modifying our choice of representatives or of orientation of
\overline{\mathcal{F}^{u}}
, produces a new special geometric type

\mathcal{G}^{\prime\prime}=(n,(h_{i})_{i\in\llbracket 1,n\rrbracket},(v_{i})_{i\in\llbracket 1,n\rrbracket},\mathcal{H^{\prime\prime}},\mathcal{V^{\prime\prime}},\phi^{\prime\prime},u^{\prime\prime})

associated to
\overline{\mathcal{R}}
that can be obtained from
\mathcal{G}^{\prime}
by reindexing all the vertical subrectangles in
\mathcal{G}^{\prime}
as follows:

V_{i}^{j}(\mathcal{G}^{\prime})\rightarrow V_{i}^{v_{i}-j}(\mathcal{G}^{\prime\prime})

In more geometric terms, going from left to right, for any
i\in\llbracket 1,n\rrbracket
the first vertical subrectangle of
R_{i}(\mathcal{G}^{\prime})
, becomes the last vertical subrectangle of the rectangle
R_{i}(\mathcal{G}^{\prime\prime})
and vice versa.

By construction of
\mathcal{G}^{\prime\prime}
and by Theorem
4.10
, there exists
H^{*}
an equivalence between
\mathcal{G}
and
\mathcal{G}^{\prime\prime}
. Once again following the notations of Definition
3.10
, consider for every
i\in\llbracket 1,n\rrbracket
the integers
\epsilon_{i}(\mathcal{G}^{\prime\prime}),\epsilon_{i}^{\prime}(\mathcal{G}^{\prime\prime})\in\{-1,+1\}
. Since the order of the vertical subrectangles has changed with respect to
\mathcal{G}^{\prime}
, but the order of the horizontal subrectangles remains the same, we have that:

\epsilon_{i}(\mathcal{G}^{\prime\prime})=\epsilon_{i}(\mathcal{G}^{\prime})\text{ and }\epsilon^{\prime}_{i}(\mathcal{G}^{\prime\prime})=-\epsilon^{\prime}_{i}(\mathcal{G}^{\prime})

Therefore, by eventually changing our initial choice of orientation on
\overline{\mathcal{F}^{s}}
, we can assume that for every
i\in\llbracket 1,n\rrbracket
we have
\epsilon_{i}(\mathcal{G}^{\prime})\cdot\epsilon_{i}^{\prime}(\mathcal{G}^{\prime})=+1
.

Suppose that there exists
i_{0}\in\llbracket 1,n\rrbracket
such that
\epsilon_{i_{0}}(\mathcal{G}^{\prime})=\epsilon^{\prime}_{i_{0}}(\mathcal{G}^{\prime})=-1
. Take
g\in\overline{G}
such that
\overline{\rho}
reverses the orientation of the foliations
\overline{\mathcal{F}^{s,u}}
and replace in our initial choice of representatives
r_{i_{0}}
by
\overline{\rho}(g)(r_{i_{0}})
. Changing our representatives in this way without modifying the orientations of the foliations, produces a new special geometric type

\mathcal{G}^{\prime\prime\prime}=(n,(h_{i})_{i\in\llbracket 1,n\rrbracket},(v_{i})_{i\in\llbracket 1,n\rrbracket},\mathcal{H^{\prime\prime\prime}},\mathcal{V^{\prime\prime\prime}},\phi^{\prime\prime\prime},u^{\prime\prime\prime})

associated to
\overline{\mathcal{R}}
that can be obtained from
\mathcal{G}^{\prime}
by reindexing the horizontal and vertical subrectangles of the rectangle
R_{i_{0}}(\mathcal{G}^{\prime})
in
\mathcal{G}^{\prime}
as follows:

V_{i_{0}}^{j}(\mathcal{G}^{\prime})\rightarrow V_{i_{0}}^{v_{i_{0}}-j}(\mathcal{G}^{\prime\prime\prime})\text{ and }H_{i_{0}}^{j}(\mathcal{G}^{\prime})\rightarrow H_{i_{0}}^{h_{i_{0}}-j}(\mathcal{G}^{\prime\prime\prime})

In more geometric terms, going from left to right (resp. bottom to top), the first vertical (resp. horizontal) subrectangle of
R_{i_{0}}(\mathcal{G}^{\prime})
becomes the last vertical (resp. horizontal) subrectangle of the rectangle
R_{i_{0}}(\mathcal{G}^{\prime\prime\prime})
and vice versa.

By construction of
\mathcal{G}^{\prime\prime\prime}
and by Theorem
4.10
, there exists
H^{**}
an equivalence between
\mathcal{G}
and
\mathcal{G}^{\prime\prime\prime}
. Once again following the notations of Definition
3.10
, consider for every
i
the integers
\epsilon_{i}(\mathcal{G}^{\prime\prime\prime}),\epsilon_{i}^{\prime}(\mathcal{G}^{\prime\prime\prime})\in\{-1,+1\}
. Since the orders of the vertical and horizontal subrectangles in
R_{i_{0}}(\mathcal{G}^{\prime})
have both changed and the order of the horizontal (or vertical) subrectangles of any other rectangle of
\mathcal{G}^{\prime}
remained the same, we have that :

\epsilon_{i}(\mathcal{G}^{\prime\prime\prime})=\epsilon_{i}(\mathcal{G}^{\prime})\text{ and }\epsilon^{\prime}_{i}(\mathcal{G}^{\prime\prime\prime})=\epsilon^{\prime}_{i}(\mathcal{G}^{\prime})\text{ for $i\neq i_{0}$}

\epsilon_{i_{0}}(G^{\prime\prime\prime})=-\epsilon_{i_{0}}(G^{\prime})\text{ and }\epsilon^{\prime}_{i_{0}}(\mathcal{G}^{\prime\prime\prime})=-\epsilon^{\prime}_{i_{0}}(\mathcal{G}^{\prime})

By eventually changing our initial choice of representatives, we can thus assume that for every
i\in\llbracket 1,n\rrbracket
the numbers
\epsilon_{i}(\mathcal{G}^{\prime})
and
\epsilon_{i}^{\prime}(\mathcal{G}^{\prime})
are all equal to
+1
and thus that
\mathcal{G}^{\prime}=\mathcal{G}
, which gives us the desired result.

∎

We end this section with an important corollary of the two previous lemmas.

Corollary 8.7.1
.

Consider
\rho_{1},\rho_{2}
two strong Markovian actions on the planes
\mathcal{P}_{1},\mathcal{P}_{2}
preserving the strong Markovian families
\mathcal{R}_{1},\mathcal{R}_{2}
, whose boundary periodic points are respectively
\Gamma_{1},\Gamma_{2}
. Denote by
\overline{\mathcal{P}_{1}},\overline{\mathcal{P}_{2}}
the bifoliated planes of
\rho_{1},\rho_{2}
up to surgeries on
\Gamma_{1},\Gamma_{2}
and by
\overline{\mathcal{R}_{1}},\overline{\mathcal{R}_{2}}
the lifts of
\mathcal{R}_{1},\mathcal{R}_{2}
on
\overline{\mathcal{P}_{1}},\overline{\mathcal{P}_{2}}
.

If
\mathcal{R}_{1},\mathcal{R}_{2}
are associated to the same equivalence class of geometric types, then
\overline{\mathcal{R}_{1}},\overline{\mathcal{R}_{2}}
are also associated to the same equivalence class of geometric types. Furthermore, the special geometric types associated to
\overline{\mathcal{R}_{1}},\overline{\mathcal{R}_{2}}
coincide.

8.2.
Rectangle paths in the bifoliated plane up to surgeries

Following the notations of the previous section,

Definition 8.8
.

A finite sequence of rectangles in
\overline{\mathcal{R}}
of the form
R_{0}
,
R_{1}
,…,
R_{n}
will be called a
rectangle path
(of
length

n+1
) going from
R_{0}
to
R_{n}
if for every
i\in\llbracket 0,n-1\rrbracket
the rectangle
R_{i+1}
is either a successor or a predecessor of
R_{i}
. We will say that the rectangle path
R_{0}
,
R_{1}
,…,
R_{n}
is

•

trivial
if it is of length one

•

closed
if it also verifies
R_{n}=R_{0}

•

increasing
(resp.
decreasing
) if it verifies that
R_{i+1}
is a successor (resp. predecessor) of
R_{i}
for every
i

•

monotonous
if it is increasing or decreasing

∎

The reader may think of a rectangle path as a discretized curve inside the bifoliated plane up to surgeries. In order to illustrate the close connection between curves and rectangle paths, our goal in this section consists in defining the notion of rectangle path associated to a (good polygonal) curve in
\overline{\mathcal{P}}
and the notion of (good polygonal) curve associated to some rectangle path.

Definition 8.9
.

A continuous curve defined by a topological immersion
\gamma:[0,1]\rightarrow\overline{\mathcal{P}}
will be called a
polygonal curve
if it is a finite juxtaposition of alternating stable and unstable segments in
\overline{\mathcal{F}^{s,u}}
. Furthermore, if the curve
\gamma
is closed, we will call it a
closed
polygonal curve.
∎

Definition 8.10
.

By definition, for any polygonal curve
\gamma
there exists a unique finite sequence
d_{0}=0<d_{1}<...<d_{n}=1
such that the
\gamma([d_{i},d_{i+1}])
are alternating stable and unstable segments. Any such stable or unstable segment will be called a
side
of
\gamma
. The number
n
will be called the
length
of the polygonal curve
\gamma
. Also, a polygonal curve
\gamma
will be called
good
if

(1)

none of its sides belongs to the stable or unstable leaf of a point in
\overline{\Gamma}

(2)

there do not exist
i\neq j\in\llbracket 0,n-1\rrbracket
such that
\gamma([d_{i},d_{i+1}])
and
\gamma([d_{j},d_{j+1}])
belong to the same stable or unstable leaf in
\overline{\mathcal{F}^{s,u}}
.

∎

Remark 8.11
.

Notice that by our above definition if
\gamma
is a closed and good polygonal curve, then
\gamma(0)
is necessarily a corner point of
\gamma
(i.e. not in the interior of any stable or unstable segment in
\gamma([0,1])
).

Lemma 8.12
.

Any immersed curve
\gamma:[0,1]\rightarrow\overline{\mathcal{P}}
that is disjoint from
\overline{\Gamma}
and for which
\gamma(0)
and
\gamma(1)
do not belong to a stable or unstable leaf of a point in
\overline{\Gamma}
is homotopic relatively to its boundary to a good polygonal curve in
\overline{\mathcal{P}}

Proof.

Indeed, we can locally deform
\gamma
to a finite juxtaposition of alternating stable and unstable segments. By compactness,
\gamma
is homotopic relatively to its boundary to a polygonal curve
\gamma^{\prime}
in
\overline{\mathcal{P}}-\overline{\Gamma}
. Without any loss of generality, we can assume that
\gamma^{\prime}
is the juxtaposition of a stable segment
s_{1}
, followed by an unstable segment
u_{1}
,…, ending with an unstable segment
u_{n}
.

Suppose that one of the stable segments in
\gamma^{\prime}
, say
s_{k}
, belongs to a stable leaf of a point in
\overline{\Gamma}
. Notice that
s_{k}\neq s_{1}
, because of our initial hypothesis. The points in
\overline{\Gamma}
forming a finite set of
\overline{\rho}
-orbits (see for instance Proposition
6.14
) and
\overline{G}
being countable (see Remark
6.11
), we get that
\overline{\Gamma}
is countable and thus their stable and unstable leaves too. Since
\gamma^{\prime}
does not intersect
\overline{\Gamma}
, by changing a little bit the “lengths” of the segments
u_{k-1}
and
u_{k}
, we can replace
s_{k}
by another stable segment in
s_{k}
’s neighborhood that does not belong to the countable set of stable leaves of
\overline{\Gamma}
. By a repeated application of the previous argument, we get that
\gamma^{\prime}
is homotopic relatively to its boundary to a polygonal curve satisfying the first axiom of Definition
8.10
.

By the exact same procedure, we can show that
\gamma^{\prime}
is homotopic relatively to its boundary to a polygonal curve satisfying also the second axiom of Definition
8.10
, which gives us the desired result.

∎

Lemma 8.13
.

Consider
s
a closed stable segment in
\overline{\mathcal{F}^{s}}
that does not belong to the stable leaf of any point in
\overline{\Gamma}
. There can not exist a sequence
(R^{(i)})_{i\in\mathbb{N}}
of rectangles in
\overline{\mathcal{R}}
such that

(1)

an extremity of
s
lies in the interior of
R^{(0)}

(2)

R^{(i+1)}
is a crossing successor of
R^{(i)}
for every
i\in\mathbb{N}

(3)

R^{(0)}\cap s\subsetneq...\subsetneq R^{(i)}\cap s\subsetneq s
for every
i\in\mathbb{N}

Proof.

Assume that a sequence of rectangles with the above properties exists. Denote by
s_{0}
the extremity of
s
in
\overset{\circ}{R^{(0)}}
and by
s_{1}
the other extremity of
s
(see Figure
8.1
). Since the sequence of closed segments
R^{(i)}\cap s
is strictly increasing, there exists
x
a first point of
s
that is not contained in any of the
R^{(i)}
. Suppose that the
R^{(i)}\cap s
approach
x
from the left and consider
[t,x]
a small stable segment in
s
on the left of
x
. Since by Remark
8.2
,
\overline{\mathcal{R}}
satisfies the conditions of Definition
3.14
, by the finite return time axiom applied for
\overline{\mathcal{R}}
, there exists
R\in\overline{\mathcal{R}}
containing
[t,x]
. Notice also that, since
s
does not belong to the stable leaf of a point in
\overline{\Gamma}
, by Remark
8.4
, the segment
s
cannot contain a non-trivial segment of the stable boundary of
R
. It follows that
[t,x]-\{x\}\subset\overset{\circ}{R}
.

On the other hand, for every
i
,
R^{(i+1)}\cap R^{(i)}
is a non-trivial horizontal subrectangle of
R^{(i)}
; hence, by Remark
8.4
,
R^{(n)}
will be a very thin along the vertical direction and a very long along the horizontal direction rectangle approaching
x
from the left. Hence, for
n
big
\overset{\circ}{R^{(n)}}\cap\overset{\circ}{R}\neq\emptyset
, which contradicts the Markovian intersection property for
\overline{\mathcal{R}}
(see Remark
8.2
and Definition
3.14
) and leads to an absurd.

Figure 8.1
.

∎

Naturally, a similar lemma holds for unstable segments in
\overline{\mathcal{F}^{u}}
too.

Consider
\gamma:[0,1]\rightarrow\overline{\mathcal{P}}
a good polygonal curve in
\overline{\mathcal{P}}
and
r_{0}\in\overline{\mathcal{R}}
such that
\gamma(0)\in\overset{\circ}{r_{0}}
. Assume without any loss of generality that
\gamma
is the juxtaposition of the stable segment
s
, followed by the unstable segment
u
, …, followed at the end by the segment
f
.

Suppose now that
s
crosses the unstable boundary component
U
of
r_{0}
. Because of Definition
8.10
and Remark
8.4
,
s
does not belong to the stable leaf of a boundary arc or boundary periodic point of
\overline{\mathcal{R}}
. Therefore, by Lemmas
5.5
,
5.6
applied for
\overline{\mathcal{R}}
, there exists a unique
U
-crossing successor of
r_{0}
, say
R^{(1)}
, containing
r_{0}\cap s
. If
s
exits
R^{(1)}
, by applying the same argument,
s
will enter a unique crossing successor of
R^{(1)}
, say
R^{(2)}
. Thanks to Lemma
8.13
, by applying a finite number of times the previous procedure, we will eventually obtain a rectangle
R^{(n)}
, a successor of some generation of
r_{0}
, for which
s\subset R^{(n)}
. By Lemma
4.5
applied for
\overline{\mathcal{R}}
(see Remark
8.2
), there exists a unique increasing rectangle path
R_{0}=r_{0},R_{1},...,R_{m}=R^{(n)}
going from
R_{0}
to
R^{(n)}
.

Definition 8.14
.

We will call the rectangle path
R_{0}=r_{0},R_{1},...,R_{m}=R^{(n)}
,
the rectangle path associated to
s
starting from
r_{0}
. One defines similarly the
rectangle path associated to an unstable segment starting from a rectangle in
\mathcal{R}
.

We now define recursively
the rectangle path associated to
\gamma
starting from
r_{0}
, say
path_{r_{0}}(\gamma)
, as follows:

•

when
\gamma
has length one,
path_{r_{0}}({\gamma})
is defined as the rectangle path associated to
s
starting from
r_{0}

•

when
\gamma
has length
n>1
, consider
d_{0}=0<d_{1}<...<d_{n}=1
such that the
\gamma([d_{i},d_{i+1}])
are alternating stable and unstable segments (the
d_{i}
are uniquely defined by the previous properties). Denote by
\gamma^{\prime}:=\gamma_{|[d_{1},1]}
the polygonal curve of length
n-1
obtained by removing from
\gamma
its first segment, namely the segment
s
. We define
path_{r_{0}}(\gamma)
as the concatenation of
path_{r_{0}}(s)
followed by
path_{R^{(n)}}(\gamma^{\prime})
.

∎

Following our previous notations, let us remark here that when the length of
\gamma
is greater than
1
,
path_{r_{0}}(\gamma)
is well defined, since by Definition
8.10
and Remark
8.4
both
\gamma^{\prime}:[d_{1},1]\rightarrow\overline{\mathcal{P}}
and
s:[0,d_{1}]\rightarrow\overline{\mathcal{P}}
are good polygonal curves and
\gamma(d_{1})\in\overset{\circ}{R^{(n)}}
.

Proposition 8.15
.

For every two rectangles
R_{0},R\in\overline{\mathcal{R}}
there exists a rectangle path starting from
R_{0}
and ending at
R
.

Proof.

We consider an immersed curve
\gamma:[0,1]\rightarrow\overline{\mathcal{P}}
such that
\gamma(0)\in\overset{\circ}{R_{0}}
,
\gamma(1)\in\overset{\circ}{R}
,
\gamma
does not intersect
\overline{\Gamma}
and
\gamma(0),\gamma(1)
don’t belong to the stable or unstable leaf of a point in
\overline{\Gamma}
. By Lemma
8.12
, there exists
\gamma^{\prime}
a good polygonal curve homotopic to
\gamma
relatively to its boundary. Consider
R_{0},R_{1},...R_{n}
the rectangle path associated to
\gamma^{\prime}
starting from
R_{0}
. Let us remark that
R_{n}
is not necessarily equal to
R
. However, thanks to Remark
8.4
, since
\gamma(1)
does not belong to the stable or unstable leaf of a point in
\overline{\Gamma}
,
\gamma(1)\in\overset{\circ}{R}\cap\overset{\circ}{R_{n}}
and by Lemma
4.5
applied for
\overline{\mathcal{R}}
, the rectangle
R
is a
k
-th successor or predecessor of
R_{n}
for some
k\in\mathbb{N}
. Assume without any loss of generality that
R
is a
k
-th successor of
R_{n}
. In this case, by Lemma
4.5
, there exists a unique increasing rectangle path
R_{n},...,R_{n+k}=R
from
R_{n}
to
R
. The rectangle path
R_{0},...,R_{n+k}=R
is the desired rectangle path.
∎

Of course the rectangle path of the previous corollary is not unique; it depends on the choice of the original curve
\gamma
and on how we deformed it into a good polygonal curve
\gamma^{\prime}
. In fact, for any two rectangles
R_{0},R\in\overline{\mathcal{R}}
there exist infinitely many distinct rectangle paths going from
R_{0}
to
R
: if
R_{0},R_{1},...,R
is one such rectangle path, then
R_{0},R_{1},R_{0},R_{1},...,R
is also a rectangle path from
R_{0}
to
R
.

As we have previously explained, it is possible to associate a set of rectangle paths to any good polygonal curve in
\overline{\mathcal{P}}
. The inverse association is also possible:

Proposition 8.16
.

Fix a rectangle path
R_{0},...,R_{n}
. There exists a good polygonal curve
\gamma
such that
R_{0},...,R_{n}
is the rectangle path starting from
R_{0}
associated to
\gamma
. Furthermore, if
R_{0},...,R_{n}
is closed, then we can choose
\gamma
to also be closed.

Proof.

Consider
R_{0},...,R_{n}
a rectangle path and
x\in\overset{\circ}{R_{0}}
that does not belong to the stable or unstable leaf of a point in
\overline{\Gamma}
. Consider now a polygonal path starting from
x\in R_{0}
, exiting
R_{0}
in order to enter
R_{1}
(we use here the fact that
R_{1}\not\subset R_{0}
see Remark
5.8
), then exiting
R_{1}
in order to enter
R_{2}
and so on until we reach
R_{n}
. We ask that our polygonal path ends to a point that does not belong to the stable or unstable leaf of a point in
\overline{\Gamma}
and that the path does not intersect any point of
\overline{\Gamma}
. If
R_{n}=R_{0}
, we will also ask that our polygonal path ends at
x
.

By the same arguments as in the proof of Lemma
8.12
, using the fact that
x
does not belong in the stable or unstable leaf of a point in
\overline{\Gamma}
, it is possible to prove that the previous (resp. closed when
R_{0}=R_{n}
) polygonal curve can be deformed to a good (resp. and closed) polygonal curve
\gamma
starting from
x
, exiting
R_{0}
in order to enter
R_{1}
, then exiting
R_{1}
in order to enter
R_{2}
and so on until we reach
R_{n}
.

It is now easy to check, using Definition
8.14
, that the rectangle path starting from
R_{0}
associated to
\gamma
is exactly
R_{0},...,R_{n}
.
∎

Remark 8.17
.

•

The construction of
\gamma
in Proposition
8.16
is not unique.

•

If
r_{0},...,r_{n}
is a rectangle path associated to a good polygonal curve
\gamma:[0,1]\rightarrow\overline{\mathcal{P}}
, then there exist
0=c_{0}<c_{1}<...<c_{n+1}=1
such that
\gamma([c_{i},c_{i+1}])\subset r_{i}
. The
c_{i}
are not unique. Using our choice of
c_{i}
, we define
\textit{Rect}_{\gamma,r_{0}}:[0,1]\rightarrow\left\{r_{0},...,r_{n}\right\}
as the function sending every interval of the form
[c_{i},c_{i+1})
to
r_{i}
and such that
\textit{Rect}_{\gamma,r_{0}}(1)=r_{n}
.

As we have previously proven, good polygonal curves in
\overline{\mathcal{P}}
are intimately related with rectangle paths in
\overline{\mathcal{R}}
. The function
\textit{Rect}_{\gamma,r_{0}}
provides a way to draw the link between a continuous curve and a rectangle path, which the reader can think as the discretization of a continuous good polygonal curve in
\overline{\mathcal{P}}
.

Convention 8.18
.

Even though
\textit{Rect}_{\gamma,r_{0}}
is a function that associates to every point of
\gamma
a rectangle that contains it, from now on and unless explicitly said otherwise we will think of the image by
\textit{Rect}_{\gamma,r_{0}}
of any interval in
[0,1]
as a rectangle path in
\overline{\mathcal{R}}
.

Even though
\textit{Rect}_{\gamma,r_{0}}
, is not uniquely defined, in the following pages, it will be useful to us to define the function
\textit{Rect}_{\gamma,r_{0}}
as in the following lemma:

Lemma 8.19
.

Let
\gamma:[0,1]\rightarrow\overline{\mathcal{P}}
be a good polygonal curve and
r_{0},...,r_{n}
its associated rectangle path starting from
r_{0}
. We can choose
0=c_{0}<c_{1}<...<c_{n+1}=1
and define
\textit{Rect}_{\gamma,r_{0}}:[0,1]\rightarrow\{r_{0},r_{1},...,r_{n}\}
so that:

•

\gamma([c_{i},c_{i+1}])\subset r_{i}
for every
i

•

for any stable (resp. unstable) segment
S
of
\gamma
the rectangle path
\textit{Rect}_{\gamma,r_{0}}(\gamma^{-1}(S))
is either trivial or increasing (resp. decreasing)

•

if
d_{0}=0<d_{1}<...<d_{n}=1
are such that the segments
\gamma([d_{i},d_{i+1}])
are alternating stable and unstable segments in
\gamma
, then
\textit{Rect}_{\gamma,r_{0}}([0,d_{i}])
coincides with the rectangle path starting from
R_{0}
associated to the good polygonal curve
\gamma_{|[0,d_{i}]}

Proof.

Recall that to any choice of
0=c_{0}<c_{1}<...<c_{n+1}=1
such that
\gamma([c_{i},c_{i+1}])\subset r_{i}
, we can associate a function
\textit{Rect}_{\gamma,r_{0}}
. Let us now construct a set of
c_{i}
, such that
\textit{Rect}_{\gamma,r_{0}}
verifies the desired properties.

Assume without any loss of generality, that
\gamma
is the juxtaposition of the stable segment
s_{1}
, followed by the unstable segment
u_{1}
,…, ending with the unstable segment
u_{m}
. Let
c_{0}=0
,
c_{n+1}=1
,
t_{r_{0}}=\text{max}\{t\in[0,1]\penalty\ |\penalty\ \gamma([0,t])\subset r_{0}\}
,
t_{s_{l}}
,
t_{u_{l}}
be the unique elements in
[0,1]
such that

\gamma([0,t_{s_{l}}])=s_{1}\cup u_{1}\cup s_{2}\cup...\cup s_{l}

\gamma([0,t_{u_{l}}])=s_{1}\cup u_{1}\cup s_{2}\cup...\cup s_{l}\cup u_{l}

Fix
\epsilon>0
a very small positive number. We begin by defining
c_{1}
. In order to do so, we will consider two cases.

Case 1.
Suppose first that
s_{1}
does not exit
r_{0}
. If
u_{1},s_{2},...,u_{m}
do not exit
r_{0}
, then by Definition
8.14
the rectangle path associated to
\gamma
is trivial; hence
n=0
and
c_{1}=c_{n+1}=1
. If
u_{1},s_{2},...,s_{l}
do not exit
r_{0}
and
u_{l}
exits
r_{0}
, then we define
c_{1}=t_{s_{l}}+\epsilon
. Similarly, if
u_{1},s_{2},...,u_{l}
do not exit
r_{0}
and
s_{l+1}
exits
r_{0}
, then we define
c_{1}=t_{u_{l}}+\epsilon
. Notice that in both of the previous cases, if
\epsilon
is sufficiently small, thanks to the fact that
\gamma
is good, we have that
\gamma([c_{0},c_{1}])\subset r_{0}
. Indeed, by definition none of the segments
s_{1},u_{1},s_{2},...,u_{l}
can begin or end at the boundary of a rectangle in
\overline{\mathcal{R}}
.

Case 2.
Suppose now that
s_{1}
exits
r_{0}
in order to visit
r_{k_{1}}
, a crossing successor of
r_{0}
, then exits
r_{k_{1}}
in order to visit
r_{k_{2}}
, a crossing successor of
r_{k_{1}}
and so on till (see Lemma
8.13
) it reaches
r_{k_{l}}
that verifies
s_{1}\subset r_{k_{l}}
(see Figure
8.2
). Recall that by Definition
8.14
, every time that
\gamma
leaves a rectangle
R
in order to visit
R^{\prime}
, a crossing successor of
R
, we add to the rectangle path associated to
\gamma
the unique increasing rectangle path from
R
to
R^{\prime}
. Therefore,
r_{k_{1}},...,r_{k_{l}}
belong in the rectangle path starting from
r_{0}
associated to
\gamma
. We will assume that for every
i\in\llbracket 1,l\rrbracket
the rectangle
r_{k_{i}}
corresponds to
(k_{i}+1)
-th rectangle of the rectangle path starting from
r_{0}
associated to
\gamma
. It follows from our previous remark that for every
i\in\llbracket 0,k_{l}-1\rrbracket
the rectangle
r_{i+1}
is a successor of
r_{i}
containing
\gamma([0,t_{r_{0}}])
. Choose now arbitrarily
c_{1},...,c_{k_{l}-1}\in[0,1]
such that
0<c_{1}<c_{2}<c_{3}<...<c_{k_{l}-1}<t_{r_{0}}
and let
c_{k_{l}}:=t_{s_{1}}+\epsilon
. Notice that by our previous arguments, if
\epsilon
is sufficiently small, then we have that
\gamma([c_{k_{l}-1},c_{k_{l}}])\subset r_{k_{l}}
and also that for every
i\in\llbracket 0,k_{l}-2\rrbracket
we have
\gamma([c_{i},c_{i+1}])\subset\gamma([0,t_{r_{0}}])\subset r_{i}
.

Figure 8.2
.
The red curve in the above figure represents a good polygonal curve, whose associated rectangle path starting from
r_{0}
is
r_{0},r_{1},r_{2},r_{3}
.

By our previous construction of the
c_{i}
and by Remark
8.17
,
\textit{Rect}_{\gamma,r_{0}}(\gamma^{-1}(s_{1}))
corresponds to the rectangle path starting from
R_{0}
associated to the good polygonal curve
s_{1}
and for every stable segment
s\subset s_{1}
, the rectangle path
\textit{Rect}_{\gamma,r_{0}}(\gamma^{-1}(s))
is either trivial or increasing. Therefore, our construction verifies the desired properties. Once the previous procedure ends for
s_{1}
, we will repeat it for
u_{1}
,
s_{2}
,…, till we finally reach
u_{m}
; we thus get the desired set of
c_{i}
.
∎

8.3.
Rectangle paths as coordinate systems

Let
\rho_{1}:G_{1}\rightarrow\text{Homeo}(\mathcal{P}_{1})
,
\rho_{2}:G_{2}\rightarrow\text{Homeo}(\mathcal{P}_{2})
be two strong Markovian actions acting on the planes
\mathcal{P}_{1}
and
\mathcal{P}_{2}
together with their stable and unstable foliations
\mathcal{F}_{1}^{s}
,
\mathcal{F}_{1}^{u}
(resp.
\mathcal{F}_{2}^{s}
,
\mathcal{F}_{2}^{u}
). Assume that
\rho_{1}
and
\rho_{2}
preserve two strong Markovian families
\mathcal{R}_{1}
and
\mathcal{R}_{2}
, whose associated classes of geometric types coincide.

For every
i\in\{1,2\}
, let us denote by
\Gamma_{i}
the boundary periodic points associated to
\mathcal{R}_{i}
in
\mathcal{P}_{i}
,
\overline{\mathcal{P}_{i}}
the bifoliated plane of
\rho_{i}
up to surgeries on
\Gamma_{i}
,
\overline{\rho_{i}}:\overline{G_{i}}\rightarrow\text{Homeo}(\overline{\mathcal{P}_{i}})
the lift of
\rho_{i}
on
\overline{\mathcal{P}_{i}}
,
\overline{\mathcal{F}^{s}_{i}},\overline{\mathcal{F}^{u}_{i}}
the stable and unstable foliations of
\overline{\rho_{i}}
,
\overline{\mathcal{R}_{i}}
the lift of
\mathcal{R}_{i}
on
\overline{\mathcal{P}_{i}}
and
\overline{\Gamma_{i}}
the lift of
\Gamma_{i}
on
\overline{\mathcal{P}_{i}}
.

Thanks to Corollary,
8.7.1
, by appropriately choosing representatives for every rectangle orbit in
\overline{\mathcal{R}_{1}}
and
\overline{\mathcal{R}_{2}}
, and orientations for the foliations
\overline{\mathcal{F}_{1}^{s,u}}
,
\overline{\mathcal{F}_{2}^{s,u}}
, we may assume that
\overline{\mathcal{R}_{1}}
and
\overline{\mathcal{R}_{2}}
are associated to the same (special) geometric type

\mathcal{G}=(R_{1},...,R_{n},(h_{i})_{i\in\llbracket 1,n\rrbracket},(v_{i})_{i\in\llbracket 1,n\rrbracket},\mathcal{H},\mathcal{V},\phi,u)

Notice that we are using here the geometric interpretation of a geometric type and thus we are thinking of
\mathcal{G}
as a set of rectangles and subrectangles. Using our previous choice of representatives and orientations, we would like to associate to any rectangle path in
\overline{\mathcal{P}_{1}}
a rectangle path in
\overline{\mathcal{P}_{2}}
. This will allow us to simultaneously navigate into
\overline{\mathcal{P}_{1}}
and
\overline{\mathcal{P}_{2}}
by using rectangle paths and to compare (locally) the actions
\overline{\rho_{1}}
and
\overline{\rho_{2}}
.

Recall that every
\overline{\rho_{1}}
-orbit of rectangles in
\overline{\mathcal{R}_{1}}
corresponds to a unique rectangle of
\mathcal{G}
. Same for
\overline{\mathcal{R}_{2}}
.

Definition 8.20
.

We will say that
R^{1}\in\overline{\mathcal{R}_{1}}
(resp.
R^{2}\in\overline{\mathcal{R}_{2}}
) is of
type
i
if the
\overline{G_{1}}
-orbit (resp.
\overline{G_{2}}
-orbit) of
R^{1}
(resp.
R^{2}
) in
\overline{\mathcal{P}_{1}}
(resp.
\overline{\mathcal{P}_{2}}
) corresponds to the
i
-th rectangle of
\mathcal{G}
.
∎

Fix
r_{0}^{1}\in\overline{\mathcal{R}_{1}}
and
r_{0}^{2}\in\overline{\mathcal{R}_{2}}
the unique rectangles of type
1
among our choices of representatives in
\overline{\mathcal{R}_{1}}
and
\overline{\mathcal{R}_{2}}
, thanks to which we previously defined
\mathcal{G}
. From now on, we will call
r_{0}^{1}
,
r_{0}^{2}
the
origin rectangles
in
\overline{\mathcal{P}_{1}}
,
\overline{\mathcal{P}_{2}}
and we will also call any rectangle path in
\overline{\mathcal{P}_{1}}
,
\overline{\mathcal{P}_{2}}
starting from
r_{0}^{1}
,
r_{0}^{2}
a
centered rectangle path
.

To any centered rectangle path
r^{1}_{0},...,r^{1}_{N}
in
\overline{\mathcal{P}_{1}}
it is possible to canonically associate a unique centered rectangle path
r^{2}_{0},...,r^{2}_{N}
in
\overline{\mathcal{P}_{2}}
such that for every
i\in\llbracket 0,N\rrbracket

r^{1}_{i}
and
r^{2}_{i}
are of the same type.

Indeed, take
r^{1}_{0},...,r^{1}_{N}
a centered rectangle path in
\overline{\mathcal{P}_{1}}
. Let us start by constructing
r^{2}_{1}
. Since
r^{1}_{0},...,r^{1}_{N}
is a rectangle path
r^{1}_{1}
is a predecessor or a successor of
r^{1}_{0}
. Assume without any loss of generality that it is a successor. Using our choice of orientation of
\overline{\mathcal{F}^{u}_{1}}
, assume that
r^{1}_{1}
is the
k-
th successor of
r^{1}_{0}
from bottom to top. Take
r^{2}_{1}
to be the
k
-th successor of
r^{2}_{0}
from bottom to top for our choice of orientation on
\overline{\mathcal{F}^{u}_{2}}
. By our definition of
r^{1}_{0},r^{2}_{0}
and also thanks to the fact that
\overline{\mathcal{R}_{1}}
and
\overline{\mathcal{R}_{2}}
correspond to the same geometric type (for our choices of representatives and orientations), we have that both
r^{1}_{0}
and
r^{2}_{0}
correspond in
\mathcal{G}
to the rectangle
R_{1}
, that the orientations of
\overline{\mathcal{F}^{u}_{1}}
and
\overline{\mathcal{F}^{u}_{2}}
inside
r^{1}_{0}
and
r^{2}_{0}
correspond both to the bottom to top orientation in
R_{1}
and therefore
r^{1}_{1}
and
r^{2}_{1}
both correspond to
k
-th horizontal subrectangle of
R_{1}
from bottom to top. Hence,
r^{1}_{1}
and
r^{2}_{1}
are of the same type.

Consider next
\overline{g_{1}}
(resp.
\overline{g_{2}}
) the unique element in
\overline{G_{1}}
(resp.
\overline{G_{2}}
) such that
\overline{\rho_{1}}(\overline{g_{1}})
(resp.
\overline{\rho_{2}}(\overline{g_{2}})
) takes
r_{1}^{1}
(resp.
r_{1}^{2}
) to the representative of its orbit that was initially chosen during the construction of
\mathcal{G}
. Once again, since
\overline{\mathcal{R}_{1}}
and
\overline{\mathcal{R}_{2}}
correspond to the same geometric type (for our choices of representatives and orientations), we have that
\overline{\rho_{1}}(\overline{g_{1}})
reverses the orientation of the foliations if and only if
\overline{\rho_{2}}(\overline{g_{2}})
does (see our definition of the function
u
in Definition
4.7
). It follows that
r^{1}_{1}
and
r^{2}_{1}
correspond both to some rectangle
R_{i}
in
\mathcal{G}
and that the orientations of
\overline{\mathcal{F}^{u}_{1}}
and
\overline{\mathcal{F}^{u}_{2}}
inside
r^{1}_{1}
and
r^{2}_{1}
correspond both to the bottom to top orientation in
R_{i}
or both to the top to bottom orientation in
R_{i}
. Using this fact, we can easily construct by the same exact argument the rectangles
r^{2}_{2},...,r_{N}^{2}
by induction.

Definition 8.21
.

We will call the rectangle path
r^{2}_{0},...,r^{2}_{N}

the (centered) rectangle path in
\overline{\mathcal{P}_{2}}
associated to the (centered) rectangle path

r^{1}_{0},...,r^{1}_{N}
.
∎

The main idea behind the proof of Theorem B lies in the fact that we can use rectangle paths and associated rectangle paths as coordinate systems in order to compare
\overline{\mathcal{P}_{1}}
and
\overline{\mathcal{P}_{2}}
. The most important part of the proof of Theorem B consists in proving the following theorem, according to which the two coordinate systems given by rectangle paths and associated rectangle paths are compatible:

Theorem 8.22
.

Fix any two centered rectangle paths in
\overline{\mathcal{P}_{1}}
ending by the same rectangle. Their associated centered rectangle paths in
\overline{\mathcal{P}_{2}}
also end by the same rectangle.

The previous theorem is equivalent to the following:

Theorem 8.23
.

Fix any closed centered rectangle path in
\overline{\mathcal{P}_{1}}
. Its associated centered rectangle path in
\overline{\mathcal{P}_{2}}
is also closed.

Proof of the equivalence of Theorems
8.22
and
8.23
.

(
8.22

\Rightarrow

8.23
): Take
r^{1}_{0}
the trivial rectangle path and
C_{path}
any closed centered rectangle path in
\overline{\mathcal{P}_{1}}
. Since
C_{path}
is closed, it also ends at
r^{1}_{0}
. By Theorem
8.22
, the rectangle paths in
\overline{\mathcal{P}_{2}}
associated to
C_{path}
and
r_{0}^{1}
end by the same rectangle. But the trivial rectangle path
r^{1}_{0}
is associated (see our discussion prior to Definition
8.21
) to the trivial rectangle path
r^{2}_{0}
. Therefore, the centered rectangle path in
\overline{\mathcal{P}_{2}}
associated to
C_{path}
is closed.

(
8.23

\Rightarrow

8.22
):
Take two centered rectangle paths
r^{1}_{0},...r^{1}_{N}
and
R^{1}_{0},R^{1}_{1},...,R^{1}_{m}
in
\overline{\mathcal{P}_{1}}
such that
r^{1}_{0}=R^{1}_{0}
and
R^{1}_{m}=r^{1}_{N}
. The rectangle path
r^{1}_{0},...r^{1}_{N},R^{1}_{m-1},R^{1}_{m-2},...,R^{1}_{1},r^{1}_{0}
is a closed centered rectangle path in
\overline{\mathcal{P}_{1}}
. Therefore, by Theorem
8.23
its associated rectangle path in
\overline{\mathcal{P}_{2}}
is also closed. Denote the previous rectangle path by
r^{2}_{0},...r^{2}_{N},R^{2}_{m-1},R^{2}_{m-2},...,R^{2}_{1},r^{2}_{0}
. It is not difficult to see, that
r^{2}_{0},...r^{2}_{N}
is the centered rectangle path associated to
r^{1}_{0},...r^{1}_{N}
and that
r^{2}_{0},R^{2}_{1},...,R^{2}_{m-1},r^{2}_{N}
is the centered rectangle path associated to
R^{1}_{0},R^{1}_{1},...,R^{1}_{m}
. We deduce that the centered rectangle paths in
\overline{\mathcal{P}_{2}}
associated to
r^{1}_{0},...r^{1}_{N}
and
R^{1}_{0},R^{1}_{1},...,R^{1}_{m}
end by the same rectangle.

∎

9.
A homotopy theory for rectangle paths - Proof of Theorem
8.23

Our goal in this section will be to prove Theorem
8.23
, the most difficult step in the proof of Theorem B. Our proof of Theorem
8.23
will consist in three steps:

(1)

deforming a closed rectangle path in
\overline{\mathcal{P}_{1}}
to a trivial one, by the use of three combinatorial operations that we call
homotopies

(2)

showing that a rectangle path that can be deformed to the trivial one is necessarily closed

(3)

proving that a rectangle path in
\overline{\mathcal{P}_{1}}
can be deformed to a trivial one if and only if its associated rectangle path in
\overline{\mathcal{P}_{2}}
can be deformed to a trivial one

Concerning the organization of this section, in Section
9.1
we will define the notion of
tangency
for a good polygonal curve and we will state a combinatorial result involving the number of different tangencies of a simple closed polygonal curve. The previous result will be crucial for us during the proof of Theorem
8.23
. Next, in Section
9.2
we will define the notion of homotopy for rectangle paths in the bifoliated plane up to surgeries. Finally, the rest of this section will be dedicated to the proof of the above mentioned three steps.

9.1.
Singularities of polygonal curves

Fix
\rho:G\rightarrow\text{Homeo}(\mathcal{P})
an orientation preserving strong Markovian action, preserving the pair of singular foliations
\mathcal{F}^{s}
and
\mathcal{F}^{u}
and leaving invariant a strong Markovian family
\mathcal{R}
. We denote by
\Gamma
the set of boundary periodic points of
\mathcal{R}
, by
\overline{\mathcal{P}}
the bifoliated plane of
\rho
up to surgeries on
\Gamma
, by
\overline{\mathcal{R}}
the lift of
\mathcal{R}
on
\overline{\mathcal{P}}
, by
\overline{\Gamma}
the lift of
\Gamma
on
\overline{\mathcal{P}}
and by
\overline{\rho}:\overline{G}\rightarrow\text{Homeo}(\overline{\mathcal{P}})
the lift of the action
\rho
on
\overline{\mathcal{P}}
.

Let us immediately remark, that contrary to the case of a plane, Jordan’s theorem does not hold in
\overline{\mathcal{P}}
:

Remark 9.1
.

There exists a simple closed curve
\gamma
in
\overline{\mathcal{P}}
, such that
\overline{\mathcal{P}}-\gamma
contains more than two connected components.

Indeed, take
s
an arc in
\overline{\mathcal{P}}
connecting two different points in
\overline{\Gamma}
. Notice that since
\overline{\mathcal{P}}
is topologically a plane to which we have added a countable set of points at infinity, namely the points
\overline{\Gamma}
, the arc
s
disconnects
\overline{\mathcal{P}}
. Consider now an arc
s^{\prime}
close to
s
, having the same extremities as
s
and intersecting nowhere else
s
. By parametrizing
s\cup s^{\prime}
, we can obtain a simple, closed curve
\gamma
in
\overline{\mathcal{P}}
such that
\overline{\mathcal{P}}-\gamma
consists of two unbounded connected components and one bounded connected component.

Despite the existence of such curves and since
\overline{\mathcal{P}}-\overline{\Gamma}
is a topological plane, Jordan’s theorem applies for simple, closed curves in
\overline{\mathcal{P}}
that do not intersect
\overline{\Gamma}
. This justifies the following definition :

Definition 9.2
.

A closed polygonal curve
\gamma:[0,1]\rightarrow\overline{\mathcal{P}}
will be called
simple
if the function
\gamma
restricted on
[0,1)
is injective.

A simple closed polygonal curve
\gamma
in
\overline{\mathcal{P}}-\overline{\Gamma}
defines two complementary regions: a bounded region that we will name the
interior
of
\gamma
and an unbounded region that we will name the
exterior
of
\gamma
.

∎

Notice that, since
\overline{\mathcal{P}}
is homeomorphic to a plane together with a countable set of points at infinity:

Remark 9.3
.

The interior of a simple closed polygonal curve in
\overline{\mathcal{P}}-\overline{\Gamma}
can never contain a point of
\overline{\Gamma}

Take
\gamma
a simple closed polygonal curve in
\overline{\mathcal{P}}-\overline{\Gamma}
,
D
the interior of
\gamma
and
U
a neighborhood of
D\cup\gamma
. Endow
\overline{\mathcal{F}^{s}}
and
\overline{\mathcal{F}^{u}}
with an orientation (see Remark
6.7
). Using the previous orientations, we will define 4 types of tangencies of
\gamma
to the foliation
\overline{\mathcal{F}^{s}}
.

Definition 9.4
.

Consider
\gamma
as a function from
\mathbb{S}^{1}
(endowed with an orientation) to
\overline{\mathcal{P}}
. Fix
s
a stable segment of
\gamma
. We will denote by
u^{-}
the unstable segment in
\gamma
coming right before
s
(for our choice of orientation of
\mathbb{S}^{1}
) and by
u^{+}
the unstable segment in
\gamma
coming right after
s
. Using the orientation on
\mathbb{S}^{1}
, we can naturally orient
u^{-}
and
u^{+}
. We will say that
s
is a
stable tangency
of
\gamma
if one of the two previous segments is negatively oriented and the other is positively oriented with respect to the orientation of
\overline{\mathcal{F}^{u}}
.

Furthermore, we will say that a stable tangency
s
of
\gamma
is a tangency of type (a) (resp. (d)) if for any small positively oriented unstable segment
u
starting from a point in
s
we have that
u\subset\overline{D}
and if any stable leaf of
\overline{\mathcal{F}^{s}}
intersecting
u
intersects also (resp. does not intersect)
u^{-}
and
u^{+}
.

By changing the positively oriented segment
u
to a negatively oriented segment, we can similarly define a tangency of type (b) or (c). We define in an analogous way
unstable tangencies
of type (a), (b), (c) or (d) for
\gamma
.

Figure 9.1
.
Types of singularities for
\gamma

∎

Endow the plane
\overline{\mathcal{P}}-\overline{\Gamma}
with a smooth structure. Using the fact that the leaves of
\overline{\mathcal{F}^{s}}
are closed inside
\overline{\mathcal{P}}-\overline{\Gamma}
(see Remark
6.7
), we have that up to conjugating
\overline{\mathcal{F}^{s}}
by a homeomorphism, we can assume that
\overline{\mathcal{F}^{s}}
is a smooth foliation inside
\overline{\mathcal{P}}-\overline{\Gamma}
(see more generally Exercise 8.4.9 of
[
CaCo
]
). Consider now
D^{\prime}
a small perturbation of the closed disk
\overline{D}
inside
U
such that
\partial D^{\prime}
is smooth and in general position with respect to
\overline{\mathcal{F}^{s}}
. The curve
\partial D^{\prime}
has a finite number of tangencies with
\overline{\mathcal{F}^{s}}
.

Take
x
a point of tangency between
\partial D^{\prime}
and
\overline{\mathcal{F}^{s}}
. We will say that
x
is an
interior
(resp.
exterior
)
tangency
if any vector based at
x
and tangent to
\overline{\mathcal{F}^{s}(x)}
points towards the interior (resp. exterior) of
D^{\prime}
.

By considering any non-singular vector field
X
on
U
tangent to
\overline{\mathcal{F}^{s}}
-such a vector field exists, since
\overline{\mathcal{F}^{s}}
is orientable (see Remark
6.7
)- and by applying the Poincaré Index theorem for the vector field
X
and the disk
D^{\prime}
, we get that:

(\text{number of exterior tangencies of }\partial D^{\prime})-(\text{number of interior tangencies of }\partial D^{\prime})=2

Therefore, there are always more exterior tangencies in
\partial D^{\prime}
than interior ones.

By eventually changing our initial perturbation
D^{\prime}
, we may assume that:

(1)

every stable tangency of type (a) or (b) (resp. (c) or (d)) of
\gamma
becomes after perturbation an exterior (resp. interior) tangency of
\partial D^{\prime}

(2)

every stable segment of
\gamma
that is not a tangency becomes after perturbation transverse to
\overline{\mathcal{F}^{s}}

(3)

the tangencies of
\partial D^{\prime}
are bijectively associated to stable tangencies of
\gamma

As a consequence of this, we obtain the following result:

Lemma 9.5
.

Consider the set of stable tangencies of any simple closed polygonal curve
\gamma
in
\overline{\mathcal{P}}-\overline{\Gamma}
. We have that:

(\text{number of tangencies of type (a) or (b)})-(\text{number of tangencies of type (c) or (d)})=2

Remark 9.6
.

Notice that the type (a) (resp. (b),(c) or (d)) of a stable tangency of
\gamma
, depends on our choice of orientation of
\overline{\mathcal{F}^{u}}
. By changing our choice of orientation, tangencies of type (a) (resp. (b), (c), (d)) become tangencies of type (b) (resp. (a), (d), (c)). This is why, the previous lemma remains true independently from our choices of orientation.

Naturally, the above lemma is also true for unstable tangencies. Let us finish this section with the following result:

Lemma 9.7
.

Let
\gamma
be a simple closed polygonal curve in
\overline{\mathcal{P}}-\overline{\Gamma}
,
s
a stable tangency of
\gamma
of type (a) or (b) and
D
the interior of
\gamma
. There exists a unique rectangle
R
contained in
D\cup\gamma
such that

•

s
is a stable boundary component of
R

•

the unstable boundaries of
R
are contained in
\gamma

•

R
is maximal for the previous properties

Proof.

Consider
\gamma
as a function from
\mathbb{S}^{1}
(endowed with an orientation) to
\overline{\mathcal{P}}-\overline{\Gamma}
and
u^{-}
(resp.
u^{+}
) the unstable segment in
\gamma
coming right before (resp. after)
s
. Denote by
I^{-}
(resp.
I^{+}
) the set of points
x
in
u^{-}
(resp.
u^{+}
) for which there exists a stable segment inside
D\cup\gamma
going from
x
to
u^{+}
(resp.
u^{-}
). Denote also by
x_{0}^{-}
(resp.
x_{0}^{+}
) the unique point in
s\cap u^{-}
(resp.
s\cap u^{+}
). Notice that
I^{-}
(resp.
I^{+}
) contains a neighbordhood of
x_{0}^{-}
(resp.
x_{0}^{+}
) in
u^{-}
(resp.
u^{+}
), since
s
intersects both
u^{-}
and
u^{+}
. Let us also remark that, thanks to Remark
9.3
, if
x\in I^{-}
, then all points of
u^{-}
between
x
and
x^{-}_{0}
belong in
I^{-}
, hence
I^{-}
is a segment. The same applies for
I^{+}
.

Next, by the definitions of
I^{-}
and
I^{+}
, for every
x\in I^{-}
the stable segment in
D\cup\gamma
going from
x
to
u^{+}
intersects
u^{+}
along a point of
I^{+}
and vice versa. Let us now show that
I^{-}
is a closed segment in
u^{-}
. Assume that
I^{-}
is of the form
[x_{0}^{-},X^{-})\subset u^{-}
. By our previous arguments, this implies that
I^{+}
is also of the form
[x_{0}^{+},X^{+})
.

If the stable leaf of
X^{-}
intersects
u^{+}
, since it can be accumulated by stable segments in
D\cup\gamma
going from
u^{-}
to
u^{+}
, then there exists a stable segment in
D\cup\gamma
going from
X^{-}
to
u^{+}
and thus
X^{-}\in I^{-}
. We deduce that the stable leaf of
X^{-}
(resp.
X^{+}
) does not intersect
u^{+}
(resp.
u^{-}
). Moreover, the stable leaves of
X^{-}
and
X^{+}
can be accumulated from one side by segments going from
u^{-}
to
u^{+}
; thus the previous two leaves are non-separated stable leaves.

Denote by
\overline{\pi}
the projection from
\overline{\mathcal{P}}
to
\mathcal{P}
and by
s_{x}
the stable segment in
D\cup\gamma
going from
x\in I^{-}
to
u^{+}
. By applying Corollary 2.19 of
[
BaBaMa
]
8
8

8

We remark here that Corollary 2.19 of
[
BaBaMa
]
is proven for non-transitive Anosov-like actions. The definition of such an action in
[
BaBaMa
]
consists of 5 axioms, denoted by A1-A5. The axiom A5 is not used in the proof of Corollary 2.19, the axioms A1, A2, A4 are included in our definition of strong Markovian action and finally A3 follows from Remark
5.3
for the non-separated stable leaves in
\mathcal{P}
containing
\overline{\pi}(\overline{\mathcal{F}^{s}}(X^{+}))
and
\overline{\pi}(\overline{\mathcal{F}^{s}}(X^{-}))
, we get that there exists an unstable leaf
L\in\overline{\mathcal{F}^{u}}
such that the set
L\cap\underset{x\in I^{-}}{\cup}s_{x}\subset D\cup\gamma
contains the entire unstable separatrix of some point in
L
. Since
D\cup\gamma
is compact and does not intersect
\overline{\Gamma}
, the previous statement contradicts the fact that the leaves of
\overline{\mathcal{F}^{u}}
are properly embedded in
\overline{\mathcal{P}}-\overline{\Gamma}
(see Remark
6.7
). We deduce that
I^{-}
and
I^{+}
are closed. The segments in
D\cup\gamma
going from
I^{-}
to
I^{+}
form the rectangle with the desired properties.

∎

The rectangle constructed in the previous lemma forms a canonical neighborhood of the tangency
s
inside
D\cup\gamma
. We will call this neighborhood the
domain
of
s
. Furthermore, if the domain of
s
covers completely either
u^{-}
or
u^{+}
, we will call it a
complete domain
. In any other case, we will call it an
incomplete domain
. By our proof of Lemma
9.7
we can deduce that:

Remark 9.8
.

If the domain
\mathcal{D}
of
s
is incomplete, there exists a stable segment of
\gamma
intersecting the interior of the stable boundary component of
\mathcal{D}
that is not
s
.

9.2.
Three types of homotopies of rectangle paths and good polygonal curves

Fix
\rho:G\rightarrow\text{Homeo}(\mathcal{P})
an orientation preserving strong Markovian action, preserving the pair of singular foliations
\mathcal{F}^{s}
and
\mathcal{F}^{u}
and leaving invariant a strong Markovian family
\mathcal{R}
. We denote by
\Gamma
the boundary periodic point of
\mathcal{R}
, by
\overline{\mathcal{P}}
the bifoliated plane of
\rho
up to surgeries on
\Gamma
, by
\overline{\mathcal{R}}
the lift of
\mathcal{R}
on
\overline{\mathcal{P}}
, by
\overline{\Gamma}
the lift of
\Gamma
on
\overline{\mathcal{P}}
and by
\overline{\rho}:\overline{G}\rightarrow\text{Homeo}(\overline{\mathcal{P}})
the lift of the action
\rho
on
\overline{\mathcal{P}}
. Consider
\overline{\mathcal{F}^{s}}
and
\overline{\mathcal{F}^{u}}
the stable and unstable foliations of
\overline{\rho}
endowed with an orientation.

Fix for the rest of this section
R_{0},...,R_{n}
a rectangle path in
\overline{\mathcal{R}}
.

Definition 9.9
.

We will say that two rectangle paths are
homotopic by a homotopy of type
\mathcal{A}
if they are equal.

Two good polygonal curves
\gamma,\delta:[0,1]\rightarrow\overline{\mathcal{P}}
whose associated rectangle paths starting from
R_{0}
are equal to
R_{0},...,R_{n}
will be called
homotopic by a homotopy of type
\mathcal{A}
(
relatively to
R_{0},...,R_{n}
).

∎

Convention 9.10
.

By an abuse of language, whenever the context makes it clear, we will omit the rectangle paths relatively to which we will perform homotopies of good polygonal curves.

Figure 9.2
.
\gamma
and
\delta
are homotopic by a homotopy of type
\mathcal{B}

Definition 9.11
.

Consider
R_{0},...,R_{n}
a rectangle path such that there exists
k\in\llbracket 0,n-2\rrbracket
for which
R_{k}=R_{k+2}
. We are going to say that the rectangle paths
R_{0},..,R_{k},R_{k+3},R_{k+4},...
and
R_{0},...,R_{n}
are
homotopic by a homotopy of type
\mathcal{B}
.

Consider
\gamma
,
\delta
two good polygonal curves whose associated rectangle paths starting from
R_{0}
are equal to
R_{0},...,R_{n}
and
R_{0},..,R_{k},R_{k+3},R_{k+4},...,R_{n}
respectively (see Figure
9.2
). We will say that
\gamma
and
\delta
are
homotopic by a homotopy of type
\mathcal{B}
(
relatively to
R_{0},...,R_{n}
and
R_{0},..,R_{k},R_{k+3},

R_{k+4},...,R_{n}
).
∎

Homotopies of type
\mathcal{A}
or type
\mathcal{B}
correspond to “movements” of good polygonal curves in the interior of the rectangles of
\overline{\mathcal{R}}
. As it turns out, the previous two elementary movements are not enough to deform any closed rectangle path to a trivial one. This is why we will now define a third type of homotopy allowing good polygonal curves to cross the boundary of some rectangle in
\overline{\mathcal{R}}
and more specifically to cross a boundary arc point in
\overline{\mathcal{P}}
. We will name this homotopy,
homotopy of type
\mathcal{C}
. In order to introduce this homotopy, we will first need to define the notion of
cycle
around a boundary arc point.

Take
p\in\overline{\mathcal{P}}
a boundary arc point of
\overline{\mathcal{R}}
and
