L_{0}\in\overline{\mathcal{R}}
such that
L_{0}
contains
p
and two germs of quadrants of
p
(i.e. two small neighborhoods of
p
inside two distinct quadrants of
p
). Assume without any loss of generality that
p\in\partial^{s}L_{0}
(see Figure
9.3
) and denote by
r
the stable boundary component of
L_{0}
containing
p
. By
Lemmas
5.5
and
5.6
applied for
\overline{\mathcal{R}}
(see Remark
8.2
), there exists
L_{1}
an
r
-crossing predecessor of
L
such that
p\in\partial{L_{0}}\cap\partial{L_{1}}
.

Consider
r_{1}
the unstable boundary component of
L_{1}
containing
p
. Again by
Lemmas
5.5
and
5.6
, there exists
L_{2}
an
r_{1}
-crossing successor of
L_{1}
such that
p\in\partial{L_{2}}\cap\partial{L_{1}}
and
\overset{\circ}{L_{2}}\cap\overset{\circ}{L_{0}}=\emptyset
. By the same exact procedure, we construct
L_{3}
and
L_{4}
such that
\overset{\circ}{L_{3}}\cap\overset{\circ}{L_{1}}=\emptyset
and
\overset{\circ}{L_{4}}\cap\overset{\circ}{L_{2}}=\emptyset
.

Definition 9.12
.

We are going to call
(L_{0},L_{1},L_{2},L_{3},L_{4})
a
cycle
around
p
starting from
L_{0}
.
∎

Figure 9.3
.

Remark 9.13
.

For any boundary arc point
p\in\overline{\mathcal{P}}
, there exists a cycle around
p
.

Indeed, by our discussion prior to Definition
9.12
, it suffices to show that there exists
L\in\overline{\mathcal{R}}
containing
p
and the germs of two quadrants of
p
. Take
L\in\overline{\mathcal{R}}
containing
p
. Since
p
is a boundary arc point,
p\in\partial L
. If
p
is not a corner point of
L
, then
L
contains the germs of two quadrants of
p
. If
p
is a corner point of
L
, let us name
s
the stable boundary of
L
in which it is contained and
L^{\prime}
the
s
-crossing predecessor of
L
that contains
p
. The rectangle
L^{\prime}
contains the germs of two quadrants of
p
.

The set of boundary arc points of
\overline{\mathcal{R}}
, by definition, is disjoint from
\overline{\Gamma}
; hence boundary arc points are regular points of the foliations
\overline{\mathcal{F}^{s}}
and
\overline{\mathcal{F}^{u}}
. Take
p
a boundary arc point of
\overline{\mathcal{R}}
and
(L_{0},L_{1},L_{2},L_{3},L_{4})
a cycle around of
p
. Using the orientations of
\overline{\mathcal{F}^{s}}
and
\overline{\mathcal{F}^{u}}
fixed in the beginning of this section, denote by
(+,+),(+,-),(-,+),(-,-)
the 4 quadrants of
p
, where
(+,+)
(resp.
(+,-),(-,+),(-,-)
) denotes the quadrant of
p
bounded by the positive (resp. positive, negative, negative) stable separatrix of
p
and the positive (resp. negative, positive, negative) unstable separatrix of
p
. Denote also by
G_{(\epsilon,\epsilon^{\prime})}
a germ of the
(\epsilon,\epsilon^{\prime})
quadrant of
p
, where
\epsilon,\epsilon^{\prime}\in\{-,+\}
. By the definition of a cycle, up to cyclic permutation of the indices of the
L_{i}
, we have one of the two following statements:

Positive cycle

•

\overset{\circ}{L_{0}}\cap G_{(-,-)}\neq\emptyset
,
\overset{\circ}{L_{0}}\cap G_{(+,-)}\neq\emptyset

•

\overset{\circ}{L_{1}}\cap G_{(+,-)}\neq\emptyset
,
\overset{\circ}{L_{1}}\cap G_{(+,+)}\neq\emptyset

•

\overset{\circ}{L_{2}}\cap G_{(+,+)}\neq\emptyset
,
\overset{\circ}{L_{2}}\cap G_{(-,+)}\neq\emptyset

•

\overset{\circ}{L_{3}}\cap G_{(-,+)}\neq\emptyset
,
\overset{\circ}{L_{3}}\cap G_{(-,-)}\neq\emptyset

•

\overset{\circ}{L_{4}}\cap G_{(-,-)}\neq\emptyset
,
\overset{\circ}{L_{4}}\cap G_{(+,-)}\neq\emptyset

Negative cycle

•

\overset{\circ}{L_{0}}\cap G_{(+,-)}\neq\emptyset
,
\overset{\circ}{L_{0}}\cap G_{(-,-)}\neq\emptyset

•

\overset{\circ}{L_{1}}\cap G_{(-,-)}\neq\emptyset
,
\overset{\circ}{L_{1}}\cap G_{(-,+)}\neq\emptyset

•

\overset{\circ}{L_{2}}\cap G_{(-,+)}\neq\emptyset
,
\overset{\circ}{L_{2}}\cap G_{(+,+)}\neq\emptyset

•

\overset{\circ}{L_{3}}\cap G_{(+,+)}\neq\emptyset
,
\overset{\circ}{L_{3}}\cap G_{(+,-)}\neq\emptyset

•

\overset{\circ}{L_{4}}\cap G_{(+,-)}\neq\emptyset
,
\overset{\circ}{L_{4}}\cap G_{(-,-)}\neq\emptyset

In the first case, we will call the cycle
(L_{0},L_{1},L_{2},L_{3},L_{4})

positive
and in the second one
negative
. Notice in particular, that for every cycle
(L_{0},L_{1},L_{2},L_{3},L_{4})
around
p
, we have that
\overset{\circ}{L_{4}}\cap\overset{\circ}{L_{0}}\neq\emptyset
.

Lemma 9.14
.

Take
p\in\overline{\mathcal{P}}
a boundary arc point of
\overline{\mathcal{R}}
and
L_{0}\in\overline{\mathcal{R}}
such that
L_{0}
contains
p
and the germs of two quadrants of
p
. There exist exactly two cycles around
p
starting from
L_{0}
: one positive and one negative.

Proof.

Without any loss of generality, we can assume that
p
is contained in a stable boundary component of
L_{0}
, say
s
, and that a germ of the
(-,-)
and
(+,-)
quadrants of
p
is contained in
L_{0}
. By Remark
5.9
and
Lemmas
5.5
,
5.6
applied for
\overline{\mathcal{R}}
(see also Remark
8.2
), since
p
is a boundary arc point and any two distinct
s
-crossing predecessors of
L_{0}
intersect only along their boundaries, there exist exactly two
s
-crossing predecessors
L_{1},L_{1}^{\prime}
of
L_{0}
containing
p
. Assume without any loss of generality that
L_{1}
contains a germ of the
(+,+)
and
(+,-)
quadrants of
p
and
L_{1}^{\prime}
a germ of the
(-,+)
and
(-,-)
quadrants of
p
. Since
L_{1}
is an
s
-crossing predecessor of
L_{0}
, the point
p
is contained in the interior of some unstable boundary component of
L_{1}
. Therefore, by our previous argument the rectangle
L_{2}
of Definition
9.12
is uniquely defined. Similarly, the rectangles
L_{3}
and
L_{4}
of Definition
9.12
are uniquely defined. The same argument applies for
L_{1}^{\prime}
. We thus obtain exactly two cycles around
p
starting from
L_{0}
: one positive and one negative.
∎

Lemma 9.15
.

Take
p\in\overline{\mathcal{P}}
a boundary arc point of
\overline{\mathcal{R}}
and
L_{0}\in\overline{\mathcal{R}}
containing the germs of two quadrants of
p
. If
(L_{0},L_{1},L_{2},L_{3},L_{4})
is a cycle around
p
, then
(L_{0},L_{3},L_{2},L_{1},L_{4})
is the other cycle around
p
starting from
L_{0}
.

Proof.

Let us assume without any loss of generality that
p\in\partial^{s}L_{0}
. Denote by
s
the stable boundary component of
L_{0}
containing
p
. Assuming that
(L_{0},L_{1},L_{2},L_{3},L_{4})
is a cycle around
p
, let us show that
L_{3}
is an
s
-crossing predecessor of
L_{0}
.

If
s^{\prime}
is the stable boundary component of
L_{2}
containing
p
, then since
(L_{0},L_{1},L_{2},L_{3},L_{4})
is a cycle, we have that
L_{3}
is an
s^{\prime}
-crossing predecessor of
L_{2}
. By Remark
5.9
applied for
\overline{\mathcal{R}}
, the unstable boundary of
L_{3}
intersects
s^{\prime}
along boundary arc points. By the Markovian intersection property,
L_{3}\cap L_{0}
is a vertical subrectangle of
L_{0}
(see Figure
9.3
). Since
\partial^{u}L_{3}\cap s
consists of two boundary arc points, we deduce from Remark
5.9
that
L_{3}
is an
s
-crossing predecessor of
L_{0}
. We show in the exact same way, that
L_{2}
is a crossing successor of
L_{3}
, that
L_{1}
is a crossing predecessor of
L_{2}
and that
L_{4}
is a crossing successor of
L_{1}
. Finally, since
(L_{0},L_{1},L_{2},L_{3},L_{4})
is a cycle, we have that
\overset{\circ}{L_{2}}\cap\overset{\circ}{L_{0}}=\emptyset
,
\overset{\circ}{L_{3}}\cap\overset{\circ}{L_{1}}=\emptyset
and
\overset{\circ}{L_{4}}\cap\overset{\circ}{L_{2}}=\emptyset
. By Lemma
9.14
, we conclude that
(L_{0},L_{3},L_{2},L_{1},L_{4})
is the second cycle around
p
starting from
L_{0}
.
∎

Lemma 9.16
.

Take
p\in\overline{\mathcal{P}}
a boundary arc point and
L_{0},L^{\prime}_{0}\in\overline{\mathcal{R}}
two rectangles containing the germs of the same two quadrants of
p
. If
(L_{0},L_{1},L_{2},L_{3},L_{4})
is a positive (resp. negative) cycle around
p
, then
(L^{\prime}_{0},L_{1},L_{2},L_{3},L_{4})
is also a positive (resp. negative) cycle around
p
.

Proof.

Indeed, assume without any loss of generality that
(L_{0},L_{1},L_{2},L_{3},L_{4})
is a positive cycle around
p
and that
L_{0},L^{\prime}_{0}
contain the germs of the
(-,-)
and
(+,-)
quadrants of
p
.

Denote by
r
and
r^{\prime}
the stable boundary components of
L_{0}
and
L^{\prime}_{0}
containing
p
. By definition of a positive cycle,
L_{1}
contains the germs of the
(+,-)
and
(+,+)
quadrants of
p
and is an
r
-crossing predecessor of
L_{0}
. By the Markovian intersection axiom,
L_{1}\cap r=L_{1}\cap r^{\prime}
and
L_{1}\cap L^{\prime}_{0}
is a non-trivial vertical subrectangle of
L^{\prime}_{0}
. Furthermore, thanks to Remark
5.9
, the two extremities of
L_{1}\cap r
consist of two boundary arc points and by the same remark applied to
L_{1}\cap r^{\prime}
, we get that
L_{1}
is an
r^{\prime}
-crossing predecessor of
L^{\prime}_{0}
. By using the same construction as the one that was given prior to Definition
9.12
, we get that
(L^{\prime}_{0},L_{1},L_{2},L_{3},L_{4})
is a cycle around
p
visiting the quadrants of
p
in the same order as
(L_{0},L_{1},L_{2},L_{3},L_{4})
, which gives us the desired result.
∎

The above lemma implies that the rectangles
L_{1},L_{2},L_{3},L_{4}
are canonically associated to
p
independently of the choice of
L_{0}
. In particular, this means that
(L_{4},L_{1},L_{2},L_{3},L_{4})
defines a cycle around
p
.

Definition 9.17
.

Consider
L_{0},...,L_{n}
a sequence of rectangles in
\overline{\mathcal{R}}
such that for every
i
, the rectangle
L_{i+1}
is a successor or predecessor of some generation of
L_{i}
. We will call
L_{0},...,L_{n}
a
generalised rectangle path
. By Lemma
4.5
applied for
\overline{\mathcal{R}}
(see also Remark
8.2
), for every
i
there exists a unique monotonous rectangle path (see Definition
8.8
)
L_{i}=L_{i0},L_{i1},...,L_{is_{i}},L_{i(s_{i}+1)}=L_{i+1}
going from
L_{i}
to
L_{i+1}
.
We are going to call

L_{0},L_{01},...,L_{0s_{0}},L_{1},L_{11},...,L_{1s_{1}},L_{2},....,L_{n-1},L_{(n-1)1},...,L_{(n-1)s_{n-1}},L_{n}

the rectangle path
associated
to
L_{0},...,L_{n}
.
∎

Definition 9.18
.

Consider
R_{0},...,R_{n}
a rectangle path,
p\in\overline{\mathcal{P}}
a boundary arc point,
L_{0}\in\overline{\mathcal{R}}
such that
L_{0}
contains
p
and the germs of two quadrants of
p
. Denote by
(L_{0},L_{1},L_{2},L_{3},L_{4})
and
(L_{0},L_{3},L_{2},L_{1},L_{4})
the two cycles around
p
starting from
L_{0}
. Consider
(L_{0},L_{1}...,L_{k})
the first
k+1
terms of the first cycle with
k\in\llbracket 1,4\rrbracket
and
(L_{0},L_{3}...,L_{k})
the part of the second cycle starting from
L_{0}
and ending at
L_{k}
.

Following the notations of Definition
9.17
, we can associate to
L_{0},L_{1},...,L_{k}
and
L_{0},L_{3},...,L_{k}
the rectangle paths
L_{0},L_{01},...,L_{0s_{0}},L_{1},L_{11},...,L_{1s_{1}},L_{2},...,L_{k}
and

L_{0},L^{\prime}_{01},...,L^{\prime}_{0s^{\prime}_{0}},L_{3},L^{\prime}_{31},...,L^{\prime}_{3s^{\prime}_{3}},...,L_{k}
respectively. Assume that the first rectangle path is contained in
R_{0},...,R_{n}
. In other words, assume that
R_{0},...,R_{n}
is of the form

R_{0},...,R_{m},L_{0},L_{01},...,L_{0s_{0}},L_{1},L_{11},...,L_{1s_{1}},...,L_{k},R_{l},R_{l+1},...,R_{n}

Consider now the rectangle path
R^{\prime}_{0},...,R^{\prime}_{n^{\prime}}
defined as follows

R_{0},...,R_{m},L_{0},L^{\prime}_{01},...,L^{\prime}_{0s^{\prime}_{0}},L_{3},L^{\prime}_{31},...,L^{\prime}_{3s^{\prime}_{3}},...,L_{k},R_{l},R_{l+1},...,R_{n}

We are going to say that the rectangle paths
R_{0},...,R_{n}
and
R^{\prime}_{0},...,R^{\prime}_{n^{\prime}}
are
homotopic by a homotopy of type
\mathcal{C}
.

Take
\gamma
and
\delta
two good polygonal curves whose associated rectangle paths starting from
R_{0}
are
R_{0},...,R_{n}
and
R^{\prime}_{0},...,R^{\prime}_{n^{\prime}}
respectively (see Figure
9.4
). We will say that
\gamma
is homotopic to
\delta
by a
homotopy of type
\mathcal{C}
(
relatively to
R_{0},...,R_{n}
and
R^{\prime}_{0},...,R^{\prime}_{n^{\prime}}
).
∎

Figure 9.4
.

Definition 9.19
.

Take
P:=r_{0},r_{1},...,r_{n}
and
P^{\prime}:=r_{0},r^{\prime}_{1},...,r^{\prime}_{m}
two rectangle paths. We are going to say that
P
and
P^{\prime}
are
homotopic
if there exists
P_{0}:=P,P_{1},...,P_{s}:=P^{\prime}
a finite sequence of rectangle paths such that
P_{i}
is homotopic to
P_{i+1}
by a homotopy of type
\mathcal{A}
,
\mathcal{B}
or
\mathcal{C}
.
∎

Let us remark that thanks to our definition of homotopies of type
\mathcal{A},\mathcal{B}
and
\mathcal{C}
, any two homotopic rectangle paths in
\overline{\mathcal{R}}
begin and end by the same rectangles. In particular, a closed rectangle path in
\overline{\mathcal{R}}
is only homotopic to closed rectangle paths. Therefore, a rectangle path homotopic to the trivial rectangle path is necessarily closed. It turns out that the converse is also true:

Theorem 9.20
.

Any closed rectangle path
r_{0},r_{1},...,r_{n}
in
\overline{\mathcal{P}}
is homotopic to the trivial rectangle path
r_{0}

The above theorem constitutes the key argument in the proof of Theorem
8.23
, which will be used to prove Theorem B in Section
10
.

9.3.
Theorem
9.20
implies Theorem
8.23

Let
\rho_{1}:G_{1}\rightarrow\text{Homeo}(\mathcal{P}_{1})
,
\rho_{2}:G_{2}\rightarrow\text{Homeo}(\mathcal{P}_{2})
be two strong orientation preserving Markovian actions acting on the planes
\mathcal{P}_{1}
and
\mathcal{P}_{2}
, endowed with their stable and unstable foliations
\mathcal{F}^{s,u}_{1}
and
\mathcal{F}^{s,u}_{2}
. Assume that
\rho_{1},\rho_{2}
preserve two strong Markovian families
\mathcal{R}_{1}
and
\mathcal{R}_{2}
, whose associated classes of geometric types coincide.

Denote by
\Gamma_{1},\Gamma_{2}
the set of boundary periodic points of
\mathcal{R}_{1},\mathcal{R}_{2}
, by
\overline{\mathcal{P}_{1}}
,
\overline{\mathcal{P}_{2}}
the bifoliated planes of
\rho_{1}
,
\rho_{2}
up to surgeries on
\Gamma_{1},\Gamma_{2}
, by
\overline{\mathcal{R}_{1}}
and
\overline{\mathcal{R}_{2}}
the lifts of
\mathcal{R}_{1}
and
\mathcal{R}_{2}
on
\overline{\mathcal{P}_{1}}
,
\overline{\mathcal{P}_{2}}
, by
\overline{\Gamma_{1}},\overline{\Gamma_{2}}
the lifts of
\Gamma_{1},\Gamma_{2}
on
\overline{\mathcal{P}_{1}}
,
\overline{\mathcal{P}_{2}}
and by
\overline{\rho_{1}}:\overline{G_{1}}\rightarrow\text{Homeo}(\overline{\mathcal{P}_{1}})
,
\overline{\rho_{2}}:\overline{G_{2}}\rightarrow\text{Homeo}(\overline{\mathcal{P}_{2}})
the lifts of
\rho_{1}
,
\rho_{2}
on
\overline{\mathcal{P}_{1}}
,
\overline{\mathcal{P}_{2}}
. By Proposition
8.6
the Markovian families
\overline{\mathcal{R}_{1}}
and
\overline{\mathcal{R}_{2}}
are associated to the same class of geometric types and thanks to the realisability lemma, we can choose a set of representatives of every orbit in
\overline{\mathcal{R}_{1}}
and
\overline{\mathcal{R}_{2}}
and we can orient the stable and unstable foliations in
\overline{\mathcal{P}_{1}}
,
\overline{\mathcal{P}_{2}}
so that the geometric types associated to
\overline{\mathcal{R}_{1}}
and
\overline{\mathcal{R}_{2}}
for the previous choices of orientations and representatives coincide and are equal to

\mathcal{G}=(n,(h_{i})_{i\in\llbracket 1,n\rrbracket},(v_{i})_{i\in\llbracket 1,n\rrbracket},\mathcal{H},\mathcal{V},\phi,u)

Fix
r_{0}^{1}\in\overline{\mathcal{R}_{1}}
and
r_{0}^{2}\in\overline{\mathcal{R}_{2}}
two of the previously chosen representatives such that
r_{0}^{1}
and
r_{0}^{2}
are of the same type. For the rest of this section,
r_{0}^{1}
and
r_{0}^{2}
will be our reference rectangles in
\overline{\mathcal{R}_{1}}
and
\overline{\mathcal{R}_{2}}
.

Assuming that Theorem
9.20
stands, let us show that:

Theorem

(Theorem
8.23
)
.

Any closed centered rectangle path in
\overline{\mathcal{R}_{1}}
is associated (see Definition
8.21
) to a closed centered rectangle path in
\overline{\mathcal{R}_{2}}
.

Proof.

It suffices to prove that two centered and homotopic rectangle paths in
\overline{\mathcal{P}_{1}}
correspond to centered and homotopic rectangle paths in
\overline{\mathcal{P}_{2}}
. Indeed, let
r^{1}_{0},...,r^{1}_{n}
be a closed and centered rectangle path in
\overline{\mathcal{P}_{1}}
and
r^{2}_{0},...,r^{2}_{n}
its associated centered rectangle path in
\overline{\mathcal{P}_{2}}
. By Theorem
9.20
,
r^{1}_{0},...,r^{1}_{n}
is homotopic to the trivial rectangle path
r_{0}^{1}
. If homotopic rectangle paths in
\overline{\mathcal{P}_{1}}
correspond to homotopic rectangle paths in
\overline{\mathcal{P}_{2}}
, then
r^{2}_{0},...,r^{2}_{n}
is homotopic to
r^{2}_{0}
. By our discussion at the end of the last section, this implies that
r^{2}_{0},...,r^{2}_{n}
is closed, which gives us the desired result. Let us now show that homotopic rectangle paths in
\overline{\mathcal{P}_{1}}
correspond to homotopic rectangle paths in
\overline{\mathcal{P}_{2}}
.

Since any homotopy of rectangle paths can be described as a sequence of homotopies of type
\mathcal{A},\mathcal{B}
or
\mathcal{C}
, it suffices to show that homotopies of type
\mathcal{A}
(resp.
\mathcal{B}
,
\mathcal{C}
) in
\overline{\mathcal{P}_{1}}
correspond to homotopies of type
\mathcal{A}
(resp.
\mathcal{B}
,
\mathcal{C}
) in
\overline{\mathcal{P}_{2}}
. The result is trivially true for homotopies of type
\mathcal{A}
.

Consider now
C_{1},C_{2}
two centered rectangle paths in
\overline{\mathcal{P}_{1}}
that are homotopic by a homotopy of type
\mathcal{B}
. By Definition
9.11
, the paths
C_{1},C_{2}
are of the form
r^{1}_{0},...,r^{1}_{k},r^{1}_{k+1},r^{1}_{k},r^{1}_{k+2},...r^{1}_{n}
and
r^{1}_{0},...,r^{1}_{k},r^{1}_{k+2},...,r^{1}_{n}
. By our construction in Definition
8.21
, it is not difficult to see that
C_{1},C_{2}
correspond in
\overline{\mathcal{P}_{2}}
to two rectangle paths of the form
r^{2}_{0},...,r^{2}_{k},r^{2}_{k+1},r^{2}_{k},r^{2}_{k+2},...r^{2}_{n}
and
r^{2}_{0},...,r^{2}_{k},r^{2}_{k+2},...,r^{2}_{n}
. The previous two rectangle paths are homotopic by a homotopy of type
\mathcal{B}
in
\overline{\mathcal{P}_{2}}
.

Finally, consider
C_{1},C_{2}
two centered rectangle paths in
\overline{\mathcal{P}_{1}}
that are homotopic by a homotopy of type
\mathcal{C}
. Following the notations of Definition
9.18
, we may assume that
C_{1}
and
C_{2}
are respectively of the form:

r^{1}_{0},...,r^{1}_{k},L^{1}_{0},L^{1}_{01},...,L^{1}_{0s_{0}},L^{1}_{1},L^{1}_{11},...,L^{1}_{1s_{1}},...,L^{1}_{k},r^{1}_{l},r^{1}_{l+1},...,r^{1}_{n}

r^{1}_{0},...,r^{1}_{k},L^{1}_{0},L^{\prime 1}_{01},...,L^{\prime 1}_{0s^{\prime}_{0}},L^{1}_{3},L^{\prime 1}_{31},...,L^{\prime 1}_{3s^{\prime}_{3}},...,L^{1}_{k},r^{1}_{l},r^{1}_{l+1},...,r^{1}_{n}

where
(L^{1}_{0},L^{1}_{1},L^{1}_{2},L^{1}_{3},L^{1}_{4})
and
(L^{1}_{0},L^{1}_{3},L^{1}_{2},L^{1}_{1},L^{1}_{4})
are the two cycles starting from
L^{1}_{0}
around a boundary arc point
p^{1}\in L^{1}_{0}-\{\text{corners of $L^{1}_{0}$}\}
,
k\in\llbracket 1,4\rrbracket
,
L^{1}_{0},L^{1}_{01},...

,L^{1}_{0s_{0}},L^{1}_{1},L^{1}_{11},

...,L^{1}_{1s_{1}},...,L^{1}_{k}
is the rectangle path associated to the generalized rectangle path
(L^{1}_{0},L^{1}_{1},...,L^{1}_{k})
and
L^{1}_{0},L^{\prime 1}_{01},...,L^{\prime 1}_{0s^{\prime}_{0}},L^{1}_{3},L^{\prime 1}_{31},...,L^{\prime 1}_{3s^{\prime}_{3}},...,L^{1}_{k}
is the rectangle path associated to the generalized rectangle path
(L^{1}_{0},L^{1}_{3},...,L^{1}_{k})
.

Assume without any loss of generality that
L_{0}^{1}
contains a germ of the
(-,-)
and
(+,-)
quadrants of
p^{1}
and that
(L^{1}_{0},L^{1}_{1},L^{1}_{2},L^{1}_{3},L^{1}_{4})
is a positive cycle around
p^{1}
(see Figure
9.3
). Denote by

r^{2}_{0},...,r^{2}_{k},L^{2}_{0},L^{2}_{01},...,L^{2}_{0s_{0}},L^{2}_{1},L^{2}_{11},...,L^{2}_{1s_{1}},...,L^{2}_{k},r^{2}_{l},r^{2}_{l+1},...,r^{2}_{n}

r^{2}_{0},...,r^{2}_{k},L^{2}_{0},L^{\prime 2}_{01},...,L^{\prime 2}_{0s^{\prime}_{0}},L^{2}_{3},L^{\prime 2}_{31},...,L^{\prime 2}_{3s^{\prime}_{3}},...,L^{2}_{k},r^{2}_{l},r^{2}_{l+1},...,r^{2}_{n}

the centered rectangle paths in
\overline{\mathcal{P}_{2}}
associated to
C_{1}
and
C_{2}
respectively. We would like to show that there exists
p^{2}
a boundary arc point in
\overline{\mathcal{P}_{2}}
such that
(L^{2}_{0},L^{2}_{1}...,L^{2}_{k})

(
resp.
(L^{2}_{0},L^{2}_{3}...,L^{2}_{k})
)
is part of the positive (resp. negative) cycle of
p^{2}
starting from
L^{2}_{0}
.

(L^{2}_{0},L^{2}_{1}...,L^{2}_{k})
is part of a positive cycle in
\overline{\mathcal{P}_{2}}

Let
s^{1}_{0}
be the uppermost stable boundary component of
L_{0}^{1}
. Notice that by our previous hypotheses
p^{1}\in s^{1}_{0}
and also by our definition of a positive cycle,
L_{1}^{1}
is an
s^{1}_{0}
-crossing predecessor of
L_{0}^{1}
containing
p^{1}
in its leftmost unstable boundary. Let us show that
L^{2}_{1}
is an
s^{2}_{0}
-crossing predecessor of
L_{0}^{2}
, where
s^{2}_{0}
is the uppermost stable boundary component of
L_{0}^{2}
(we use here our choice of orientation of the unstable foliation in
\overline{\mathcal{P}_{2}}
fixed in the beginning of this section).

Indeed, using the geometric interpretation of
\mathcal{G}
, denote by
R^{\mathcal{G}}
the rectangle in the geometric type
\mathcal{G}
associated to
R\in\overline{\mathcal{R}_{1}}
or
R\in\overline{\mathcal{R}_{2}}
(see Definition
4.7
). By our definition of an
s_{0}^{1}
-crossing predecessor and thanks to Definition
9.17
,
L^{1}_{01}
is a predecessor of
L^{1}_{0}
that does not cross
s_{0}^{1}
. Recall that every successor of
L^{1}_{01}
corresponds to a unique horizontal subrectangle of
(L^{1}_{01})^{\mathcal{G}}
. By our previous arguments and depending on our choice of representatives (which determines the vertical and horizontal orientations of the rectangles of
\mathcal{G}
),
L^{1}_{0}
corresponds to the highermost or lowermost horizontal subrectangle of
(L^{1}_{01})^{G}
and
s_{0}^{1}\cap L^{1}_{01}
to the highermost or lowermost respectively stable boundary component of
(L^{1}_{01})^{\mathcal{G}}
. Therefore, by our definition of the associated rectangle path,
L^{2}_{01}
corresponds to a predecessor of
L^{2}_{0}
that does not cross
s_{0}^{2}
. Similarly, for every
i\in\llbracket 2,s(0)\rrbracket
, we get that
L^{2}_{0i}
is a predecessor of
L^{2}_{0(i-1)}
that does not cross
s_{0}^{2}\cap L^{2}_{0(i-1)}
and that
L_{1}^{2}
is an
s_{0}^{2}
-crossing predecessor of
L^{2}_{0}
.

We will associate to
p^{1}
the unique point of intersection, say
p^{2}\in\overline{\mathcal{P}_{2}}
, of
s^{2}_{0}
with the leftmost unstable boundary component of
L_{1}^{2}
. Notice that, since
L_{1}^{1}
is not the leftmost
s^{1}_{0}
-crossing predecessor of
L_{0}^{1}
(
L^{1}_{3}
is an
s^{1}_{0}
-crossing predecessor of
L_{0}^{1}
at the left of
L_{1}^{1}
, see Figure
9.3
), neither is
L_{1}^{2}
. Hence,
p^{2}
is not a corner point of
L_{0}^{2}
and
L_{0}^{2}
contains a germ of the
(-,-)
and
(+,-)
quadrants of
p^{2}
.

By a similar argument, one can prove that
L_{2}^{2}
is a crossing successor of
L_{1}^{2}
in
\overline{\mathcal{P}_{2}}
, such that
p^{2}\in\partial L_{1}^{2}\cap\partial L_{2}^{2}
and
\overset{\circ}{L_{0}^{2}}\cap\overset{\circ}{L_{2}^{2}}=\emptyset
and by a finite repetition of this argument that
(L^{2}_{0},L^{2}_{1}...,L^{2}_{k})
is part of the positive cycle of
p^{2}
starting from
L^{2}_{0}
. Similarly, we also get that
(L^{2}_{0},L^{2}_{3}...,L^{2}_{k})
is part of the negative cycle of
p^{2}
starting from
L^{2}_{0}
.

End of the proof of the theorem

By our previous arguments, the rectangle paths
C_{1}
and
C_{2}
are respectively associated two rectangle paths in
\overline{\mathcal{P}_{2}}
of the form:

r^{2}_{0},...,r^{2}_{k},L^{2}_{0},L^{2}_{01},...,L^{2}_{0s(0)},L^{2}_{1},L^{2}_{11},...,L^{2}_{1s(1)},...,L^{2}_{k},r^{2}_{l},r^{2}_{l+1},...,r^{2}_{n}

r^{2}_{0},...,r^{2}_{k},L^{2}_{0},L^{\prime 2}_{01},...,L^{\prime 2}_{0s^{\prime}_{0}},L^{2}_{3},L^{\prime 2}_{31},...,L^{\prime 2}_{3s^{\prime}_{3}},...,L^{2}_{k},r^{2}_{l},r^{2}_{l+1},...,r^{2}_{n}

where
(L^{2}_{0},L^{2}_{1},L^{2}_{2},L^{2}_{3},L^{2}_{4})
and
(L^{2}_{0},L^{2}_{3},L^{2}_{2},L^{2}_{1},L^{2}_{4})
are the two cycles starting from
L^{2}_{0}
around
p^{2}\in L^{2}_{0}
, the path
L^{2}_{0},L^{2}_{01},...,L^{2}_{0s_{0}},L^{2}_{1},L^{2}_{11},...,L^{2}_{1s_{1}},...,L^{2}_{k}
is the rectangle path associated to the generalized rectangle path
(L^{2}_{0},L^{2}_{1},...,L^{2}_{k})
and
L^{2}_{0},L^{\prime 2}_{01},...,L^{\prime 2}_{0s^{\prime}(0)},L^{2}_{3},L^{\prime 2}_{31},

...,L^{\prime 2}_{3s^{\prime}_{3}},...,L^{2}_{k}
is the rectangle path associated to the generalized rectangle path
(L^{2}_{0},L^{2}_{3},...,L^{2}_{k})
. By Definition
9.18
, the previous rectangle paths are homotopic by a homotopy of type
\mathcal{C}
, which gives us the desired result.
∎

9.4.
Proof of Theorem
9.20

Fix
\rho:G\rightarrow\text{Homeo}(\mathcal{P})
an orientation preserving strong Markovian action, preserving the pair of singular foliations
\mathcal{F}^{s}
and
\mathcal{F}^{u}
and leaving invariant a strong Markovian family
\mathcal{R}
. We denote by
\Gamma
the boundary periodic point of
\mathcal{R}
, by
\overline{\mathcal{P}}
the bifoliated plane of
\rho
up to surgeries on
\Gamma
, by
\overline{\mathcal{R}}
the lift of
\mathcal{R}
on
\overline{\mathcal{P}}
, by
\overline{\Gamma}
the lift of
\Gamma
on
\overline{\mathcal{P}}
and by
\overline{\rho}:\overline{G}\rightarrow\text{Homeo}(\overline{\mathcal{P}})
the lift of the action
\rho
on
\overline{\mathcal{P}}
. Consider
\overline{\mathcal{F}^{s}}
and
\overline{\mathcal{F}^{u}}
the stable and unstable foliations of
\overline{\rho}
endowed with an orientation.

Our main goal in this section consists in showing Theorem
9.20
, the proof of which will be split in two parts:

(1)

Proving Theorem
9.20
in the case of rectangle paths that can be associated to simple closed polygonal curves:

Proposition 9.21
.

Let
\gamma
be a simple closed good polygonal curve in
\overline{\mathcal{P}}
and
R_{0},..,R_{n}
one of its associated rectangle paths.

•

If
R_{0}=R_{n}
, then
R_{0},...,R_{n}
is homotopic to the trivial rectangle path.

•

If
R_{n}
is a successor or predecessor of generation
k
of
R_{0}
and
R_{0}=r_{0},r_{1},..r_{k}=R_{n}
is the unique monotonous rectangle path from
R_{0}
to
R_{n}
(see Lemma
4.5
), then
R_{0},...,R_{n}
is homotopic to
r_{0},...,r_{k}
.

(2)

Proposition
9.21
implies Theorem
9.20

Proof of Proposition
9.21

We will prove Proposition
9.21
by induction.

Let
\gamma:[0,1]\rightarrow\overline{\mathcal{P}}
be a simple good closed polygonal curve,
R_{0}\in\overline{\mathcal{R}}
such that
\gamma(0)\in\overset{\circ}{R_{0}}
,
R_{0},...,R_{n}
the rectangle path starting from
R_{0}
associated to
\gamma
and
M
be the number of boundary arc points of
\overline{\mathcal{R}}
in the interior of
\gamma
(see Definition
9.2
). Notice that since
\gamma
is good, by Remark
5.9
applied for
\overline{\mathcal{R}}
, it can not contain any boundary arc or boundary periodic points; hence, the number of boundary arc points in the closure of the interior of
\gamma
is also equal to
M
.

Let us show that
M<\infty
. Indeed, by compactness, we can cover the closure of the interior of
\gamma
by a finite number of rectangles of our Markovian family
\overline{\mathcal{R}}
. By Lemmas
5.5
,
5.6
and Remark
5.9
applied for
\overline{\mathcal{R}}
, the number of boundary arc points in the closure of the interior of
\gamma
is finite except if a point of
\overline{\Gamma}
lies in the interior of
\gamma
. This is impossible, since
\gamma
is good and no loop in
\overline{\mathcal{P}}-\overline{\Gamma}
can go around a point in
\overline{\Gamma}
(see Remark
9.3
).

Let
l(\gamma)
be the length of the good polygonal curve
\gamma
. We will apply an induction on the well ordered set of couples
(M,l)\in\mathbb{N}\times\mathbb{N}
endowed with the lexicographic order:
(M_{1},l_{1})<(M_{2},l_{2})
if and only if
M_{1}<M_{2}
or
M_{1}=M_{2}
and
l_{1}<l_{2}
. At every step of our induction, we will perform a finite sequence of homotopies of type
\mathcal{A},\mathcal{B}
or
\mathcal{C}
on
\gamma
-therefore also on its associated rectangle path-, that will produce a new simple, good and closed polygonal curve
\gamma^{\prime}
such that
(M(\gamma^{\prime}),l(\gamma^{\prime}))<(M(\gamma),l(\gamma))
.

Notice that any simple and good polygonal curve has length at least equal to 4. Hence, the shortest such curves bound a rectangle in
\overline{\mathcal{P}}
. We will therefore initialize our induction by considering the case where
\gamma
bounds a rectangle containing no boundary arc points in its interior.

Let us first fix some notations. Recall that
R_{0},...,R_{n}
is a rectangle path associated to
\gamma
and that by Remark
8.17
, there exists
0=c_{0}<c_{1}<...c_{n+1}=1
a
(n+2)
-uple such that
\gamma([c_{i},c_{i+1}])\subset R_{i}
and a function
\textit{Rect}_{\gamma,R_{0}}
associated to
c_{0}<c_{1}<...c_{n+1}
sending points of
[0,1]
to rectangles in
\{R_{0},...,R_{n}\}
. Fix such a collection of
c_{i}
satisfying the results of Lemma
8.19
. Next, for the sake of simplicity, for any interval
I\subset[0,1]
if
A=\gamma(I)
, we will denote
\textit{Rect}_{\gamma,R_{0}}(I)
also by
\textit{Rect}_{\gamma,R_{0}}(A)
. By following the rectangles associated to the different points in
I
(see Convention
8.18
), we will think of
\textit{Rect}_{\gamma,R_{0}}(A)=\textit{Rect}_{\gamma,R_{0}}(I)
as a rectangle path, instead of just a set of rectangles.

Initializing the induction

Assume that
(M(\gamma),l(\gamma))=(0,4)
. Otherwise said, assume that the simple and good polygonal curve
\gamma
bounds a rectangle in
\overline{\mathcal{P}}
containing no boundary arc points in its interior. We will show that under this hypothesis, the rectangle path
R_{0},...,R_{n}
is either trivial or monotonous, which gives us the desired result.

By Remark
8.11
,
\gamma(0)=\gamma(1)
is a corner of the rectangle
\gamma
. Let us denote by
u^{\prime},s,u,s^{\prime}
the four sides of
\gamma
, following the order in which
\gamma
visits them.

We will associate to one of the previous segments of
\gamma
, say
S
, the number
0
if the rectangle path
\textit{Rect}_{\gamma,R_{0}}(S)
is trivial and the number
1
if not. We write
S\rightarrow 0
in the first case and
S\rightarrow 1
in the latter. In this way, we can associate to
\gamma
a unique element of
\{0,1\}^{4}
. Our choice of
c_{i}
(see Lemma
8.19
) and our method for associating rectangle paths to curves (see Definition
8.14
) restrict the elements of
\{0,1\}^{4}
that can be associated to
\gamma
.

Lemma 9.22
.

If
\gamma
bounds a rectangle, then the only elements of
\{0,1\}^{4}
that can be associated to
\gamma
are
(0,0,0,0),(0,1,1,1)
,
(0,1,0,0),(1,0,0,0)
and
(1,1,1,1)
. Furthermore,
\gamma
is associated to
(0,0,0,0),(0,1,1,1)
or
(0,1,0,0)
if and only if it contains no boundary arc points in its interior.

Proof.

Without any loss of generality, let us assume that
u,u^{\prime}
are unstable segments and
s,s^{\prime}
are stable segments. Recall that,
R_{0},R_{1},...,R_{n}
, the rectangle path starting from
R_{0}
associated to
\gamma
, can be obtained by the juxtaposition of
\textit{Rect}_{\gamma,R_{0}}(u^{\prime})
, followed by
\textit{Rect}_{\gamma,R_{0}}(s)
, followed by
\textit{Rect}_{\gamma,R_{0}}(u)
and finally followed by
\textit{Rect}_{\gamma,R_{0}}(s^{\prime})
.

If
(u^{\prime},s)\rightarrow(0,0)
, then by our choice of
c_{i}
(see Lemma
8.19
), the rectangle path starting from
R_{0}
associated to the juxtaposition of
u^{\prime}
followed by
s
is trivial and therefore, the segments
u^{\prime}
and
s
do not exit
R_{0}
. Since
R_{0}
is trivially bifoliated and
\gamma
bounds a rectangle, we also have that
s^{\prime},u\subset R_{0}
. Therefore, in this case,
\gamma
does not exit
R_{0}
at all and its associated element in
\{0,1\}^{4}
is
(0,0,0,0)
(recall that
the rectangle path associated to
\gamma
starting from
R_{0}
is not trivial if and only if
\gamma
exits
R_{0}
).

Suppose now that
(u^{\prime},s)\rightarrow(1,1)
. Endow
[0,1]
with the left to right orientation. Endow also
u^{\prime}
and
s
with the orientation given by
\gamma:[0,1]\rightarrow\overline{\mathcal{P}}
and assume without any loss of generality that the segments
u^{\prime}
and
s
are positively oriented for our choice of orientations of
\overline{\mathcal{F}^{s,u}}
given in the beginning of this section. In the following lines, for any rectangle
R\in\overline{\mathcal{R}}
, using the orientations of
\overline{\mathcal{F}^{s,u}}
, we will denote by
\partial^{s}_{+}R
(resp.
\partial^{s}_{-}R
) its upper (resp. lower) stable boundary. We similarly define
\partial^{u}_{+}R
and
\partial^{u}_{-}R
.

Thanks to the fact that
u^{\prime}\rightarrow 1
and
s\rightarrow 1
, to our choice of
c_{i}
and to our definition of
\textit{Rect}_{\gamma,R_{0}}
, we have that

•

u^{\prime}
exits
R_{0}
. Since
u^{\prime}
is positively oriented, we can suppose that
u^{\prime}
will exit
R_{0}
in order to visit
R_{u_{1}}
, a
\partial^{s}_{+}R_{0}
-crossing predecessor of
R_{0}
, then it will exit
R_{u_{1}}
in order to visit
R_{u_{2}}
, a
\partial^{s}_{+}R_{u_{1}}
-crossing predecessor of
R_{u_{1}}
, and so on, until it exits
R_{u_{N-1}}
and visits
R_{u_{N}}:=\textit{Rect}_{\gamma,R_{0}}(u^{\prime}\cap s)
, a predecessor of some generation of
R_{0}
that contains
u^{\prime}

•

\textit{Rect}_{\gamma,R_{0}}(u^{\prime})=R_{0},R_{1},...,R_{u_{1}},...,R_{u_{2}},...R_{u_{N}}

•

Similarly,
s
exits
R_{u_{N}}
. Since
s
is positively oriented, we can suppose that
s
will exit
R_{u_{N}}
in order to visit
R_{u_{N+1}}
, a
\partial^{u}_{+}R_{u_{N}}
-crossing successor of
R_{u_{N}}
, next
s
will exit
R_{u_{N+1}}
in order to visit a
\partial^{u}_{+}R_{u_{N+1}}
-crossing successor of
R_{u_{N+1}}
, and so on until it exits
R_{u_{m-1}}
and visits
R_{u_{m}}:=\textit{Rect}_{\gamma,R_{0}}(s\cap u)
(see Figure
9.5
), a successor of some generation of
R_{u_{N}}
containing
s
.

•

\textit{Rect}_{\gamma,R_{0}}(s)=R_{u_{N}},...,R_{u_{N}+1},...,R_{u_{m}}

Notice that since
u^{\prime}
exits
R_{u_{N-1}}
, thanks to Lemma
4.5
, we have that
R_{u_{N+1}}
and
R_{u_{N-1}}
correspond two successors (of some generation) of
R_{u_{N}}
with disjoint interiors. Also,
R_{u_{m}}
(resp.
R_{0}
) is a successor of some generation of
R_{u_{N+1}}
(resp.
R_{u_{N-1}}
). By the previous facts and Lemma
4.6
applied for
\overline{\mathcal{R}}
(see also Remark
8.2
),
\overset{\circ}{R_{u_{m}}}\cap\overset{\circ}{R_{0}}=\emptyset
. Consequently, if
u
does not exit
R_{u_{m}}
, then the curve
\gamma
cannot bound a rectangle. Indeed, this would imply that the stable leaf containing
s^{\prime}
intersects
R_{u_{N}}
along more than one connected components, which would contradict Item (5) of Proposition
2.62
. Hence, as before,
u
exits
R_{u_{m}}
in order to visit
R_{u_{m+1}}
, a
\partial^{s}_{-}R_{u_{m}}
-crossing predecessor of
R_{u_{m}}
, next
u
exits
R_{u_{m+1}}
in order to visit a crossing predecessor of
R_{u_{m+1}}
and so on until it exits
R_{u_{k-1}}
and visits
R_{u_{k}}:=\textit{Rect}_{\gamma,R_{0}}(u\cap s^{\prime})
, a predecessor of some generation of
R_{u_{m}}
that contains
u
. By the same arguments as before,
\overset{\circ}{R_{u_{k}}}\cap\overset{\circ}{R_{u_{N}}}=\emptyset
; hence
s^{\prime}
has to exit
R_{u_{k}}
in order to reach
\gamma(0)\in\overset{\circ}{R_{u_{N}}}
. We conclude that if
(u^{\prime},s)\rightarrow(1,1)
, then the element of
\{0,1\}^{4}
associated to
(u^{\prime},s,u,s^{\prime})
is
(1,1,1,1)
.

Figure 9.5
.

If
(u^{\prime},s)\rightarrow(1,0)
, then as before
u^{\prime}
exits
R_{0}
in order to visit
R_{u_{1}}
, a crossing predecessor of
R_{0}
, next
u^{\prime}
exits
R_{u_{1}}
in order to visit a crossing predecessor of
R_{u_{1}}
and so on until it exits
R_{u_{N-1}}
and visits
R_{u_{N}}:=\textit{Rect}_{\gamma,R_{0}}(u^{\prime}\cap s)
a predecessor of some generation of
R_{0}
that contains the entire segment
u^{\prime}
. Since, by hypothesis,
s
does not exit
R_{u_{N}}
and
\gamma
bounds a rectangle,
u,s^{\prime}
do not exit
R_{u_{N}}
either (once again we use here Item (5) of Proposition
2.62
). Therefore, if
(u^{\prime},s)\rightarrow(1,0)
, then the element of
\{0,1\}^{4}
associated to
(u^{\prime},s,u,s^{\prime})
is
(1,0,0,0)
.

We show in a similar way that if
(u^{\prime},s)\rightarrow(0,1)
, then the element of
\{0,1\}^{4}
associated to
(u^{\prime},s,u,s^{\prime})
is either
(0,1,0,0)
or
(0,1,1,1)
.

Finally, notice that when
\gamma
is associated to
(0,0,0,0),(1,0,0,0)
or
(0,1,0,0)
, then by our previous arguments (see cases
(u^{\prime},s)\rightarrow(0,0)
and
(u^{\prime},s)\rightarrow(1,0)
),
\gamma
is contained in the interior of a rectangle of
\overline{\mathcal{R}}
. Hence, its interior cannot contain boundary arc points. Let us now show that if
\gamma
is associated to either
(1,1,1,1)
or
(0,1,1,1)
, then the interior of
\gamma
necessarily contains boundary arc points. Assume that
(u^{\prime},s,u,s^{\prime})\rightarrow(1,1,1,1)
(the case
(0,1,1,1)
can be treated in a similar way). Following our previous notations (see the case
(u^{\prime},s)\rightarrow(1,1)
),
u^{\prime}\subset\overset{\circ}{R_{u_{N}}}
,
s
exits
R_{u_{N}}
in order to visit
R_{u_{N+1}}
, a
\partial^{u}_{+}R_{u_{N}}
-crossing successor of
R_{u_{N}}
and
\overset{\circ}{R_{u_{N-1}}}\cap\overset{\circ}{R_{u_{N+1}}}=\emptyset
. Since
\gamma
bounds a rectangle, its interior contains the point
\partial^{u}_{+}R_{u_{N}}\cap\partial^{s}_{-}R_{u_{N+1}}
(see Figure
9.5
), which is a boundary arc point according to Remark
5.9
.
∎

We are now ready to describe the initialization of our induction, in which we suppose
\gamma
to be the boundary of a rectangle, whose interior contains no boundary arc points of
\overline{\mathcal{R}}
. By the previous lemma, we will consider the following 3 cases:

(1)

\gamma
is associated to
(0,0,0,0)
: in this case, by our proof of Lemma
9.22
, we have that
\gamma
never exits
R_{0}
; its associated rectangle path is therefore trivial.

(2)

\gamma
is associated to
(1,0,0,0)
: in this case, following the notations of Lemma
9.22
(see the case where
(u^{\prime},s)\rightarrow(1,0)
),
u^{\prime}
exits
R_{0}
in order to visit
R_{u_{1}}
, a crossing predecessor of
R_{0}
, next
u^{\prime}
exits
R_{u_{1}}
in order to visit a crossing predecessor of
R_{u_{1}}
and so on until it exits
R_{u_{N-1}}
and visits
R_{u_{N}}:=\textit{Rect}_{\gamma,R_{0}}(u^{\prime}\cap s)
a predecessor of some generation of
R_{0}
that, as we have previously shown, contains completely
u^{\prime},s,u,s^{\prime}
. It follows that the rectangle path starting from
R_{0}
associated to
\gamma
coincides with the unique decreasing rectangle path from
R_{0}
to
R_{u_{N}}
.

(3)

\gamma
is associated to
(0,1,0,0)
: in this case, similarly to the previous case, one can show that the rectangle path starting from
R_{0}
associated to
\gamma
is increasing.

We deduce that if
(M(\gamma),l(\gamma))=(0,4)
, then Proposition
9.21
is true. Let us now describe the step of our induction.

Induction step: the case where
l(\gamma)=4
and
M(\gamma)>0

Following our previous notations, thanks to Lemma
9.22
, the element of
\{0,1\}^{4}
associated to
\gamma
is either
(1,1,1,1)
or
(0,1,1,1)
. Suppose first that
\gamma
corresponds to
(1,1,1,1)
. Assume once again, without any loss of generality, that the (oriented by
\gamma
) segments
u^{\prime}
and
s
are positively oriented for our choice of orientation of
\overline{\mathcal{F}^{s,u}}
.

Figure 9.6
.

During our proof of Lemma
9.22
, we established that
u^{\prime}
will exit
R_{0}
in order to visit
R_{u_{1}}
, a
\partial^{s}_{+}R_{0}
-crossing predecessor of
R_{0}
, then it will exit
R_{u_{1}}
in order to visit
R_{u_{2}}
, a
\partial^{s}_{+}R_{u_{1}}
-crossing predecessor of
R_{u_{1}}
, and so on, until it exits
R_{u_{N-1}}
and visits
R_{u_{N}}
, a predecessor of some generation of
R_{0}
containing
u^{\prime}
. We also showed that
s
exits
R_{u_{N}}
in order to visit
R_{u_{N+1}}
and that
\partial^{u}_{+}R_{u_{N}}\cap\partial^{s}_{-}R_{u_{N+1}}
is a boundary arc point contained in the interior of
\gamma
(see Figure
9.6
). Consider the negative cycle
(R_{u_{N}},R_{u_{N+1}},L_{2},L_{3},L_{4})
around the previous boundary arc point starting from
R_{u_{N}}
. Using the previous cycle, we can deform
\gamma
by a homotopy of type
\mathcal{C}
to a good and simple polygonal curve
\gamma^{\prime}
containing strictly less boundary arc points in its interior (see Figure
9.6
).

We treat the case where
\gamma
corresponds to
(0,1,1,1)
in a similar way.

Induction step in the general case

We will assume here that
l(\gamma)>4
. In this case, we begin by choosing one stable or unstable tangency
s
such that :

(1)

s
is of type (a) or (b) and has a complete domain
Dom
(see Lemma
9.7
)

(2)

\gamma(0)\notin Dom
or
\gamma(0)
is a corner point of the rectangle
Dom
that is not in
s

(3)

the interior of one side of
\partial Dom
is disjoint from
\gamma

The existence of such a tangency is proven in the following lemma. We will call a tangency with the above properties, a tangency with
property
(\star)
. If furthermore
\gamma(0)\notin Dom
, we will say that the tangency satisfies the
strong property
(\star)
.

Lemma 9.23
.

Let
\gamma
be a good, simple and closed polygonal curve of length strictly greater than 4. There exists
s
a stable or unstable tangency of
\gamma
with property
(\star)
.

Proof.

Indeed, take
s
to be a stable tangency of
\gamma
of type (a) or (b) (the existence of at least 2 of such tangencies is assured by Lemma
9.5
). Suppose that the domain of
s
, that we will denote by
Dom
, is not complete. In that case, by Remark
9.8
, there exists a stable segment
s^{\prime}
of
\gamma
intersecting the interior of the stable boundary component of
Dom
that is not
s
. Since
\overset{\circ}{Dom}\cap\gamma=\emptyset
and
Dom
is incomplete,
s^{\prime}
is a stable tangency of
\gamma
of type (c) or (d). Therefore, to every tangency of
\gamma
of type (a) or (b) with an incomplete domain we can injectively associate a tangency of type (c) or (d). We deduce by Lemma
9.5
that there exist at least two stable tangencies of type (a) or (b) for
\gamma
, whose domains are complete.

Consider now
s
a stable tangency of
\gamma
of type (a) or (b) with a complete domain
Dom
. The function
\gamma:\mathbb{S}^{1}\rightarrow\overline{\mathcal{P}}
induces a cyclic order on the stable/unstable segments forming
\gamma
. Denote by
u
(resp.
u^{\prime}
) the unstable segment of
\gamma
after (resp. before)
s
and
s^{\prime}
the stable boundary component of
Dom
that is not
s
(see Figure
9.7
). By definition, the domain of
s
satisfies,
\overset{\circ}{Dom}\cap\gamma=\emptyset
, hence if
\gamma(0)\in Dom
then
\gamma(0)\in\partial Dom
.

If
\gamma(0)\in Dom
, then by Remark
8.11
,
\gamma(0)
cannot belong to the interior of
s
,
u
or
u^{\prime}
. Therefore,
\gamma(0)\in s^{\prime}
or
\gamma(0)\in\partial s
. Since there are at least 2 stable tangencies of
\gamma
of type (a) or (b) with a complete domain, we can assume without any loss of generality that
\gamma(0)\notin s
.

Figure 9.7
.

Assume now that the interior of
s^{\prime}
intersects
\gamma
. Assume without any loss of generality that
u\subset Dom
(since
Dom
is complete). Since
\gamma
is good, any two stable sides of
\gamma
do not belong to the same stable leaf of
\overline{\mathcal{F}^{s}}
. Therefore, we have necessarily that the stable segment of
\gamma
after
u
, say
S
, is contained in
s^{\prime}
. Since
\gamma
does not bound a rectangle
S\subsetneq s^{\prime}
; we are therefore in the situation of Figure
9.7
. Hence,
u
is an unstable tangency of type (a) or (b) with a complete domain. If
\gamma(0)\notin u
, then
u
has property
(\star)
. If
\gamma(0)\in u
, then take
s_{fin}\neq s
to be another stable tangency of
\gamma
of type (a) or (b) with a complete domain (we showed previously that there are at least 2 such tangencies) and notice that
\gamma(0)\notin s_{fin}
. If
s_{fin}
does not satisfy
(\star)
, then by repeating the arguments of this paragraph, either the unstable segment of
\gamma
before or after
s_{fin}
is an unstable tangency of
\gamma
with property
(\star)
.
∎

Figure 9.8
.
This simple, good, closed polygonal curve does not admit stable or unstable tangencies with strong property
(\star)

Even when
\gamma
does not bound a rectangle, it is not always possible to find a tangency with strong property
(\star)
(see for instance Figure
9.8
). However, there exist numerous cases where this is possible:

Lemma 9.24
.

Let
\gamma
be a simple, closed and good polygonal curve that does not bound a rectangle. Assume that
\gamma(0)
belongs to a stable or unstable tangency of
\gamma
of type (a) or (b). Then there exists a stable or unstable tangency of
\gamma
with strong property
(\star)
.

Proof.

Indeed, assume without any loss of generality that
\gamma(0)\in S
, where
S
is a stable tangency of
\gamma
of type (a) or (b). By our proof of Lemma
9.23
, there exists
s
a stable tangency of
\gamma
of type (a) or (b) with a complete domain
Dom
such that
\gamma(0)\notin s
. Notice that
Dom
cannot contain
\gamma(0)
, since the domain of a stable tangency of type (a) or (b) cannot intersect another stable tangency of type (a) or (b), except when
\gamma
bounds a rectangle. By our proof Lemma
9.23
, either
s
has strong property
(\star)
or we are in the case of Figure
9.7
. In the latter case, using the notations of Figure
9.7
, the segment
u
has property
(\star)
and its domain
D^{\prime}\subset Dom
does not contain
\gamma(0)
; hence
u
has strong property
(\star)
.
∎

We will now describe our induction step in the following two subcases:

(1)

there exists
s
a tangency in
\gamma
with strong property
(\star)

(2)

no such tangency exists

Induction step in the case where a tangency with strong
(\star)
property exists

Assume without any loss of generality that the stable tangency
s
of
\gamma
has strong property
(\star)
. The function
\gamma:[0,1]\rightarrow\overline{\mathcal{P}}
endows the segments forming
\gamma
with a total order, for which
s
is neither the first nor the last segment (since
\gamma(0)\notin s
). Let us denote by
U^{\prime}
(resp.
U
) the unstable segment of
\gamma
before (resp. after)
s
and by
s^{\prime}
the stable side of the domain
Dom
of
s
that is not
s
. We define
u^{\prime}:=U^{\prime}\cap Dom
and
u:=U\cap Dom
. We are therefore -up to a change of orientation and up to interchanging
u^{\prime}
and

- in the case of Figure
9.9
.

Figure 9.9
.

As before, we will associate to a stable or unstable segment of
\gamma
, say
S
, the number

if
\textit{Rect}_{\gamma,R_{0}}(S)
consists of one rectangle and the number

if not. We will write

in the first case and

in the latter. We can therefore associate to
U^{\prime},s,U
a unique element of
\{0,1\}^{3}
.

We are now ready to describe the induction step in this case:

).

Let us show that the previous movement corresponds to a homotopy of type
\mathcal{A}
. Indeed, our movement pushes the segments

of

•

\textit{Rect}_{\gamma^{\prime},R_{0}}([0,a])

.
Before describing the induction step in this case, let us first prove a very useful lemma:

Lemma 9.25
.

Let
B:=L_{1},...,L_{k}
be a decreasing rectangle path. Consider any rectangle path of the form
L_{p},w_{B},L_{p^{\prime}}
, where
p,p^{\prime}\in\llbracket 1,k\rrbracket
and
w_{B}
is a rectangle path formed by rectangles in
B
. If
p^{\prime}>p
(resp.
p>p^{\prime}
,
p=p^{\prime}
), the previous rectangle path is homotopic by a sequence of homotopies of type
\mathcal{B}
to a decreasing (resp. increasing, trivial) rectangle path.

Proof.

We will show the desired result in the case where
p^{\prime}>p
. The other cases can be similarly treated. If
L_{p},w_{B},L_{p^{\prime}}
is not decreasing, we can find a non-monotonous rectangle path in
L_{p},w_{B},L_{p^{\prime}}
of length 3. The previous rectangle path is of the form
L_{k},L_{k+1},L_{k}
and can be “erased” by a homotopy of type
\mathcal{B}
. This reduces the length of
w_{B}
and thus by a finite number of homotopies of type
\mathcal{B}
, we get the desired result.
∎

(8
′
a)

Assume first that
\gamma_{|[a,1]}
eventually exits
\underset{i=l}{\overset{N}{\cup}}R_{i}
in order to visit a crossing predecessor (or successor)
L\notin\{R_{l},...,R_{N}\}
of
R_{k}\in\{R_{l},...,R_{N}\}
. By eventually changing our choice of
R_{k}
, we will assume that if
L
is a crossing predecessor (resp. successor) of
R_{k}
, then there is no predecessor (resp. successor) of
R_{k}
in
\{R_{l},...,R_{N}\}
, say
R_{p}
, such that
L
is a crossing predecessor (resp. successor) of
R_{p}
. In this case, by pushing
\gamma
along
Dom
so that
s
be identified with
s^{\prime}
(see Figure
9.10
a), we obtain a simple, good and closed polygonal curve
\gamma^{\prime}
of strictly smaller length and such that
M(\gamma)=M(\gamma^{\prime})
. Let us show that the previous movement corresponds to a sequence of homotopies of type
\mathcal{B}
.

We will assume that
U=u
(the case
U^{\prime}=u^{\prime}
follows from similar arguments). As in the Case
(2^{\prime})
, our movement pushes the segments
u^{\prime},s,u
of
\gamma
to the segment
s^{\prime}
of
\gamma^{\prime}
, while keeping
\gamma-(u^{\prime}\cup s\cup u)
fixed. By eventually reparametrizing
\gamma^{\prime}
, we can assume that
\gamma^{\prime}([a,b])=u^{\prime}_{\text{after}}\cup s^{\prime}
, where
u^{\prime}_{\text{after}}:=U^{\prime}-u^{\prime}\subset\gamma^{\prime}
. Therefore,
\gamma\equiv\gamma^{\prime}
on
[0,a]\cup[b,1]
and the rectangle paths starting from
R_{0}
associated to
\gamma_{|[0,a]}
and
\gamma^{\prime}_{|[0,a]}\equiv\gamma_{|[0,a]}
are identical. In particular, both rectangle paths end by
R_{l}
. It therefore suffices to show that the rectangle paths starting from
R_{l}
associated to
\gamma_{|[a,1]}
and
\gamma^{\prime}_{|[a,1]}
are homotopic by a sequence of homotopies of type
\mathcal{B}
.

As
u^{\prime}_{\text{after}}\cup s^{\prime}\subset\underset{i=l}{\overset{N}{\cup}}R_{i}
and
\gamma_{|[b,1]}\equiv\gamma^{\prime}_{|[b,1]}
, both
\gamma([a,1])
and
\gamma^{\prime}([a,1])
remain in

\underset{i=l}{\overset{N}{\cup}}R_{i}
until they exit this region in order to enter the crossing predecessor (or successor)
L
of
R_{k}
. Therefore, there exist
R\notin\{R_{l},...,R_{N}\}
a predecessor (resp. successor) of
R_{k}
, two rectangle paths, say
w_{A},w^{\prime}_{A}
, formed by rectangles in
\{R_{l},...,R_{N}\}
and
W,W^{\prime}
two rectangle paths such that

•

the rectangle path starting from
R_{l}
associated to
\gamma_{|[a,1]}
is
R_{l},w_{A},R_{k},R,W

•

the rectangle path starting from
R_{l}
associated to
\gamma^{\prime}_{|[a,1]}
is
R_{l},w^{\prime}_{A},R_{k},R,W^{\prime}

Since
\gamma_{|[b,1]}\equiv\gamma^{\prime}_{|[b,1]}
, the curves
\gamma
and
\gamma^{\prime}
after exiting
\underset{i=l}{\overset{N}{\cup}}R_{i}
are exactly the same; hence
W=W^{\prime}
. We thus get the desired result by remarking that
R_{l},w_{A},R_{k}
and
R_{l},w^{\prime}_{A},R_{k}
are homotopic by a sequence of homotopies of type
\mathcal{B}
, thanks to Lemma
9.25
.

(8
′
b)

Assume finally that
\gamma([a,1])\subset\underset{i=l}{\overset{N}{\cup}}R_{i}
. In this case, we will show directly, using the induction hypothesis, that the rectangle path associated to
\gamma
is homotopic to a trivial or monotonous rectangle path.

Consider
\gamma^{\prime}
the curve obtained by pushing
\gamma
along
Dom
so that
s
be identified with
s^{\prime}
. The curve
\gamma^{\prime}
is simple, good, closed and of strictly smaller length than
\gamma
. By eventually reparametrizing
\gamma^{\prime}
, assume once again that
\gamma\equiv\gamma^{\prime}
on
[0,a]\cup[b,1]
; hence the rectangle paths starting from
R_{0}
associated to
\gamma_{|[0,a]}
and
\gamma^{\prime}_{|[0,a]}
are identical. Recall that the last rectangle of the previous rectangle paths is
R_{l}
. Since
\gamma([a,1]),\gamma^{\prime}([a,1])\subset\underset{i=l}{\overset{N}{\cup}}R_{i}
, there exist
R_{k},R_{k^{\prime}}\in\{R_{l},...,R_{N}\}
and
w_{A},w^{\prime}_{A}
rectangle paths formed by rectangles in
\{R_{l},...,R_{N}\}
such that the rectangle paths starting from
R_{l}
associated to
\gamma^{\prime}_{|[a,1]}
and
\gamma_{|[a,1]}
are respectively
R_{l},w^{\prime}_{A},R_{k^{\prime}}
and
R_{l},w_{A},R_{k}
. Notice that
R_{k}
is not necessarily equal to
R_{k^{\prime}}
and thus the rectangle paths starting from
R_{0}
associated to
\gamma
and
\gamma^{\prime}
are not homotopic. However, it is true that
\overset{\circ}{R_{k}}\cap\overset{\circ}{R_{k^{\prime}}}\neq\emptyset
, since
\gamma(1)=\gamma^{\prime}(1)
.

Assume without any loss of generality that
R_{k^{\prime}}
is a predecessor of some generation of
R_{0}
(recall that
\gamma(0)=\gamma(1)\in\overset{\circ}{R_{0}}
) and apply the induction hypothesis for
\gamma^{\prime}
, which verifies
(M(\gamma^{\prime}),l(\gamma^{\prime}))<(M(\gamma),l(\gamma))
. We have that the rectangle path associated to
\gamma^{\prime}
is homotopic to the decreasing path
R_{0}=r_{0}^{\prime},r^{\prime}_{1},...,r^{\prime}_{p}=R_{k^{\prime}}
. Consider the rectangle path associated to
\gamma
, which is of the form:

R_{0},R_{1},...,R_{l},w_{A},R_{k}

By Lemma
9.25
, the previous rectangle path is homotopic by a sequence of homotopies of type
\mathcal{B}
to the rectangle path

R_{0},R_{1},...,R_{l},w^{\prime}_{A},R_{k^{\prime}},w^{\prime-1}_{A},R_{l},w_{A},R_{k}

where
w^{\prime-1}_{A}
is the rectangle path
w_{A}^{\prime}
followed from its end to its beginning. The first part of this rectangle path is by hypothesis homotopic to the decreasing rectangle path
R_{0}=r_{0}^{\prime},r^{\prime}_{1},...,r^{\prime}_{p}=R_{k^{\prime}}
. Therefore, the previous rectangle path is homotopic to

R_{0}=r_{0}^{\prime},r^{\prime}_{1},...,r^{\prime}_{p}=R_{k^{\prime}},w^{\prime-1}_{A},R_{l},w_{A},R_{k}

Recall that the second part of this rectangle path consists only of rectangles in
\{R_{l},...,R_{N}\}
and that
\overset{\circ}{R_{k}}
intersects
\overset{\circ}{R_{k^{\prime}}}
. If
R_{k}=R_{k^{\prime}}
, then by Lemma
9.25
the path
R_{k^{\prime}},w^{\prime-1}_{A},R_{l},w_{A},R_{k}
is homotopic to a trivial path and therefore
the rectangle path associated to
\gamma
is homotopic to the decreasing path
R_{0},r^{\prime}_{1},...,r^{\prime}_{p}=R_{k}
, which gives us the desired result. If
R_{k}
is a predecessor of some generation of
R_{k^{\prime}}
, then by Lemma
9.25
,
R_{k^{\prime}},w^{\prime-1}_{A},R_{l},w_{A},R_{k}
is homotopic to the decreasing rectangle path
r^{\prime}_{p}=R_{k^{\prime}},r^{\prime}_{p+1},...,r^{\prime}_{p+s}=R_{k}
. We deduce in this case that the rectangle path associated to
\gamma
is homotopic to the decreasing rectangle path
R_{0}=r_{0}^{\prime},r^{\prime}_{1},...,r^{\prime}_{p},r^{\prime}_{p+1},...,r^{\prime}_{p+s}=R_{k}
, which gives us the desired result. Finally, assume that
R_{k}
is a successor of some generation of
R_{k^{\prime}}
. By the same argument, the rectangle path associated to
\gamma
is homotopic to the concatenation of the decreasing rectangle path
R_{0},r^{\prime}_{1},...,r^{\prime}_{p}=R_{k^{\prime}}
and of an increasing rectangle path of the form
r^{\prime}_{p}=R_{k^{\prime}},r^{\prime}_{p+1},...,r^{\prime}_{p+s}=R_{k}
. However, by Lemma
4.5
,
r^{\prime}_{p}=R_{k^{\prime}},r^{\prime}_{p+1},...,r^{\prime}_{p+s}=R_{k}
(resp.
r^{\prime}_{p}=R_{k^{\prime}},r^{\prime}_{p-1}...,r^{\prime}_{1},R_{0}
) is the unique increasing rectangle path from
r^{\prime}_{p}
to
R_{k}
(from
r^{\prime}_{p}
to
R_{0}
). As
\overset{\circ}{R_{k}}\cap\overset{\circ}{R_{0}}\neq\emptyset
, thanks to Lemma
4.6
applied for
\overline{\mathcal{R}}
, we get that
r^{\prime}_{p+1}=r^{\prime}_{p-1}
. Therefore, by a homotopy of type
\mathcal{B}
, we can decrease the length of the path
R_{0}=r_{0}^{\prime},r^{\prime}_{1},...r^{\prime}_{p-1},r^{\prime}_{p},r^{\prime}_{p+1},...,r^{\prime}_{p+s}=R_{k}
and we can keep doing this until we obtain an increasing, decreasing or trivial rectangle path, which gives us the desired result.

Induction step in the case where no tangency with strong
(\star)
property exists

Assume that the domains of all stable and unstable tangencies of
\gamma
with property
(\star)
contain
\gamma(0)
. Consider
s
a tangency with property
(\star)
and
Dom
its domain (its existence is ensured by Lemma
9.23
). We will assume without any loss of generality that
s
is a stable tangency. The function
\gamma:[0,1]\rightarrow\overline{\mathcal{P}}
induces a cyclic order on the stable and unstable segments forming
\gamma
. Let us denote once again by
U^{\prime}
(resp.
U
) the unstable segment of
\gamma
before (resp. after)
s
, by
s^{\prime}
the stable side of
Dom
that is not
s
,
u^{\prime}:=U^{\prime}\cap Dom
and
u:=U\cap Dom
. Recall that by definition of property
(\star)
,
\gamma(0)\notin s
and
\gamma(0)
is a corner point of
Dom
, therefore
\gamma(0)\in s^{\prime}\cap U^{\prime}
or
\gamma(0)\in s^{\prime}\cap U
.

Lemma 9.26
.

Let
R_{0}
and
R_{n}
be the first and last rectangles of the rectangle path associated to
\gamma
. If
\gamma(0)\in U^{\prime}
(resp.
\gamma(0)\in U
) and
U^{\prime}\subset R_{0}\cap R_{n}
(resp.
U\subset R_{0}\cap R_{n}
), then
\gamma
is homotopic by a homotopy of type
\mathcal{A}
to a simple, closed and good polygonal curve having at least one tangency with strong property
(\star)
.

Proof.

Assume that
\gamma(0)\in U^{\prime}
. Let
\gamma^{\prime}:[0,1]\rightarrow\overline{\mathcal{P}}
be a simple, closed, good polygonal curve defined by the following properties:
\gamma^{\prime}(0)
is the unique point in
U^{\prime}\cap s
, the topological circles
\gamma([0,1])
and
\gamma^{\prime}([0,1])
coincide, and they are identically oriented by
\gamma
and
\gamma^{\prime}
. Notice that since
U^{\prime}\subset R_{0}
and
\gamma
is good, we have that
\gamma^{\prime}(0)\in\overset{\circ}{R_{0}}
. Moreover, by definition of
\gamma^{\prime}
and using the fact that
U^{\prime}\subset R_{n}
, one can easily check that the rectangle paths starting from
R_{0}
associated to the good polygonal curves
\gamma
and
\gamma^{\prime}
coincide; hence
\gamma
and
\gamma^{\prime}
are homotopic by a homotopy of type
\mathcal{A}
, which gives us the desired result by Lemma
9.24
.
∎

We are now ready to describe the induction step in this final case:

(1
′′
)

If
(U^{\prime},s,U)\rightarrow(0,0,0)
, by our argument in Case
(1^{\prime})
, we can push
\gamma
by a homotopy of type
\mathcal{A}
along
Dom
so that
s
comes arbitrarily close to
s^{\prime}
. By Lemma
9.26
, up to a homotopy of type
\mathcal{A}
,
\gamma
has a tangency with strong property
(\star)
. We deduce the induction step by applying Cases
(1^{\prime})-(8^{\prime})
.

(2
′′
)

If
(U^{\prime},s,U)\rightarrow(1,0,1)
, as we have showed in Case
(2^{\prime})
, we are in the case of Figure
9.10
b, with
u^{\prime}=U^{\prime}
and therefore
\gamma(0)\in u^{\prime}
. By our argument in (2
′
), we can push
\gamma
by a homotopy of type
\mathcal{A}
along
Dom
so that
s
comes arbitrarily close to
s^{\prime}
. By doing so
\textit{Rect}_{\gamma,R_{0}}(u^{\prime})
becomes trivial and therefore by Lemma
9.26
up to a homotopy of type
\mathcal{A}
,
\gamma
has a tangency with strong property
(\star)
. We deduce the induction step by applying Cases
(1^{\prime})-(8^{\prime})
.

(3
′′
)

If
(U^{\prime},s,U)\rightarrow(0,0,1)
, we are in the case of Figure
9.10
b, with
R_{l}=R_{l+1}=...=R_{N}
. We apply in this case the same argument as in Case
(2^{\prime\prime})
.

(4
′′
)

If
(U^{\prime},s,U)\rightarrow(1,1,0)
, then we are in the case of Figure
9.11
, where
\{R_{l},...,R_{N}\}=\textit{Rect}_{\gamma,R_{0}}(u^{\prime})
,
\{R_{N},R_{N+1},...,R_{m}\}=\textit{Rect}_{\gamma,R_{0}}(s)
,
u=U
and
\gamma(0)\in U
. By the same arguments as before, up to a homotopy of type
\mathcal{A}
,
\gamma
has a tangency with strong property
(\star)
. We deduce the induction step by applying Cases
(1^{\prime})-(8^{\prime})
.

(5
′′
)

If
(U^{\prime},s,U)\rightarrow(0,1,0)
, then we are in the case of Figure
9.11
with
R_{l}=R_{l+1}=...=R_{N}
. In this case either
\gamma(0)\in u\cap s^{\prime}
or
\gamma(0)\in u^{\prime}\cap s^{\prime}
. In both cases, by the same arguments as before, up to a homotopy of type
\mathcal{A}
,
\gamma
has a tangency with strong property
(\star)
. We deduce the induction step by applying Cases
(1^{\prime})-(8^{\prime})
.

(6
′′
)

If
(U^{\prime},s,U)\rightarrow(1,1,1)
, then we are in the case of Figure
9.12
. In this case, we can apply in the exact same way the homotopy performed in Case (6
′
).

(7
′′
)

Assume that
(U^{\prime},s,U)\rightarrow(0,1,1)
. Denote by
R_{u^{\prime}},...,R_{s}
the rectangle path
\textit{Rect}_{\gamma,R_{0}}(s)
. As we have shown in Case (
7^{\prime}
):

•

If
U^{\prime}\not\subset R_{s}
, we are in the case of Figure
9.12
. In this case, we can apply in the exact same way the homotopy performed in Case (7
′
).

•

If
U^{\prime}\subset R_{s}
, then we are in the case of Figure
9.13
. In this case,
u^{\prime}=U^{\prime}
and therefore
\gamma(0)\in u^{\prime}
. Once again, we can push
\gamma
by a homotopy of type
\mathcal{A}
along
Dom
so that
s
comes arbitrarily close to
s^{\prime}
. By Lemma
9.26
, up to a homotopy of type
\mathcal{A}
,
\gamma
has a tangency with strong property
(\star)
. We deduce the induction step by applying Cases
(1^{\prime})-(8^{\prime})
.

(8
′′
)

If
(U^{\prime},s,U)\rightarrow(1,0,0)
, we are in the case of case of Figure
9.10
a, where
\textit{Rect}_{\gamma,R_{0}}(U^{\prime})
corresponds to the non-trivial decreasing rectangle path
R_{l},...,R_{N}
. Let
a,b\in[0,1]
such that
\gamma([a,b])=U^{\prime}\cup s\cup U
.
Assume first that
\gamma(0)\in U^{\prime}\cap s^{\prime}
. In this case, we have that
R_{l}=R_{0}
.

(8
′′
a)

If
\gamma_{|[b,1]}
eventually exits
\underset{i=l}{\overset{N}{\cup}}R_{i}
in order to visit a crossing predecessor (or successor) of
R_{k}\in\{R_{l},...,R_{N}\}
, as in Case (8
′
a), we can push
\gamma
along
Dom
by a sequence of homotopies of type
\mathcal{B}
so that
s
comes arbitrarily close to
s^{\prime}
. Hence, by Lemma
9.26
up to a homotopy,
\gamma
has a tangency with strong property
(\star)
. We obtain the induction step by applying the Cases
(1^{\prime})-(8^{\prime})
.

(8
′′
b)

If
\gamma([a,1])\subset\underset{i=l}{\overset{N}{\cup}}R_{i}
, since
\gamma(0)\in U^{\prime}\cap s^{\prime}
, we get that
\gamma([0,1])\subset\underset{i=l}{\overset{N}{\cup}}R_{i}=\underset{i=0}{\overset{N}{\cup}}R_{i}
. Therefore, the rectangle path associated to
\gamma
is a
formed by rectangles in
\{R_{0},...,R_{N}\}
. The previous rectangle path is homotopic to a monotonous or trivial rectangle path, thanks to Lemma
9.25
.

(8
′′
c)

Assume now that
\gamma(0)\in s^{\prime}\cap U
. Notice that in this case, since
s
does not satisfy the strong
(\star)
property, we have that
\gamma(0)\in Dom
and thus
U\subset Dom
and
U=u
. Push
\gamma
along
Dom
so that
s
comes very close to
s^{\prime}
. By Lemma
9.26
, up to a homotopy of type
\mathcal{A}
, we can assume that this new simple, closed, good polygonal curve, say
\gamma^{\prime}
, has a tangency with strong property
(\star)
. By applying our algorithm (Cases
(1^{\prime})-(8^{\prime})
),
\gamma^{\prime}
is homotopic to a simple, good, closed polygonal curve
\gamma^{\prime\prime}
of strictly smaller length or with less boundary arc points in its interior. By the induction hypothesis, the rectangle path associated to
\gamma^{\prime\prime}
is homotopic to a monotonous or trivial path. Hence, the same applies to the rectangle path associated to
\gamma^{\prime}
. By the exact same argument as in Case
(8^{\prime}b)
, we can now conclude that the rectangle path associated to
\gamma
is homotopic to a monotonous or trivial rectangle path.

This finishes the description of our induction step. In all the previous cases, we have either defined a homotopy producing a simple, closed and good polygonal curve
\gamma^{\prime}
such that
(M(\gamma^{\prime}),l(\gamma^{\prime}))<(M(\gamma),l(\gamma))
or we have shown that
\gamma
satisfies the desired result. This finishes the proof by induction of Proposition
9.21
.

Proposition
9.21
implies Theorem
9.20

Consider
r_{0},...,r_{n}
a closed rectangle path in
\overline{\mathcal{P}}
and
\gamma
a closed and good polygonal curve associated to the previous rectangle path (the existence of such a
\gamma
is guaranteed by Proposition
8.16
). We will show that
r_{0},...,r_{n}
is homotopic to a trivial rectangle path.

By our definition of a good polygonal curve, no two stable (resp. unstable) sides of
\gamma
belong to the same leaf in
\overline{\mathcal{F}^{s}}
(resp.
\overline{\mathcal{F}^{u}}
). Consequently, every self-intersection of
\gamma
is contained in the intersection of a stable and an unstable segment of
\gamma
. By Item (4) of Proposition
2.62
, this implies that
\gamma
has only a finite number, say
M
, of self-intersections, all of which are topologically transverse. We will prove Theorem
9.20
by induction on
M
. If
M=0
, then we obtain the desired result from Proposition
9.21
. Suppose now that
M>0
and that the result holds for all
N\in\llbracket 0,M-1\rrbracket
.

Consider
x_{0}\in[0,1]
the biggest element in
[0,1]
for which
\gamma_{|[0,x_{0})}
is injective. Take
y\in[0,x_{0})
such that
\gamma(y)=\gamma(x_{0})
. Let us remark that
\gamma(x_{0})=\gamma(y)
lies in the intersection of a stable and unstable segment of
\gamma
, therefore it is not contained in the stable or unstable leaf of a boundary periodic point. Consider

•

r_{0},...,r_{k}
the rectangle path starting from
r_{0}
associated to the simple and
good polygonal curve
\gamma_{|[0,y]}

•

r_{k},...,r_{l}
the rectangle path starting from
r_{k}
associated to the simple, closed and
good polygonal curve
\gamma_{|[y,x_{0}]}
(the curve
\gamma_{|[y,x_{0}]}
is indeed good, since
\gamma
is good and the intersection at
\gamma(x_{0})
is transverse)

•

r_{l},...,r_{n}
the rectangle path starting from
r_{l}
associated to the
good polygonal curve
\gamma_{|[x_{0},1]}

Notice that by our construction of the rectangle path associated to a good polygonal curve (see Definition
8.14
), the juxtaposition of the previous rectangle paths gives the rectangle path starting from
r_{0}
associated to
\gamma
.

Since
\gamma_{|[y,x_{0}]}
is simple and closed, by Proposition
9.21
its associated rectangle path
r_{k},...,r_{l}
is homotopic to a monotonous or trivial rectangle path

r_{k}=R_{0},R_{1}...,R_{s}=r_{l}

Similarly, consider
\gamma^{\prime}
the good, closed polygonal curve formed by the juxtaposition of
\gamma([x_{0},1])
followed by
\gamma([0,y])
. Notice that
\gamma^{\prime}
has at most
M-1
self-intersections and therefore by our induction hypothesis its associated rectangle path starting from
r_{l}

r_{l},...,r_{n}=r_{0},...,r_{k}
is homotopic to monotonous or trivial rectangle path

r_{l}=R^{\prime}_{0},R^{\prime}_{1}...,R^{\prime}_{m}=r_{k}

Consider now the rectangle path starting from
r_{0}
associated to
\gamma
:
r_{0},...,r_{n}
. By a sequence of homotopies of type
\mathcal{B}
the previous rectangle path is homotopic to

r_{0},..r_{k},...r_{l},..,r_{n}=r_{0},r_{1},...,r_{k-1},r_{k},r_{k-1},...,r_{0}

By our previous discussion the above rectangle path is homotopic to

(9.1)

r_{0},..r_{k}=R_{0},R_{1}...,R_{s}=r_{l}=R_{0}^{\prime},R^{\prime}_{1}...,R^{\prime}_{m}=r_{k},r_{k-1},...,r_{0}

If
r_{k}=R_{0},R_{1}...,R_{s}=r_{l}
is the trivial rectangle path, then
r_{k}=r_{l}
and
r_{l}=R^{\prime}_{0},R^{\prime}_{1}...,R^{\prime}_{m}=r_{k}
is also the trivial rectangle path. In this case,
r_{0},...,r_{n}
is homotopic to the rectangle path
r_{0},...,r_{k-1},r_{k},r_{k-1}...,r_{0}
, which is homotopic to a trivial rectangle path by a sequence of homotopies of type
\mathcal{B}
.

Assume now without any loss of generality that
r_{k}=R_{0},R_{1}...,R_{s}=r_{l}
is a decreasing rectangle path; hence
r_{l}
is a predecessor of some generation of
r_{k}
. We deduce that
r_{l}=R_{0}^{\prime},R^{\prime}_{1}...,R^{\prime}_{m}=r_{k}
is an increasing rectangle path. Furthermore, by Lemma
4.5
, we have that there exists a unique decreasing (resp. increasing) rectangle path from
r_{k}
to
r_{l}
(resp. from
r_{l}
to
r_{k}
). We deduce that the rectangle paths
r_{k}=R_{0},R_{1}...,R_{s}=r_{l}
and
r_{k}=R_{m}^{\prime},R_{m-1}^{\prime}...,R^{\prime}_{1},R^{\prime}_{0}=r_{l}
are exactly the same. Therefore, by a sequence of homotopies of type
\mathcal{B}
the rectangle path (
9.1
) is homotopic to:

r_{0},...r_{k},r_{k-1},..,r_{0}

Finally, the above rectangle path is homotopic to the trivial rectangle path by a sequence of homotopies of type
\mathcal{B}
; we thus get
the desired result.

10.
Proof of Theorem B

Let
\rho_{1}:G_{1}\rightarrow\text{Homeo}(\mathcal{P}_{1})
,
\rho_{2}:G_{2}\rightarrow\text{Homeo}(\mathcal{P}_{2})
be two strong Markovian actions acting on the planes
\mathcal{P}_{1}
and
\mathcal{P}_{2}
endowed with their stable and unstable singular foliations
\mathcal{F}_{1}^{s,u}
,
\mathcal{F}_{2}^{s,u}
. Assume that
\rho_{1}
and
\rho_{2}
preserve two strong Markovian families
\mathcal{R}_{1}
and
\mathcal{R}_{2}
whose associated classes of geometric types coincide.

For every
i\in\{1,2\}
, let us denote by
\Gamma_{i}
the boundary periodic points of
\mathcal{R}_{i}
, by
\overline{\mathcal{P}_{i}}
the bifoliated plane of
\rho_{i}
up to surgeries on
\Gamma_{i}
, by
\overline{\rho_{i}}:\overline{G_{i}}\rightarrow\text{Homeo}(\overline{\mathcal{P}_{i}})
the lift of
\rho_{i}
on
\overline{\mathcal{P}_{i}}
, by
\overline{\mathcal{F}^{s}_{i}},\overline{\mathcal{F}^{u}_{i}}
the stable and unstable foliations of
\overline{\rho_{i}}
, by
\overline{\mathcal{R}_{i}}
the lift of
\mathcal{R}_{i}
on
\overline{\mathcal{P}_{i}}
and by
\overline{\Gamma_{i}}
the lift of
\Gamma_{i}
on
\overline{\mathcal{P}_{i}}
.

Thanks to Corollary,
8.7.1
, by appropriately choosing representatives for every rectangle orbit in
\overline{\mathcal{R}_{1}}
and
\overline{\mathcal{R}_{2}}
, and orientations for the foliations
\overline{\mathcal{F}_{1}^{s,u}}
,
\overline{\mathcal{F}_{2}^{s,u}}
, we may assume that
\overline{\mathcal{R}_{1}}
and
\overline{\mathcal{R}_{2}}
are associated to the same (special) geometric type

\mathcal{G}=(R_{1},...,R_{n},(h_{i})_{i\in\llbracket 1,n\rrbracket},(v_{i})_{i\in\llbracket 1,n\rrbracket},\mathcal{H},\mathcal{V},\phi,u)

We would like to show the following generalization of Theorem B in the setting of strong Markovian actions:

Theorem 10.1

(Theorem B’)
.

There exists a homeomorphism
h:\overline{\mathcal{P}_{1}}\rightarrow\overline{\mathcal{P}_{2}}
and an isomorphism
\alpha:\overline{G_{1}}\rightarrow\overline{G_{2}}
such that:

•

the image by
h
of any stable (resp. unstable) leaf in
\overline{\mathcal{F}_{1}^{s}}
(resp.
\overline{\mathcal{F}_{1}^{u}}
) is a stable (resp. unstable) leaf in
\overline{\mathcal{F}_{2}^{s}}
(resp.
\overline{\mathcal{F}_{2}^{u}}
)

•

for every
g\in\overline{G_{1}}
and every
x\in\overline{\mathcal{P}_{1}}
we have that

h(\overline{\rho_{1}}(g)(x))=\overline{\rho_{2}}(\alpha(g))(h(x))

We remark here that Theorem B is an immediate consequence of Theorems B’ and C. We will split the proof of the previous theorem in two parts:

(1)

the construction of an isomorphism
\alpha:\overline{G_{1}}\rightarrow\overline{G_{2}}

(2)

the construction of a homeomoprhism
h:\overline{\mathcal{P}_{1}}\rightarrow\overline{\mathcal{P}_{2}}
with the desired properties

Let us begin with the construction of
\alpha
.

Convention 10.2
.

By a small abuse of notation and in order to simplify our notations, from now on we will denote for any
g\in\overline{G_{1}}
(resp.
g\in\overline{G_{2}}
) the homemomorphism
\overline{\rho_{1}}(g)
(resp.
\overline{\rho_{2}}(g)
) by
g
.

Consider
r_{0}^{1}\in\overline{\mathcal{R}_{1}},r_{0}^{2}\in\overline{\mathcal{R}_{2}}
two rectangles of the same type taken among our previously chosen set of representatives. We will fix
r_{0}^{1}
and
r_{0}^{2}
as the origin rectangles in
\overline{\mathcal{P}_{1}}
and
\overline{\mathcal{P}_{2}}
respectively. Let
\mathcal{O}(r_{0}^{1})\subset\overline{\mathcal{R}_{1}}
be the
\overline{G_{1}}
-orbit of
r_{0}^{1}
in
\overline{\mathcal{P}_{1}}
and
g^{1}\in\overline{G_{1}}
. Consider
R_{0}^{1}:=r_{0}^{1},...,R_{n}^{1}=g^{1}(R_{0}^{1})
a centered rectangle path in
\overline{\mathcal{R}_{1}}
going from
r_{0}^{1}
to
g^{1}(r_{0}^{1})
(such a rectangle path exists thanks to Proposition
8.15
) and
R_{0}^{2}:=r_{0}^{2},...,R_{n}^{2}
its associated centered rectangle path in
\overline{\mathcal{R}_{2}}
. Since
R_{0}^{1},g^{1}(R_{0}^{1}),R_{0}^{2},R_{n}^{2}
are by definition of the associated rectangle path (see Definition
8.21
) of the same type, there exists
g^{2}\in\overline{G_{2}}
such that
R_{n}^{2}=g^{2}(R_{0}^{2})
. We define

\displaystyle\alpha:\overline{G_{1}}

\displaystyle\rightarrow\overline{G_{2}}

\displaystyle g^{1}

\displaystyle\rightarrow g^{2}

Proposition 10.3
.

The map
\alpha
does not depend on our previous choices of rectangle paths and defines an isomorphism between
\overline{G_{1}}
and
\overline{G_{2}}
.

Proof.

Let us begin by showing that the map
\alpha
is well defined. Indeed, if
g\in\overline{G_{1}}
fixes one rectangle
R
in
\mathcal{O}(r_{0}^{1})
, then it has a fixed point in
R
and by Proposition
6.12
we get that
g=id
. A similar result is true for the elements in
\overline{G_{2}}
. Consequently, following our previous notations,
g^{1}
(resp.
g^{2}
) is the unique element in
\overline{G_{1}}
(resp.
\overline{G_{2}}
) that sends
r_{0}^{1}
(resp.
r_{0}^{2}
) to
R_{n}^{1}
(resp.
R_{n}^{2}
).

Furthermore, thanks to Theorem
8.22
, the rectangle
R_{n}^{2}
does not depend on our initial choice of rectangle path going from
r_{0}^{1}
to
R_{n}^{1}=g^{1}(r_{0}^{1})
. It follows that
g^{2}
also does not depend on our initial choice of rectangle path and thus
\alpha
is well defined. Let us now show that
\alpha
defines an isomorphism.

Fix
L^{1}_{1},L_{2}^{1}\in\mathcal{O}(r_{0}^{1})
. Consider
G_{1}^{1},G^{1}_{2}\in\overline{G_{1}}
the unique elements of
\overline{G_{1}}
sending
r_{0}^{1}
to
L^{1}_{1},L_{2}^{1}
respectively. Let
r_{0}^{1},r_{1}^{1},...,r_{n}^{1}=L^{1}_{1}
(resp.
{r^{\prime}}_{0}^{1}:=r_{0}^{1},{r^{\prime}}_{1}^{1},...,{r^{\prime}}_{m}^{1}=L^{1}_{2}
) be a centered rectangle path in
\overline{\mathcal{R}_{1}}
going from
r_{0}^{1}
to
L^{1}_{1}
(resp.
L_{2}^{1}
) and
r_{0}^{2},r_{1}^{2},...,L^{2}_{1}:=r_{n}^{2}
(resp.
{r^{\prime}}_{0}^{2}:=r_{0}^{2},{r^{\prime}}_{1}^{2},...,L^{2}_{2}:={r^{\prime}}_{m}^{2}
) its associated centered rectangle path in
\overline{\mathcal{R}_{2}}
. By definition of
\alpha
,
\alpha(G_{1}^{1})
(resp.
\alpha(G_{2}^{1})
) is the unique element of
\overline{G_{2}}
sending
r_{0}^{2}
to
L_{1}^{2}
(resp.
L_{2}^{2}
).

Consider now the rectangle path
r_{0}^{1},r_{1}^{1},...,L_{1}^{1}=G_{1}^{1}(r_{0}^{1}),G_{1}^{1}({r^{\prime}}_{1}^{1}),G_{1}^{1}({r^{\prime}_{2}}^{1}),...,G_{1}^{1}(L_{2}^{1})=G_{1}^{1}\circ G_{2}^{1}(r_{0}^{1})
. It is not difficult to see that the associated rectangle path in
\overline{\mathcal{R}_{2}}
of the previous previous path is
r_{0}^{2},r_{1}^{2},...,L_{1}^{2}=G_{1}^{2}(r_{0}^{2}),G_{1}^{2}({r^{\prime}}_{1}^{2}),...,G_{1}^{2}(L_{2}^{2})=G_{1}^{2}\circ G_{2}^{2}(r_{0}^{2})
. It follows by the definition of
\alpha
that
\alpha(G_{1}^{1}\circ G_{2}^{1})=G_{1}^{2}\circ G_{2}^{2}=\alpha(G_{1}^{1})\circ\alpha(G_{2}^{1})
and thus
\alpha
defines a morphism from
\overline{G_{1}}
to
\overline{G_{2}}
. By changing the roles of
\overline{G_{1}}
and
\overline{G_{2}}
, we can produce by the same exact procedure a morphism
\alpha^{\prime}:\overline{G_{2}}\rightarrow\overline{G_{1}}
such that
\alpha
and
\alpha^{\prime}
are inverses of each other. Consequently,
\alpha
defines an isomorphism from
\overline{G_{1}}
to
\overline{G_{2}}
and we get the desired result.

∎

We are now ready to proceed to the construction of the homeomorphism
h:\overline{\mathcal{P}_{1}}\rightarrow\overline{\mathcal{P}_{2}}
. We are first going to define a bijection
H
from
\overline{\mathcal{R}_{1}}
to
\overline{\mathcal{R}_{2}}
that is equivariant with respect to the group actions and then we are going to show that a map
h:\overline{\mathcal{P}_{1}}\rightarrow\overline{\mathcal{P}_{2}}
such that
h(R)=H(R)
for every rectangle
R\in\overline{\mathcal{R}_{1}}
defines a unique homeomorphism from
\overline{\mathcal{P}_{1}}
to
\overline{\mathcal{P}_{2}}
that has the desired properties.

Indeed, let
R\in\overline{\mathcal{R}_{1}}
. Consider a centered rectangle path in
\overline{\mathcal{P}_{1}}
ending at
R
and its corresponding centered rectangle path in
\overline{\mathcal{P}_{2}}
ending at
R^{\prime}\in\overline{\mathcal{R}_{2}}
. We define
H(R)=R^{\prime}
. Notice that
R^{\prime}
does not depend on our initial choice of rectangle path thanks to Theorem
8.22
and that
H
sends predecessors (resp. successors) to predecessors (resp. successors). Also, by our definition of
\alpha
, we have that for any
g\in\overline{G_{1}}
and
R\in\overline{\mathcal{R}_{1}}
:

(10.1)

H(g(R))=\alpha(g)(H(R))

Finally, by changing the roles of
\overline{\mathcal{R}_{1}}
,
\overline{\mathcal{R}_{2}}
and by applying the same arguments as before, we can construct a right and left inverse for
H
. It follows that
H:\overline{\mathcal{R}_{1}}\rightarrow\overline{\mathcal{R}_{2}}
is a bijection that is equivariant with respect to the actions of
\overline{G_{1}},\overline{G_{2}}
.

In order to prove Theorem B’ it suffices to prove the following proposition:

Proposition 10.4
.

There exists a unique homeomorphism
h
from
\overline{\mathcal{P}_{1}}
to
\overline{\mathcal{P}_{2}}
such that:

•

for every
R\in\overline{\mathcal{R}_{1}}
we have
h(R)=H(R)

•

the image by
h
of any stable (resp. unstable) leaf in
\overline{\mathcal{F}_{1}^{s}}
(resp.
\overline{\mathcal{F}_{1}^{u}}
) is a stable (resp. unstable) leaf in
\overline{\mathcal{F}_{2}^{s}}
(resp.
\overline{\mathcal{F}_{2}^{u}}
)

•

for every
g\in\overline{G_{1}}
and every
x\in\overline{\mathcal{P}_{1}}
we have

h(g(x))=\alpha(g)(h(x))

Proof.

Recall that in the beginning of this section we fixed a choice of representatives of every rectangle orbit in
\overline{\mathcal{R}_{1}}
,
\overline{\mathcal{R}_{2}}
and a choice of orientations on
\overline{\mathcal{F}^{s,u}_{1}}
,
\overline{\mathcal{F}^{s,u}_{2}}
such that the (special) geometric types associated to
\overline{\mathcal{R}_{1}}
,
\overline{\mathcal{R}_{2}}
for the previous choices of representatives and orientations are the same.

Let
x\in\overline{\mathcal{P}_{1}}
. Recall that a quadrant of
x
is the closure of a connected component of
\overline{\mathcal{P}_{1}}
minus the union of the stable and unstable leaves crossing
x
. For any quadrant
Q
of
x
, there exists a unique (up to reindexation) bi-infinite sequence
...R^{Q}_{-1},R^{Q}_{0},R^{Q}_{1},...
of rectangles in
\overline{\mathcal{R}_{1}}
such that:

•

for every
i
,
R^{Q}_{i+1}
is a predecessor of
R^{Q}_{i}

•

for every
i
,
R^{Q}_{i}
contains a neighborhood of
x
in
Q

Indeed, by the definition of a Markovian family there exists one rectangle
R^{Q}_{0}
containing a neighborhood of
x
in the quadrant
Q
. By Lemma
4.2
applied to
\overline{\mathcal{R}_{1}}
(see also Remark
8.2
), there exists a unique predecessor and a unique successor of
R^{Q}_{0}
containing a neighborhood of
x
in
Q
. We will denote the previous rectangles by
R^{Q}_{1}
and
R^{Q}_{-1}
respectively. By a repeated application of this argument, we can construct a bi-infinite sequence of rectangles in
\overline{\mathcal{R}_{1}}
with the desired properties. Furthermore, by Lemma
4.5
applied for
\overline{\mathcal{R}_{1}}
, any rectangle in
\overline{\mathcal{R}_{1}}
containing a neighborhood of
x
in
Q
is a predecessor or a successor of some generation of
R^{Q}_{0}
. In other words, any such rectangle appears in our previously constructed bi-infinite sequence. It follows that
...R^{Q}_{-1},R^{Q}_{0},R^{Q}_{1},...
is uniquely defined by the above properties up to a reindexation of the rectangles.

By Lemmas
3.21
and
3.22
applied for
\overline{\mathcal{R}_{1}}
, we have that
\{x\}=\overset{+\infty}{\underset{k=-\infty}{\cap}}R^{Q}_{k}
. We now define
h(x):=\overset{+\infty}{\underset{k=-\infty}{\cap}}H(R^{Q}_{k})\in\overline{\mathcal{P}_{2}}
. Notice that by definition of
H
, for every
k\in\mathbb{Z}
the rectangle
H(R^{Q}_{k})
is a predecessor of
H(R^{Q}_{k-1})
. Therefore by the Lemmas
3.21
and
3.22
, the set
\overset{+\infty}{\underset{k=-\infty}{\cap}}H(R^{Q}_{k})
corresponds to a unique point in
\overline{\mathcal{P}_{2}}
. We would now like to show that this point does not depend on the initial choice of quadrant for
x
.

Independence from the choice of quadrant

Assume first that
x
is not a boundary periodic point of
\overline{\mathcal{R}_{1}}
or equivalently that
