x\notin\overline{\Gamma_{1}}
. Denote by
\overline{(\mathcal{F}^{s}_{1})_{+}}(x)
(resp.
\overline{(\mathcal{F}^{u}_{1})_{+}}(x)
,
\overline{(\mathcal{F}^{s}_{1})_{-}}(x)
,
\overline{(\mathcal{F}^{u}_{1})_{-}}(x)
) the positive (resp. positive, negative, negative) stable (resp. unstable, stable, unstable) separatrix of
x
given by our choice of orientation of
\overline{\mathcal{F}^{s}_{1}}
and
\overline{\mathcal{F}^{u}_{1}}
. For every
\epsilon,\epsilon^{\prime}\in\{+,-\}
, denote also by
(\epsilon,\epsilon^{\prime})
the quadrant of
x
bounded by
\overline{(\mathcal{F}^{s}_{1})_{\epsilon}}(x)
and
\overline{(\mathcal{F}^{u}_{1})_{\epsilon^{\prime}}}(x)
. Assume that the quadrant
Q
was the
(\epsilon,\epsilon^{\prime})
quadrant of
x
, where
\epsilon,\epsilon^{\prime}\in\{+,-\}
. Consider the quadrant
(\epsilon,-\epsilon^{\prime})
of
x
and

i:=\inf\{k\in\mathbb{Z}|\forall j\geq k\penalty\ R^{(\epsilon,\epsilon^{\prime})}_{j}\text{ contains a germ of the $(\epsilon,\epsilon^{\prime})$ and $(\epsilon,-\epsilon^{\prime})$ quadrants of $x$ }\}

Let us first show that
i
belongs in
\mathbb{Z}\cup\{-\infty\}
. Indeed, suppose that
R^{(\epsilon,\epsilon^{\prime})}_{0}
contains a germ of the
(\epsilon,\epsilon^{\prime})
quadrant of
x
, but not of the
(\epsilon,-\epsilon^{\prime})
quadrant of
x
. This implies that
x
belongs to a stable boundary component of
R^{(\epsilon,\epsilon^{\prime})}_{0}
, say
s
. By Lemmas
5.5
,
5.6
there exists a unique
s
-crossing predecessor of
R^{(\epsilon,\epsilon^{\prime})}_{0}
that contains a germ of the
(\epsilon,\epsilon^{\prime})
and
(\epsilon,-\epsilon^{\prime})
quadrants of
x
. Next, by Lemma
4.5
, there exists
k_{0}\in\mathbb{N}
such that the previous
s
-crossing predecessor is of the form
R^{(\epsilon,\epsilon^{\prime})}_{k_{0}}
. By the Markovian intersection property, for every
k\geq k_{0}
,
R^{(\epsilon,\epsilon^{\prime})}_{k}
also contains a germ of the
(\epsilon,\epsilon^{\prime})
and
(\epsilon,-\epsilon^{\prime})
quadrants of
x
. We thus get that
i\in\mathbb{Z}\cup\{-\infty\}
.

If
i=-\infty
, then the sequence associated to the quadrant
(\epsilon,-\epsilon^{\prime})
is the exact same sequence as for
(\epsilon,\epsilon^{\prime})
. This can happen for instance when
x
does not belong to the stable or unstable leaf of some boundary periodic point. Therefore, in this case we get
\overset{+\infty}{\underset{k=-\infty}{\cap}}H(R^{(\epsilon,\epsilon^{\prime})}_{k})=\overset{+\infty}{\underset{k=-\infty}{\cap}}H(R^{(\epsilon,-\epsilon^{\prime})}_{k})
.

If
i\in\mathbb{Z}
, then for every
j\geq i
the rectangle
R_{j}^{(\epsilon,\epsilon^{\prime})}
is contained in the bi-infinite sequence associated to the quadrant
(\epsilon,-\epsilon^{\prime})
. We therefore get up to changing our initial choice of indexes,
R^{(\epsilon,-\epsilon^{\prime})}_{i}=R^{(\epsilon,\epsilon^{\prime})}_{i},R^{(\epsilon,-\epsilon^{\prime})}_{i+1}=R^{(\epsilon,\epsilon^{\prime})}_{i+1},...,R^{(\epsilon,-\epsilon^{\prime})}_{n}=R^{(\epsilon,\epsilon^{\prime})}_{n},...
.
The rectangle
R^{(\epsilon,-\epsilon^{\prime})}_{i-1}
is the unique successor of
R^{(\epsilon,-\epsilon^{\prime})}_{i}=R^{(\epsilon,\epsilon^{\prime})}_{i}
that contains
x
and that is not equal to
R^{(\epsilon,\epsilon^{\prime})}_{i-1}
. Since any two distinct successors of
R^{(\epsilon,-\epsilon^{\prime})}_{i}=R^{(\epsilon,\epsilon^{\prime})}_{i}
have disjoint interiors, we have that
x\in\partial^{s}{R^{(\epsilon,\epsilon^{\prime})}_{i-1}}\cap\partial^{s}{R^{(\epsilon,-\epsilon^{\prime})}_{i-1}}
.

Assume without any loss of generality that
R^{(\epsilon,-\epsilon^{\prime})}_{i-1}
is the successor of
R^{(\epsilon,\epsilon^{\prime})}_{i}
that is right above
R^{(\epsilon,\epsilon^{\prime})}_{i-1}
(see Figure
10.1
). In this case, thanks to our choice of representatives and orientations and to our construction of
H
, the map
H
will send
R^{(\epsilon,-\epsilon^{\prime})}_{i-1}
to the successor of
H(R^{(\epsilon,-\epsilon^{\prime})}_{i})
that is right above
H(R^{(\epsilon,\epsilon^{\prime})}_{i-1})
. Furthermore, since
R^{(\epsilon,-\epsilon^{\prime})}_{i-2}
contains
x
and a germ of its
(\epsilon,-\epsilon^{\prime})
quadrant,
R^{(\epsilon,-\epsilon^{\prime})}_{i-2}
is the unique successor of
R^{(\epsilon,-\epsilon^{\prime})}_{i-1}
that contains
x
, which corresponds to the lowermost successor of
R^{(\epsilon,-\epsilon^{\prime})}_{i-1}
. Therefore,
H(R^{(\epsilon,-\epsilon^{\prime})}_{i-2})
will correspond to the lowermost successor of
H(R^{(\epsilon,-\epsilon^{\prime})}_{i-1})
or in other words the successor of
H(R^{(\epsilon,-\epsilon^{\prime})}_{i-1})
that contains
h(x)=\overset{+\infty}{\underset{k=-\infty}{\cap}}H(R^{(\epsilon,\epsilon^{\prime})}_{k})\in\overline{\mathcal{P}_{2}}
. Similarly, one can prove that for every
k<i
the rectangle
H(R^{(\epsilon,-\epsilon^{\prime})}_{k})
contains
h(x)=\overset{+\infty}{\underset{k=-\infty}{\cap}}H(R^{(\epsilon,\epsilon^{\prime})}_{k})\in\overline{\mathcal{P}_{2}}
. Thanks to Lemma
3.22
, we have that
\overset{i-1}{\underset{k=-\infty}{\cap}}H(R^{(\epsilon,-\epsilon^{\prime})}_{k})
corresponds to a stable segment in
\overline{\mathcal{F}_{2}^{s}}
crossing
H(R^{(\epsilon,-\epsilon^{\prime})}_{i-1})
and containing
h(x)
.

Figure 10.1
.

Thanks to Lemma
3.21
, we also know that
\overset{+\infty}{\underset{k=i}{\cap}}H(R^{(\epsilon,-\epsilon^{\prime})}_{k})=\overset{+\infty}{\underset{k=i}{\cap}}H(R^{(\epsilon,\epsilon^{\prime})}_{k})
corresponds to an unstable segment in
\overline{\mathcal{F}^{u}}
crossing
H(R^{(\epsilon,-\epsilon^{\prime})}_{i})=H(R^{(\epsilon,\epsilon^{\prime})}_{i})
and containing
h(x)
. We conclude that
\overset{\infty}{\underset{k=-\infty}{\cap}}H(R^{(\epsilon,-\epsilon^{\prime})}_{k})=\{h(x)\}
. By a similar argument we can show that
\overset{\infty}{\underset{k=-\infty}{\cap}}H(R^{(-\epsilon,-\epsilon^{\prime})}_{k})=\overset{\infty}{\underset{k=-\infty}{\cap}}H(R^{(-\epsilon,\epsilon^{\prime})}_{k})=\{h(x)\}
, which gives us the desired result.

Assume now that
x
is a boundary periodic point or equivalently that
x\in\overline{\Gamma_{1}}
. In this case, we can define
h(x)
by the same exact argument for some choice of quadrant
Q
of
x
(
x
has infinitely many quadrants in
\overline{\mathcal{P}_{1}}
). Consider now
Q^{\prime}\neq Q
the quadrant of
x
that intersects
Q
along a stable leaf of
x
. By a similar argument, we can show that for every
N
,
\overset{N}{\underset{k=-\infty}{\cap}}H(R^{Q^{\prime}}_{k})
corresponds to a stable segment in
\overline{\mathcal{F}_{2}^{s}}(h(x))
. Furthermore, if
t\in\text{Stab}(x)
acts as a contraction on each stable leaf of
x
, then we have that for every
k\in\mathbb{Z}
and every quadrant
q
of
x
,
t^{k}(R^{q}_{0})
intersects a neighborhood of
x
in
q
and therefore
(t^{k}(R^{q}_{0}))_{k\in\mathbb{Z}}
is a subsequence of
(R^{q}_{k})_{k\in\mathbb{Z}}
. It follows that
\big(\alpha(t)^{k}(H(R^{q}_{0}))\big)_{k\in\mathbb{Z}}
is also a subsequence of
(H(R^{q}_{k}))_{k\in\mathbb{Z}}
. Notice that by Lemmas
3.21
and
3.22
, the sets
\overset{+\infty}{\underset{k=-\infty}{\cap}}\alpha(t)^{k}(H(R^{Q^{\prime}}_{0}))
,
\overset{+\infty}{\underset{k=-\infty}{\cap}}\alpha(t)^{k}(H(R^{Q}_{0}))
consist each of a unique point fixed by
\alpha(t)
. Our previous arguments imply that both
\overset{+\infty}{\underset{k=-\infty}{\cap}}H(R^{Q^{\prime}}_{k})
and
\overset{+\infty}{\underset{k=-\infty}{\cap}}H(R^{Q}_{k})
are fixed by
\alpha(t)
and belong to the same stable leaf in
\overline{\mathcal{F}^{s}}
. By Proposition
6.14
, we conclude that the previous two points correspond to the same point
h(x)
, which gives us the desired result.

h(\overline{\Gamma_{1}})\subset\overline{\Gamma_{2}}
and
h(\overline{\mathcal{P}_{1}}-\overline{\Gamma_{1}})\subset\overline{\mathcal{P}_{2}}-\overline{\Gamma_{2}}

Indeed, assume that
x\in\overline{\Gamma_{1}}
and
h(x)\in\overline{\mathcal{P}_{2}}-\overline{\Gamma_{2}}
. There are infinitely many quadrants around
x
, therefore there exists an infinite family of rectangles
(R_{i})_{i\in\mathbb{N}}
of
\overline{\mathcal{R}_{1}}
intersecting
x
and having pairwise disjoint interiors. For every
i
the rectangle
H(R_{i})
intersects
h(x)
that has only finitely many quadrants. Therefore, there exist
i\neq j
such that
\overset{\circ}{H(R_{i})}\cap\overset{\circ}{H(R_{j})}\neq\emptyset
. Therefore,
H(R_{i})
is a predecessor or successor of some generation of
H(R_{j})
. This contradicts that the fact that
H
defines a bijection from
\overline{\mathcal{R}_{1}}
to
\overline{\mathcal{R}_{2}}
that respects the relations of predecessor/successor.

By a similar argument we can show that
h(\overline{\mathcal{P}_{1}}-\overline{\Gamma_{1}})\subset\overline{\mathcal{P}_{2}}-\overline{\Gamma_{2}}
.

h
is a bijection for which
h(R)=H(R)
for all
R\in\overline{\mathcal{R}_{1}}

By changing the roles of
\overline{\mathcal{P}_{1}}
and
\overline{\mathcal{P}_{2}}
and using the fact that
H
is a bijection between
\overline{\mathcal{R}_{1}}
and
\overline{\mathcal{R}_{2}}
, we can show that
h
admits a right and left inverse, therefore
h
is a bijection.

Next, take
R\in\overline{\mathcal{R}_{1}}
. For every
x\in R
there exists a quadrant
Q
of
x
such that
R
contains a neighborhood of
x
in
Q
. Therefore,
R
belongs in the sequence of rectangles
(R^{Q}_{k}(x))_{k\in\mathbb{Z}}
defined in the previous paragraphs and by definition of
h
, we get that
h(x)\in H(R)
and by extension that
h(R)\subset H(R)
. By a similar argument, we can show that
h^{-1}(H(R))\subset R
. By combining the two previous results, we get that
h(R)=H(R)
.

h
is a homeomorphism

Recall that by our previous arguments
h
is a bijection. Consider now
x\in\overline{\mathcal{P}_{1}}-\overline{\Gamma_{1}}
. Denote by
(R^{(\epsilon,\epsilon^{\prime})}_{k}(x))_{k\in\mathbb{Z}}
the sequence of rectangles defined in the previous paragraphs for the quadrant
(\epsilon,\epsilon^{\prime})
of
x
. In order to show that
h
is continuous on
x
, it suffices to show that for every
(\epsilon,\epsilon^{\prime})\in\{+,-\}^{2}
and for any sequence
x_{n}
converging to
x
in the
(\epsilon,\epsilon^{\prime})
quadrant of
x
,
h(x_{n})
converges to
h(x)
. Fix
\epsilon,\epsilon^{\prime}\in\{+,-\}
and consider a sequence
x_{n}
in the
(\epsilon,\epsilon^{\prime})
quadrant of
x
converging to
x
. For any
N\in\mathbb{N}
, if
n
is sufficiently big
x_{n}\in\overset{N}{\underset{k=-N}{\cap}}R^{(\epsilon,\epsilon^{\prime})}_{k}(x)
, hence
h(x_{n})\in\overset{N}{\underset{k=-N}{\cap}}H(R^{(\epsilon,\epsilon^{\prime})}_{k}(x))
. By Lemmas
3.21
and
3.22
, the set
\overset{N}{\underset{k=-N}{\cap}}H(R^{(\epsilon,\epsilon^{\prime})}_{k}(x))
converges when
N\rightarrow+\infty
to
h(x)
for the Hausdorff topology. We thus get that
h(x_{n})
converges to
h(x)
.

Consider now
x\in\overline{\Gamma_{1}}
. Recall that
x
has infinitely many quadrants. By an argument similar to the one we used in the previous paragraph, if
(x_{n})_{n\in\mathbb{N}}
converges to
x
and
(x_{n})_{n\in\mathbb{N}}
visits only a finite number of quadrants of
x
, then
(h(x_{n}))_{n\in\mathbb{N}}
converges to
h(x)
. In order to show that
h
is continuous on
x
, it suffices to show that if
(x_{n})_{n\in\mathbb{N}}
visits an infinite number of quadrants of
x
, then
(x_{n})_{n\in\mathbb{N}}
can not converge to
x
. Indeed, take
(x_{n})_{n\in\mathbb{N}}
such a sequence. By eventually considering a subsequence, we can assume that for every
n
,
x_{n}
belongs in a different quadrant of
x
, say
q_{n}
. For every quadrant
q
of
x
that belongs in
\{q_{n}|n\in\mathbb{N}\}
, take
U_{q}
a neighborhood of
x
in
q
that does not contain
x_{n}
. For every quadrant
q
of
x
such that
q\notin\{q_{n}|n\in\mathbb{N}\}
, take
U_{q}
any neighborhood of
x
in
q
. By Proposition
6.4
, the union of the
U_{q}
contains an open set of
x
that by construction does not intersect
x_{n}
. Hence,
x_{n}
does not converge to
x
.

The above prove that
h
is continuous. By applying the same arguments to
h^{-1}
, we get that
h
is a homeomorphism.

h
is equivariant with respect to the actions of the group actions

Consider
x\in\overline{\mathcal{P}_{1}}
and
g\in\overline{G_{1}}
. For any quadrant
q
of
x
, using our previous notations, we have that:

h(g(x))=h(g(\overset{+\infty}{\underset{l=-\infty}{\cap}}R^{q}_{l}(x)))=\overset{+\infty}{\underset{l=-\infty}{\cap}}H(g(R^{q}_{l}(x)))=\overset{+\infty}{\underset{l=-\infty}{\cap}}\alpha(g)(H(R^{q}_{l}(x)))=\alpha(g)(h(x))

h
sends oriented stable/unstable leaves to oriented stable/unstable leaves

Consider
p
a periodic point in
\overline{\mathcal{P}_{1}}-\overline{\Gamma_{1}}
,
x\in\overline{\mathcal{F}_{1}^{s}}(p)
and
g\in\text{Stab}(p)
such that
g^{n}(x)\rightarrow p
when
n\rightarrow+\infty
. By eventually replacing
g
by
g^{2}
, assume that
g
preserves the orientations of the foliations
\overline{\mathcal{F}_{1}^{s,u}}
.

Since
h
is a homeomorphism that is equivariant with respect to the actions of
\overline{G_{1}}
and
\overline{G_{2}}
, we have that
\alpha(g)\in\text{Stab}(h(p))
and
\alpha(g)^{n}(h(x))\rightarrow h(p)
when
n\rightarrow+\infty
. Furthermore, for any rectangle
R\in\overline{\mathcal{R}_{1}}
containing
p
, we get by the definition of
g
and by Lemma
4.5
that
g(R)
is a predecessor of some generation of
R
. Since
H
and thus also
h
sends predecessors to predecessors, this implies that
\alpha(g)(h(R))
is a predecessor of some generation of
h(R)
and therefore
\alpha(g)\in\text{Stab}(h(p))
acts as a contraction on
\overline{\mathcal{F}_{2}^{s}}(h(p))
. Finally, since
\alpha(g)^{n}(h(x))\rightarrow h(p)
when
n\rightarrow+\infty
, we get that
h(x)\in\overline{\mathcal{F}_{2}^{s}}(h(p))
. We have thus proven that
h(\overline{\mathcal{F}_{1}^{s}}(p))\subset\overline{\mathcal{F}_{2}^{s}}(h(p))
. By using the same argument for
h^{-1}
, we deduce that
h(\overline{\mathcal{F}_{1}^{s}}(p))=\overline{\mathcal{F}_{2}^{s}}(h(p))
.

By similar arguments one can prove that
h(\overline{\mathcal{F}_{1}^{u}}(p))=\overline{\mathcal{F}_{2}^{u}}(h(p))
and also that
h
sends a stable (resp. unstable) leaf of a point

p^{\prime}\in\overline{\Gamma_{1}}
to a stable (resp. unstable) leaf of the point
h(p^{\prime})\in\overline{\Gamma_{2}}
. Moreover, by Definition
3.29
, the periodic stable (resp. unstable) leaves form a dense set in the leaf space of
\overline{\mathcal{F}_{1}^{s}}
(resp.
\overline{\mathcal{F}_{1}^{u}}
). Using the fact that
h
is a homeomorphism, our previous arguments imply that
h(\overline{\mathcal{F}_{1}^{s}})=\overline{\mathcal{F}_{2}^{s}}
and
h(\overline{\mathcal{F}_{1}^{u}})=\overline{\mathcal{F}_{2}^{u}}
.

Finally, recall that
\overline{\mathcal{F}_{1}^{s,u}}
and
\overline{\mathcal{F}_{2}^{s,u}}
have been endowed with orientations for which the map
H
sends the predecessors of any rectangle
r\in\overline{\mathcal{R}_{1}}
ordered from left to right, to the predecessors of
H(r)
ordered from left to right. Same for the successors of any rectangle in
\overline{\mathcal{R}_{1}}
. We deduce that
h
respects the orientations of
\overline{\mathcal{F}_{1}^{s,u}}
and
\overline{\mathcal{F}_{2}^{s,u}}
.

∎

11.
An invariant of a strong Markovian action up to conjugacy

Fix
\rho:G\rightarrow\text{Homeo}(\mathcal{P})
an orientation preserving strong Markovian action, preserving the pair of singular foliations
\mathcal{F}^{s}
and
\mathcal{F}^{u}
and leaving invariant a strong Markovian family
\mathcal{R}
. Denote by
\Gamma
the set of boundary periodic points of
\mathcal{R}
.

In Section
8
, we introduced the notion of cycle around a boundary arc point of
\overline{\mathcal{P}}
. We would like in this section to adapt this notion of cycle for boundary periodic points in
\mathcal{P}
, in order to obtain information about the neighborhood of these orbits in
\mathcal{P}
. This will allow us to prove the analogues of Theorem-Definition D and Theorem E for strong Markovian actions.

According to the following lemma, we can not extend Definition
9.12
for boundary periodic points in
\mathcal{P}
.

Lemma 11.1
.

Let
\gamma\in\Gamma
and
R,R^{\prime}\in\mathcal{R}
two rectangles containing
\gamma
such that
\overset{\circ}{R}\cap\overset{\circ}{R^{\prime}}\neq\emptyset
. For any quadrant of
\gamma
, say
Q
, we have that
R
contains a neighborhood of
\gamma
in
Q
if and only if
R^{\prime}
contains a neighborhood of
\gamma
in
Q
.

Proof.

Assume that
R
contains a neighborhood of
\gamma
in
Q
, but not
R^{\prime}
. Since
\overset{\circ}{R}\cap\overset{\circ}{R^{\prime}}\neq\emptyset
and
\gamma\in R\cap R^{\prime}
, by the Markovian intersection axiom, both
R
and
R^{\prime}
must contain the a neigbhorhood of
\gamma
in some quadrant
Q^{\prime}
of
\gamma
that is adjacent to
Q
. The previous facts imply that
R
must be a predecessor or successor of
R^{\prime}
of some generation crossing a stable or unstable boundary of
R
. Assume without any loss of generality, that
R
is a predecessor of
R^{\prime}
of some generation crossing its stable boundary. Since
\gamma\in R\cap R^{\prime}
and
R^{\prime}
does not contain a neighborhood of
\gamma
in
Q
, we have that
\gamma\in\partial^{s}R^{\prime}\cap\partial^{u}R
. Consider now
g\in\text{Stab}(\gamma)
such that the stable boundaries of
\rho(g)(R^{\prime})
become very thin and the unstable ones very long. The intersection of the rectangles
\rho(g)(R^{\prime})
and
R
is not Markovian, which leads to an absurd.
∎

Lemma 11.2
.

Take
\gamma\in\Gamma
and
q
one of its quadrants in
\mathcal{P}
. The set of rectangles in
\mathcal{R}
containing a neighborhood of
\gamma
in
q
is finite up to the action of
\text{Stab}^{+}(\gamma)
, the subgroup of
\text{Stab}(\gamma)
preserving all the stable and unstable separatrices of
\gamma
.

Proof.

Indeed, fix
R
a rectangle in
\mathcal{R}
containing a neighborhood of
\gamma
in
q
. Take
R^{\prime}
a rectangle in
\mathcal{R}
with the same property that is not in the
G
-orbit of
R
. By Lemma
4.5
,
R^{\prime}
is a successor or predecessor of
R
of some generation. Denote by
t
the generator of
\text{Stab}^{+}(\gamma)
that acts as a topological expansion on every unstable separatrix of
\gamma
. Notice that
\rho(t)(R)
is a predecessor of some generation, say
n
(with
n\in\mathbb{N}^{*}
), of
R
containing a neighborhood of
\gamma
in
q
. By eventually replacing
R^{\prime}
by
\rho(t^{k})(R^{\prime})
for some
k\in\mathbb{Z}
, we can assume that
R^{\prime}
is a predecessor of some generation of
R
and a successor of some generation of
t(R)
. It follows that
R^{\prime}
is a predecessor of generation
m
of
R
, where
m<n
and since the predecessors of
R
of generation less than
n
form a finite set (see Lemma
4.2
), we get the desired result.
∎

Take
\gamma\in\Gamma
a boundary periodic point of
\mathcal{R}
and
L_{0}\in\mathcal{R}
containing
\gamma
. Denote by
p\geq 2
the total number of stable separatrices of
\gamma
and by
Q_{0},...,Q_{2p-1}
the cyclically ordered set of quadrants of
\gamma
. By eventually reindexing the previous set of quadrants, we can assume without any loss of generality that
L_{0}
contains a neighborhood of
\gamma
in the quadrant
Q_{0}
and does not intersect the interior of
Q_{2p-1}
. Notice that by Lemma
11.2
, there exist finitely many such rectangles up to the action of
\text{Stab}^{+}(\gamma)
. Assume without any loss of generality that the quadrant
Q_{1}
intersects
Q_{0}
along an unstable separatrix
u_{0}
of
\gamma
. Denote by
R^{Q_{1}}
the set of rectangles
r\in\mathcal{R}
containing a neighborhood of
\gamma
in
Q_{1}
and such that
L_{0}\cap u_{0}\subseteq r\cap u_{0}
. Consider also
L_{1}\in R^{Q_{1}}
such that for all
r\in R^{Q_{1}}

L_{1}\cap\mathcal{F}^{u}(\gamma)\subseteq r\cap\mathcal{F}^{u}(\gamma)

Claim
:
Such a rectangle
L_{1}
exists,
L_{1}
is uniquely defined by the above property, that we will call the
minimality condition
, and finally either
L_{1}=L_{0}
or
\overset{\circ}{L_{1}}\cap\overset{\circ}{L_{0}}=\emptyset
.

Proof of the claim.

Indeed, thanks to Lemma
11.1
, we have exactly one of the following three cases:

(1)

The rectangles of
R^{Q_{1}}
contain neighborhoods of
\gamma
in the quadrants
Q_{0}
and
Q_{1}

(2)

The rectangles of
R^{Q_{1}}
contain neighborhoods of
\gamma
in the quadrants
Q_{1}
and
Q_{2}

(3)

The rectangles of
R^{Q_{1}}
contain neighborhoods of
\gamma
only in the quadrant
Q_{1}

Using this fact together with the Markovian intersection axiom, for any
r,r^{\prime}\in R^{Q_{1}}
we have that
r\cap\mathcal{F}^{u}(\gamma)\subseteq r^{\prime}\cap\mathcal{F}^{u}(\gamma)
or
r^{\prime}\cap\mathcal{F}^{u}(\gamma)\subseteq r\cap\mathcal{F}^{u}(\gamma)
. Moreover, even though the set
R^{Q_{1}}
is not invariant by the action of
\text{Stab}^{+}(\gamma)
, it is contained by Lemma
11.2
in the union of a finite number of
\text{Stab}^{+}(\gamma)
-orbits of rectangles. By combining the previous facts, it follows that there exists at least one rectangle in
R^{Q_{1}}
satisfying the minimality condition.

If there exist two rectangles
L_{1},L^{\prime}_{1}\in R^{Q_{1}}
satisfying the minimality condition, then
L_{1}\cap\mathcal{F}^{u}(\gamma)=L^{\prime}_{1}\cap\mathcal{F}^{u}(\gamma)
. In any of the above three cases, this implies by the Markovian intersection property that either
\partial^{s}L_{1}\subseteq\partial^{s}L^{\prime}_{1}
or
\partial^{s}L^{\prime}_{1}\subseteq\partial^{s}L_{1}
. Equivalently, the existence of two distinct rectangles
L_{1},L^{\prime}_{1}\in R^{Q_{1}}
satisfying the minimality condition, would imply that
L_{1}\cap L_{1}^{\prime}=L_{1}
or
L_{1}\cap L_{1}^{\prime}=L^{\prime}_{1}
. This is impossible, since by the Markovian intersection axiom,
L_{1}\cap L^{\prime}_{1}
is a non-trivial horizontal or vertical subrectangle of
L_{1}
(resp.
L^{\prime}_{1}
). We deduce the uniqueness of
L_{1}
.

Finally, if
\overset{\circ}{L_{1}}\cap\overset{\circ}{L_{0}}\neq\emptyset
, then by Lemma
11.1
, all the rectangles of
R^{Q_{1}}
contain neighborhoods of
\gamma
in the quadrants
Q_{0}
and
Q_{1}
. This implies by Lemma
11.1
that
L_{0}\in R^{Q_{1}}
and since
L_{0}
clearly satisfies the minimality condition, by the uniqueness of
L_{1}
, we have that
L_{1}=L_{0}
.
∎

Next, denote by
s_{0}
the stable separatrix of
\gamma
separating the quadrants
Q_{1}
and
Q_{1}
. Denote by
R^{Q_{2}}
the set of rectangles
r\in\mathcal{R}
containing a neighborhood of
\gamma
in
Q_{2}
and such that
L_{1}\cap s_{0}\subseteq r\cap s_{0}
. Again, we define
L_{2}
as the unique rectangle in
R^{Q_{2}}
for which all
r\in R^{Q_{2}}
satisfy

L_{2}\cap\mathcal{F}^{s}(\gamma)\subseteq r\cap\mathcal{F}^{s}(\gamma)

We repeat the previous construction a finite number of times, in order to produce a finite set of rectangles
L_{3},...,L_{2p-1}
and the sets of rectangles
R^{Q_{3}},...,R^{Q_{2p-1}}
.

Figure 11.1
.
Some examples of pre-cycles starting from
L_{0}
around a regular boundary periodic point

By Lemma
11.1
and our previous construction, we have that any two rectangles among
L_{0},L_{1},...,L_{2p-1}
have either disjoint interiors or are equal (see Figure
11.1
).

By eventually removing some rectangles from
L_{0},L_{1},...,L_{2p-1}
so that every rectangle appears exactly once and by reindexing the remaining rectangles, we obtain a sequence of
k\in\llbracket 2,2p-1\rrbracket
rectangles
L_{0},...,L_{k-1}
.

Definition 11.3
.

We will call
L_{0},...,L_{k-1}

a pre-cycle around
\gamma
starting from
L_{0}
.
∎

Remark 11.4
.

•

To every rectangle
L_{0}
containing
\gamma
and to every choice of cyclic order of the quadrants of
\gamma
, we associated by our previous construction a unique pre-cycle around
\gamma
starting from
L_{0}

•

By changing the cyclic order of the quadrants of
\gamma
, we can define by the same procedure a second pre-cycle around
\gamma
starting from
L_{0}
. It follows that for every rectangle containing
\gamma
, there exist two pre-cycles around
\gamma
starting from this rectangle

•

The image of a pre-cycle around
\gamma
by an element of
\text{Stab}(\gamma)
is a pre-cycle around
\gamma
. Therefore, by Lemma
11.2
there exist only a finite number of pre-cycles around
\gamma
up to the action of
\text{Stab}(\gamma)
or
\text{Stab}^{+}(\gamma)
.

We will now associate to any pre-cycle around
\gamma
a closed rectangle path of
\mathcal{P}
that we will call a
cycle
.

Consider
L_{0}\in\mathcal{R}
containing
\gamma
,
Q_{0},Q_{1},...,Q_{2p-1}
the set of cyclically ordered quadrants of
\gamma
(for some choice of cyclic order). By eventually reindexing the quadrants of
\gamma
, we can assume without any loss of generality that
L_{0}
contains a neighborhood of
\gamma
in
Q_{0}
and does not intersect the interior of
Q_{2p-1}
. Assume also for the sake of simplicity that the pre-cycle starting from
L_{0}
associated to our choice of cyclic order of the
Q_{i}
is of the form
(L_{0},L_{1},...,L_{2p-1})
, where the
L_{i}
have pairwise disjoint interiors. Finally, assume without any loss of generality that
Q_{0}
and
Q_{1}
intersect along the unstable separatrix
u_{0}
of
\gamma
.

Let
l_{0}=L_{0}\cap u_{0}
and
r_{0}
be the
l_{0}
-crossing successor of
L_{0}
containing the endpoint of
l_{0}
that is not
\gamma
(this rectangle exists by Lemma
5.6
). By the construction of
L_{1}
(see our discussion prior to Definition
11.3
),
r_{0}
is a successor of
L_{1}
of some generation (see Figure
11.1
). Similarly, if
s_{0}
is the separatrix of
\gamma
lying in the intersection of
Q_{1}
and
Q_{2}
, we define
l_{1}:=L_{1}\cap s_{0}
and
r_{1}
as the
l_{1}
-crossing predecessor of
L_{1}
containing the endpoint of
l_{1}
that is not
\gamma
. Again,
r_{1}
is a predecessor of
L_{2}
of some generation. We repeat this construction in order to construct the rectangles
r_{2},...,r_{2p-2}
which verify the following property:
r_{i}
is a crossing successor (resp. predecessor) of
L_{i}
when
i
is even (resp. odd).

Finally, if
s_{p}
is the stable separatrix of
\gamma
lying in the intersection of
Q_{2p-1}
and
Q_{0}
and
L_{0}\cap s_{p}\subset L_{3}\cap s_{p}
we define
l_{2p-1}:=L_{0}\cap s_{p}
and
r_{2p-1}
as the
l_{2p-1}
-crossing predecessor of
L_{0}
intersecting the endpoint of
l_{2p-1}
that is not
\gamma
. If instead,
L_{2p-1}\cap s_{p}\subset L_{0}\cap s_{p}
we define
l_{2p-1}:=L_{2p-1}\cap s_{p}
and
r_{2p-1}
as the
l_{2p-1}
-crossing predecessor of
L_{2p-1}
intersecting the endpoint of
l_{2p-1}
that is not
\gamma
. In any case,
r_{2p-1}
is a predecessor of some generation of
L_{0}
and also of
L_{2p-1}
.

We have thus constructed a generalized rectangle path in
\mathcal{P}
, namely

(L_{0},r_{0},L_{1},r_{1},...,L_{2p-1},r_{2p-1},L_{0})

Definition 11.5
.

The closed rectangle path associated to the generalized rectangle path
(L_{0},r_{0},L_{1},r_{1},...,L_{2p-1},r_{2p-1},L_{0})
(see Definition
9.17
) is called the
cycle associated to the pre-cycle
(L_{0},L_{1},...,L_{2p-1})
. We define similarly the cycle associated to a pre-cycle with less than
2p-1
rectangles.

The set of cycles associated to all pre-cycles around
\gamma
will be called the set of
cycles
of
\gamma
and will be denoted by
\text{Cycl}(\gamma)
.
∎

Remark 11.6
.

If
c
is a pre-cycle around
\gamma
and
g\in\text{Stab}(\gamma)
. The image by
\rho(g)
of the cycle associated to
c
, is the cycle associated to the pre-cycle
\rho(g)(c)
. Therefore, by Remark
11.4
, the set of cycles around any boundary periodic orbit
\gamma
consists of a finite number of orbits by the action of
\text{Stab}(\gamma)
or
\text{Stab}^{+}(\gamma)
.

More generally,

Remark 11.7
.

For any
g\in G
and any boundary periodic point
\gamma
,
\rho(g)(\text{Cycl}(\gamma))=\text{Cycl}(\rho(g)(\gamma))
. Therefore, the set of all cycles around any boundary periodic point in the
G
-orbit of
\gamma
, consists of a finite number of rectangle paths up to the action of
\rho
.

We will now prove that the set of rectangle paths
\underset{g\in G}{\cup}\text{Cycl}(\rho(g)(\gamma))
can be described as a unique
closed rectangle path
in any geometric type associated to
\mathcal{R}
.

Definition 11.8
.

Consider a geometric type
\mathcal{G}=(n,(h_{i})_{i\in\llbracket 1,n\rrbracket},(v_{i})_{i\in\llbracket 1,n\rrbracket},\mathcal{H},\mathcal{V},\phi,u)
, where
\mathcal{H}=\{H_{i}^{j},i\in\llbracket 1,n\rrbracket,j\in\llbracket 1,h_{i}\rrbracket\}
and
\mathcal{V}=\{V_{i}^{j},i\in\llbracket 1,n\rrbracket,j\in\llbracket 1,v_{i}\rrbracket\}
. We define a
rectangle path
in
\mathcal{G}
as a finite sequence of the form
i_{0},s_{0},i_{1},s_{1},i_{2},...,s_{m},i_{m+1}
where

•

for all
k\in\llbracket 0,m+1\rrbracket
,
i_{k}\in\llbracket 1,n\rrbracket

•

for all
k\in\llbracket 0,m\rrbracket
,
s_{k}
is of the form
V_{i_{k}}^{\bullet}
or
H_{i_{k}}^{\bullet}
.

•

for all
k\in\llbracket 0,m\rrbracket
, if
s_{k}\in\mathcal{V}
(resp.
s_{k}\in\mathcal{H}
) then it is the image by
\phi
(resp.
\phi^{-1}
) of an element of
\mathcal{H}
(resp.
\mathcal{V}
) of the form
H_{i_{k+1}}^{\bullet}
(resp.
V_{i_{k+1}}^{\bullet}
).

Furthermore, the rectangle path
i_{0},s_{0},i_{1},s_{1},i_{2},...,s_{m},i_{m+1}
will be called
closed
when

i_{0}=i_{m+1}

∎

Recall that, using its geometric interpretation, a geometric type
\mathcal{G}
is a set of rectangles
R_{1},...,R_{n}
, endowed each with a collection of horizontal and vertical subrectangles identified by the map
(\phi,u)
. In these terms, by identifying every
i_{l}\in\llbracket 1,n\rrbracket
with
R_{i_{l}}
, a rectangle path
i_{0},s_{0},i_{1},s_{1},i_{2},...,s_{m},i_{m+1}
in
\mathcal{G}
, can be thought as a sequence of rectangles and subrectangles of the form
R_{0},s_{0},...,R_{m},s_{m},R_{m+1}
, where for every
k\in\llbracket 0,m\rrbracket
,
s_{k}
corresponds to a vertical (resp. or horizontal) subrectangle of
R_{k}
and
\phi^{-1}(s_{k})
(resp.
\phi(s_{k})
) to a horizontal (resp. or vertical) subrectangle of
R_{k+1}
.

The relation between rectangle paths in
\mathcal{P}
and rectangle paths in
G

Choose
r_{1},...,r_{n}
representatives of every rectangle orbit in
\mathcal{R}
, an orientation of
\mathcal{P}
and choose also an orientation of the stable and unstable foliations inside every
r_{i}
such that the positive stable direction followed by the positive unstable direction inside every
r_{i}
define an orientation compatible with our choice of orientation of
\mathcal{P}
. Let
\mathcal{G}=(R_{1},...,R_{n},(h_{i})_{i\in\llbracket 1,n\rrbracket},(v_{i})_{i\in\llbracket 1,n\rrbracket},\mathcal{H},\mathcal{V},\phi,u)
be the geometric type associated to
\mathcal{R}
by Remark
4.9
for the previous choices of representatives and orientations (we use here the geometric interpretation of
\mathcal{G}
).

Denote by
R_{i}
the rectangle in
G
associated to
r_{i}
. Recall that by Definition
4.7
, the vertical (resp. horizontal) subrectangles of the rectangles
R_{1},...,R_{n}
(in other words the elements of the sets
\mathcal{V}
and
\mathcal{H}
) correspond to the predecessors (resp. successors) of the rectangles
r_{1},...,r_{n}
.

Consider now
l_{0},l_{1},...,l_{k}
a rectangle path in
\mathcal{P}
. Using the fact that for every
i\in\llbracket 0,k\rrbracket
there exists a unique
g_{i}\in G
such that
\rho(g_{i})(l_{i})\in\{r_{1},...,r_{n}\}
, we get that

•

to every
l_{i}
we can associate a unique rectangle
R_{l_{i}}
of the geometric type
\mathcal{G}

•

since for every
i\in\llbracket 1,k\rrbracket
the rectangle
l_{i}
is a successor or a predecessor of
l_{i-1}
, it follows that
\rho(g_{i-1})(l_{i})
is also a successor or a predecessor of
\rho(g_{i-1})(l_{i-1})\in\{r_{1},...,r_{n}\}
. Therefore, the rectangle
l_{i}
corresponds to a unique horizontal or vertical subrectangle
s_{l_{i}}
of
R_{l_{i-1}}
.

It is now easy to check that the sequence
R_{l_{0}},s_{l_{1}},....,R_{l_{k-1}},s_{l_{k}},R_{l_{k}}
is a rectangle path in
\mathcal{G}
, which we will call
the projection of the rectangle path
l_{0},...,l_{k}
in
\mathcal{G}
.

Conversely, consider
R_{0},s_{0},R_{1},s_{1}....,R_{k-1},s_{k-1},R_{k}
a rectangle path in
\mathcal{G}
. As before, the rectangle
R_{0}
corresponds to a unique
\rho
-orbit of rectangles in
\mathcal{R}
. Let
l_{0}
be an element of the previous orbit of rectangles and fix
g_{0}
the unique element in
G
that verifies
\rho(g_{0})(l_{0})\in\{r_{1},...,r_{n}\}
. Notice that
s_{0}
corresponds to a unique successor or predecessor of
\rho(g_{0})(l_{0})
, whose
\rho
-orbit corresponds to the rectangle
R_{1}
in
\mathcal{G}
. Thanks to the uniqueness of
g_{0}
, we get that
s_{0}
corresponds to a unique successor or predecessor of
l_{0}
, say
l_{1}
. By repeating the previous procedure, we can construct a rectangle path
l_{0},...,l_{k}
in
\mathcal{P}
, that we will call
the lift on
\mathcal{P}
of
R_{0},s_{0},R_{1},s_{1}....,R_{k-1},s_{k-1},R_{k}
starting from
l_{0}
.

The previous rectangle path depends on our choice of
l_{0}
. For any
g\in G
, it is easy to check that the lift on
\mathcal{P}
of
R_{0},s_{0},R_{1},s_{1}....,R_{k-1},s_{k-1},R_{k}
starting from
\rho(g)(l_{0})
, corresponds to the image by
\rho(g)
of
l_{0},...,l_{k}
. We conclude that the rectangle path
R_{0},s_{0},R_{1},s_{1}....,R_{k-1},s_{k-1},R_{k}
can be lifted to a
\rho
-orbit of rectangle paths in
\mathcal{P}
.

Remark 11.9
.

One can check, using our previous construction, that the set of lifts on
\mathcal{P}
of the projection on
\mathcal{G}
of any rectangle path
l_{0},...,l_{k}
in
\mathcal{P}
, coincides with the
\rho
-orbit of
l_{0},...,l_{k}
. Conversely, it is also true that the projection on
\mathcal{G}
of any lift on
\mathcal{P}
of a rectangle path
R_{0},s_{0},R_{1},s_{1}....,R_{k-1},s_{k-1},R_{k}
in
\mathcal{G}
coincides with
R_{0},s_{0},R_{1},s_{1}....,R_{k-1},s_{k-1},R_{k}
.

On the proofs of Theorems D and E

According to Theorem B’, an equivalence class of geometric types of
\mathcal{R}
describes up to conjugacy the lift of
\rho
on the bifoliated plane up to surgeries along
\Gamma
, the set of boundary periodic points of
\mathcal{R}
. We would like at this point to define a combinatorial object describing a strong Markovian action up to conjugacy.

Definition 11.10
.

Let
\mathcal{G}
be a geometric type and
\mathcal{A}
a finite set of closed rectangle paths in
\mathcal{G}
. We will call
(\mathcal{G},\mathcal{A})
a
geometric type with cycles
.

Two geometric types with cycles
(\mathcal{G},\mathcal{A})
and
(\mathcal{G}^{\prime},\mathcal{A}^{\prime})
will be called
equivalent
if there exists an equivalence
h
between
G
and
G^{\prime}
(see Definition
3.10
) that bijectively associates closed rectangle paths in
\mathcal{A}
to closed rectangle paths in
\mathcal{A}^{\prime}
.
∎

Remark 11.11
.

Using Remark
3.11
, we can show that any equivalence class of geometric types with cycles contains only a finite number of geometric types with cycles.

Once again, choose a set of representatives
\{r_{1},...,r_{n}\}
for every rectangle orbit in
\mathcal{R}
, an orientation on
\mathcal{P}
and an orientation of the stable and unstable foliations in every
r_{i}
such that inside every
r_{i}
the positive stable direction followed by the positive unstable direction define an orientation on
\mathcal{P}
compatible with our initial choice of orientation. By Remark
4.9
, together with this choice of representatives and orientations we can associate to
\mathcal{R}
a unique geometric type
\mathcal{G}
.

Recall that to every boundary periodic point
\gamma\in\Gamma
we can associate the infinite set of cycles around
\gamma
, namely the set of closed rectangle paths
\text{Cycl}(\gamma)
(see Definition
11.5
). Denote by
\mathcal{A}
the projection in
G
of the infinite set of closed rectangle paths
\underset{\gamma\in\Gamma}{\cup}\text{Cycl}(\gamma)
.

Definition 11.12
.

We will call
(G,\mathcal{A})
a
geometric type with cycles associated to
\mathcal{R}
or more simply a
geometric type with cycles of
\mathcal{R}
. We will also call a geometric type with cycles of some strong Markovian action
\rho
, a
geometric type with cycles associated to
\rho
or a
geometric type with cycles of
\rho
.
∎

A geometric type with cycles of
\mathcal{R}
is a geometric type with cycles in the sense of Definition
11.10
. Indeed, using our previous notations, since
\Gamma
consists of a finite number of
\rho
-orbits of points in
\mathcal{P}
(see Proposition
5.2
), by Remark
11.7
we have that
\underset{\gamma\in\Gamma}{\cup}\text{Cycl}(\gamma)
consists of finitely many closed rectangle paths in
\mathcal{P}
up to the action of
\rho
. Hence, thanks to Remark
11.9
, we get that
\mathcal{A}
is a finite set of closed rectangle paths in
G
.

Moreover, as in the case of geometric types:

Remark 11.13
.

By our previous construction, the following choices of representatives and orientations define a unique geometric type with cycles of
\mathcal{R}
:

(1)

a choice of representatives
\{r_{1},...,r_{n}\}
for every rectangle orbit in
\mathcal{R}

(2)

a choice of orientation on
\mathcal{P}

(3)

a choice of orientation of the stable and unstable foliations in every
r_{i}
such that inside every
r_{i}
the positive stable direction followed by the positive unstable direction define an orientation on
\mathcal{P}
compatible with our previous choice of orientation

If
\gamma
is a boundary periodic point in
\mathcal{R}
, the construction of
\text{Cycl}(\gamma)
(see Definition
11.5
) does not depend on our choice of representatives of rectangle orbits in
\mathcal{R}
, but also it does not depend on our choice of orientations of
\mathcal{P}
or of the stable and unstable foliations inside the
r_{i}
. Hence,
\underset{\gamma\in\Gamma}{\cup}\text{Cycl}(\gamma)
is an infinite set of closed rectangle paths canonically associated to
\mathcal{R}
. It follows that by changing our choices of representatives and orientations, we produce
(G^{\prime},\mathcal{A}^{\prime})
a new geometric type with cycles associated to
\mathcal{R}
, such that the rectangle paths in
\mathcal{A}^{\prime}
and in
\mathcal{A}
lift to the exact same set of rectangle paths in
\mathcal{P}
. By repeating our proof of Theorem
4.10
, it is now not hard to show that

Theorem 11.14

(Theorem-Definition D’)
.

Take
\rho:G\rightarrow\text{Homeo}(\mathcal{P})
an orientation preserving strong Markovian action on
\mathcal{P}
leaving invariant a strong Markovian family
\mathcal{R}
. The set of geometric types with cycles associated to
\mathcal{R}
is included in a unique class of equivalent geometric types with cycles.

We would like now to prove the following generalization of Theorem E for strong Markovian actions, according to which any equivalence class of geometric types with cycles associated to some strong Markovian action describes the action up to conjugacy. More precisely,

Theorem 11.15

(Theorem E’)
.

Let
\rho_{1}:G_{1}\rightarrow\text{Homeo}(\mathcal{P}_{1})

\rho_{2}:G_{2}\rightarrow\text{Homeo}(\mathcal{P}_{2})
be two orientation preserving strong Markovian actions on
\mathcal{P}_{1}
and
\mathcal{P}_{2}
preserving the strong Markovian families
\mathcal{R}_{1},\mathcal{R}_{2}
. Denote by
\mathcal{F}_{1}^{s,u},\mathcal{F}_{2}^{s,u}
the stable and unstable foliations of
\rho_{1},\rho_{2}
. If the equivalence classes geometric types with cycles associated to
\mathcal{R}_{1},\mathcal{R}_{2}
are the same, then there exists
\beta
an isomorphism from
G_{1}
to
G_{2}
and
H
a homeomorphism from
\mathcal{P}_{1}
to
\mathcal{P}_{2}
such that :

•

the image by
H
of any stable/unstable leaf in
\mathcal{F}_{1}^{s,u}
is a stable/unstable leaf in
\mathcal{F}_{2}^{s,u}

•

for every
g\in G_{1}
and every
x\in\mathcal{P}_{1}
we have

H(\rho_{1}(g)(x))=\rho_{2}(\beta(g))(H(x))

We remark here that Theorem E is a direct consequence of Theorem E’ and Barbot’s theorem (see Theorem
2.59
).

Proof.

Let
\Gamma_{1},\Gamma_{2}
be the boundary periodic points of
\mathcal{R}_{1},\mathcal{R}_{2}
in
\mathcal{P}_{1},\mathcal{P}_{2}
and
\mathcal{C}_{1},\mathcal{C}_{2}
the set of all cycles around the points in
\Gamma_{1}
and
\Gamma_{2}
respectively. Denote by
\overline{\mathcal{P}_{1}}
,
\overline{\mathcal{P}_{2}}
the bifoliated planes of
\rho_{1}
,
\rho_{2}
up to surgeries along
\Gamma_{1}
,
\Gamma_{2}
together with
\overline{\rho_{1}}:\overline{G_{1}}\rightarrow\text{Homeo}(\overline{\mathcal{P}_{1}})

\overline{\rho_{2}}:\overline{G_{2}}\rightarrow\text{Homeo}(\overline{\mathcal{P}_{2}})
the lifts of
\rho_{1},\rho_{2}
on
\overline{\mathcal{P}_{1}}
,
\overline{\mathcal{P}_{2}}
and
\overline{\pi_{1}},\overline{\pi_{2}}
the projections from
\overline{\mathcal{P}_{1}}
,
\overline{\mathcal{P}_{2}}
to
\mathcal{P}_{1},\mathcal{P}_{2}
. Denote also by
\overline{\mathcal{F}_{1}^{s,u}},\overline{\mathcal{F}_{2}^{s,u}}
the stable and unstable foliations of
\overline{\rho_{1}},\overline{\rho_{2}}
, by
\overline{\Gamma_{1}},\overline{\Gamma_{2}}
the lifts of
\Gamma_{1},\Gamma_{2}
on
\overline{\mathcal{P}_{1}},\overline{\mathcal{P}_{2}}
and by
\overline{\mathcal{R}_{1}},\overline{\mathcal{R}_{2}}
the lifts of
\mathcal{R}_{1},\mathcal{R}_{2}
on
\overline{\mathcal{P}_{1}},\overline{\mathcal{P}_{2}}
. Finally, let
\mathfrak{p_{1}},\mathfrak{p_{2}}
be the morphisms from
\overline{G_{1}},\overline{G_{2}}
to
G_{1},G_{2}
and
\overline{\mathcal{C}_{1}},\overline{\mathcal{C}_{2}}
be the sets of rectangle paths in
\overline{\mathcal{R}_{1}},\overline{\mathcal{R}_{2}}
, whose projections by
\overline{\pi_{1}},\overline{\pi_{2}}
belong in
\mathcal{C}_{1},\mathcal{C}_{2}
.

Choose
\{\overline{r^{1}_{1}},...,\overline{r^{1}_{n}}\}
,
\{\overline{r^{2}_{1}},...,\overline{r^{2}_{n}}\}
a set of representatives for every rectangle orbit in
\overline{\mathcal{R}_{1}}
and
\overline{\mathcal{R}_{2}}
, and also orient the foliations
\overline{\mathcal{F}_{1}^{s,u}}
and
\overline{\mathcal{F}_{2}^{s,u}}
so that the unique geometric types associated to
\overline{\mathcal{R}_{1}}
and
\overline{\mathcal{R}_{2}}
for the previous choices of representatives and orientations coincide (see Proposition
8.6
and Corollary
8.7.1
). Denote by
\mathcal{G}
the previous geometric type and recall that
\mathcal{G}
belongs to the common equivalence class of geometric types associated to
\mathcal{R}_{1},\mathcal{R}_{2}
.

Given a rectangle path in
\overline{\mathcal{R}_{1}}
, by projecting it by
\overline{\pi_{1}}
to a rectangle path in
\mathcal{R}_{1}
and then to a rectangle path in
\mathcal{G}
, we define similarly to the case of
\mathcal{R}_{1}
, the
projection in
\mathcal{G}
of a rectangle path in
\overline{\mathcal{R}_{1}}
. We similarly define the projection in
\mathcal{G}
of a rectangle path in
\overline{\mathcal{R}_{2}}
. Conversely, given a rectangle path in
\mathcal{G}
, by considering the set of all its lifts in
\mathcal{R}_{1}
and then the set of rectangle paths in
\overline{\mathcal{R}_{1}}
that project to the previous set of rectangle paths in
\overline{\mathcal{R}_{1}}
, we define the
set of lifts in
\overline{\mathcal{R}_{1}}
of a rectangle path in
\mathcal{G}
. We similarly define the
set of lifts in
\overline{\mathcal{R}_{2}}
of a rectangle path in
\mathcal{G}
.

By our hypothesis, if
\mathcal{C}_{1}^{\mathcal{G}},\mathcal{C}_{2}^{\mathcal{G}}
denote the projections on
\mathcal{G}
of
\mathcal{C}_{1},\mathcal{C}_{2}
(or equivalently of
\overline{\mathcal{C}_{1}},\overline{\mathcal{C}_{2}}
), then we know that the geometric types with cycles
(\mathcal{G},\mathcal{C}_{1}^{\mathcal{G}})
and
(\mathcal{G},\mathcal{C}_{2}^{\mathcal{G}})
are equivalent. By eventually using the previous equivalence, we can assume without any loss of generality that
(\mathcal{G},\mathcal{C}_{1}^{\mathcal{G}})=(\mathcal{G},\mathcal{C}_{2}^{\mathcal{G}})
. For the sake of simplicity, we will denote
\mathcal{A}:=\mathcal{C}_{1}^{\mathcal{G}}=\mathcal{C}_{2}^{\mathcal{G}}
. Consider a rectangle in
\{\overline{r^{1}_{1}},...,\overline{r^{1}_{n}}\}
and another rectangle in
\{\overline{r^{2}_{1}},...,\overline{r^{2}_{n}}\}
such that the two rectangles are of the same type. We will fix those rectangles as the origin rectangles in
\overline{\mathcal{R}_{1}}
and
\overline{\mathcal{R}_{2}}
.

Thanks to Theorem B’, there exist an isomorphism
\alpha:\overline{G_{1}}\rightarrow\overline{G_{2}}
and a homeomorphism
h:\overline{\mathcal{P}_{1}}\rightarrow\overline{\mathcal{P}_{2}}
such that:

•

the image by
h
of any oriented stable/unstable leaf in
\overline{\mathcal{F}_{1}^{s,u}}
is an oriented stable/unstable leaf in
\overline{\mathcal{F}_{2}^{s,u}}

•

for every
g\in\overline{G_{1}}
and every
x\in\overline{\mathcal{P}_{1}}
we have

h(\overline{\rho_{1}}(g)(x))=\overline{\rho_{2}}(\alpha(g))(h(x))

Let us now show that that under the additional hypothesis on the cycles of
\mathcal{R}_{1}
and
\mathcal{R}_{2}
the map
h
projects to a conjugacy between
\rho_{1}
and
\rho_{2}
. Recall first that by construction of
h
(see Proposition
10.4
), if
path_{1}
is a centered rectangle path in
\overline{\mathcal{R}_{1}}
, then its image by
h
is the centered rectangle path in
\overline{\mathcal{R}_{2}}
associated to
path_{1}
. In other words, the image of
path_{1}
by
h
defines a rectangle path in
\overline{\mathcal{R}_{2}}
, say
path_{2}
, such that the projections of
path_{1}
and
path_{2}
on
\mathcal{G}
coincide. It follows that
h(\overline{\mathcal{C}_{1}})
corresponds to a rectangle path in
\overline{\mathcal{C}_{2}}
and vice versa.

The positive stable direction followed by the positive unstable direction define in
\overline{\mathcal{P}_{1}}
and
\overline{\mathcal{P}_{2}}
two orientations. Endow
\overline{\mathcal{P}_{1}}
and
\overline{\mathcal{P}_{2}}
with the previous orientations.

Let
\overline{\gamma}\in\overline{\Gamma_{1}}
and
...,s_{-1},s_{0},s_{1},s_{2},...
be the stable leaves in
\overline{\mathcal{F}_{1}^{s}}
containing
\overline{\gamma}
ordered counter-clockwisely. In other words,
s_{i+1}
is not separated from
s_{i}
(see Proposition
6.8
) and for any point
x\in s_{i}
, the direction in
s_{i}
pointing away from
\overline{\gamma}
followed by the direction pointing inside the connected component of
\overline{\mathcal{P}_{1}}-s_{i}
containing
s_{i+1}
(see Remark
6.7
) defines an orientation compatible with our choice of orientation of
\overline{\mathcal{P}_{1}}
.

In Proposition
7.3
, we showed that using the previous orientations, one can define a canonical base of
\text{Stab}(\overline{\gamma})
as follows
9
9

9

We remark here that the section in which Proposition
7.3
was proven dealt only with strong Markovian actions arising from pseudo-Anosov flows. However, Proposition
7.3
does not use at all the pseudo-Anosov hypothesis
. Consider
\gamma:=\overline{\pi}(\overline{\gamma})
,
n
the smallest positive integer such that
\overline{\pi}(s_{n})=\overline{\pi}(s_{0})
(
n
corresponds to the number of stable prongs of
\gamma
) and define

•

m^{1}_{\overline{\gamma}}
as the unique element in
\text{ker}(\mathfrak{p_{1}})\leq\overline{G_{1}}
taking
s_{0}
to
s_{n}

•

p_{\overline{\gamma}}^{1}
as the unique element in
\overline{G_{1}}
taking
s_{0}
to a stable leaf in
\{s_{1},...,s_{n-1},s_{n}\}
and such that
\mathfrak{p}(p_{\overline{\gamma}}^{1})=g_{\overline{\gamma}}^{1}
, where
g_{\overline{\gamma}}^{1}
is the generator of
\text{Stab}(\gamma)
in
G_{1}
acting as an expansion on
\mathcal{F}^{s}(\gamma)

We similarly define
m^{2}_{h(\overline{\gamma})}
and
p^{2}_{h(\overline{\gamma})}
for
h(\overline{\gamma})\in\overline{\Gamma_{2}}
, using our choice of orientation of
\overline{\mathcal{P}_{2}}
. Let us now prove that

\alpha(m^{1}_{\overline{\gamma}})=m^{2}_{h(\overline{\gamma})}

Indeed, take any cycle
l_{0},...,l_{k}=l_{0}
in
\mathcal{C}_{1}
around
\gamma
. By definition of a cycle, the previous closed rectangle path goes once around
\gamma
in
\mathcal{P}_{1}
. It is therefore not hard to see that if
\overline{l_{0}},\overline{l_{1}},...,\overline{l_{k}}
is a rectangle path in
\overline{\mathcal{C}_{1}}
that lifts
l_{0},...,l_{k}
, then
\overline{l_{k}}=\overline{\rho_{1}}(m^{1}_{\overline{\gamma}})(\overline{l_{0}})
or
\overline{l_{k}}=\overline{\rho_{1}}((m^{1}_{\overline{\gamma}})^{-1})(\overline{l_{0}})
. Naturally, the same applies for any element of
\overline{\mathcal{C}_{2}}
. Assume without any loss of generality that
\overline{l_{k}}=\overline{\rho_{1}}(m^{1}_{\overline{\gamma}})(\overline{l_{0}})
. Since
h
sends the rectangle paths in
\overline{\mathcal{C}_{1}}
to rectangle paths in
\overline{\mathcal{C}_{2}}
and is equivariant with respect to the group actions, we get that

\alpha(m^{1}_{\overline{\gamma}})=(m^{2}_{h(\overline{\gamma})})^{\pm}

Thanks to our proof of Theorem B’,
h
sends positively oriented stable/unstable segments in
\overline{\mathcal{F}_{1}^{s,u}}
to positively oriented stable/unstable segments in
\overline{\mathcal{F}_{2}^{s,u}}
. It follows that if
\overline{\rho_{1}}(m^{1}_{\overline{\gamma}})
acts as a positive translation on the counter-clockwise ordered set of stable separatrices on
\overline{\gamma}
, then
\overline{\rho_{2}}(\alpha(m^{1}_{\overline{\gamma}}))
must also act as a positive translation on the counter-clockwise ordered set of stable separatrices on
h(\overline{\gamma})
. This implies that

\alpha(m^{1}_{\overline{\gamma}})=m^{2}_{h(\overline{\gamma})}

Next, let us prove that

\alpha(p^{1}_{\overline{\gamma}})=p^{2}_{\overline{\gamma}}

Once again since
h
is equivariant with respect to the group actions,
\alpha
must send a basis of
\text{Stab}(\overline{\gamma})
to a basis of
\text{Stab}(h(\overline{\gamma}))
. It follows that there exist
a\in\mathbb{Z}
such that

\alpha(p^{1}_{\overline{\gamma}})=(m^{2}_{h(\overline{\gamma})})^{a}\cdot(p^{2}_{\overline{\gamma}})^{\pm}

Using once more the equivariance of
h
, we have that
\alpha(p^{1}_{\overline{\gamma}})
must act as an expansion on the set of stable leaves of
h(\overline{\gamma})
. Using the fact that
m^{2}_{\overline{\gamma}}\in\text{ker}(\mathfrak{p_{2}})
and Proposition
6.13
, we get that

\alpha(p^{1}_{\overline{\gamma}})=(m^{2}_{h(\overline{\gamma})})^{a}\cdot p^{2}_{\overline{\gamma}}

Finally, recall that
\rho_{1}(p^{1}_{\overline{\gamma}})
(resp.
\rho_{1}(m^{1}_{\overline{\gamma}})
) acts as a translation on the set of stable leaves of
\overline{\gamma}
by
+1,+2,...
or
+n
(resp.
+n
), where
n
is the total number of stable prongs of
\gamma
. By the equivariance of
h
, a similar statement should be true for
\rho_{2}(\alpha(p^{1}_{\overline{\gamma}}))
. This forces
a=0
and implies that

\alpha(p^{1}_{\overline{\gamma}})=p^{2}_{\overline{\gamma}}

To conclude, thanks to Proposition
6.12
,
\text{ker}(\mathfrak{p_{1}})
is normally generated in
\overline{G_{1}}
by the set
\{m^{1}_{\overline{\gamma}}|\overline{\gamma}\in\overline{\Gamma}\}
. Same for
\text{ker}(\mathfrak{p_{2}})
. Our previous arguments imply that
\alpha(\text{ker}(\mathfrak{p_{1}}))=\text{ker}(\mathfrak{p_{2}})
. By Propositions
6.12
,
6.13
, this implies that the isomorphism
\alpha
projects to an isomorpshism
\beta
between
G_{1}
and
G_{2}
and that
h
projects to a homeomorphism
H
between
\mathcal{P}_{1}
and
\mathcal{P}_{2}
such that

•

the image by
H
of any stable/unstable leaf in
\mathcal{F}_{1}^{s,u}
is a stable/unstable leaf in
\mathcal{F}_{2}^{s,u}

•

for every
g\in G_{1}
and every
x\in\mathcal{P}_{1}
we have

H(\rho_{1}(g)(x))=\rho_{2}(\beta(g))(H(x))

We thus get the desired result.
∎
